# Colossians

## 1:1 Introduction
[Philipp Melanchthon, Scholia in Epistolam Pauli ad Colossenses (Wittenberg, 1545). English: machine translation, The Faith Received (Mere Orthodoxy); the original follows each comment.]
SCHOLIA ON THE EPISTLE OF PAUL TO THE COLOSSIANS, REVISED AGAIN BY THE AUTHOR.

PHIL. MEL.

M. D. XLV.

AS IS CUSTOMARY IN OTHER WRITINGS to establish at the beginning what the argument of the work is. So in the Pauline Epistles, the reader must first be warned about what is being said, what the status of each Epistle is, which as the Greeks say ὑποκειμενον, so that he may know what he ought to seek and expect from the whole writing. Nor will you read usefully if you only excerpt mutilated sentences from it; the series of the whole discourse must be known, so that from it a certain opinion may be gathered which can fortify and teach the conscience, which those who do not do, they often wander in general from the scope and purpose of the whole writing. This could happen to the incautious in this Epistle, so that he might think Paul teaches nothing except that Mosaic ceremonies are antiquated, if he only excerpts this little sentence, Let no one judge you in part of a feast day &c. But that is to tear apart the scriptures, not to expound them; the whole Epistle must be known, and all the parts must be compared among themselves, and it must be seen how they agree among themselves.

The argument and status of this Epistle is, therefore, what the Gospel is. The Apostles promise a certain new doctrine to the world; he defines it here, and he does not define it briefly, but copiously distinguishes from Christian righteousness human righteousness, collected by our industry, by our strengths, either from the precepts of men, or from the Mosaic law, or the Decalogue.

Finally, he delivers certain precepts concerning morals. For thus the Apostles are accustomed first to teach the Gospel and justification, afterwards they deliver the precepts of morals. The world, however, delivers only the precepts of morals, and establishes him to be righteous who expresses and performs those in appearance. But Paul teaches that reconciliation does not happen to us on account of any merits of ours, but through faith, if we believe that our sins have been freely forgiven us on account of Christ, just as Isaiah also wrote, By his knowledge he shall justify many. That, indeed, is the greatest consolation to consciences, to perceive that God freely grants us the remission of sins, and freely justifies us. For when God begins to judge hearts and to terrify, and to show wrath against sin, no works of ours can pacify the conscience, or be opposed to the wrath and judgment of God. Just as the Prophet also testifies, when he says, Enter not into judgment with thy servant, for in thy sight shall no man living be justified. It would be necessary, therefore, to despair of salvation, unless God had exhibited to us Christ, through whom he promises himself to be willing to forgive, and to justify freely. There is therefore here a harbor and refuge for the afflicted conscience, to know that we are thus justified before God, and thus please God, if we trust that sins are forgiven through Christ. Minds conceive faith in this way, if previously terrified they have acknowledged their sins. For it is necessary that fear precede faith, or that repentance precede the remission of sins, just as Christ says Luke 24, Preaching repentance and remission of sins. Now many teach faith and the remission of sins, but do not teach repentance. But faith without repentance is nothing but an empty dream. For thus wrote Isaiah, chap. 66 Where shall the Lord dwell? In a contrite and humbled spirit.

When, however, the heart conceives faith through the Holy Spirit, it is already reborn and puts on new affections, humility, chastity, charity, and then obedience pleases God because the person pleases Him.

Latin:

SCHOLIA IN EPISTOLAM PAULI AD COLOSSENSES ITERUM AB AUTORE RECO GNITA.

PHIL. MEL.

M. D. XLV.

SICUT IN ALIIS SCRIPTIS principio constitui solet, quod sit argumentum operis. Ita in Paulinis Epistolis, Primum monendus est lector, qua de re dicatur, qui sit cuiusque Epistolae status, quod ut graeci dicunt ὑποκειμενον, ut quid petere & expectare ex toto scripto debeat, sciat. Nec utiliter legeris, sitantum mutilatas sententias inde excerpseris, totius orationis series cognoscenda est, ut inde colligatur certa sententia, quae munire conscientiam & docere possit, quod qui non faciunt, ii saepe in universum aberrant a totius scripti scopo ac proposito. Id in hac Epistola accidere possit incauto, ut Paulum nihil docere existimet, nisi caeremonias Mosaicas esse antiquatas, si hanc tantum sententiolam excerpat, Nemo iudicet vos in parte diei festi &c. Verum, id est, discerpere scripturas, non enarrare, Tota Epistola cognoscenda est, & inter se partes omnes conferendae, & quomodo inter se consentiant videndum.

Est itaque argumentum huius Epistolae & status, quid sit Evangelium. Novam quandam doctrinam orbi terrarum pollicentur Apostoli, eam hic defivit, nec definit breviter, sed copiose discernit a iusticia Christiana iusticiam humanam, collectam nostra industria, nostris viribus, vel ex praeceptis hominum, vel ex lege Mosaica, seu Decalogo.

Postremo tradit quaedam praecepta de moribus. Nam ita solent Apostoli primum docere Evangelium & iustificationem, postea tradunt praecepta morum. Mundus autem tantum praecepta morum tradit, & iustum eum esse constituit, qui illa in speciem exprimit & facit. Sed Paulus docet reconciliationem nobis non propter ulla nostra merita contingere, sed per fidem, si credamus nobis propter Christum gratis condonata esse peccata, sicut & Esaias scripsit, Noticia eius iustificabit multos. Ea vero maxima consolatio est conscientiis, sentire quod gratis donet nobis Deus remissionem peccatorum, & gratis iustificet nos. Quando enim incipit Deus iudicare corda ac terrere, & iram adversus peccatum ostendere, nulla opera nostra possunt conscientiam pacificare, aut opponi irae ac iudicio Dei. Sicut & Propheta testatur, cum inquit. Non intres in iudicium cum servo tuo, quia non iustificabitur coram te omnis vivens. Necesse esset igitur desperare de salute, nisi Deus exhibuisset nobis Christum, per quem pollicetur se velle ignoscere, ac gratis iustificare. Est ergo hic portus & receptus adflictae conscientiae, scire, quod ita iustificemur coram Deo, ita placemus Deum, si confidamus quod remisse sint peccata per Christum. Fidem vero ita concipiunt animi, si ante perterrefacti agnoverint sua peccata. Necesse est. n. timorem praecedere fidem, seu paenitentiam praecedere remissionem peccatorum, sicut Christus Lucae ultimo ait, Praedicantes paenitentiam & remissionem peccatorum, Nunc multi fidem, & remissionem peccatorum docent, paenitentiam non docent. At nihil est fides sinc paenitentia, nisi inane somnium. Sic enim scripsit Esaias, cap. 66. Ubi habitabit Dominus? In spiritu contrito & humiliato.

Cum autem cor concipit fidem per Spiritum sanctum, iam renatum est induitque novos adfectus, humilitatem, castitatem, charitatem, & deinde placet obedientia deo propterea quia personae placet.

## 1:1 Argument
CHAP. I.

Latin:

CAP. I.

## 1:1-2
Paul, an Apostle of Jesus Christ by the will of God, and Timothy the brother, to the saints who are at Colossae, and to the faithful brethren in Christ.

Latin:

Paulus Apostolus Iesu Christi per voluntatem Dei, & Timotheus fra-ter, sanctis qui sunt Colossis, & fidelibus fratribus in Christo.

## 1:3
Grace to you & peace from God our Father, & the Lord Jesus Christ.

In the epigraph or inscription, Paul is accustomed to proclaim his office or function, so that we may know that what he himself teaches is the word of God. Not otherwise than Moses & the other Prophets preface, The Lord has spoken, Thus says the Lord, The mouth of the Lord has spoken, & similar things. Thus Paul signifies that he was not made an Apostle by human election, but was called to this function by a divine voice. Therefore, just as God willed that the Israelites should believe Moses, so He wills that we should believe Paul, who is scattering into the world a certain new doctrine & one unlike the Mosaic law. Therefore, just as Moses testified by miracles that he had been sent by God, so also did the miracles performed testify that Paul was sent by God. Therefore, the rule which is in the Gospel is to be observed concerning Paul, He who hears you, hears me. It is safe to believe Paul's doctrine, no differently than a manifest voice of God from heaven.

Strabo counts Colossae among the towns of Phrygia, not that one where Ilium was, but that which they call the Great. Strabo makes it a part of προριον φρυγιαν, where he says that the greatest cities are Apamea and Laodicea, and that around these are situated the towns of Aphrodisias, Colossae, and Themisonium. The location of these towns can be seen in the tables of Ptolemy. Paul, however, mentions Laodicea here. It is ridiculous that some believed the Colossians to be Rhodians, because a Colossus of great size was there.

Latin:

Gratia vobis & pax a Deo patre nostro, & domino Iesu Christo.

In Epigrapha seu inscriptione solet Paulus officium suum, seu functionem praedicare, ut sciamus verbum Dei esse, id quod ipse docet. Non secus atque Moses & caeteri Prophetae praefantur, Locutus est Dominus, Haec dixit Dominus, Os domini locutum est, & similia. Sic Paulus significat se non humana electione, factum esse Apostolum, sed divina voce vocatum esse ad hunc functionem. Proinde quemadmodum voluit Deus, ut Mosi crederent Israelitae, ita vult Paulo nos credere, novam quandam doctrinam & dissimilem legis Mosaicae in mundum spargenti. Ideo sicut Moses miraculis testatus est, se divinitus missum fuisse, ita & Paulum miracula facta testabantur a Deo missum esse. Est igitur de Paulo servanda regula, quae est in Evangelio, Qui vos audierit, me audiet. Tutum est Pauli doctrinae credere, non aliter atque manifestae de caelo voci Dei.

Colossas numerat Strabo inter oppida Phrygiae, non illius ubi Ilium fuit, sed eius quam magnam vocant. Strabo eius partem facit προριον φρυγιαν, ubi dicit maximas urbes esse Apameam & Laodiceam, & circa has sita esse oppida Aphrosiada, Colossas, Themisonium. Horum oppidorum situs cerni in Ptolemaei tabulis potest. Paulus autem hic Laodiceae meminit. Ridiculum est, quod quidam Colossenses crediderunt Rhodios esse, quiae ibi magná mole Colossus fuerit.

## 1:3
Grace and peace.

Grace signifies simply the remission of sins or the favor of God. Peace, however, signifies not only the tranquility of conscience, or joy, in the way Paul uses it Rom. 5 Being justified by faith we have peace toward God, but it signifies also generally prosperous circumstances, according to Hebrew custom. Therefore Paul wishes for grace and the gifts of the Holy Spirit, which accompany the remission of sins, faith, joy of mind, and similar goods. Where we ought to learn, since Paul wishes for these to happen to them divinely, that they cannot be obtained by our industry alone, or by our own powers, the peace of conscience toward God, trust toward God, patience in adverse circumstances, true humility, and chastity, etc. Therefore, we ought not to ask for and expect goods of this kind from God, according to that saying of James 1: If any of you lacks wisdom, let him ask of God, who gives to all liberally.

Latin:

Gratia & pax.

Gratia significat simpliciter remissionem peccatorum seu favorem Dei. Pax autem significat non tantum conscientiae tranquillitatem, seu gaudium, quomodo usurpat Paulus Roma. v. Iustificati ex fide pacem habemus crga Deum, sed significat etiam generaliter res secundas, Hebraica consuetudine. Optat igitur Paulus gratiam & dona Spiritus sancti, quae comitantur remissionem peccatorum, fidem, gaudium mentis & similia bona. Ubi discere debemus, cum optet Paulus haec divinitus illis contingere, quod sola nostra industria, aut nostris viribus non possint comparari, pax conscientiae erga Deum, fiducia erga Deum, pacientia in rebus adversis, vera humilitas, & castitas, &c. Itaque non huiusmodi bona postulare & expectare divinitus debemus, iuxta illud Iacobi. i. Si quis vestrum indiget sapientia, postulet a Deo, qui dat omnibus adfluenter.

## 1:3
From God the Father and the Lord Jesus.

Therefore he joins the Father and the Son, either to signify that the Son is also God, who grants grace and peace. For Christ also says of himself in John, my peace I give to you. Likewise, The Holy Spirit shall receive of mine, etc. Or because these things are granted to us on account of Christ, for God promised that he would give them to us on account of the Son, just as Christ also says, If you ask anything of the Father in my name, he will give it to you; ask and you shall receive.

Hence this brief verse not only teaches us that all good things are to be asked for and expected from God, but it also builds up faith and encourages us to ask, since it makes mention of Christ. For through him it signifies that goods of every kind are promised to us, and that our hope is not deceived, provided only that we look to him, and on account of him ask the Father for all things of which there is need.

Latin:

A Deo patre & domino Iesu.

Ideo coniungit patrem & filium, sive ut significet & filium Deum esse, qui donet gratiam & pacem. Nam de se quoque in Ioanne inquit Christus, pacem de vobis. Item, Spiritus sanctus de meo accipiet &c. Sive quod propter Christum ista donentur nobis, promisit enim Deus se daturum nobis propter filium, sicut & Christus ait, Si quid petieritis patrem in omine meo, dabit vobis, petite & accipietis.

Proinde hic brevis versus non tantum docet nos, omnia bona divinitus postulanda & expectanda esse, sed etiam erigit fidem, & ad petendum hortatur, cum Christi mentionem facit. Nam per illum significat omnis generis bona nobis promissa esse, nec falli spem nostram, si modo hunc respiciamus, & propter hunc petamus a patre omnia quibus opus est.

## 1:3
We give thanks to God.

I might perhaps seem inept if I were to demand that Paul's discourse conform to the precepts of Rhetoric. I, however, think that Paul's oration can be better understood if the sequence and arrangement of all its parts are considered. For Paul did not write entirely without order or without reason, which the matter itself shows. He has his own places by which he prepares minds; he has his own certain method of teaching and narrating, which not to notice in explaining is nothing other than what the Greeks call dancing in the dark, or as Chrysostom says νυκτομαχειν..

There are, therefore, two places of the exordium: congratulation and imprecation. And this is almost the sum of the sentence: I congratulate you that the knowledge of the Gospel has come to you, and I pray that God may increase for you the knowledge of His will, and confirm you, so that you may be able to live rightly and endure persecutions, strengthened by the Holy Spirit.

The prior place of the exordium, namely the congratulation, has nothing obscure; it is only to be observed that Paul has used distribution, for when he could have said, I hear your piety being preached, he himself divided the name of the whole, κατα μερισμον I hear being preached . your faith, and charity and hope.

But this seems absurd, which he says concerning charity , that they do good to the saints on account of the hope of a reward or recompense, when they ought to do good on account of Christ, not for the sake of their own advantage. For Matth. vi. Christ says concerning the Pharisees, who bestow alms for the sake of their own advantage, Amen I say to you, they have received their reward. Furthermore, many similar examples exist in the scriptures, Jacob says Gen. xxviii. If the Lord shall give me bread to eat, and raiment to put on &c. the Lord shall be my God, as if indeed he worships God, only for the sake of reward or of the belly.

Concerning this passage I know it is accustomed to be disputed subtly. But we shall explain it, as best we can, most plainly. It is a matter of clearly spiritual judgment, and of most exercised minds, to understand what it is to do good works simply on account of the will of God, and not to seek a reward. For the flesh is so mercenary, that even the saints cannot cast off the love of reward. And so the doctrine of the law is both a pedagogy, to invite with rewards, and to deter with punishments. But in the saints the depraved affection seeking its own is gradually mortified. And the saints obey the will of God in such a way, that even if He were going to give no rewards, they would desire to obey.

Therefore, on account of the will of God, they willingly suffer the cross, death, and afflictions of every kind. What, then, is the use of promises? Why was it necessary to make mention of a reward, if it ought not to sharpen one to work well? I answer, both in punishments and in rewards, the saints look upon the will of God, as is notable. Punishments signify wrath; rewards signify grace and mercy. Therefore, the saints are terrified by the mention of punishments, not because they would not willingly suffer all hard things, provided they had God propitious, but because they understand that in those punishments God is angry, and therefore they dread the punishments. And again, rewards excite faith, because in them the mercy and grace of God are discerned. Consequently, the saints do not embrace the rewards, but the grace of God, when they seek rewards. If Jacob knows that he has God propitious, he can easily do without water, and bread, and clothing. If Paul knows that he has God propitious, he can easily be cast into the depths of hell. So also Job easily suffers all his goods to be snatched from him. For with his fortunes lost, we see how much patience he still exhibits. He blesses God, he confesses that those riches were gifts of God; The Lord gave, he says, the Lord has taken away. But finally, it is asked when he begins to doubt concerning the grace of God, when he seems to himself to have been cast off by God.

To these things, rewards show the magnitude of the goodness of God, for because God signifies that He not only favors those who believe, but also promises goods of every kind, faith is much more kindled, estimating the magnitude of goodness from the promises.

Finally, from the promises the saints also learn that all goods are the gifts and works of God; for God promises so that they may know from whom they ought to expect and request all things of which there is need.

Hope therefore, to return to the proposal, looks upon the will of God, expects goods, not on account of our dignity, but on account of the promises of God, and seeks rewards, because it discerns the grace and favor of God in the rewards.

Therefore also we from the day &c.

It is a prayer, by which he prays for the Colossians first, that a most abundant, or most firm, knowledge of the will of God may happen to them. Second, that they may bring forth good fruits. Third, that they may endure the cross, and in the cross learn the presence and benevolence of God toward them.

To know the will of God is seriously and truly to feel that God is angry with those who sin, and to shudder and fear the wrath of God. Again, also to feel and believe seriously and truly that God, on account of Christ, forgives sins, defends those received into grace, governs them by His Spirit, preserves them, wills that they be afflicted, and in some measure mortified, but yet that He consoles and helps them in the cross. That this is to know God appears from Jeremiah 9, 'Let him who glories glory in this, that he knows Me, that I am the Lord doing mercy and judgment and justice on the earth.' And from the Psalm, 'The beginning of wisdom is the fear of the Lord.' And 1 John 5, 'He who believes in the Son of God has the testimony of God in himself,' that is, he knows God certainly.

Latin:

Gratias agimus Deo.

Videar fortassis ineptus, si Pauli sermonem ad Rhetorica praecepta exigam. Ego tamen sic existimo intelligi melius posse orationem Paulinam, si series & dispositio omnium partium consideretur. Neque enim omnino nullo ordine, aut nulla ratione scripsit Paulus, id quod res ipsa ostendit. Habet suos locos, quibus praeparat animos, habet suam quandam docendi & narrandi rationem, quam in enarrando non animadvertere, quid aliud est, quam quod graeci dicunt in tenebris saltare seu ut Chrysostomus ait νυκτομαχειν.

Sunt itaque duo exordii loci, gratulatio & imprecatio. Estque haec fere sententiae summa, Gratulor vobis contigisse cognitionem Evangelii, et precor, ut Deus augeat vobis cognitionem voluntatis suae, & confirmet vos, ut recte vivere, & persecutiones perferre Spiritu sancto corroborati possitis.

Prior locus exordii, gratulatio videlicet nihil habet obscuri, tantum observandum est, Paulum esse distributione usum, nam cum posset dicere, audio praedicari vestram pietatem, ipse partitus est nomen totius, κατα μερισμον audio praedicari fidem vestram, & caritatem & spem.

Sed hoc videtur absurdum, quod de caritate dicit, eos benefacere sanctis propter spem praemii seu mercedis, cum benefacere propter Christum, non sui commodi caussa, debebant. Nam Matth. vi. inquit Christus de Pharisaeis, qui sui commodi causa Eleemosynas largiuntur, Amen dico vobis, receperunt mercedem suam, Porro multa similia exempla extant in scripturis, Iacob ait Gen. xxviii. Si Dominus dederit mihi panem & vescendum, et vestimentum ad induendum &c. erit mihi Dominus in Deum, quasi vero colat Deum, tantum mercedis seu ventris caussa.

De hoc loco scio subtiliter disputari solere. Nos vero explicabimus eum, quam poterimus, crassissime, Res est plane spiritualis iudicii, & exercitatissimarum mentium, intelligere quid sit bona opera facere simpliciter propter voluntatem Dei, nec quaerere mercedem. Nam caro ita est mercenaria, ut etiam sancti non possint abiicere amorem mercedis. Itaque doctrina legis est ac paedagogia, invitare premiis, ac poenis deterrere. Sed in sanctis paulatim mortificatur pravus adfectus sua quaerens. Et obtemperant sancti voluntatati Dei ita, ut etiam si premia nulla daturus esset, cupiant obtemperare.

Itaque propter voluntatem Dei libenter patiuntur crucem, mortem, & omnis generis adflictiones. Quid igitur prosunt promissiones? quid oportuit praemii mentionem facere, si non debet acuere ad bene operandum? Respondeo, & in paenis, et in premiis sancti voluntatem Dei intuentur, sicut insignis. Poenae significant iram, praemia significant gratiam & misericordiam. Itaque terrentur sancti mentione poenarum, non quin omnia dura libenter paterentur, modo haberent Deum propicium, Sed quia intelligunt in illis poenis. Deum irasci, ideo reformidant poenas, Et rursus premia excitant fidem, quia cernuntur in illis misericordia & gratia Dei, Proinde non praemia, sed gratiam Dei amplectuntur sancti cum praemia quaerunt. Iacob si sciat se habere Deum propicium, facile possit aqua, & pane, ac veste carere. Paulus si sciat se habere Deum propitium facile possit in inferos abiici. Ita & Iob facile sibi patitur eripi omnia bona. Nam amissis fortunis vidermus quantum adhuc pacientiam praestet. Benedicit Deo, fatetur illas opes Dei dona fuisse, Dominus dedit, inquit, Dominus abstulit. Sed postremo quaeritur cum de gratia Dei dubitare incipit, cum videtur sibi a Deo abiectus esse.

Ad haec, premia ostendunt magnitudinem bonitatis Dei, Quia enim significat Deus, se non solum favere aredentibus, sed etiam omnis generis bona pollicetur, multo magis accenditur fides, magnitudinem bonitatis ex promissionibus aestimans.

Postremo ex promissionibus etiam discunt sancti, omnia bona, Dei dona atque opera esse, Ideo enim promittit Deus ut sciant unde expectare ac postulare omnia quibus opus est, debeant.

Spes igitur, ut ad propositum redeam, intuetur voluntatem Dei, expectat bona, non propter nostram dignitatem, Sed propter promissiones Dei, ac premia quaerit, quia gratiam ac favorem Dei in premiis cernit.

Propterea & nos ex quo die &c.

Precatio est, qua imprecatur Colossensibus primum, ut contingat eis cognitio uberrrima, seu firmissima voluntatis Dei, Secundo, ut bonos fructus pariant. Tertio, ut perferant crucem, & in cruce addiscant praesentiam & benevolentiam Dei erga se.

Cognoscere voluntatem Dei, est serio & reucra sentire, quod Deus irascatur peceantibus, & perhorescere ac timere iram Dei. Rursus etiam serio & revera sentire & credere, quod Deus propter Christum condonet peccata, receptos in gratiam defendat, gubernet suo spiritu, servet, velit affligi, & carmen mortificari, sed tamen in cruce consoletur & iuvet. Quod autem hoc sit nosse Deum, apparet ex ix. Ieremiae, In hoc glorietur vir nosse me, quod sim Dominus faciens misericordiam & iudicium & iusticiam & in terra. Et ex Psalmo. Inicium sapientiae timor Domini. Et i. Ioan. v. Qui credit in filium Dei, habet testimonium Dei in se, hoc est, novit Deum certo.

## 1:9
That you may be filled with the knowledge, etc.

What he says, that you may be filled with the knowledge of His will, is to be understood thus, that there may be a firm and certain knowledge, because the flesh does not have a firm knowledge that God regards us, and either punishes or forgives, nor can it in any way conceive that immense power and magnitude, either of wrath or of goodness, therefore it is both devoid of fear, and rushes into sins. And because it lacks faith, and does not believe that God forgives or helps, it rushes afterwards into desperation. With this impiety one must always struggle, therefore he prays that a firm knowledge may befall them, so that the heart may seriously and truly feel both the wrath of God and His grace, and may be terrified and gladdened, as if a shipwrecked man knew for certain that he could swim to safety, and did not doubt about his life, he would be of a cheerful mind. For he is of an idle mind for this reason, because he perceives a certain firm reason and a sure way of emerging, and one which cannot fail. Thus here he wishes for us that we may hold a firm opinion, that God is present, and wills to have mercy on us, and to save us. Thus he used the word of fulfilling in Romans 4. Abraham was confirmed by faith, giving glory to God, and holding for certain a firm etc.

Latin:

Ut impleamini agnitione &c.

Quod ait, ut impleamini agnitione voluntatis eius, sic est accipiendum, ut firma & certa cognitio sit, quia caro non habet firmam cognitionem, quod Deus respiciat nos, & vel puniat vel ignoscat, nec ullo modo illam immensam vim et magnitudinem, aut irae aut bonitatis concipere potest, ideo & timore vacat, & ruit in peccata. Et quiae fide caret, nec credit Deum ignoscere aut iuvare, ruit postea in desperationem. Cum hac impietate semper est luctandum, ideo precatur, ut istis firmae cognitio continget, ut serio & vere cor sentiat cum iram Dei, tum gratiam, & pertimescat & exhilaretur, ut si naufragus sciret certo, se posse incolumen enatare, nec dubitaret de vita, esset hilari animo. Is enim ideo ocioso animo est, quia firmam quandam rationem, & certamuiam emergendi, & quae fallere non possit animadvertit. Ita hic optat nobis, ut firmam sententiam teneamus, quod Deus adsit, & velit nostri misereri, & nos servare. Sic usus est verbo implendi ad Roma. iiii. Abraham confirmatus est fide, dans gloriam Deo, & certo tenens firmam &c.

## 1:9
In all wisdom and prudence etc.

It is ταυτολογια, because the knowledge of the will of God is spiritual wisdom itself. For the flesh is ignorant of the will of God, although for the sake of amplification he added it, so that he might warn that God is not only to be known in this way, as I have said, but also that one must take care lest through false doctrine they allow this knowledge to be snatched from them, as for example. They lose this knowledge and faith simply, who by their own dignity and greatness of works, who by their own satisfactions wish the wrath of God to appease. Item he added on account of the cross. For it is a truly spiritual and secret wisdom, so to know God, that He then saves and guards us on account of Christ, when He allows us to be afflicted, when He does not help us in appearance, just as David says, Many say who shows us good, there is spiritual wisdom, to believe and to expect help from God, just as the Syrophoenician woman expects etc. and in the Psalm it is, From the morning watch even until the night, and Romans 12. That you may prove what is the will of God.

Latin:

In omni sapientia & prudentia &c.

Est ταυτολογια, quia cognitio voluntatis Dei est ipsa spiritualis sapientia. Caro enim ignorat voluntatem Dei, quanquam amplificandi causa adiecit, ut moneat non solum sic cognoscendum esse Deum, ut dixi, sed etiam cavendum, ne per falsam doctrinam sinant sibi hanc cognitionem cripi, ut verbi caussa. Amittunt hanc cognitionem & fidem simpliciter, qui sua dignitate & magnitudine operum, qui suis satisfactionibus volunt iram Dei placare. Item adiecit propter crucem. Est enim sapientia vere spiritualis & arcana, sic cognoscere Deum, quod tunc servet & custodiat nos propter Christum, cum sinit nos affligi, cum non iuvat nos in speciem, sicut David ait, Multi dicunt quis ostendit nobis bonum, ibi sapientia spiritualis est, credere & expectare opem a Deo, sicut mulier Syrophoenissa expectat &c. & in Psal. est, A custodia matutina usque ad noctem, & Roma. xii. Ut experiamini quae sit voluntaes Dei.

## 1:23
If indeed you continue in the faith grounded &c.

Up to this point he has defined the Gospel, now he adds a circumstance, how the benefits shown in the Gospel happen to us, as if he were saying, You promise huge things, the remission of sins, the Holy Spirit, eternal life. This is a huge treasure, but how shall we obtain such a treasure? Here he answers, but as in a short Epistle, briefly. Therefore these things are not to be passed over negligently, although it is a short particle, he says thus, You are redeemed, if you remain firm in the faith, that is, you have obtained the remission of sins, if you have believed that sins have been remitted on account of Christ. He pursued the same sentiment copiously in Romans iii. We reckon that a man is justified by faith without the works of the law.

And because this passage demands it, we shall speak briefly concerning justification, so that it may be understood how works and how faith are required. For faith is not praised for the reason that license is granted to omit works, since faith itself is even the chief and most difficult work. But this is the opinion. In conversion, some contrition is necessary, that is, dread and sorrow of conscience; a change of morals for the better is also necessary, just as Christ says: Repent. But to that contrition, faith or trust must be added, which determines that sins are remitted to us, and that we are pronounced righteous through mercy on account of Christ, not on account of the dignity of our contrition or works. For if the matter depended upon the dignity of contrition, consciences would be rendered uncertain. Therefore, the benefit of the remission of sins and of justification is transferred only to Christ. For we cannot oppose our dignity or our works to the wrath of God. Indeed, contrition would become despair and eternal death if the conscience felt that sins were not remitted to it until a sufficiently great sorrow merited remission. But after the conscience has been raised up again by trust in Christ and has obtained the remission of sins, now a new life must be obedience toward God, and it is now necessary that there be a righteousness of good works, which pleases God, not because we satisfy the law, but because the person, already reconciled by trust in Christ, pleases God. Wherefore, although this righteousness and obedience of ours is necessary, yet in order that the conscience may be certain that it pleases God, it ought always to determine that the person pleases, not on account of our dignity or the fulfillment of the law, but on account of Christ. Thus this faith is always necessary. For although the person has good works, yet he still has the remnants of sin, therefore there is need of Christ the mediator. When therefore it is said We are justified by faith, this ought to be understood relatively, that we are pronounced righteous by trust in the mercy promised on account of Christ. This is, through mercy on account of Christ we are pronounced righteous, that is, accepted, not on account of our dignity. For justification should be understood relatively for the remission of sins, and for the acceptance or imputation of righteousness. Therefore good works are not disapproved, but are most highly required. Only this is taught, that the person, in order to be certain of mercy, ought to determine that he pleases God on account of Christ, and thus also that this new and begun obedience pleases, even if it is far from perfection. This doctrine contains the most holy and most abundant consolation, if anyone receives it rightly.

And in the reconciled one there is that obedience, a certain justice, but one which pleases not because of its own perfection, but because the person is in Christ.

Latin:

Siquidem permanetis in fide fundati &c.

Hactenus definivit Evangelium, nunc adiicit circumstantiam, quomodo nobis contingant beneficia ostensa in Evangelio, quasi diceret, Ingentia promittis, remissionem peccatorum, Spiritum sanctum, vitam aeternam. Ingens hic thesaurus est, sed quomodo conse quemur tantum thesaurum? Hic respondet, sed ut in brevi Epistola, breviter. Itaque non negligenter haec, quanquam brevis particula praetercunda est, sic inquit, Estis redempti, si manetis in fide firmi, id est, consecuti estis remissionem peccatorum, si credideritis propter Christum remissa esse peccata, Eandem sententiam persecutus est copiose ad Rom: iii. Arbitramur iustificari homivem fide sine operibus legis.

Et quoniam hic locus postulat, breviter dicemus de iustificatione, ut quomodo opera, quomodo fides requiratur intelligi possit. Non enim ideo fides laudatur, ut licentia concedatur omittendi opera, cum fides ipsa sit vel precipuum ac difficilimum opus. Sed haec est sententia. In conversione necessaria est contritio aliqua, hoc est, pavor & dolor conscientiae, necessaria est & morum mutatio in melius, sicut Christus inquit. Agite paenitentiam. Sed ad contritionem illam accedere debet fides seu fiducia, quae statuit nobis remitti peccata,nosque pronunciari iustos per misericordiam propter Christum, non propter dignitatem nostrae contritionis aut operum. Nam sires ex dignitate contritionis penderet, conscientiae redderentur incertae. Ideo beneficium remissionis peccatorum & iustificationis transfertur tantum in Christum. Neque enim possumus irae Dei opponere nostram dignitatem aut nostra opera. Imo contritio fieret desperatio & mors aeterna, si conscientia sentiret sibi non remitti peccata, donec satis magnus dolor mereatur remissionem. Postquam autem fiducia Christi iterum conscientia erecta est & consecuta est remissionem peccatorum, iam nova vita debet esse obedientia erga Deum, & necessaria iam est iusticia bonorum operum, quae placet Deo, non quod legi satisfaciamus, Sed quia persona reconciliata iam fiducia Christi, placet Deo. Quare etsi necessaria est haec nostra iusticia & obedientia, tamen ut conscientia certa sit placere Deo, semper statuere debet, personam placere, non propter nostram dignitatem aut legis impletionem, sed propter Christum. Ita haec fides semper necessaria est. Nam persona etsi habet bona opera, tamen habet etiam adhuc reliquias peccati, Ideo opus est mediatore Christo. Quando igitur dicitur Fide iustificamur, relative hoc intelligi debet, fiducia misericordiae propter Christum promissae iusti pronunciamur. Hoc est per misericordiam propter Christum iusti, hoc est, accepti pronunciamur non propter dignitatem nostram. Iustificatio enim relative intelligatur pro remissione peccatorum, & pro acceptatione seu imputatione iusticiae. Non igitur improbantur bona opera, sed maxime requiruntur. Tantum hoc docetur, quod persona ut sit certa de misericordia, statuere debeat se propter Christum Deo placere, atque ita etiam placere novam illam & inchoatam obedientiam, etsi procul abest a perfectione. Haec doctrina sanctissimam & uberrimam consolationem continet, si quis eam dextre accipit.

Estque in reconciliato obedientia illa, iusticia quaedam, sed quae placet non propter suam perfectionem, sed quia persona est in Christo.

## 1:23
Of which I have been made a minister.

Concerning his ministry he inserts a digression, so that they might not doubt that the word of God is preached by him.

Latin:

Cuius factus sum ego minister.

De suo ministerio inserit excursum, ne dubitent, a se verbum Dei praedicari.

## 1:24
Now I rejoice in my sufferings.

The sufferings of the saints are the sufferings of Christ, for the saints are members of Christ. Therefore, something is lacking to the sufferings of Christ for as long as there remain saints who are afflicted. I remember someone shamefully abusing this passage, who reasoned that the passion of Christ did not make satisfaction for our sins, because Paul says here that he supplies what is lacking to the sufferings of Christ. It is an unbearable blasphemy to twist this passage to this opinion. Nor does Paul say that the passion of Christ did not make satisfaction for our sins. Indeed, in the Epistle to the Hebrews he argues copiously that the one passion of Christ made satisfaction for the sins of all. By one oblation, he says, he has perfected the saints. Although, therefore, they are common the afflictions of the saints and of Christ, yet by the passion of Christ satisfaction has been made to God. Our afflictions do not make satisfaction for sins, they do not justify us. Just as the labors of the emperor and of the soldiers are common, the power is not common. Thus the afflictions of the saints and of Christ are common, but the glory of satisfaction, and of sanctification, pertains to Christ. Therefore he attributes a new opinion to Paul, if anyone from this passage contends that the passion of Christ did not make satisfaction for our sins, or attempts to bring it about that by our afflictions we merit the remission of guilt.

For as Paul argues concerning works, so we can argue concerning afflictions: if we are justified by our afflictions, Christ died for nothing. Because those who draw us away from Christ and forbid us to trust in Christ teach terrified consciences nothing but despair, it is necessary. For a conscience must despair which, when it feels the wrath of God, and can neither obtain peace from its own righteousness, or its own afflictions, yet does not dare to look upon Christ, and to trust that he freely grants righteousness.

Moreover, this consolation is to be sought from this passage. For since our afflictions are common to Christ with us, we ought not to doubt that he is about to rescue us, and to govern our struggles. For he is affected by our afflictions just as the Epistle to the Hebrews teaches, that we have a high priest who knows how to sympathize. Therefore we ought to believe that he will not be lacking to us, who are engaged in the greatest dangers, with whom the world and the devil wage a horrible war.

Latin:

Nunc gaudeo super adflictionibus.

Adflictiones sanctorum sunt Christi adflictiones, sunt enim sancti membra Christi. Proinde tantisper deest aliquid adflictionibus christi, donec supersunt sancti qui adfliguntur. Memini quendam hoc loco turpiter abuti, qui ratiocinabatur, Christi passionem non satisfecisse pro peccatis nostris, quiae Paulus hic diceret, se quod deesset adflictionibus Christi supplere. Non ferenda blasphemia est, ad hanc sententiam detorquere hunc locum. Neque nPaulus dicit, Christi passionem non satisfecisse pro peccatis nostris. Imo in Epistola ad Hebraeos copiose disputat, unius Christi passionem pro omnium peccatis satis fecisse. Una inquit, oblatione consummavit sanctos. Quanquam igitur communes sint adflictiones sanctorum & Christi, tamen Christi passione satisfactum est Deo. Nostrae adflictiones non satisfaciunt pro peccatis, non iustificant nos, Sicut communes sunt imperatoris ac militum labores, potestas non est communis. Ita adflictiones sanctorum ac Christi communes sunt, Sed gloria satisfactionis, et sanctificationis, pertinet ad Christum. Proinde adfingit paulo novam sententiam, si quis ex hoc loco contendit Christi passionem non satisfecisse pro peccatis nostris, aut efficere conatur, quod nostris adflictionibus mereamur remissionem culpae.

Etenim ut de operibus Paulus argumentatur, ita de adflictionibus argumentari possumus, si ex adflictionibus nostris iustificamur, gratis Christus mortuus est. Quia aliud docent pavidas conscientias, nisi desperationem isti, qui nos a Christo abstrahunt ac vetant confidere Christo, necesse est. n. desperare conscientiam quae, cum sentit iram Dei, nec potest pacem consequi ex propria iusticia, aut propriis adflictionibus, non audet tamen intueri Christum, & confidere, quod gratis donet iusticiam.

Est autem ex hoc loco haec consolatio petenda. Cum enim adflictiones nostrae sint communes Christo nobiscum, dubitare non debemus, "quin sit nos erepturus, & nostra certamina gubernaturus. Adficitur enim nostris adflictionibus quemadmodum docet Epistola ad Hebraeos, habere nos pontificem, qui sciat compati. Proinde credere debemus cum nobis non defuturum esse, qui in maximis periculis versamur, quibus cum mundus ac diabolus horribile bellum gerunt.

## 1:28
Strengthened with all might etc.

That is, while you fight with your flesh, with the world, with the devil, you will feel divine help, and you will feel that God desires to help you, that is, however, to know and to grow in the knowledge of God.

To all endurance and patience with joy etc.

It pertains to afflictions, what he says growing in the knowledge of God etc. For in affliction He commands us to feel that it pleases God that we be afflicted. For it is necessary for us to be made like the image of His Son.

Secondly, he commands us to expect help from God, according to that saying, All who expect you shall not be confounded.

Thirdly, he says, strengthened with the might of the glory of God, that is, God strengthens believers in affliction through the Holy Spirit and produces peaceful consciences and joy in God, just as Paul says, The Spirit intercedes for us. For neither free will nor reason can overcome the fear of death or of the underworld.

Fourthly, he demands patience, for both terms, tolerance and long-suffering, signify patience, but he adds with joy, that is, let the heart not be angry at God, and this particle is to be observed above all. This is a most abundant and most effective consolation in adverse circumstances, to know that God demands patience, and indeed joined with joy. Human reason does not grasp this, this does not ascend into the heart of man, to use the Prophet's words, but God has taught it. For our reason in adverse circumstances is disturbed for this reason, because it thinks that we are afflicted because God either hates us, or does not regard us, nor wishes to help. God removes these false opinions from us, and teaches us that we are afflicted, not because He hates us, but because He wishes us either to be corrected or to remain corrected, according to that saying, The son whom He loves, He chastises.

Item, I do not desire the death of the sinner. Item, He has shut up all things under sin, not that He might destroy all, but that He might have mercy on all.

Secondly, God demands that in affliction we believe that He will bring aid to us, and He commands that help be sought from Him, according to that, Call upon me in the day of trouble, and I will deliver you. This matter produces joy in souls, to know that in adverse things God wishes to help and save us, provided we ask for help from Him. Paul to Timothy has embraced both in two words, teaching how we ought to be affected in tribulation, Pray, he says, without anger, and without hesitation, that is, show patience, and do not be angry with God, nor think that you have been rejected by God, when He allows you to be afflicted, and believe that He is about to bring aid.

Giving thanks to the Father.

Here he begins the narrative, in which he defines the Gospel, and since he has prayed before that a more abundant knowledge of the Gospel might befall them, he subjoins what the Gospel is. Now the Gospel is a discourse, by which the benefits which have been given through Christ are recounted; Paul commemorates those benefits in this place by a rhetorical accumulation, and the narrative is begun from the affection, Give thanks to God, because He has made you fit for the portion of the inheritance of the saints, that is, for obtaining the benefits of the Gospel.

These, however, which he recites afterwards through a collection, are to be in the light, to be snatched from the power of darkness, to be translated into the Kingdom of the beloved Son, to be redeemed by the blood of Christ, to have the remission of sins, to have peace with God, to be sanctified. A description of the person of Christ is, however, inserted in passing.

Who has snatched us from the power &c.

To be in darkness is not to know God, that is, not to perceive or determine that He is truly angry at sinners, and that He forgives sins. Furthermore, neither to know what sin is, for reason does not discern these sins, the concupiscence of the heart, the ignorance of God, to be void of the fear of God, to be void of faith, but it sees only these exterior sins, the same, theft, adultery, and the like. Therefore Christ says in John, the Holy Spirit will convict the world of sin, that is, He will convict the world because it does not know what sin is, and He will show sin. Thirdly, reason does not know what righteousness is before God, for it dreams that He is appeased He will convict the world of justice, as in John , by our works. For the Gospel shows a certain other justice, by which we are justified before God, than the justice of works. For it teaches us that we are pronounced just through mercy.

Fourthly, besides these things Paul says that we are not simply snatched from darkness, but from the power of darkness. For Satan impels the impious to perpetrate flagrant acts of every kind, nor is reason any longer firm enough to guard against exterior sins, but it is snatched and cast headlong by the devil. To the Ephesians ii. Paul says that the devil is effective in the unbelieving. For it is the power of darkness to impel to sin and to hold them captive and liable to eternal death.

Who is the image of the invisible God.

Here is inferred the προσωπογραφια and the description of the person of Christ, which teaches that Christ is truly and by nature God and man.

He is called, however, the image of the Father, and in the Epistle to the Heb. he is called the character of the Father's substance, because, to use some likeness, he is thus born from the Father, just as light from the sun, and as the ancients wrote, as light from light. And therefore he is also called in the Epistle to the Hebrews ἀπαυγασμα. He is likewise the image, because he is the word, for just as words among us are likenesses of things, so the son is the true effigy of the father, born from the mind of the father. And because on earth the son was made flesh, he explained to us the will of the father, he showed the father as a certain image according to that. The son who is in the bosom of the father, he has declared him to us. Here, however, in the signs is the place, that all things were created through the son, and all things consist through him. The same is in the Epistle to the Hebrews, upholding all things by the word of his power. We learn therefore from this what creation is, for things were not so created, that they now consist without the help of God, but God governs them, and moves them constantly, just as in the Acts Paul says, In him we are, we live, and we move. That things endure, that we breathe, that we live, that we eat, that we speak, that we perform other natural duties. They are the works of God. Nor ought we to dream, that God has departed from created things, just as we see a craftsman depart from a ship that has been made, and hand it over to others to be steered. And I think it is useful to know these things for fear, and for nourishing faith.

But here it is usually asked, if God moves nature, is he the author of evils, or of sins. This question it is not pleasing to treat more prolixly at present. Let it be enough to hold this, that God preserves nature and makes it efficacious, just as he also says here, whether thrones, or dominions, or powers, all things consist in him, that is, whatever is powerful, efficacious, has power and efficacy from God; God bestows life, strength, wisdom, and vigor. But because Christ himself says John 8 when we speak a lie, he speaks from his own, I will not make God the author of sin, but the one preserving nature and imparting life and motion, which life and motion the devil or the impious do not use rightly, just as Hosea also signifies, Your destruction is of yourself, O Israel, only in me is your help.

It is also asked here, is there any freedom of our will, if indeed God moves all creatures? Although it is not our intention to explain such grave and intricate questions in this place, yet because many litigate about this controversy, I wished to explain it briefly, so that I might heal, as much as I could, weak minds doubting about it somewhere.

When it is asked about the faculty and powers of free will, this is not what is asked, whether it is in our power to eat, to drink, to go, to see, to hear, and similar things ψύχικα to do works. But it is asked whether without the Holy Spirit we can fear God, and believe in God, and love the cross, etc. Or, to speak in other words, the question is not about creation, how God moves all creatures, trees, beasts, men. But the question is about justification and sanctification, and about those actions which do not fall to a man living only by natural life, which God imparts equally to the good and the bad. This, therefore, must be held: that the nature of man, by natural powers without the help of the Holy Spirit, cannot produce true fear of God, and true trust toward God, and the remaining spiritual affections and motions. As Paul says, The ψυχικος man does not perceive the things that are of God. He signifies by ψυχικος all natural powers, reason, mind, will. And in the 8th chapter to the Romans φρόνημα σακρος is death, nor can it obey the law of God. And John 6 No one can come to me, unless the Father has drawn him. Likewise, Without me you can do nothing. By such clear sentences it is handed down that the human will does not have such liberty that it can produce spiritual motions without the help of the Holy Spirit. But when human minds hear the word of God and do not reject it, the Holy Spirit at the same time moves them, so that they may both be terrified and again be raised up and believe the promises and truly let them establish that God forgives us, is present, hears us, helps, defends, and governs us, and He helps human minds in the effecting of true virtues by chastity, love of neighbor, patience, etc.

They err greatly who think that the powers of free will suffice for changing concupiscence; they err much more who dream that they can be defended against the devil by the powers of free will, who perpetually lies in wait for us, not only to drag us to these and other flagrant sins, but also to insert impious opinions about God and blasphemies. For if Satan did not hesitate to tempt and attack Christ with the weapons of blasphemy, when he says, If falling down you will worship me, etc. Matth. 4., he undoubtedly lies in wait for us as well. Finally, against death and the underworld, reason cannot console itself, or stand and justify itself when God is judging and terrifying our hearts. For thus says the scripture, All the glory of the flesh is as the glory of hay, etc. Therefore, let us think thus: that human nature has need of the help of the Holy Spirit for the effecting of spiritual motions. The works of the Holy Spirit through the word of God are faith, chastity, and humility in souls. As Christ says, The Holy Spirit will glorify me, that is, the Holy Spirit will bring it about that hearts believe my word, that they acknowledge me.

This is the principal place to be sought in the dispute concerning free will; there is no reason to dispute curiously about natural and civil actions, whether it is in our power to put on a black or red garment, to go here or there, to eat or drink, to read or write, to wear shoes or to ride. This must be understood, that the whole of nature is governed and moved by God, and that God imparts life and motion to all things. As it is written, In him we are, we live, and we move. And this action of God theologians call general, by which he sustains, preserves, and stirs all things, cattle, plants, men, alike good and bad.

But just as he stirs plants differently than cattle, so he stirs men differently than cattle, but he stirs each nature just as he created it. It is a natural gift in a tree to bear fruit; thus he stirs and makes the tree fruitful. So a certain natural gift and work of God is in man, human prudence, and liberty in the choice of things; the general action of God does not take away this liberty, or this choice.

Scripture attributes to man a certain prudence and justice, which reason effects by its own powers; consequently, it attributes a certain freedom for the effecting of that justice. Wherefore I judge that there is in the reason of man some freedom, or some choice of bodily things. Among these I also number civil justice. Nor is it obscure what I call civil justice, namely, to keep one's hands from murder, theft, violence, to keep pacts, to abstain from another's wife, and the like.

When some hear that freedom is taken away from the will, they think in vain that laws are established for restraining the flesh, and out of a certain desperation they loosen the bridle on their desires. These must be taught that God has commanded that carnal men, who are not ruled by the Holy Spirit, be restrained by the bonds and punishments of laws, just as Paul wrote. The law is laid down for the unjust; they must also be taught that there is some freedom of the human will, and a certain faculty for effecting the justice of the flesh. For that reason Scripture calls it the justice of the flesh, because it is effected by human powers.

But here again a correction of this opinion must be added, lest anyone attribute too much to free will. Even if there is a certain freedom and faculty in man of effecting in some way civil justice, or the justice of the law. Just as in trees there is a certain natural power of producing fruit, yet that very power is not intact in man, but, since human nature is corrupted by original sin, affections and concupiscence drag man toward manifest crimes. Thus a corrupted tree produces corrupted fruits, and cannot even everywhere provide civil justice.

To these things, since the impious are in the power of the devil, they are driven and incited by the devil, so that they cannot be deterred from manifest crimes by any punishments, however horrible. We see what a horrible punishment is established for thieves, by which, however, it is evident that many are not moved at all. What of those things which are altogether abhorrent to nature, to commit suicide, to kill parents or children, from where do these portents proceed? Certainly from the devil, who holds captive the hearts of the impious, as Paul teaches. And Christ says to the impious: You are of your father the devil.

Let us learn therefore that we cannot even provide civil justice without the help of God. And some example of this thing is to be discerned in the philosophers, who, although they strove with the greatest zeal to provide civil justice, nevertheless did not attain that very thing, but most of them all were liable to notable scandals, and I think they were marked by such vices, so that we might discern how great is the infirmity of the human mind, how great the power of the devil, and how nothing we can achieve by our own strengths, while ignorant of God. Who among the philosophers did not seek the cause of such great infirmity in the souls of men, why men cannot be restrained by so many bonds of laws and punishments? And they did not discover the cause of such a great matter, but the sacred letters showed us that original sin and the devil are the cause. Therefore, those who greatly proclaim the powers of free will follow the philosophers, who carry the power of human reason with immoderate praises. But both the philosophers and those who follow these authors are deceived, nor do they consider what the sacred letters teach. Let us therefore pray to God that He may govern us, that we may be able to see our infirmity with spiritual eyes, and that He may lead us to the knowledge of Christ, that we may be ruled, purified, and sanctified by the Holy Spirit.

We have written these things with pious zeal, that we might help certain weak ones, who anxiously torment themselves with this question concerning free will, who should know that in this disputation this must be held first of all, that in justification and the consolation of consciences by faith the Holy Spirit is conceived. Furthermore, I judge that reason has a certain choice and freedom of performing civil justice, even though that freedom is hindered by the infirmity of nature and the chains of the devil.

That he might have the preeminence in all things,

It was necessary for Christ to have the first place, because he himself was about to reign. For the Holy Spirit would not have been given, unless Christ were reigning. For that is the kingdom of Christ, to give the Holy Spirit, and to sanctify and rule others, just as it is written in the Psalm. You have ascended on high, you have led captivity captive, you have received gifts for men. For because Christ received the Kingdom, he received from the Father gifts, which he might pour out upon us. Thus, therefore, let us think concerning the kingdom of Christ, that he is truly present, bestows gifts, hears, and defends us. But reason dreams far otherwise, for it cannot understand that presence of Christ or the power of his mercy. But as Homer imagines Jupiter feasting among the Ethiopians and not being free to hear the prayers of men. So the flesh dreams, I say, that Christ is some Tyrant living happily somewhere, and managing a kingdom for himself, not regarding or hearing us. Against such destitute thoughts, hearts must be fortified by the word of God. Let us therefore diligently fix this opinion of Paul in our mind, that Christ holds the primacy, and in him dwells all fullness.

Moreover, since he holds the primacy of his kingdom, it is necessary that he draws the Kingdom with him as a leader, and dwells with it, just as he promised that he would be with us, even unto the consummation of the age. To these things, all fullness dwells in him, just as it is written by the Prophet, The Spirit of the Lord is upon me. But it is not to be understood that he enjoys those riches of divinity in such a way that he does not share them; thus that fullness dwells in him, so that he may impart it to us. As John says, Of his fullness we have all received. Thus it must be thought concerning the kingdom of Christ, that it is present at hand, that it defends us against the Devil, death, sin, and the world, that it leads us to the Father, as co-heirs of the kingdom. And it nourishes faith, to understand the kingdom of Christ in this way.

Making peace through the blood of his cross by himself.

First, it must be noted here that no victim, no works merit reconciliation for us besides Christ alone. As it is written 1 Tim. 2, There is one mediator of God and men, the man Christ Jesus, who gave himself a redemption for all. And 1 John 2, He is the propitiation for our sins. Likewise, Behold the lamb of God, behold him who takes away the sins of the world. To the same point pertain those things which Paul wrote in this place, that all things have been reconciled through Christ. Likewise that peace has been made in heaven and on earth. In heaven, because God is now appeased toward us, and has become a father, having forgotten our offenses, ruling and preserving us, just as he promises in the Prophets Mal. 3 And they shall be to me, says the Lord of hosts, in the day that I make, for a peculiar treasure, and I will spare them, as a man spares his son, who serves him.

But on earth peace has been made, because consciences are pacified, and now in turn we love God. For the flesh by itself cannot but hate God, when it sees how great punishments hang over us for our sins, when it sees that we are exercised by many calamities in this life, which when human reason considers, it either thinks that human affairs are not a care to God. Thus it happens that it despises him, or judges him to be cruel, who seems to take delight in our evils. Thus it happens that it is vehemently angry with him, that it thinks him to be unjust,

But after we have heard that we are forgiven on account of Christ, and we believe, we now feel that we have been received into grace, and that we are a care to God, and are ruled and defended by him, and that adverse things are directed to our salvation. Thus it happens that we think well of God, that we confess him to be merciful and just, that we expect and ask for help from him, and that we always feel that we are pleasing to God, not on account of any works of ours, however good, but on account of Christ. This is the true peace which Christ effects on earth toward God, which is clearly the very same eternal life, and the highest and ineffable consolation in all afflictions. Moreover, such peace also subsequently produces peace toward men, because the Holy Spirit having been received, all the fruits of the Holy Spirit, charity, patience, and the like, are born in the pious. Thus I also accept that in Luke, Glory to God in the highest, and on earth peace, and good pleasure toward men. For it is the glory of God to have had mercy on us, and to have given Christ, and indeed that glory is made known in this way, because God cannot be known except by those to whom the Son has shown him. On earth there is peace, as I have said, God is appeased, and in turn we love him. And to men there is good pleasure, that is, God is pleasing to men, now they see that they are a care to God, that they are loved by God.

Paul seems to me to have intended the same, in the Epistle to the Ephesians when he says, ἀνακεφαλαιώσασθαι τὰπαντα ἔν τῷ χριστω τὰ τε ἐν τοις οὐρανοις καὶ Τά ἐπὶ τῆς ἐν αὐτῶ. i.e., to reunite again all things which are in heaven, and upon earth, and to join God with men, and men with God, so that God may be propitious to men, and in turn men may have peaceful consciences and love God.

Latin:

Omni robore corroberati &c.

Id est, dum pugnatis cum carne vestra, cum mundo, cum diabolo, sentietis auxilium divinum, & sentietis, quod cupiat Deus vos iuvare, id est, autem cognoscere & crescere in agnitione Dei.

Ad omnem tolerantiam ac pacien-tiam cum gaudio &c.

Ad afflictiones pertinet, quod ait crescentes in cognitione Dei &c. Nam in adflictione iubet nos sentire, quod Deo placeat, ut affligamur. Oportet enim nos fieri similes imaginis filii eius.

Secundo iubet nos expectare a Deo opem, iuxta illud, Universi qui te expectant, non confundentur.

Tertio ait, confortati robore gloriae Dei, id est, Deus confortat per Spiritum sanctum credentes in afflictione & efficit pacatas conscientias & gaudium in Deo, sicut Paulus ait, Spiritus interpellat pro nobis. Neque enim liberum arbitrium seu ratio potest vincere metum mortis, autinferorum.

Quarto exigit pacientiam, nam utrunque vocabulum, tolerantia & longanimitas, significant pacientiam, sed addit cum gaudio, id est, cor non irascatur Deo, estque haec particula in primis observanda. Haec in adversis rebus uberrima consolatio est & efficacissima, scire quod exigat Deus pacientiam, & quidem cum gaudio coniunctam. Hoc non humana ratio deprehendit, hoc non ascendit in cor hominis, ut Prophetae verbis utar, sed Deus docuit. Nostra enim ratio in rebus adversis ideo perturbatur, quia cogitat nos ideo adfligi, quia Deus aut oderit nos, aut non respiciat, nec velit iuvare. Has falsas opiniones eximit nobis Deus, & docet nos adfligi, non quia oderit nos, sed quia velit nos aut emendari aut emendatos durare, iuxta illud, Filium quem diligit, corripit.

Item, nolo mortem peccatoris. Item, conclusit omnia sub peccatum, non ut omnes perdat, sed ut omnium miseretur.

Secundo Postulat Deus ut in adflictione credamus eum nobis opem laturum esse, & iubet a se peti auxilium, iuxta illud, Invoca me in die tribulationis, & ego eripiam. Ea res gaudium parit in animis, scire quod in adversis rebus Deus velit nos iuvare & servare, modo postulemus ab ipso auxilium. Paulus ad Timotheum, utrunque complexus est duobus verbis, docens quomodo in tribulatione adfecti esse debeamus, Orate, inquit, sine ira, & sine haesitatione, id est, praestate pacientiam, nec irascamini Deo, nec putate vos reiectos esse a Deo, cum adfligi vos sinit, & credite quod sit opitulaturus.

Gratias agentes patri.

Hic orditur narrationem, in qua definit Evangelium, & cum ante precatus sit, ut uberior cognitio Evangelii illis contingat, subiicit quid sit Evangelium. Est autem Evangelium sermo, quo beneficia quae per Christum donata sunt, recitantur, ea beneficia commemorat hoc loco Paulus Rhetorica congerie, & incepta est narratio ab affectu, Agite gratias Deo, quod fecerit vos idoneos ad partem haereditatis sanctorum, id est, ad consequenda Evangelii beneficia.

Sunt autem haec, quae per congeriem postea recitat, esse in luce, esse ereptum de potestate tenebrarum, esse translatum in Regnum filii dilecti, redemptum esse sanguine Christi, habere remissionem peccatorum, habere pacem cum Deo, esse sanctificatum. Est autem obiter inserta personae Christi descriptio.

Qui eripuit nos de potestate &c.

Esse in tenebris, est non nosse Deum, hoc est, non sentire seu statuere, quod vere irascatur peccantibus, & quod condonet peccata. Deinde nec scire quid sit peccatum, nam ratio non cernit haec peccata, concupiscentiam cordis, ignorationem Dei, vacare timore Dei, vacare fide, sed videt tantum haec peccata exteriora, eaedem, furtum, adulterium, & similia. Ideo dicit Christus apud Ioannem, Spiritus sanctus arguet mundum de peccato, id est, arguet mundum, quod nesciat quid sit peccatum, & ostendet peccatum. Tertio nescit ratio, quae sit iusticia coram Deo, somniat enim placari Deum nostris operibus, sicut apud Ioannem, Arguet et mundum de iusticia. Nam Evangelium ostendit aliam quandam iusticiam, qua coram Deo iustificamur, quam iusticiam operum. Docet enim nos per misericordiam iustos pronunciari.

Quarto, praeter haec dicit Paulus, non simpliciter esse e tenebris ereptos, sed ex potestate tenebrarum. Nam impios impollit Satan ad omnis generis flagitia perpetranda, nec iam ratio satis firma est, adversus exteriora peccata cavenda, sed rapitur & praecipitatur a diabolo. Ad Ephesios ii. ait Paulus, diabolum efficacem esse in incredulis. Est enim potestas tenebrarum impellere ad peccandum & detinere captivos & obnoxios aeternae morti.

Qui est imago Dei invisibilis.

Inferitur hic προσωπογραφια & personae christi descriptio, quae docet Christum vere & natura, Deum & hominem esse.

Appellatur autem imago patris, & in Epistola ad Heb. vocatur character substantiae patris, quia, silicet uti aliqua similitudine, sic nascitur a patre, sicut a sole lumen, et sicut scripserunt veteres, tanquam lumen de lumine. Ideoque appellatur etiam in Epistola ad Hebraeos ἀπαυγασμα. Est item imago, quia verbum est, ut enim verba apud nos simulachra rerum sunt, sic filius est vera effigies patris, e mente patris nata. Et quia in terris filius caro factus, exposuit nobis voluntatem patris, ostendit patris tanquam quandam imaginem iuxta illud. Filius qui est in sinu patris, ipse enarravit nobis. Hic autem in signis locus est, per filium omnia condita esse, & omnia per eum consistere. Idem est in Epistola ad Hebraeos, portans omnia verbo potentiae suae. Discimus igitur hinc quid creatio sit, non enim ita conditae res sunt, ut nunc sine Dei auxilio consistam, sed Deus gubernat eas, & agitat adsiduo, sicut in actis Paulus inquit, In ipso sumus, vivimus, & movemur. Quod durant res, quod spiramus, quod vivimus, quod edimus, quod loquimur, quod cetera officia naturalia perficimus. Dei opera sunt. Nec somniare debemus, Deum a conditis rebus discessisse, sicut fabrum à navi facta discedere videmus, eamque aliis tradere gubernandam. Atque haec cognoscere ad timorem, ad fidem alendam utile esse existimo.

Sed hic quaeri solet, si Deus agitat naturam, est ne malorum, seu peccatorum autor. Hanc quaestionem in praesentia non libet tractare prolixius. Hoc satis sit tenere, quod Deus naturam conservet, & efficacem efficiat, sicut & hic dicit, sive throni, sive dominationes, sive potestates, omnia consistunt in ipso, id est, quicquid est potens, efficax, potentiam & efficaciam à Deo habet, Deus vitam, robur, sapientiam, vires, largitur. Sed quia Christus ipse dicit Ioan. 8. cum loquimur mendacium, ex propriis loquitur, non faciam Deum autorem peccati, sed naturam conservantem & vitam, & motum impartientem, qua vita & motu diabolus, aut impii non recte utuntur, Sicut & Oscas significat, Tvae est perditio Israel, tantum in me auxilium tuum.

Quaeritur & hic, sit ne aliqua libertas voluntatis nostrae, siquidem Deus agitat omnes crcaturas? Quamvis autem non sit instituti nostri tan graves quaestiones & intricatas hoc loco explicare, tamen quia multi de hac controversia litigant, volui eam breviter explicare, ut alicubi infirmos animos de ea dubitantes, quantum possem, sanarem.

Cum de Liberi arbitrii facultate ac viribus quaeritur, non hoc quaeritur, an in potestate nostra sit edere, bibere, ire, videre, audire, & similia ψύχικα opera facere. Sed quaeritur an sine spiritu sancto possimus Deum timere, ac credere Deo, & crucem amare, &c. Seu ut aliis verbis dicam, non quaeritur de creatione, quomodo agitet Deus omnes creaturas, arbores, bestias, homines. Sed quaeritur de iustificatione & sanctificatione, deque his actionibus, quae non eadunt in hominem, tantum naturali vita viventem, quam iuxta Deus impertit bonis & malis. Hoc itaque tenendum est, naturam hominis, naturalibus viribus sine spiritus sancti auxilio non posse efficere verum timorem Dei, & veram fiduciam, erga Deum. & reliquos adfectus & motus spirituales. Sicut Paulus ait, Homo ψυχικος non percipit ea quae Dei sunt. Significat autem ψυχικος naturales vires omnes, rationem, mentem, voluntatem. Et in 8. cap. ad Rom.φρόνημα σακρος mors est, nec potest legi Dei obedire. Et Ioan 6. Nemo potest venire ad me, nisi quem pater traxcrit. Item, Sine me nihil potestis facere. Adeo claris sententiis traditum est humanam voluntatem non habere eiusmodi libertatem, ut motus spirituales sine auxilio spiritus sancti efficere possit. Sed cum humanae mentes auliunt verbum Dei & non repudiant, Spiritus sanctus simul movet eas, ut & perterrefiant & rursus erigantur & credant promissionibus & vere statuant Deum nobis ignoscere, adesse, nos exaudire, iuvare, defendere, gubernare, Et adiuvat humanas mentes in efficiendis veris virtutibus castitate, amore proximi, pacientia &c.

Longe errant qui existimant ad concupiscentiam mutanda, liberi arbitrii vires sufficere, Multo magis errant, qui somniant se viribus liberi arbitrii adversus diabolum defendi posse, qui perpetuo insidiatur nobis, non modo, ut ad eaedem & alia flagitia pertrahat, Sed etiam ut inserat impias de Deo opiniones, ac blasphemias. Etenim si non dubitavit Satan Christum tentare & oppugnare blaspemiae telis, cum inquit, Si procidens adoraveris me &c. Matth. 4. haud dubie nobis etiam insidiatur. Postremo adversus mortem & inferos ratio non potest se consolari, aut iudicante Deo ac terrente corda nostra consistere & se iustificare. Sic enim inquit scriptura, Omnis gloria carnis, quasi gloria foeni &c. Proinde sic sentiamus naturam humanam opus habere auxilio Spiritus sancti ad efficiendos motus spirituales. Spiritus sancti opera sunt per verbum Dei, in animis fides, castitas, humilitas. Sicut inquit Christus, Spiritus sanctus me glorificabit, id est, Spiritus sanctus efficiet, ut verbo, meo credant corda, ut me agnoscant.

Hic praecipuus locus est in disputatione de libero arbitrio requirendus, non est curiose de naturalibus ac civilibus actionibus disputandum an sit nostrae potestatis, vestem nigram aut rubram inducre, 'ire huc, aut illuc, edere, aut bibere, legere, aut scribere, fuere calceos, aut equitare. Hoc sentiendum est totam naturam a Deo gubernari, & moveri, Deum impertiri vitam & motum omnibus rebus. Sicut scriptum est, In ipso sumus, vivimus, et movemur. Atque hanc actionem Dei vocant Theologi generalem, qua omnia sustentat, conservat, agitat, pecudes, plantas, homines, iuxta bonos & malos.

Sicut autem aliter plantas agitat, quam peeudes, Ita aliter agitat homines, quam pecudes, Sed unam quanque naturam qualem condidit, ita agitat. Naturale donum est in arbore poma ferre, Sic agitat ac fecundat arborem. Ita naturale quoddam in homine donum & opus Dei est, prudentia humana, & libertas in delectu rerum, Hanc libertatem, seu hunc delectum non tollit actio Dei gever alis.

Scriptura tribuit homini quandam prudentiam, ac iusticiam, quam efficit ratio suis viribus, proinde libertatem quandam tribuit efficiendae eius iusticiae. Quare sentio penes rationem hominis esse aliquam libertatem, seu delectum aliquem rerum corporalium. In his etiam civilem iusticiam numero. Neque est obscurum quid vocem civilem iusticiam, nempe continere manus a caede, furto, violentia, servare pacta, ab alterius uxore abstinere, & similia.

Quidam cum audiunt voluntati libertatem adimi, frustra arbitrantur leges esse positas ad cohercendam carnem & desperatione quadam cupiditatibus frenum laxant. Hi docendi sunt Deum praecepisse, ut carnales homines, qui non reguntur spiritusancto, legum vinculis ac poenis coherceantur, sicut scripsit Paulus. Lex est iniustis posita, sunt item decendi, esse aliquam libertatem humanae voluntatis, & sacultatem quandam iusticiae carnis efficiendae. Nam ideo vocat eam scriptura iusticiam carnis, quia humavis viribus efficitur.

Sed hic rursus est addenda correctio huius sententiae, Ne quis nimium libero arbitrio tribuat. Etiamsi quaedam libertas ac facultas sit in homine efficiendae aliquo modo ctuilis iusticiae, seu iusticia legis. Sicut in arboribus naturalis quaedam vis est, poma gignendi, tamen illa ipsa vis in homine non est integra, Sed, cum viciata sit humana natura peccato originis, rapiunt hominem adfectus, & concupiscentia ad manifesta delicta. Ita viciata arbor viciosos fructus gignit, & ne quidem civilem iusticiam praestare ubique potest.

Ad haec, cum impii sint in potestate diaboli, impelluntur ac incitantur a diabolo, ut nullis paenis, quantumvis horribilibus, absterreri queant a manifestis delictis. Videmus quam horribile supplicium constitutum sit in fures, quo tamen nihil moveritam multos constat. Quid, quae omnino abhorrent a natura, mortem sibi consciscere, interficere parentes aut liberos, unde haec portenta proficiscuntur? certe a diabolo, qui tenet captiva corda impiorum, ut Paulus docet. Et Christus inquit ad impios. Vos ex patre diabolo estis.

Discamus igitur quod ne quidem civilem iusticiam praestare possimus sine auxilio Dei. Atque huius rei exemplum aliquod est in Philosophio cernere, qui cum summo studio conati sint civilem iusticiam praestare, id ipsum tamen non sunt adsecuti, sed plerique omnes insignibus flagitiis obnoxii fuerunt, Et existimo talibus viciis notatos esse, ut cerneremus quanta si infirmitas humanae mentis, quanta diaboli potentia, quam nihil possimus proficere nostris viribus, ignorantes Deum. Quis ex Philosophis non quaesivit caussam tantae infirmitatis in animis hominum, cur homines non possint tot vinculis legum ac suppliciorum eoherecri? Nec deprehenderunt tantae rei caussam, sed facrae literae ostenderunt nobis in caussa esse peccatum originis, & diabolum. Proinde qui magnopere praedicant vires liberi arbitrii, sequuntur Philosophos, qui vim rationis humanae vehunt immodicis laudibus. Sed falluntur una & Philosophi, & qui hos auto res sequuntur, nec considerant quid sacrae literae doceant. Nos igitur oremus Deum, ut gubernet nos, ut spiritualib, oculis videre infirmitatem nostram possimus, & ut ad Christi agnitionem nos per ducat, ut spiritu sancto regamur, purificemur & sanctificemur.

Haec pio studio adscripsimus, ut iuvaremus quosdam infirmos, qui se anxie torquent hac questione de libero arbitrio, qui sciant in hac disputatione hoc in primis tenendum esse, quod in iustificatione & consolatione conscientiarum fide Spiritus sanctus concipiatur. Caeterum iudico rationem habere quendam delectum ac libertatem efficiendae iusticiae civilis, tametsi ea libertas sit impedita infirmitate naturae, ac vinculis diaboli.

Ut sit in omnibus primatum tenens,

Oportuit Christum primas habere, quia ipse regnaturus erat, Neque enim datus esset Spiritus sanctus, nisi regnante Christo. Id enim est Christi regnum, dare spiritum sanctum, & sanctificare ac regere alios, sicut scriptum est in Psalmo. Ascendisti in altum, captivam duxisti captivitatem, accepisti dona pro hominibus. Quia enim Christus accepit Regnum, accepit a patre dona, quae in nos effunderet. Sic igitur de Christi regno sentiamus, quod vere adsit, largiatur dona, exaudiat ac defendat nos. Sed longe aliter somniat ratio, Neque enim potest intelligere illam praesentiam Christi aut vim misericordiae. Sed ut Homerus fingit Iovem apud Aethiopas convivari nec vacare ad audiendas preces hominum. Ita somniat caro, Christum inquam Tyrannum aliquem alicubi beate vivere, ac sibi regnum gerere, nos non respicere ac audire. Adversus huiusmodi inopias cogitationes corda verbo Dei munienda sunt. Hanc igitur sententiane Pauli diligenter adfigamus animo, Christum tenere primatum, & in eo inhabitare omnem plenitudinem.

Cum autem sui regni primatum teneat, necesse est ut Regnum tanquam dux secum trahat, ac cum eo versetur, sicut promisit se nobiscum fore, usque ad consumationem seculi. Ad haec inhabitat in eo omnis plenitudo, sicut scriptum est a ProphetaSpiritus Dominisuper mc. Sed non est intelligendum illis divitiis divinitatis ipsum ita frui, ut non communicet, Sic in eo inhabitat illa plenitudo, ut de ea impertiat nobis. Sicut Ioannes inquit, De eius plenitudine omnes accepimus. Sic de regno Christi cogitandum est, quod praesto adsit, quod defendat nos adversus Diabolum, mortem, peccatum mundum, quod adducat nos ad patrem, tanquam cohaeredes regni. Et alit fidem, sic intelligere regnum Christi.

Pacem faciens per sanguinem cru-cis suae per se ipsum.

Primum hic animadvertendum est, nullam, victimam, nulla opera mereri nobis reconciliationem praeter solum Christum. Sicut 1. ad Timoth. 2. scriptum est, Unus mediator Dei & hominum homo Christus Iesus, qui dedit redemptionem semetipsum pro omnibus. Et 1. Ioan. 2. Ipse est propictatio pro peccatis nostris. Item, Ecce agnus Dei, ecce qui tollit peccata mundi. Eodem pertinent, quae hoc loco scripsit Paulus, reconciliata esse omnia per Christum. Item pacem esse factam in caelo & in terra. in caelo, quia Deus iam placatus est nobis, & factus pater, oblitus nostrorum delictorum, regens et servans nos, sicut in Prophetis pollicetur Malach. 3.
