Et erunt mihi, ait Dominus exercituum, in die qua ego faciant, in peculium, & parcam eis, sicut parcit vir filio suo, servienti sibi.

In terra vero est pax facta, quia conscientiae pacatae sunt, & iam vicissim Deum amamus. Nam caro per sese, non potest non odisse Deum, cum videt quantae poenae impendeant nobis pro peccatis nostris, cum videt multis nos calamitatibus in hac vita exerceri, quas cum considerat humana ratioaut arbitr atur Deo res humanas non esse curae. Ita fit, ut contemnat cum, aut crudelem esse iudicat, qui videatur delectari nostris malis. Ita fit ut vehementer ei succenscat, ut iniquum esse cogitet,

Sed postquam ignosci nobis propter Christum audivimus, & credimus, iam sentimus nos receptos esse in gratiam, & curae esse Deo, & regi & defendi ab eo, & exerecri adversis rebus ad salutem nostram. Ita fit, ut de Deo bene sentiamus, ut eum misericordem, & iustum esse fateamur, ut ab eo auxilium expectemus & postulemus, & semper sentiamus nos Deo placere, non propter opera ulla nostra quantumvis bona, sed propter Christum. Haec vera pax est, quam Christus in terris efficit erga Deum, quae plane est ipsissima vita aeterna, et summum & ineffabile solatium in omnibus adflictionibus. Parit autem talis pax etiam deinde pacem erga homines, quia Spiritu sancto accepto, omnes fructus spiritus sancti, caritas, pacientia, & similes piis innaseuntur. Sic accipio & illud in Luca, Gloria in excelsis Deo, & in terra pax, & hominibus beneplacitum. Est enim gloria Dei, misertum esse nostri, & dedisse Christum, & quidem ea gloria sic innotescit, quia Deus non potest cognosci, nisi ab his, quibus filius ostenderit. In terra pax est, ut dixi, placatus Deus est, & vicissim nos illum amamus. Et hominibus est beneplacitum, id est, hominibus placet Deus, nunc vident se Deo curae esse, se a Deo amari.

Idem mihi Paulus videtur voluisse, in Epistola ad Ephe. cum ait, ἀνακεφαλαιώσασθαι τὰπαντα ἔν τῷ χριστω τὰ τε ἐν τοις οὐρανοις καὶ Τά ἐπὶ τῆς ἐν αὐτῶ. i. rursus copulare omnia quae sunt in caelo, & supra terram, & coniungere Deum cum hominibus, homines cum Deo, ut Deus sit propicius hominibus, & vicissim homines habeant conscientias pacatas & ament Deum.

## 2:1 Argument
CHAPTER TWO.

Latin:

CAPUT SECUN-DUM.

## 2:1
For I want you to know &c.

HE MENTIONS his own afflictions to strengthen our faith. For when we perceive that he himself has been helped and liberated, we are uplifted by his example and believe that the help of Christ will be present for us also. Therefore he says, I want you to know how great a struggle I have sustained, so that your hearts may receive consolation. Thus he says. 2. Corint. 1. Or if we are afflicted, we are afflicted so that you may receive consolation. For just as other examples have been handed down in the scriptures in which it is possible to see how God has defended believers, so his own example to us he shows that it is to be contemplated and considered.

For those histories of the saints also were written for this reason, as Paul says, that they might build up faith in us, just as Elisha's servant conceived consolation when he saw the legions of angels, so we, seeing Paul defended and preserved, may believe that we are likewise a care to God, and are guarded by him. Nor is there any reason why we should argue that we are sinners, unlike Elisha, Paul, and other saints. For God hates this weakness and unbelief most of all, and on the contrary, he most of all requires faith. But if you believe, God will help you just as he helped other saints. Therefore he afflicts us so that he may stir up faith in us. And to believe is a most pleasing sacrifice to God, as the Psalm says, Sacrifice the sacrifice of righteousness and hope in the Lord, that is to say, the true sacrifice is to hope in the Lord.

Joined together in love, etc.

What follows will be more easily understood if you break it down: I want you to know what a struggle I have sustained for us and for those who are at Laodicea, and who have not seen my face in the flesh, that their hearts may receive consolation, and they may be joined to us, or be compacted as the weak are to the stronger. That for while I console them by the duty of charity, they are raised up and joined to us, and they adhere. Just as tender vines adhere to elms until they are strengthened, and gradually by grasping the nearest branches they climb to the summits. Therefore, Paul interprets with a certain Rhetorical amplification what he calls consolation, Thus he says, I wish them to receive consolation, so that joined to us, by this my duty of charity, they may put on a firmer faith, so that they may believe that Christ will truly help them also. And indeed they will experience that they are helped in a similar way, and that our doctrine is true and salutary. He used very elegant words, for συμβ βο--ειν signifies to join and to cement, Just as in a building beams are aptly joined, so that one does not impede another, but so that they mutually support and sustain each other. Thus in the Church the strong should join the weaker to themselves not so that they may disturb them, but so that they may raise them up, and strengthen them.

And from this similarity the same word signifies to instruct, as if to direct or compose by teaching, or to place together, In which signification although it is not used here, nevertheless in expounding it will be permissible I think to allude to it, for thus Paul consoles them, so that he may instruct, so that he may teach, so that by his exhortation he may their faith. may raise them up, so that they do not doubt concerning Christ. For this reason he says that he signifies to them that he has been helped, so that faith may be kindled in them, so that they may believe more constantly, and that the help of Christ will not be lacking to them.

He also used the word πληροφορίας, which we explained above. For πληροφορία, συνεσεως signifies a full and perfect understanding, or a knowledge that doubts nothing. For such ought faith to be, which certainly feels that God forgives on account of Christ, which certainly feels that we are heard, which certainly determines that God will defend us, just as we certainly feel our hands brought near to fire. Here let each one consult his own heart, whether he so certainly determines that he is heard by God; for thus he will understand the weakness of his own faith, by the knowledge of which we may pray to God that he may add strength to us. Far deceived are the Hypocrites, who teach that faith is effected in us by the powers of free will. For reason cannot assent to the promises of God, when the senses show the opposite. It was not a work of reason in Hezekiah when he believed that it would come to pass that he would be defended against Sennacherib. For reason, when it gazed upon the greatest forces of the Assyrians, and compared them with the meager troops of Judah, reasoned that without doubt the victory would be with the Assyrians. But God had implanted in Hezekiah certain other motions, another light, so that he might firmly determine that his prayers were heard. That faith constantly awaited the help of God, leaning upon the promises and the word of God.

We have said these things for this reason, that readers might diligently weigh the force of the word ποληρoφηρίας. For thus they will learn that faith ought to be a certain fixed conviction in the mind, not an ambiguous opinion. And this is the sum of the sentiment, I signify to you my afflictions, so that when you shall see that I have been snatched away, you may conceive faith that Christ will be present with you also. For in afflictions you experience his presence, if you have constantly believed him who so often promises grace and help.

Furthermore, this passage also teaches us to use the examples of the saints, that we should feel that the voice of Paul pertains to us, and by his example, believe that God will bring us aid. Let us consider ourselves to be those tender vines, which he wishes to be joined to himself and by his example to be raised up to look upon Christ, until we grow large; for just as the divine promises, so the examples of the saints ought to be considered in afflictions for the arousing of faith, for in those examples are as it were the expressed and depicted promises of God. And this is a part of Christian doctrine concerning the cross to be most diligently known, so that we may know from where consolation is to be sought in afflictions, namely from the divine promises and the examples of the promises, which have been shown to us in the struggles of holy men.

Latin:

Nam volo vos scire &c.

MENTIONEM facit adflictionum suarum confidet fidem nostram. Etenim cum cernimus ipsum adiutum ac liberatum esse, erigimur exemplo eius & credimus nobis etiam auxilium Christi adfuturum esse. Ideo ait, volo vos scire quantum certamen sustinuerim, ut corda vestra consolationem capiant. Sic inquit. 2. Corint. 1. Sive tribulamur, ideo tribulamur ut vos consolationem capiatis. Sicut enim tradita sunt in scripturis exempla alia in quibus est videre quomodo defenderit Deus credentes, ita suum nobis exemplum contemplandum & considerandum ostendit.

Nam & illae Historiae sanctorum ideo scriptae sunt, ut inquit Paulus, ut fidem in nobis erigant, sicut Helisci puer conspectis legionibus angelorum consolationem concepit, ita nos videntes defendi et servari Paulum, credamus nos similiter Deo curae esse, & custodiri ab eo. Neque est quod disputemus, nos esse peccatores, dissimiles Helisei, Pauli, & aliorum sauctorum. Hanc enim imbecillitatem & incredulitatem maxime odit Deus, & econtra fidem maxime requirit. Quod si credes, perinde iuvabit te Deus, sicut adiuvit alios sanctos. Ideo adfligit ut fidem exuscitet in nobis. Et credere gratissimum sacrificium Deo est, ut Psalmus inquit, Sacrificate sacrificium iusticiae & sperate in Domino, Q. d. id est verum sacrificium sperare in Domino.

Compacti in caritate &c.

Quod sequitur, si dissolvas facilius intelligetur, volo vos scire quantum certamen sustinuerim pro nobis & his qui sunt Laodiceae, & qui non viderunt faciem meam in carne, ut consolationem capiant corda eorum, & adiungantur nobis, leu coagmententur tanquam infirmi, firmioribus. Isto enim caritatis officio dum consolor eos, illi eriguntur & nobis adiunguntur, & adherescunt. Sicut tenerae vites adherescunt ulmis donec confirmentur, & paulatim adprehendendo proximos ramos cuadunt in cacumina. Interpretatur itaque Paulus Rhetorica quadam amplificatione quid vocet consolationem, Sic inquit, volo eos capere consolationem, ut nobis adiuncti, hoc meo caritatis officio, fidem in duant firmiorem, ut credant vere Christum & ipsis opitulaturum esse. Et quidem experientur similiter se adiuvari, & doctrinam nostram veram ac salutarem esse. Verbis usus est valde elegantibus, nam συμβ βο--ειν significat adiungere & coagmentare, Sicut in aedificio apte coniunguntur trabes, ne alia aliam impediat, sed ita ut se mutuo fulciant ac sustentent. Sic in Ecclesia firmi adiungant sibi imbecilliores non ut eos perturbent, sed ut erigant, & confirment.

Et ab hac similitu line significat eadem vox instituere, quasi docendo dirigere seu componere, seu una collocare, In qua significatione tametsi hic non usurpatur, tamen in enarrando licebit opinor ad eam allu dere sic enim consolatur illos Paulus, ut iustituat, ut doceat, ut sua adhortatione fidem illorum erigat, ne dubitent de Christo. Ideo enim ait se illis significare, quod sit adiutus, ut in ipsis accendatur fides, ut credant constantius, neque ipsis defuturum esse auxilium Christi.

Est & verbo πληροφορίας usus, quod supra enarravimus. Nam πληροφορία, συνεσεως significat plenam & perfectam intelligentiam, seu cognitionem nihil dubitantem. Talem enim oportet esse fidem, quae certo sentiat Deum ignoscere propter Christum, quae certo sentiat exaudiri nos, quae certo statuat Deum nos defensurum esse, quemadmodum certo sentimus vel manus admotas igni. Hic consulat unusquisque suum cor, an ita certo statuat se exaudiri a Deo, Sic enim intelliget fidei suae imbecillitatem, qua cognita oremus Deum ut addat nobis robur. Longe falluntur Hypocritae, qui docent viribus liberi arbitrii fidem effici in nobis. Ratio enim non potest assentiri promissionibus Dei, cum sensus diversum ostendunt. Non erat rationis opus in Ezechia cum crederet futurum, ut defenderetur adversus Sennacherib. Ratio enim cum maximas vires Assyriorum intueretur, easque cum exiguis copiis Iuda compararet, ratiocinabatur haud dubie victoriam penes Assyrios futuram esse. At Deus alios quosdam motus, aliam lucem indiderat Ezechiae, ut certo statueret exaudiri preces suas. Ea fides constanter expectavit auxilium Dei, promissionibus ac verbo Dei innixa.

Haec ideo diximus, ut diligenter expendant lectores vim verbi ποληρoφηρίας. Ita enim discent fidem oportere quandam certam in mente sententiam, non amiguam opinionem esse. Estque haec summa sententiae, Significo vobis meas adflictiones, ut cum videbitis me ereptum esse, fidem concipiatis Christum & vobis adfuturum esse. Nam in adflictionibus experimini praesentiam eius, si constanter credideritis ei toties pollicenti gratiam & auxilium.

Caeterum hic locus & nos docet uti exemplis sanctorum, Ad nos Pauli vocem pertinere sentiamus, & eius exemplo, credamus Deum nobis opitu laturum esse, Existimemus nos ipsos teneras illas vites esse, quas sibi adiungi & suo exemplo ad Christum intuendum erigi vult, donec grandescamus, ut enim promissiones divinae, ita exempla sanctorum considerari debent in adflictionibus ad exuscitandam fidem, nam in exemplis illis sunt veluti expressae & depictae promissiones Dei. Estque haec pars doctrinae Christianae de cruce diligentissime cognoscenda, ut sciamus, unde in adflictionibus consolatio petenda sit, nempe a promissionibus divinis & promissionum exemplis, quae nobis in sanctorum hominum certaminibus ostensa sunt.

## 2:3
In whom are all the treasures of wisdom and knowledge hidden, etc.

Those who think that Paul says all the treasures of wisdom are in Christ because Christ handed down better precepts than Moses or the philosophers do not aptly explain this passage. For those who interpret these words in this way do not see the distinction between the Law and the Gospel, and they do not understand why Christ was sent. For He did not come to bring the Law, but He came to preach the remission of sins to those who believe, just as He Himself says John 3. God did not send His Son into the world to judge the world, but that the world might be saved through Him. Origen, against Celsus, diligently collects the precepts of Christ in order to show that Christian doctrine is more excellent than Gentile philosophy, but it is a most pernicious error to think that Christ came for the purpose of handing down new laws, for He came to remit sins, to take away our iniquities, as the Prophet says, and to give the Holy Spirit, but we have often spoken of this matter elsewhere.

Nor is it enough to accept this passage speculatively, that he who knows Christ knows many arcane and sublime things about the nature of God, but rather it must be accepted concerning the knowledge of the will of God. That is finally true wisdom, which Scripture calls thus, to know the will of God, that is, to know that God wills to forgive, wills to save, wills to hear us. Christ speaks of this knowledge John 1. No one has ever seen God; the Son who is in the bosom of the Father, he has declared him to us. And Matt. 11. Nor does anyone know the Father, except the Son, and he to whom the Son has willed to reveal him. To this must also be referred this sentence of Paul. In Christ are all the treasures of wisdom, because the human heart cannot determine that God wills to have mercy on us, wills to hear us, wills to save us. But after it has heard that through Christ the wrath of the Father is appeased, and sins are forgiven, when it has acknowledged Christ in this way, and has believed in him, there for the first time it sees the will of God, and knows certainly that God has mercy, that God hears, defends, and saves those who believe. Here for the first time a certain true knowledge of God, a true light shines in the hearts. For reason by itself is ignorant of God, does not judge that we are a care to God, does not judge that we are heard, dares nothing to expect or ask from God. But when, terrified by the knowledge of our sins, we believe that our sins are forgiven us on account of Christ, we conceive a certain other opinion about God, and we truly establish that He is present with us, that He has mercy on us, and helps us, and defends us.

But this is a secret wisdom, and entirely unknown to reason. Therefore Paul says that these treasures are hidden in Christ, and he calls the Gospel a mystery. For reason or the world cannot grasp the will of God toward us. Paul says, The world did not know God in wisdom through wisdom. Although Cicero collected all the arguments about divinity, he nevertheless doubts whether we are a care to God or not. Epicurus plainly denied it, because it went well for the wicked and ill for the good, because there are so many calamities and vices in men. And he thinks that God would have remedied so many evils if He were ruling human affairs, like an excellent prince. And not only do the philosophers err about God, but the Pharisees also err, that is, those who justify themselves by the righteousness of works, for these dream that they are without sin, and they have a certain false opinion about God. For they do not discern the wrath of God against sin. And again when they are afflicted and begin to feel the wrath of God, they do not see the presence and mercy of God, they dare to expect or ask for no help from God, because they are without faith, and while they strive to satisfy God through works, they nevertheless cannot attain peace in their consciences. Therefore, they do not know God, and these Pharisees are plainly without God, such as Saul was. Rightly, therefore, Paul says that the wisdom of the Gospel is hidden before the world. Hence, the world persecutes and condemns it.

To these things, those very people who believe are sometimes tempted to despair on the cross. For this wisdom is covered by the forms of the cross even in the very hearts of the saints. So hidden is this knowledge of God, that when the saints are tempted, when they see their sin, or when they feel the wrath of God, they can scarcely raise themselves up so that they may grasp mercy and grace in Christ. The world, however, thinks it to be a plainly feigned doctrine when certain mercy is proposed.

THE SECOND PART OF THE EPISTLE.

Now this I say, that no man may impose upon you with probable reasoning.

Having finished the definition of the Gospel above, and having handed down what Christian righteousness is, he here adds an admonition, in which he commands to avoid those things which contaminate Christian doctrine. Just as a physician, when he has handed down a remedy, also warns what might harm the health. Paul will make, a little later, a notable comparison of Christian righteousness with carnal righteousness, for he who does not hold this distinction is entirely ignorant of Christ. Therefore, this passage must be diligently considered and understood.

Furthermore, he here commands Christians to beware lest they be deceived by probable reasoning. Certain inept people have understood this as the ornamentation of speech, but the mind of Paul is far otherwise; for since Christian doctrine disagrees with reason, he commands you to beware lest we be deceived by arguments taken from reason. What thing deceived Mahomet? Not the ornamentation of speech, for that barbaric nation does not understand eloquence, but reason cunningly devised deceived both him and the others. It seemed absurd to him that Christ should be called the Son of God by nature, because the divine nature is more excellent than can be communicated to man, especially since Christ has also died. But the divine nature is immortal. What drives the seditious and fanatical Anabaptists of this time mad is not eloquence. For they are men not only illiterate, but utterly rustic; superstitious reason deceives them. They hear that patience is commanded. Therefore, they dream afterwards that the defense of courts and magistrates is not to be used. Therefore, they disapprove of magistrates and courts. For sometimes heresies arise from arguments that agree with reason, which nevertheless fight against the word of God. Sometimes reason itself also raves and is deceived by superstition. And the term πιθανολογια, which the Apostle has used here signifies not the ornament of speech or elocution, but arguments thought out cunningly and plausibly. For Greek rhetoricians often use the word in that sense.

Latin:

In quo sunt omnes thesauri sapientiae atque scientiae reconditi &c.

Non enarrant apte hunc locum isti, qui arbitrantur ideo Paulum dicere in Christo omnes thesauros sapientiae esse, quia praecepta meliora tradiderit Christus, quam Moises, aut Philosophi. Qui enim sic interpretantur haec verba, non vident discrimen legis & Evangelii, non intelligunt cur Christus missus sit. Non enim venit ad legem ferendam, sed venit ad praedicandam remissionem peccatorum credentibus, Sicut ipse inquit Ioan. 3. Non misit Deus filium suum in mundum, ut iudicet mundum, Sed ut salvetur mundus per ipsum. Origenes adversus Celsum diligenter colligit praecepta Christi, ut ostendat Christianam doctrinam prestantiorem esse gentili Philosophia, Sed est perniciosissimus error arbitrari Christum ideo venisse, ut novas leges traderet, venit enim ut remitteret peccata, ut tolleret iniquitates nostras, sicut Propheta inquit, Et ut daret spiritum sanctum, Sed hac de re saepe diximus alias.

Neque satis est hunc locum speculative accipere, eum qui novit Christum, multa arcana & sublimia scire de natura Dei, Sed potius est accipiendus de cognitione voluntatis Dei. Ea demum est vera sapientia, quam sic vocat scriptura, nosse voluntatem Dei, id est, nosse quod Deus velit ignoscere, velit servare, velit nos exaudire. De hac cognitione loquitur Christus Ioan. 1. Deum nemo vidit unquam, Filius qui est in sinu patris, ipse enarravit nobis. Et Matth. 11. Neque patrem quis novit, nisi filius, & cui voluit filius revelare. Eo referenda est & haec Pauli sententia. In Christo esse omnes thesauros sapientiae, quia cor humanum non potest statuere, quod Deus velit nostri misereri, velit nos exaudire, velti nos servare. At postquam audivit per Christum esse placatam iram patris, & esse remissa peccata, cum hoc modo Christum agnovit, atque credidit ei, ibi primum videt voluntatem Dei, ac scit certo Deum misereri, Deum exaudire, defendere, & servare credentes. Hic primum vera quaedam de Deo noticia, verum lumen illucescit in cordibus. Nam ratio per se ignorat Deum, non iudicat Deo nos curae esse, non iudicat nos exaudiri, nihil audet a Deo expectare aut postulare. Sed cum perterrefacti cognitione peccatorum nostrorum credimus remitti nobis peccata propter Christum, aliam quandam de Deo sententiam concipimus, & vere statuimus, quod adsit nobis, quod misereatur nostri, & iuvet nos, quod defendat nos.

Sed haec est arcana sapientia, & prorsus ignota rationi. Ideo Paulus ait, esse absconditos hos thesauros in Christo, & vocat Evangelium mysterium. Nam ratio seu mundus non potest deprehendere voluntatem Dei erga nos. Paulus inquit, Mundus non cognovit Deum in sapientia per sapientiam. Cum omnia argumenta de divinitate collegit Cicero, tamen dubitat, utrum curae simus Deo, nec ne. Epicurus plane negavit, quia malis bene esset, bonis male, quia tam multum sit calamitatum ac viciorum in hominibus. Et arbitratur tot malis medicaturum fuisse Deum, si regeret res humanas, tanquam optimum principem. Et non solum Philosophi errant de Deo, Sed errant etiam Pharisei, hoc est, isti qui iustificant se iusticia operum, hi enim somniant se sine peccato esse, Et falsam quandam de Deo opinionem habent. Nam iram Dei adversus peccatum non cernunt. Et rursum cum adfliguntur, & incipiunt iram Dei sentire, non vident praesentiam & misericordiam Dei, nihil auxilii andent expectare aut postulare a Deo, quia sine fide sunt, & dum conantur per opera satisfacere Deo, tamen non possunt pacem in conscientiis consequi. Non igitur norunt Deum, planeque sine Deo sunt Pharisei isti, qualis Saul fuit. Recte igitur Paulus inquit absconditam esse sapientiam Evangelii coram mundo. Proinde persequitur & condemnat eum mundus.

Ad haec illi ipsi qui credunt, aliquando ad desperationem sollicitantur in cruce. Ect enim formis crucis tecta haec sapientia etiam in ipsis sanctorum cordibus Adeo arsana est haec noticia Dei, ut sancti cum tentantur, cum vident suum peccatum, seu cum iram Dei sentiunt, vix erigere se possint, ut in Christo comprehendant misericordiam & gratiam. Mundus autem plane ementitam doctrinam esse putat, cum proponitur certa misericordia.

SECUNDA EPISTOLAE PARS.

Hoc autem dico, ne quis imponat vobis probabili ratione.

Supra absoluta definitione Evangelii, cumque tradidit, quid sit iusticia Christiana, hic subiicit admonitionem, in qua iubet vitare ea, quae Christianam doctrinam contaminant. Sicut medicus cum tradidit remedium, monet etiam quae ledant valetudinem. Faciet autem Paulus aliquanto post insignem collationem iusticiae Christianae, cum iusticia carnali, Nam qui hoc discrimen non tenet, is Christum prorsus ignorat. Diligenter igitur hic locus considerandus atque cognoscendus est.

Porro hic iubet Christianos cavere ne probabili ratione decipiantur. Quidam inepti intellexerunt hic ornatum orationis, Sed longe alia mens est Pauli, cum enim doctrina Christiana dissentiat a ratione, iubet vos cavere, ne decipiamur argumentis a ratione sumptis. Quae res decepit Mahometum? Non ornatus orationis, neque enim intelligit eloquentiam illa barbarica natio, Sed ratio callide cogitata & ipsum & caeteros fefellit. Videbatur ei absurdum, Christum dici natura Filium Dei, quia divina natura sit praestantior quam communicetur homini, presertim cum & mortuus sit Christus. At natura divina est immortalis, Quid Anabaptistas seditiosos & fanaticos hoc tempore dementat, non eloquentia. Sunt enim homines non tantum illiterati, sed prorsus agrestes, supersticiosa ratio decipit eos. Audiunt praecipi de pacientia. Ideo somniant postea, non esse utendum defensione iudiciorum ac magistratuum. Ideo improbant magistratus & iudicia. Alias enim oriuntur hereses ex argumentis cum ratione consentientibus quae tamen pugnant cum verbo Dei. Alias ipsa etiam ratio delirat & superstitione decipitur. Et vocabulum πιθανολογια, quo hic est usus Apostolus, significat non ornatum orationis seu elocutionem, sed callide & verisimiliter cogitatata argumenta. Saepe enim a voce Graeci Rhetores in eam significationem utuntur.

## 2:8
See to it that no one takes you captive through philosophy and empty deceit.

What he said above as a plausible argument, he now explains in more words, saying, See to it that no one takes you captive through philosophy. In this place, a comparison of human justice to Christian justice is instituted.

It is, however, very necessary to know what the difference is between both, so that we may know how much human justice God requires, and to what extent He disapproves of it.

Philosophy, insofar as it is the science of speaking, and of natural and civil morals, and affirms and teaches only those things concerning natural matters and civil morals which are comprehended by certain reason, is a true and good creature of God. For it is the very judgment of reason, which God gave to human nature in natural and civil matters, that is true and certain, because Paul says Ro. ii. that the Gentiles have the law of God written in their hearts, that is, they have a judgment by which they can judge that no one is to be harmed, that gratitude is to be shown for benefits received, that magistrates are to be obeyed, and similar things. Therefore, men have a true and certain judgment from God concerning civil morals; likewise, they have it concerning natural matters, concerning counting, concerning measures, concerning building, and concerning remedies for diseases. When, therefore, Paul says, Beware, lest anyone deceive you through philosophy, it is not to be understood in such a way that that philosophy which teaches the natures of bodies or civil morals is vain. For He permits us to use that judgment of reason, just as we use clothing and food. He does not forbid counting or measuring bodies, He does not forbid building, He does not forbid painting, He does not forbid healing the diseases of bodies, He does not forbid exercising judgments. Indeed, because you hear that these gifts of God have been handed down to nature, you ought much more to venerate this Philosophy, which God gave for preparing the safeguards of life. For God wishes us to labor, and those live more conveniently who know the remedies of diseases, who know how to govern the morals of their citizens with judgments and laws, and to fortify public tranquility, than barbarians, who, since they have no laws, wander in the manner of wild beasts, and practice perpetual robberies, knowing no rights of marriages or of educating offspring. Nor are examples to be sought from the Cyclopes, whose cruelty Homer described. There is no more humanity found today among the Scythians on account of their ignorance of the good arts than there was among those Cyclopes.

I have seen those who, having abused this passage of Paul, spurned all good disciplines, as if the Christian religion were nothing else but extreme ignorance. But that opinion is both pernicious to human affairs and also simply impious. For since all arts have been shown to us divinely, and both are and are truly called gifts of God, it is a kind of piety to cultivate and learn them, not to despise them. If, however, as I have said, you judge that he commands not concerning the dogmas of religion, but concerning corporal things. For just as you may rightly call him impious who would kill himself by starvation, thinking it a sin to touch food, so do they err most shamefully who deny that human arts must be learned by a Christian man, since without eloquence, without letters, without the knowledge of laws, without medicine, this corporal life cannot be preserved. Nor is the human race in more need of food than of these arts. Penalties ought surely to have been established publicly, as against thieves or highwaymen, for those who draw the minds of men away from the studies of these arts. For no thieves or robbers harm the common good more than those who attempt to snatch from the human race the greatest safeguards of corporal life, which are placed in the knowledge of letters. And the wicked scoundrels still dare to say that it is piety to despise these arts, when no human mind can conceive the great grace that we owe to God for these most useful gifts. Augustine gathers in De doctrina Christiana what the knowledge of human disciplines contributes to understanding Scripture. And the thing itself speaks: without the knowledge of languages, and without these arts which teach how to speak correctly and clearly, Scripture cannot be expounded.

But suppose that the use of all arts is not in the expound- by reason of Scripture, not for that reason are none of their utilities , there is no need of agriculture for the knowledge of religion, and yet, for that reason, the matter of impiety is not to be pursued. Thus, we can use the benefits of medicine and of all Philosophy, which is occupied in investigating nature, in preserving bodily life, without reproach.

Paul calls the knowledge of nature the truth of God Rom. 1. But what madness it is, when the Holy Spirit calls the truth, and indeed that which is divinely shown to the human race, the knowledge of nature, to remove Christians from it as if it were forbidden. It should rather have been done by common studies, that we might preserve a thing so excellent and so useful, and hand it down to posterity, as illustrated as possible, lest by our inertia the divine gift should seem to have perished. Everyone strives to leave his own patrimony to his children, but this public patrimony sent down from heaven to earth we ought to defend publicly, all of us, and transmit to posterity. For what is a nobler possession, than that of TRUTH, which can exist among men only with God as its author. For thus it is written, That the eye may see, that the ear may hear, God makes both. Nor is there any doubt that those who have discovered things not commonly noticed were divinely incited to the investigation of those things. Who would have discovered the powers of so many plants, the so many forms of remedies, who would have grasped that variety of celestial motions, if God had not stirred up and governed the studies of men? And what the poets said about their own craft, that can most truly and most holily be said about the invention or the illustration of all disciplines: This impulse has the seeds of a sacred mind.

It is not, however, the place here to enumerate all the utilities of natural philosophy. I only wished to provide proof, by one or another sentence cited from Scripture, that the treatment of it does not displease God. Paul calls it the truth of God. Likewise, he commands us to use creatures with thanksgiving. i. Timoth. iiii. Therefore, it is permitted to use medicine. Likewise, it is written that the physician was created by God, that is, that medicine was divinely shown. The utilities themselves are before the eyes of healthy men, for how many benefits of medicine do we use daily? Hezekiah, although he already hears that his life has been divinely extended, nevertheless uses a remedy, and indeed a medical one from Isaiah. But medicine comprehends universal physiology and astronomy. For in healing, certain distinctions of times must be observed, concerning which matter there exists an entire Galen booklet. And in the critical days Galen disputes copiously that the bodies of living creatures are affected and their qualities tempered by celestial light, for thus he says παντωv -τωv ἀνωθεν ἀστρων ἀπολαυομὲν της δυναμεως And a little later he says that those who deny this fight against manifest experience. But that it is sophistic, having rejected manifest experience, to demand demonstrations besides. Nor indeed is this observation of natural inclinations or times superstitious, just as it is not superstition, but divine ordinance, to sow in winter or spring, to reap in summer, to forbid wine to one who is feverish. It would be superstition only then, if you wished to abstain from wine without a natural cause, and for the sake of justification, just as the Carthusians are compelled to abstain from meat.

There is also another utility of Astronomy, that it is necessary to have, for the sake of civil affairs, a certain description of the year and months, which if we did not have, what confusion there would be in public affairs. It would be the life of cattle, not of men, to know no certain turns of times, to have no described spaces of years or months. The Lord in Genesis when He speaks of the lights in heaven, Let them be for signs and seasons, and days, and years, certainly willed the motions of the lights to be observed so that the just space of a year might be apprehended. For unless the observation of motion and the description of time are added, the course of the sun no more completes a year for us than it does for the cattle. But we understand the year to be completed by the sun only if we have observed the whole course. Since, however, it can be understood that God commanded that we count the distinctions of times from the turns of motions, it is sufficiently evident that it is right to expend effort in this art.

The other part of Philosophy is also necessary, which hands down the precepts of morals, and which brought forth the laws of governing states. For there is a certain doctrine and pedagogy necessary for the privately forming of the characters of men toward humanity, for which use poems such as those of Hesiod and Homer and similar ones were first written. Afterwards, the nature of man having been diligently inspected, Philosophers sought the causes of those precepts, and described the forms of virtues in order, as we see Cicero did in the Offices, and Aristotle in the Ethics. It is wonderful, however, how much the knowledge of them contributes to morals, how much more accommodating and tractable men become in every social intercourse, how much more suitable for managing the republic, for exercising judgments, and for many other duties of humanity they are, who have perceived these things at home. On the contrary, the characters of those who have not been cultivated are so wild that they are thought not to differ much from beasts. For just as fields, if they are not cultivated and sown, become sterile or produce useless weeds, so human minds, if they are not stirred up and sharpened by doctrine, through depraved habit, not only become duller, but are plainly corrupted. Furthermore, those precepts of moral Philosophy have been dug out from nature, or collected from the laws of nature, which God has inscribed upon our souls, and He willed that they should have laws no less holy than those which He carved on the stone of Moses. Hence, from wise men, whom God stirred up to establish commonwealths, laws were also derived, according to which judgments might be exercised, things might be divided, and misdeeds might be punished. Paul does not doubt that these are a divine ordinance. You see by manifest sentences of scripture that these parts of Philosophy are approved.

But someone might say that many things are mixed into Philosophy that dissent from religion, such as those things in Aristotle, which he disputes concerning the eternity of the world. Many things. The Epicureans have trifled much concerning Atoms, and it is indeed far most unworthy of religion that they taught that pleasure is the end of goods. Ridiculous is the ἀπαθεια. of the Stoics, who [call] mercy, and most similar they blame good affections, and this is ridiculous too. that all sins are equal.

I indeed do not deny that in the disputations of the Philosophers there are many things, not only foreign to religion, but even false, and fighting against natural reason, because many things were written by those who were little prudent without certain reason. God allowed most of them to hallucinate for this reason, that He might signify that the truth cannot be discerned even in the investigation of natural and corporeal things, if He Himself does not breathe upon it, and if He does not rule minds by His word. For Paul says that they were made foolish, because they did not glorify God. I call Philosophy, therefore, only that which affirms nothing, unless it has been observed by certain reason or experience. Many ambiguous opinions are mixed with true and certain precepts, but not even the sounder ancient writers judged those to be genuine and authentic Philosophy. And it is singular prudence to understand what things are certain, what are uncertain. But to assent to uncertain and unknown things, and to affirm them as if they were known, which does not rarely happen, is a thing most unworthy and most shameful for a Philosopher.

I have said nothing yet about eloquence, which yields neither in utility, nor in dignity to other parts of Philosophy. Eloquence, however, is not, as some unlearned people think, a certain empty paint of speech. But it consists in finding those things which properly pertain to the handling of the cause you have undertaken, and then in explaining and expressing them clearly and with a certain dignity. Nor does it seek empty pleasure for the ears, but for the sake of utility or necessity every reason for speaking has been observed, since without those arts by which eloquence is contained, no more serious cause, or one that is a little more obscure, can be taught. As often as men are to be taught about religion, as often as they are to be taught about other matters in courts or councils, he is surely worthy of the highest praise who can bring light to an obscure cause, who can lead listeners or judges as if into the present matter. If we praise arithmeticians because they know how to explain intricate calculations in monetary matters, how much more ought we to admire eloquent men, whose knowledge contains not a small utility of private property, but judgments and laws, and finally the safety of the entire republic. For Eloquence is concerned with matters far greater than almost all other human arts. But I shall not speak here of the praise of perfect Eloquence, which is surely one of the highest human virtues. For this governs states, moderates judgments, defends laws, and explains to peoples what is just and unjust, of which Since we acknowledge that knowledge is necessary for life, it follows that we must also establish that eloquence is necessary, without which just and unjust things cannot be known and judged.

And in this place I shall speak only of these lighter and childish studies, which show a certain way and method of speaking, of Grammar, of Dialectic, of Rhetoric. What art committed to letters, finally, can be perceived or taught without the knowledge of languages, without the precepts of dialectics and rhetoric? How can public laws be understood or explained without these aids? He who does not attain the sermon-legum, who does not perceive where a definition must be sought, where a division must be used in controversies, which things conflict with one another, how ἀμφίβολα, is to be explained, how, when a similarity or a species of law deceives a judge, a dissimilarity must be found, and countless things of this kind, I beseech you, what will he be in the forum or in the courts other than what in the Comedies κωφα προσωπα. Since, therefore, the use of these light arts extends so widely in the greatest and most honorable matters, they are by no means to be driven out of the schools of Christians, but rather are to be learned and cultivated with the greatest care.

What of the fact that the sacred letters can neither be understood nor taught correctly without these arts? For what will he understand who is unable to judge concerning speech, who has learned no method of constructing discourse or of figures from Grammar? He who has not learned from Dialectic and Rhetoric what the parts of a somewhat longer discourse are, what the sequence of propositions and arguments is, which ones agree with each other, which ones conflict, where the parts of the arguments cohere correctly, and where they do not cohere. He who does not see these things in the sacred books, which are full of the most subtle disputations, let him simply confess that he understands nothing. Often, on account of ignorance of languages, Thomas, Hugo, and Lyranus and others like them have hallucinated in expounding scripture. But perhaps their grammatical errors could have been pardoned if, in a long exposition, they had nevertheless retained the sum of the meaning of the writers whom they were expounding. But since in expounding they depart entirely from the authors as if they had forgotten them, and often even disagree with them, and teach things alien to piety, they are not worthy of pardon. And these things happened in large part on account of ignorance of these arts of which I am now speaking. Which Psalm, which page of the Prophets do those interpreters expound skillfully? For they did not see the order of the propositions and arguments, how unhappily they have treated the Epistle of Paul to the Romans, because they did not notice the economy of the discourse.

Nor do I say this because there is no need for the Holy Spirit as a teacher in order to understand Scripture. Indeed, I judge that spiritual things cannot be rightly known unless the Holy Spirit moves and teaches our hearts. This is what Paul signified when he said that spiritual things must be examined spiritually.

And yet, since the Holy Spirit teaches us through the word, the nature of speech must be known. But since no one can judge concerning speech without the knowledge of these arts, it is necessary that we bring this instrument to the treatment of Scripture.

What else is the gift of tongues but eloquence? Since the Apostle commands us to give attention to the variety of tongues for the sake of the utility of the Church, let us consider that it is also commanded to us that we cultivate the studies of eloquence, so that we may be able to treat Scripture more rightly. And just as at the beginning of the Church the gift of tongues was given to the Apostles so that they could spread the Gospel widely among the Gentiles, so also in this age the studies of languages seem to have been stirred up for the sake of purifying ecclesiastical doctrine.

To these things, since Paul writes that a Bishop ought to be διδακτικον, he requires of him not only a common knowledge of Christian doctrine, but also a certain skill in teaching. But those who know no way of arranging a speech, no method of arguing, who say confusedly whatever comes into their mouth, are so far from teaching the listener and sending him away more certain about doubtful matters, that they send him away even more uncertain. And since controversies often arise in the Church concerning the greatest matters, he who is not sufficiently instructed by Dialectic and Rhetoric will know neither how to explain them nor how to defend the truth. And I do not know whether most controversies, if they were set forth eloquently, could be judged and removed.

For often the disagreement is not about the matter, but about the words, where the unlearned, because they do not see what is fitting, quarrel without end, δυσκωφόι with δυσκωφοις, as it is in the Proverb. And when the disagreement is about the matter, because they mix in many things foreign to the purpose in disputing, they do not explain controversies, but rather obscure them by treating them, so that they cannot be known and judged. These vices of disputations are seen by those who have a moderate use of the arts of speaking. Since this is so, their usefulness is great in teaching the Church, and at this time, in so many dissensions, it was especially necessary that those who preside over the Churches be instructed in these arts, to heal minds which, in such a variety of opinions, can establish nothing certain. Nor can these be provided for by any other reason, unless by teaching clearly and prudently. They will remove a great part of the contentions if they explain λογομαχίας in which there is litigation about words, not about the thing; if they teach that questions are to be avoided which contribute nothing to edification, such as these: Why God permitted Adam to fall, Where the souls of the dead are. Questions of this kind nourish curiosity, not piety.

The remaining questions, the knowledge of which is necessary, such as these: How we may obtain the remission of sins, by faith or on account of our works. What faith is, and how it is produced in souls. What the power of the sacraments is, To what extent it is necessary to observe human traditions; these, if they have a craftsman who knows how to treat them lucidly, will not be understood with difficulty by good minds which allow themselves to be taught. I beseech you, what can that mass of commentaries by Faber, or Roffensis, and the like, confer upon the reader, where so many things beside the point and foreign to the cause are heaped together? Those who teach in this way, doubting consciences they disturb even more. Therefore, studies of eloquence joined with the knowledge of Christian doctrine bring no small benefits to the Church.

But when reason, or Philosophy, judges concerning the will of God, then it almost always errs. And although we could go through all the articles of faith, and show that judgment concerning the articles of faith is not to be sought from reason, yet lest we make it long, we shall contract some articles into three heads.

For first, reason errs concerning the governance of things. For even if it allows that God created things, yet offended reason, because so many things are done unjustly in the world, can hardly establish that things are governed by God, but dreams that God now perhaps idly allows nature to be carried along without any impulse of His own, like a Maker who has made a ship, when he finishes it, departs from it, and commits it to the waves or to a pilot. Here Christian doctrine teaches the opposite, and warns us not to be deceived by Philosophy, that is, Philosophy then deceives when it begins to judge concerning God or concerning the counsels of God, because the natural man does not perceive the things that are of God. Moreover, natural man signifies the whole nature of man, not renewed by the Holy Spirit. Therefore, that nature of man can do nothing concerning to affirm by the will of God, which is learned only from the word of God, just as Isaiah says. To the law and to the testimony, whoever does not speak according to this Word, there shall be no morning light for him.

Therefore, certain and manifest sentences are to be chosen from scripture, by which we learn not only that things were created by God, but also that they are governed. That God feeds and clothes us, preserves some, punishes others, and that God has not departed like a craftsman from a ship, but governs things just as a pilot governs a ship, as these sentences prove, Matt. 6. Your Father who is in heaven feeds them. Matt. 10. Two sparrows are sold for a farthing, and one of them shall not fall upon the ground without your heavenly Father. John 3. My Father works until now, and I work. Eph. 1. He works all things according to the counsels of his will. Col. 1. All things consist through him. Acts 17. In him we live, and move, and are. Ps. 103. When you give to them, they shall gather. Prov. 16. All the ways of men are open to his eyes etc. And in the Lord's Prayer, Give us this day our daily bread. Deut. 9. Man does not live by bread alone, but also by every word that proceeds from the mouth of God.

Secondly, Philosophy errs concerning justification, if it establishes before God that civil justice is sufficient. But Christian doctrine teaches that justice before God is Faith in Christ. And just as natural virtues in bees do not make them Christian, or just before God, so neither do civil morals, or natural virtues, make men just before God. Bees have prudence, because they construct dwellings and establish a Republic. They also have justice, because they obey their kings most diligently. Likewise, they help one another, lifting those weary from a burden, and they abstain from violence and injuries. There is also in them a wondrous fortitude, because they fight most fiercely against the drones. Finally, there is great temperance in them, since they know not sexual union, and they prepare honey for our uses. And just as they are not called Christian on account of these virtues, so neither are men Christians on account of civil morals alone, but because they believe that on account of Christ God has forgiven our sins, and has received us into grace, as Paul says, We reckon a man to be justified by faith without the works of the law, etc. i. corinth. i. Let no flesh glory in his sight, but of him you are in Christ Jesus, who was made for us wisdom from God, and justice, and sanctification, and redemption.

Nor indeed does Christian doctrine abolish civil morals, but it demands them, and Philosophy or reason He approves of the one commanding concerning civil morals, just as he also approves of civil magistrates, and testifies that God is the author of civil ordinances Roma. 13. All things which are ordained, are ordained by God, and from those who do not have the Holy Spirit, he nevertheless demands that they be restrained by civil justice, as he teaches the Law is set for the unjust. Item the Law is a pedagogue. But reason and the Gospel disagree on this, that the Gospel denies that civil justice justifies before God.

Thirdly, Philosophy is deceived when it thinks that reason has enough strength, from its own nature, against vices, and does not see that there is need for the Holy Spirit, who both renders hearts purer and rules us, lest we be cast headlong into manifest outrages either by the infirmity of nature or by the devil. But the Gospel teaches that the heart is impure and boils with concupiscence. Item that the devil so lies in wait for us that he even drives us into manifest outrages. Therefore it promises the Holy Spirit, who transforms hearts, rules, and defends us. Ioan. 15. Without me you can do nothing, and at Roma. 8. Whoever are led by the Spirit of God, they are the sons of God. But what kind of person man is, and to what he rushes, when deserted by God, the example of Saul shows. Thus therefore Philosophy, or the judgment of reason, can affirm nothing certain concerning the divine will but it can rightly judge concerning the nature of things and concerning civil morals. Wherefore they err, who judge concerning Christian doctrine from reason or Philosophy. Indeed, it is a most abundant consolation for the pious that the will of God is not to be estimated from the judgment of our reason, and as often as we think about justification, to know that God freely forgives us on account of Christ, wonderfully consoles the soul. Again, those also err who despise Philosophy judging concerning natural things. For that is to contemn the gift of God, since we ought to use all the creatures of God with thanksgiving, and to feel that they are the benefits of God. 1. Timoth. 4. And just as it would be insanity to say, that one must judge concerning Christian doctrine from the precepts of the shoemaking art, so they are foolish who judge concerning Christian doctrine from philosophy. For Peter says in the second [epistle] that prophecies were not brought forth by human will. Again, just as those would also be foolish who would say that the shoemaking art fights with Christian doctrine, so they are foolish who say that the knowledge of nature and of civil morals fights with religion. Therefore Paul does not say that Philosophy is evil, but he says thus: See that no one deceives you through Philosophy; the abuse is blamed, the thing itself is not disapproved, as if someone should say, see that wine does not deceive you. Here also Other articles of faith could be commemorated, about which reason cannot rightly judge. But let it be enough to have warned that concerning the articles of faith, not reason, but scripture should be consulted.

Then what he adds, empty deception, here signifies arguments collected from Philosophy concerning the will of God. For it is not Philosophy when we judge about the divine will from reason, but they are empty dreams. Just as Epicurus denied that our affairs are a care to God, or that souls are immortal. Likewise, the Platonists disputed how God begets a certain thought, so that they might mock the Christian dogmas concerning the son of God. But Aristotle [argued] that the world is eternal, and never had a beginning. And someone wrote that the foundations of Christian doctrine are Platonic Philosophy. Thus, to affirm as certain those things which reason or Philosophy cannot affirm, indeed which are placed entirely outside the judgment of reason or philosophy, that is an empty deception.

And just as it is commanded in the law that no one should sow dissimilar seed in the same field, one must be careful that the doctrine of the Gospel and Philosophy are not mixed together, but the Gospel is the doctrine of spiritual life and justification before God. Philosophy, however, is the doctrine of bodily life, just as you see medicine serving health, civil customs serving the common tranquility of men and the exercising of judgments, then other arts having been discovered for the sake of other uses. Geometry measures bodies, in building. Arithmetic governs contracts, and a great part of human society. Moreover, a use of this kind is necessary, and approved by God, just as Paul teaches in many places, that the creatures of God are to be used with thanksgiving. And yet the use of such bodily things does not justify before God, just as God commanded to eat, and yet that does not justify a man. Thus, to use a diet according to a physician's prescription, to observe the dangers of the weather, do not justify. Likewise, civil customs, such as not bearing arms where the laws forbid, or dressing according to the laws, God demands, and yet that does not justify before God. Paul commands 2. Timoth. 2. ὀρθοτομειν the word of truth. Therefore, we also must be on our guard, and let us not mix the Gospel and Philosophy, or condemn the latter, in that part in which God approves it, but let us rightly separate and distinguish the doctrine of reason from Prophecy, and let us consider for the sake of what use each is taught.

Finally, it is most important of all to admonish this, that reason is most easily deceived, so that it may assent to in unknown and false things, even in judging natural things, and it approves many base things against nature, when it is not governed by the word of God, Romans 1. Their foolish heart was darkened. Item, God gave them up to a reprobate mind. Item. 2 Thess. 2. God shall send them strong delusion, that they should believe a lie. Item, Proverbs 29. Where there is no prophecy, the people perish. Of which thing we see many examples in those who have brought death upon themselves, as Seneca praises a certain Marcellinus in his book of Epistles, and how many things Diogenes did against nature is known. Moreover, God shows us such horrible examples, that he may strike fear into us, lest we trust too much in the powers of our reason, and that we may suffer ourselves to be taught and ruled by his word.

Latin:

Videte, ve quis vos depraedetur per Philosophiam, & inanem deceptionem.

Quid supra dixerit probabilem rationem, id exponit nunc pluribus verbis, imquiens, Videte, ne quis vos per Philosophiam depraedetur. Hoc in loco collatio humanae iusticiae ad Christianam instituitur.

Est autem valde necessarium cognoscere, quid inter utramque intersit, ut sciamus quot exigat Deus iusticiam humanam, & quatenus improbet eam.

Philosophia quatenus est scientia loquendi, et rerum naturalium & civilium morum, & ea tantum de rebus naturalibus ac moribus civilibus affirmat ac docet, quae certa ratione comprehenduntur, est vera & bona creatura Dei. Est enim ipsum iudicium rationis, quod in rebus naturalibus & civilibus Deus dedit humanae naturae verum & certum, quia dicit Paulus Ro. ii. quod gentes habeant legem Dei scriptam in cordibus, id est, habeant iudicium, quo iudicare possunt, Neminem ledendum esse, gratiam pro benefactis habendam esse, Magistratibus obsequendum esse, & similia, Habent ergo homines verum & certum iudicium a Deo de civilibus moribus, item habent de rebus naturalibus de numerando, de mensuris, de aedificando, de remediis morborum. Cum igitur Paulus ait, Cavete, ne quis vos decipiat per Philosophiam, non est sic accipiendum, quod illa Philosophia, quae docet naturas corporum, aut civiles mores, sit vana. Permittit enim uti nobis isto iudicio rationis, sicut vestitu & victu. Non vetat numerare aut metiri corpora, non vetat aedificare, non vetat pingere, non vetat mederi morbis corporum, non vetat iudicia exercere. Immo, quia audis haec dona Dei esse tradita naturae, multo magis debes hanc Philosophiam venerari, quam Deus dedit ad vitae praesidia paranda. Vult enim Deus nos laborare, & vivunt commodius, qui sciunt remedia morborum, qui sciunt iudiciis ac legibus mores suorum civium gubernare, & tranquillitatem publicam munire, quam barbari, qui cum nullas leges habeant, ferarum more vagantur, & perpetuae latrocinia exercent nulla iura connubiorum, vel educandae sobolis norunt. Nec a Cyclopibus petenda sunt exempla, quorum crudelitatem Homerus descripsit. Nihilo plus humanitatis hodie ferunt apud Scythas esse propter inscitiam bonarum artium, quam apud illos Cyclopas fuit.

Vidi qui hoc Pauli loco abusi, omnes bonas disci plinas aspernabantur, tanquam nihil aliud esset Christiana religio, nisi extrema inscitia. Sed ea opinio, tum perniciosa est rebus humanis, tum etiam simpliciter impia. Nam cum artes omnes, divinitus nobis ostensae sint, Deique dona & sint, & vere vocentur, pietas quaedam est eas colere ac discere, nam contemnere. Si tamen, ut dixi, ita iudices praecipere eas non de dogmatibus religionis, sed de corporalibus rebus. Ut. n. iure possis impium eum dicere, qui se fame necaret, existimans peccatum esse, cibos attingere, ita turpissime errant, qui homini Christiano negant humanas artes discendas esse, cum sine eloquentia, sine literis, sine legum cognitione, sine medicina haec vita corporalis conservari non possit. Nec magis cibis opus est generi humano, quam his artibus. Poenas profecto publice constitui oportuit, tanquam in fures aut grassatores, in eos, qui ab harum artium studiis animos hominum abstr ahunt. Neque enim, magis in commune nocent ulli fures, aut praedones, quam isti maxima vitae corporalis praesidia, quae posita sunt in cognitione literarum, generi hominum eripere conates. Et audent scelesti nebulones adhuc dicere pietatem esse, has artes contemnere, cum nulla humana mens concipere tantam gratiam possit, quantam Deo pro his tam utilibus donis debemus. Colligit Augustinus de doctrina Christiana, quid conferat humanarum disciplinarum scientia, ad intelligendam scripturam. Et res loquitur ipsa, sine scientia linguarum, et sine his artibus, quae recte & perspicue loqui docent, enarrari scripturam non posse.

Sed fac non omnium artium usum esse in enar ratione scripturae, non propterea nullae earum utilitates sunt, Nihil agricultura ad religionis cognitionem opus est, neque tamen, propterea impietatis res agenda est. Ita medicinae et totius Philosophiae, quae versatur in perscrutanda natura, beneficiis, in servanda vita corporali, sine reprehensione uti possumus.

Paulus naturae cognitionem, veritatem Dei adpellat Rom. 1. Quae est autem amentia cum Spiritus sanctus veritatem, & quidem divinitus ostensam generi hominum, naturae cognitionem adpellet, Christianos ab eatanquam inter dicto submovere. Id potius agendum erat communibus studiis, ut rem tam praeclaram, tamque utilem conservaremus, & posteris tradederemus, quam maxime illustratam, ne nostra inertia divinum donum intercidisse videretur, Patrimonium suum quisque liberis relinquere studet, at hoc publicum patrimonium caelo in terras demissum publice omnes defendere, et ad posteros transmittere debebamus. Quaeest enim nobilior possessio, quam VERITATIS, quae nulla existere inter homines potest, nisi Deo autore. Sic enim scriptum est, Ut oculus videat, ut auris audiat, Deus facit utrunque. Neque dubium est, quin hi qui res non animadversas vulgo deprehenderunt, divinitus incitati sint ad earum rerum inquisitionem. Quis tot plantarum vires, tot formas remediorum comperisset, qui illam varietatem caelestium motuum deprehendisset, nisi Deus excitasset & gubernasset hominum studia. Et quod de suo artificio Poetae dixerunt, id verissime & sanctissime de omnium disciplinarum, vel inventione, vel illustratione dici potest, Impetus hic sacrae semina mentis habet.

Non est autem huius loci utilitates omnes naturalis Philosophiae enumerare. Tantum una atque altera sententia ex scriptura citata fidem facere volui, tractationem eius Deo non displicere. Paul. veritatem Dei vocat. Item iubet nos cum gratiarum actione creaturis uti. i. Timoth. iiii. Itaque medicina uti licet. Item scriptum est, A Deo medicum creatum esse, hoc est medicinam divinitus ostensam esse. Versantur autem utilitates ipsae ab oculos sanis hominibus, quam multis enim medicinae beneficiis quo tidie utimur. Ezechias quanquam iam audis et divinitus prorogatam sibi vitam esse, tamen remedio utitur, & quidem medico Esaia. At medicina comprehendit universam Physiologiam & Astronomiam. Sunt enim in medendo temporum discrimina quaedam servanda, qua dere extat integer Galent libellus. Et in diebus Criticis copiose disputat Galenus affici corpora animantium & temperari qualitates a caelesti lumine, Sic enim ait παντωv -τωv ἀνωθεν ἀστρων ἀπολαυομὲν της δυναμεως Et paulo post inquit eos qui id negent, cum manifesta experientia pugnare. Sophisticum autem esse, repudiata manifesta experientia, insuper postulare demonstrationes. Neque vero haec observatio inclinationum naturalium, aut temporum superstitiosa est, quemadmodum nec superstitio est, sed divina ordinatio hyeme aut vere serere, aestate metere, vino interdicere febricitanti. Superstitio tum demum esset, si sine caussa naturali abstinere velis a vino, & iustificationis caussa, sicut Cartusiam a carnibus abstinere coguntur.

Est autem & alia Astronomiae utilitas, quod oportet habere civilium negociorum caussa, certam anni & mensium descriptionem, quam si non haberemus, quae confusio in rebus publicis esset. Pecudum vita, non hominum fuerit, nullas nosse certas temporum vices, non annorum, non mensium descripta spacia habere. Dominus in Genesi cum ait de luminibus in caelo, Sint in signa & tempora, & dies, & annos, voluit certe motus luminum observari ut anni iustum spacium deprehenderetur. Etenim nisi accedat observatio motus, et descriptio temporis, nihilo magis nobis, solis cursus annum conficit, quam pecudibus. Sed ita demum annum intelligimus a sole confici, si totum cursum observaverimus, Cum autem intelligi possit praecepisse Deum, ut discrimina temporum ex motuum vicibus numeremus, satis constat rectum esse in hac arte operam sumere.

Necessaria est & altera Philophiae pars, quae morum praecepta tradit, & quae leges regendarum civitatum peperit. Est enim quaedam doctrina & paedagogia privatim formandis ad humanitatem moribus hominum opus, in quem usum primum poematas qualia sunt Hesiodi & Homeri & similia, scripta sunt. Postea diligenter natura hominis inspecta, Philosophi caussas illorum praeceptorum quaesiverunt, & formas virtutum ordine descripserunt, ut in Officiis Ciceronem, in Ethicis Aristotelem fecisse videmus. Mirum est autem quantummoribus conducat illorum cognitio, quanto commodiores ac tractabiliores in omni convictu, quanto ad rempublicam gerendam, ad iudicia exercenda, & ad alia multa humanitatis officia magis idonei sint, qui domi haec perceperunt. Contra eorum, qui non exculti sunt, mores ita feri sunt, ut non multum distare a bestiis existimentur. Ut enim agri, si non colantur, & serantur, sterilescunt, aut gignunt inutiles herbas, ita mentes humanae si non excitentur & acuantur doctrina, prava consuetudine, non tantum hebetiores fiunt, sed plane corrumpuntur. Porro praecepta illa moralis Philosophiae eruta sunt ex natura, seu collecta ex legibus naturae, quas Deus animis nostris inscripsit, nec minus sanctas leges habere voluit, quam quas in saxo Motsi sculpsit. Hinc a sapientibus viris, quos Deus excitavit ad res publicas constituendas, leges etiam derivatae sunt, iuxta quas iudicia exercerentur, res dividere tur, punirentur maleficia. Has non dubitat Paulus care divinam ordinationem. Vides manifestis scripturae sententiis, has Philosophiae partes probari.

Sed dicat aliquis multa admixta esse Philosophiae dissidentia a religione, qualia sunt apud Aristotelem, quae de aeternitate mundi disputat. Multa. nugati sunt Epicurei de Atonis, longe vero dienissimum est a religione, quod docuerunt voluptatem finem bonorum esse. Ridicula est & ἀπαθεια. Stoicorum, qui misericordiam, & plerosque similes adfectus bonos vituperant, ridiculum & hoc est. omnia peccata paria esse.

Ego vero non nego in Philosophorum disputationibus multa esse, non modo aliena a religione, sed etiam falsa, & cum naturali ratione pugnantia, quia multa scripta sunt a parum prudentibus sine certa ratione. Plerosque Deus ideo hallucinari passus est, ut significaret verum ne quidem in naturalium & corporalium rerum inquisitione cerni posse, si ipse non adspiret, & si mentes non regat suo verbo. Paulus enim ait infatuatos esse, quia non glorificaverint Deum. Voco igitur Philosophiam, eam tantum, quae nihil adfirmat, nisi certa ratione aut experientia animadversum. Multae ambiguae opiniones admixtae sunt veris praeceptis ac certis, sed illas ne veteres quidem saniores scriptores, germanam ac genuinam Philosophiam esse iudicaverunt. Et singularis prudentia est, intelligere quae certa sint, quae incerta. Adsentiri vero incertis & incompertis, eaque pro compertis adfirmare, quod non raro accidit, res est Philosopho indignissima turpissimaque

Nihil adhuc de eloquentia dixi, quae neque utilitate, neque dignitate cedit aliis Philosophiae partibus Est autem eloquentia, non ut indocti quidam existinant, inanis quidam orationis fucus. Sed cum in venire ea, quae ad caussam quam susceperis tractandam, proprie pertinent, tum illa perspicue & cum quadam dignitate explicare atque eloqui. Nec inanem aurium voluptatem captat, sed utilitatis, seu necessitatis causaratio omnis dicendi animadversa est, quin sine illis artibus, quibus eloquentia continetur, nulla gravior caussa, aut paulo obscurior doceri potest. Quoties homines de religione, quoties de aliis rebus in iudiciis aut consiliis docendi sunt, profecto summa laude dignus est is, qui obscurae caussae lumen adferre, qui auditores, seu iudices velut in rem praesentem ducere potest. Si Arithmeticos laudamus, quod in re nummaria intricatas rationes explicare sciant, quanto magis disertos homines admirari debemus, quorum scientia, non exigua rei familiaris utilitas, sed iudicia ac leges, denique solus totius reipublicae continetur. Versatur enim Eloquentia circa res longe maiores, quam humanae artes aliae fere omnes. Sed ego hic non de laude perfectae Eloquentiae dicam, quae profecto una est de summis virtutibus humanis. Haec enim civitates regit, moderatur iudicia, defendit leges, iusta atque iniusta populis exponit, quorum noticiam cum fateamur necessariam esse ad vitam consequitur ut eloquentiam etiam necessariam esse statuamus, sine qua iusta atque iniusta cognosci ac diiudicari non possunt.

Ac hoc loco dicam tantum de his levioribus et puerilibus studiis, quae viam quandam ac rationem dicendi ostendunt, de Grammatica, de Dialectica, de Rhetorica. Quae tandem ars mandata literis, sine cognitione linguarum, sine praeceptis dialectices, ac Rhetorices percipi, aut tradi potest? Quomodo sine his adminiculis intelligi publicae leges aut enarrari possunt? qui sermon-legum non assequitur, qui non perspicit ubi requirenda sit definitio, ubi divisione utendum sit iν controversiis, quae inter se pugnent, quomodo sit ἀμφίβολα, explicanda, quomodo cum similitudo seu iuris species fucum facit iudici, dissimilitudo reperienda sit, & hoc genus innumera, obsecro, quid is in foro, aut iudiciis erit aliud, quam quod in Comediis κωφα προσωπα. Cum igitur tam late pateat harum levium artium usus in rebus maximis atque honestissimis, minime sunt escholis Christianorum extrudendae, sed potius summa cura discendae atque colende.

Quid, quod sacrae literae sine his artibus neque intelligi, neque doceri recte possunt. Quid enim intelliget is, qui de sermone iudicare nequit, qui nullam orationis construendae aut figurarum rationem ex Grammatica didicit. Qui non ex Dialectica & Rhetorica didicit, quae sint orationis paulo longioris partes, quae series sit propositionum, argumentorum, quae inter se consentiant, quae pugnent, ubi recte cohereant argumentorum partes, ubi non cohereant. Haec qui non videt in sacris libris, qui pleni sunt subtilissimarum disputationum, is simpliciter fateatur, se nihil intelligere. Saepe propter inscitiam linguarum hallucinati sunt in enarranda scriptura Thomas, Hugo, & Lyranus & alii similes, Sed Grammatica errata fortasse condonari eis poterant, si in longa enarratione summam tamen sententiae scriptorum quae enarrabant, retinuissent. At cum in enarrando prorsus discedant ab autoribus velut eorum obliti, saepe etiam dissentiant ab eis, ac aliena a pietate doceant, non sunt digni venia. Et haec acciderunt magna ex parte propter inscitiam harum artium de quibus nunc loquor. Quem Psalmum, quam paginam Prophetarum isti interpretes dextre enarrant? Neque enim viderunt propositionum ac argumentorum ordinem, Quam infoeliciter tractaverunt Epistolam Pauli ad Romanos, quia orationis Oeconomian non animadvertit.

Neque haec eo dico, quod ad intelligendam scripturam nihil opus sit doctore spiritu sancto. Ego vero ita censeo, res spirituales recte cognosci non posse, nisi corda nostra moveat ac doceat Spiritus sanctus. Id quod significavit Paulus cum inquit, Spiritualia spiritualiter examinanda esse.

Et tamen, quoniam Spiritus sanctus docet nos per verbum, sermonis natura cognoscenda est. Atqui cum sine harum artium scientia nemo de ser mone iudicare possit, adferre nos hoc instrumentum ad scripturae tractationem oportet.

Quid aliud est donum linguarum, nisi Eloquentia, cum autem iubeat Apostolus linguarum varietati operam dare, propter Ecclesiae utilitatem, existimemus & nobis praecipi, ut Eloquentiae studia colamus, quo rectius tractare scripturam possimus. Et quemadmodum initio Ecclesiae, donum linguarum donatum est Apostolis, ut Evangelium late spargere inter Gentes possent. Ita & hoc seculo videntur esse linguarum studia excitata ad ecclesiasticam doctrinam repurgandam.

Ad haec cum Paulus scribat Episcopum debere διδακτικον esse, requirit ab eo, non tantum communem Christianae doctrinae cognitionem, sed etiam scientiam quandam docendi. Qui autem nullam viam disponendae orationis, nullam argumentandi rationem norunt, qui confuse, quidquid in buccam venit, dicunt, tantum abest, ut doceant auditorem & dimittant de rebus dubiis certiorem, ut magis etiam incertum dimittant. Et cum saepe in Ecclesia de maximis rebus controversiae existant, nec explicare illas, nec verum defendere sciet is, qui non satis instructus est a Dialectica ac Rhetorica. Et haud scio an pleraeque controversiae, si diserte proponerentur, diiudicari ac tolli possent.

Saepe enim non de re, sed de verbis dissensio est, ubi ineruditi quia non vident quid conveniat, sine fine rixantur, δυσκωφόι cum δυσκωφοις, ut est in Proverbio. Et cum de re dissensio est, quoniam in disputando multa aliena a proposito admiscent, non explicant controversias, sed magis obscurant tractando, ut cognosci & diiudicari nequeant. Haec vitia disputationum vident isti qui mediocrem usum artium dicendi habento Quod cum ita sit, magna carum utilitas est in docenda Ecclesia, Atque hoc tempore in tot dissensionibus maxime oportuit eos qui praesunt Ecclesiis, his artibus instructos esse, ad sanandas mentes, quae in tanta varietate opinionum, nihil certi constituere possunt. Neque his aliae ratione ulla consuli potest, nisi perspicue prudenterque docendo Magnam partem contentionum tollent, si exposuerint λογομαχίας in quibus litigatur de verbis, non de re, Si docuerint fugiendas esse quaestiones, quae nihil ad aedificationem conducunt, quales sunt. Quare Deus permiserit labi Adamum, Ubi sint animae mortuorum. Huiusmodi quaestiones curiositatem alunt, non pietatem.

Reliquae quaestiones, quarum cognitio necessaria est, quales sunt, Quomodo consequamur remissionem peccatorum, fide an propter nostra opera. Quid sit fides, et quomodo fiat in animis. Quae sit vis sacramentorum, Quatenus traditiones humanas servare oporteat, hae si habeant artificem qui dilucide tractare sciet, non difficulter intelligentur a bonis mentibus quae doceri se patiuntur. Obsecro, quid illa moles commentariorum Fabri, aut Roffensis & similium, lectori conferre queat, ubi tam multa praeter rem, & aliena a caussa congeruntur. Qui sic docent, conscientias dubitantes magis etiam perturbant. Proinde non exiguas utilitates adferunt Ecclesiae eloquentiae studia coniuncta cum scientia Christianae doctrinae.

Quando autem ratio, seu Philosophia de Dei voluntate iudicat, tum fere errat. Et quanquam per omnes fidei articulos ire possemus, & ostendere, non esse a ratione petendum iudicium de articulis fidei, tamen ne longum faciamus, contrahemus aliquot articulos in tria capita.

Primo enim errat ratio de rerum gubernatione. Nam etiamsi permittat, quod Deus condiderit res, tamen offensa ratio, quia in mundo tam multa iniuste fiunt, vam potest statuere a deo res gubernari, sed somniat, quod Deus nunc propemcam ociosus sinat sine suo quodam impetu naturam ferri, sicut Faber, qui navers fecit, ubi absolvit, discedit ab ea, & committit eam fluctibus aut gubernatori. Hic doctrina Christiana diversumdocet, & monet nos, ne per Philosophiam decipiamur, id est, tum fallit Phisophia, quando de Deo aut de Dei consiliis incipit iudicare, quia animalis homo non percipit ea quae Dei sunt. Significat autem animalis homo totam naturam hominis, non renovatam a Spiritu sancto. Illa itaque natura hominis nihil potest de voluntate Dei affirmare, quae tantum discitur ex verbo Dei, sicut Esaias ait. Ad legem & ad testimonium, qui non dixerit secundum Verbum hoc, non erit ei matutina lux.

Sunt igitur sententiae certae & manifestae ex scriptura eligindae, quibus discimus, non modo res a Deo conditas esse, sed etiam gubernari. Deum nos alere ac tegere, servare alios, alios punire, nec discessisse Deum tanquam a navi fabrum, sed regere res, sicut gubernator navim regit, ut probant hae sententiae, Matth. 6. Pater vester qui in caelis est, pascit illa. Matth. 10. Duo passeres asse veneunt, & unus ex illis non cadet super terram sine patre caelesti. Ioan. 3. Pater meus usque modo operatur, & ego operor. Ephe. 1. Operatur omnia secundum consilia voluntatis suae. Coloss. 1. Omnia per ipsum consistunt. Actu. 17. In ipso viumus, & movemur, & sumus. Psal. 103. Dante te illis colligent. Prover. 16. Omnes viae hominum patent oculis eius &c. Et in oratione Dominica, Panem nostrum quotidianum da nobis hodie. Deuteronom. 9. Non in solo pane vivit homo, sed etiam in omni verbo, quod procedit de ore Dei.

Secundo, errat Philosophia de iustificatione, si statuat coram Deo, satis esse civilem iusticiam. At Christiana doctrina docet, iusticiam coram Deo, Fidem esse in Christum. Et sicut in apibus naturales virtutes non efficiunt eas Christianas, aut iustas coram Deo, sic nec civiles mores, seu naturales virtutes non faciunt iustos coram Deo. Habent apes prudentiam, quia tecta construunt & constituunt Rempub. Habent & iusticiam, quia suis regibus diligentissime obtemperant. Item iuvant se mutuo levant onere fessas, a vi & iniuriis abstinent. Est in eis & mira fortitudo, quia acerrime dimicant cum fucis. Postremo magna temperantia in eis est, quis & venerem nesciunt, & mella ad usus nostros conficiunt. Et sicut ob has virtutes non dicuntur Christianae, ita neque homines sunt Christiani propter solos civiles mores, sed quia credunt, quod propter Christum Deus peccata nobis condonaverit, & receperit nos in gratiam, sicut Paulus dicit, Arbitramur iustificari hominem per fidem sine operibus legis, &c. i. corinth. i. Non glorietur omnis caro in conspectu eius, ex ipso autem vos estis in Christo lesu, qui factus est nobis sapientia a Deo, iusticia, & sanctificatio, & redemptio.

Neque vero tollit Christiana doctrina civiles mores, sed eos exigit, & Philosophiam seu rationem praecipientem de civilibus moribus adprobat, sicut & civiles magistratus adprobat, & testatur se Deus auctorem esse civilium ordinationum Roma. 13. Omnia quae ordinata sunt, a Deo ordinata sunt, & ab his qui non habent spiritum sanctum, tamen exigit, ut frenentur civili iusticia, ut docet Lex est iniustis posita. Item Lex est paedogogus. Sed eo dissentiunt ratio & Evangelium, quod Evangelium negat civilem iusticiam iustificare coram Deo.
