Tertio fallitur Philosophia, cum putat rationem satis habere virium, ex sua natura contravitia, nec videt opus esse spiritu sancto, qui et corda reddat puriora, & regat nos, ne vel ab infirmitate naturae, vel a diabolo praecipitemur in manifesta flagitia. Sed Evangelium docet cor esse impurum, & fervere concupiscentia. Item diabolum nobis ita insidiari, ut etiam in manifesta flagitia impellat. Ideo promittit spiritum sanctum, qui corda transformit, regat, & defendat nos. Ioan. 15. Sine me nihil potestis facere, & ad Roma. 8. Qui spiritu Dei aguntur, ii sunt filii Dei. Qualis autem sit homo, & quo ruat, desertus a Deo, Saulis exemplum ostendit, Sic igitur Philosophia, seu iudicium rationis, de divina voluntate nihil certi affirmare potest, sed de natura rerum, deque civilibus moribus recte iudicare potest. Quare errant, qui ex ratione aut Philosophia iudicant de doctrina Christiana. Immo uberrima consolatio est piorum, quod non ex rationis nostrae iudicio aestimanda est voluntas Dei, & quoties de iustisicatione cogitamus scire, quod Deus gratispropter Christum nobis ignoscat, mirifice consolatur animum. Rursus etiamerrant ii, qui aspernantur Philosophiam iudicantem de naturalibus rebus. Nam id est, contemnere Dei donum, cum omnibus creaturis Dei, cum gratiarum actione uti debeamus, & sentire, quod sint Dei beneficia. 1. Timoth. 4. Et sicut insania esset dicere, quod ex artis sutoriae praeceptis sit iudicundum de Christiana doctrina, ita desipiunt, qui ex philosophia iudicant de doctrina Christiana. Dicit enim Petrus in ii. Prophetias non esse humana voluntate editas. Rursus, ut desiperent etiam ii, qui dicerent artem sutoriam pugnare cum doctrina Christiana, it a desipiunt, qui naturae & morum civilium cognitionem dicunt pugnare cum religione. Ideo Paulus non ait Philosophiam malam esse, sed sic ait Videte ne quis vos decipiat per Philosophiam, abusus vituperatur, res ipsa non improbatur, ut si quis dicat, vide ne decipiat te vinum. Hic etiam commemorari alii articuli fidei possent, de quibus ratio non potest recte iudicare. Sed satis sit monuisse, quod de articulis fidei, non ratio, sed scriptura consulenda sit.

Deinde quod addit, inanem deceptionem, hic significat argumenta de Dei voluntate ex Philosophia collecta. Non est enim Philosophia, cum de divina voluntate ex ratione iudicamus, sed sunt inania somnia. Sicut Epicurus negavit Deo res nostras curae esse, animas immortales esse. Item Platonici disputaverunt, quomodo Deus gignat quandam cogitationem, uteluderent dogmata Christiana de filio Dei. At Aristoteles mundum esse aeternum, nec aliquando cepisse. Et quidam scripsit fundamenta doctrinae Christianae, Platonicam Philosophiam esse. Sic pro certis affirmare, quae ratio aut Philosophia non potest affirmare, immo quae prorsus sunt extra iudicium rationis aut philosophie posita, ea est inanis deceptio.

Et sicut in lege praeceptum est, ne quis dissimile semen serat in eodem agro, cavendum est, ne commisceantur Evangelii doctrina et Philosophia, sed Evangelium est doctrinae vitae spiritualis & iustificationis coram Deo. Philosophia vero est doctrina vitae corporalis, sicut vides medicinam valetudini servire, mores civiles communi hominum tranquillitati & iudiciis exercendis, deinde alias artes aliorum usuum caussa repertas esse. Geometria metitur corpora, in aedificando. Arithmetica contractus, & magnam partem societatis humanae gubernat. Est autem huiusmodi usus necessarius, & a Deo probatus, sicut multis locis Paulus docet, utendum esse creaturis Dei, cum gratiarum actione. Et tamen usus talium rerum corporalium non iustificat coram Deo, sicut praecepit Deus edere, neque tamen id iustificat hominem. Ita dieta uti ex medici praescripto, tempestatum discrimina observare, non iustificant. Item civiles mores, ut non gerere arma, ubi leges prohibent, aut vestire secundum leges, exigit Deus, neque tamen id iustificat coram Deo. Paulus praecipit 2. Timoth. 2.ὀρθοτομειν verbum veritatis. Ideo & nobis cavendum est, ac misceamus Evangelium & Philosophiam, aut hanc damnemus, ea ex parte, qua adprobat eam Deus, sed rite separemus, & discernamus rationis doctrinam a Prophetia, & cuius usus caussa utraque doceatur consideremus.

Postremo hoc omnium maxime refert admonere, quod facilime ratio decipitur, ut adsentiatur incompertis & falsis, etiam in diiudicandis naturalibus rebus, & multa turpia contra naturam probet, quando non gubernatur verbo Dei, Roman. 1. Obscuratum est in sipiens cor eorum. Item, tradidit illos Deus in reprobum sensum. Item. 2. Thessa. 2. Mittet illis Deus efficacem deceptionem, ut credant mendacio. Item, Proverbi. 29. Cum defecerit Prophetia, dissipabitur populus. Cuius rei multa exempla videmus in iis, qui sibi mortem consciverunt, ut Seneca Marcellinum quendam praedicat libro Epistolarum, Et quam multa contra naturam Diogenes fecerit, notum est. Ostendit autem Deus nobis huiusmodi horribilia exempla, ut incutiat nobis metum, ne nimium confidamus viribus nostrae rationis, & ut verbo suo doceri & regi nos patiamur.

## 2:8
According to the tradition of men.

He calls the traditions of men rites instituted for meriting righteousness, or what they commonly call the worship of God, such as celibacy, or monastic orders, or distinctions of clothing or foods. Moreover, this passage is also to be understood in such a way that they ought to beware, lest they place righteousness in such observ ations. To observe rites does no harm, because the kingdom of God, is not food, nor drink, but to think that those rites are necessary things for justification, that Paul forbids, so that if any monk, having observed his rule, thinks that he is therefore righteous before God, he errs. As Christ says, In vain do they worship me with the commandments of men, but concerning traditions it will be spoken of again below.

Latin:

Secundum traditionem hominum.

Traditiones hominum vocat, ritus institutos ad promerendam iusticiam, seu quod vulgo vocant, ad cultum Dei, sicut caelibatus, aut ordinationes monasticae aut discrimina vestium vel ciborum. Est autem & hic locus sic accipiendus, quod debeant cavere, ne ponant iusticiam in eiusmodi observationibus onibus. Ritus servare nihil nocet, quia regnum Dei, nec esca, nec potus est, sed sentire, quod illi ritus sint res necessariae ad iustificationem, id prohibet Paulus, ut si aliquis monachus servata sua regula, sentiat se propterea iustum esse coram Deo, is errat. Sicut Christus dicit, Frustra colunt me mandatis hominum, Sed de traditionibus infra rursus dicetur.

## 2:8
According to the ordinances of the world.

He calls the ordinances of the world civil and political ordinances, such as are, to obey magistrates, to render to whom you owe according to the laws, to defraud no one, to do injury to no one, not to steal, not to kill, not to bear arms when the laws forbid it.

Furthermore, God demands the observation of such ordinances of the world, which have been made by magistrates for the sake of public peace, Romans 13. Let every soul be subject to the powers. And he wishes men to be restrained from crimes by the doctrine of the law and by punishments, according to that, The law is laid down for the unjust. This civil righteousness, which reason can in some way effect by its own powers, is to be performed, even though we are not yet pronounced righteous before God on account of it, as Paul teaches, If by the law justice is, in vain Christ has died, wherefore beyond human or civil justice, it is necessary that faith be added, by which we believe that we are forgiven on account of Christ. It is therefore the opinion of Paul, that Christian justice is not merely that political and civil justice, such as it is sometimes possible to find even in the impious.

Latin:

Secundum ordinationes mundi.

Ordinationes mundi, vocat civiles & politicas ordinationes, ut sunt, parere magistratibus reddere quibes iuxta leges debes, neminem defraudare, nulli iniuriam facere, non furari, non occidere, non gerere arma cum leges prohibent.

Porro Dein exigit observationem huiusmodi mundi ordinationum, quae pacis publicae caussa a magistratibus factae sunt, Roma. i. Omnis anima potestatibus subdita sit. Et vult doctrina legis & poenis coherceri homines a flagitiis, iuxta illud, Lex est iniustis posita. Haec iusticia civilis, quam ratio suis viribus aliquo modo efficere potest, praestandae est, tametsi coram Deo propter ea nondum pronunciamur iusti, Sicut Paulus docet, Siper legem iusticia est, frustra Christus mortuus est, Quare praeter humanam seu civilem iusticiam, necesse est fidem accedere, qua credamus nobis propter Christum ignosci. Est igitur Pauli sententia, Christianam iusticiam, non esse tantum politicam illam & civilem iusticiam, qualem etiam in impiis aliquando est reperire.

## 2:8
And not according to Christ.

I said above that a comparison of human justice with Christian justice had been instituted, here therefore the other member of the comparison is added, when he says, Not according to Christ. That is to say, Philosophy, human traditions, the ordinances of the world hand down a certain justice. But that is not Christian justice.

Latin:

Et non secundum Christum.

Dixi supra institutam esse collationem iusticiae humanae cum Christiana, hic igitur alterum collationis membrum additur, cum ait, Non secundum Christum. Q. d. Philosophia, traditiones humanae, ordinationes mundi quandam iusticiam tradunt. sed ea non est Christiana iusticia.

## 2:9
Because in him dwells all the fullness of the Godhead.

Moreover, this is the cause why we are made complete through Christ. Wherefore in Christ dwells the whole fullness of the divinity bodily, that is, because God is in Christ, therefore he both satisfies for sin, and deletes sin, and abolishes eternal death, and sanctifies those who believe in him, just as ii. Corin. v. says, For God was in Christ, reconciling the world to himself, not imputing their sins to them &c. For neither can sin be taken away, or death be abolished, and eternal life be given, except by God. Human justice does not abolish death, does not bring eternal life, but for dying men, it itself also is abolished and confounded, as the Psalm says, No flesh shall be justified in his sight. Therefore Isaiah, when he calls Christ God and the father of the world to come, testifies that we are justified and vivified by divine power. Therefore Paul also says that we have been perfected through Christ because God dwells in him.

Latin:

Quoniam in illo inhabitat omnis ple-nitudo deitatis.

Est autem haec caussa, quare per Christum consummati sumus. Quare in Christo habitat tota plenitudo divinitatis corporaliter, id est, quia in Christo Deus est, ideo & satisfacit pro peccato, & delet peccatum, & aeternam mortem abolet, & sanctificat eos, qui in ipsum credunt, sicut & ii. Corin. v. ait, Quia Deus erat in Christo, mundum reconcilians sibi, non imputans eis peccata &c. Neque enim potest aut peccatum tolli, aut mors aboleri, & vita aeterna donari, nisi a Deo. Humana iusticia non abolet mortem, non adfert vitam aeternam, sed morientibus hominibus, ipsa quoque aboletur & confunditur, sicut ait Psal. Non iustificabitur in conspectu eius omnis caro. Ideo Esaias cum vocat Christum Deum & patrem futuri seculi, testatur, quod diviva potentia iustificemur & vivificemur. Igitur & Paulus dicit, ideo nos consummatos esse per Christum, quia Deus in eo habitet.

## 2:9
Corporally.

I understand Paul to mean not only that God is truly with Christ, but also that the divine nature has put on the human, and has been made flesh, as if Paul were saying: In other saints God also dwells, but spiritually, that is, he moves and stirs and governs them, as Paul says Ro. 8. Those who are led by the spirit of God are the sons of God. But in Christ he dwells corporally, that is, he is in him in such a way, not only that he moves him, but that he is the same person with the body. And therefore Christ is also properly redemption, sanctification, the father of the world to come, because he is by nature God sanctifying and vivifying, and therefore we are perfected through Christ, because he is by nature God, who both redeemed and sanctified us, etc.

But someone might say, why does he say the whole fullness of the divinity, when only the Son put on flesh? I answer. Paul calls it the whole fullness, speaking not of the persons, but of the power, as if he were saying: There are not in Christ certain gifts, just as in other saints. For there are divisions of graces, but the divine nature itself infused itself into the flesh with all its power, and it so infuses itself, that it dwells there bodily, just as John also says, Of his fullness we have all received.

Vulgarius thus explains this passage, for he opposes the word bodily to the name of shadow or figure, following the example placed a little further down, where Paul made an antithesis of shadow and body, saying, which are a shadow of things to come, but the body is of Christ. Vulgarius alludes to that passage, and thinks that the word bodily is to be accepted in this place in this way, not concerning the nature of Christ, but concerning the action, or effect, as if Paul were saying, The ceremonies of the law, because they are a shadow, did not justify but only signified the righteousness of faith, which was to be revealed in the Gospel. But Christ exhibits that righteousness truly, abolishes sin, death, and sanctifies believers. This interpretation does not displease me, nor does it dissent from mine. We, however, have said that not only the effects of Christ, but also the nature is described.

Latin:

Corporaliter.

Ego sic intelligo Paulum velle dicere non modo, quod vere Deus sit Cum Christo, sed etiam, quod divina natura induerit humanam, & facta sit caro quasi diceret Paulus, In aliis sanctis habitat etiam Deus, sed spiritualiter, hoc est, movet & agitat & gubernet eos, sicut Paulus Ro. 8. Qui aguntur spiritu Dei, filii Dei sunt. sed in Christo corporaliter habitat, hoc est, sic est in eo, non tantum ut moveat eum, sed ut sit eadem persona cum corpore. Ideoque & Christus proprie est redemptio, sanctificatio, pa ter futuri seculi, quia ipse est natura Deus sanctificans & vivificans, ideoque per Christum consummati sumus, quia est natura Deus, qui & redemit & sauctificavit nos &c.

Sed dicat aliquis, cur ait tota plenitudo divini tatis, eum filius tantum, induerit carnem Respondeo. Paulus vocat totam plenitudinem, non de personis, sed de potentia loquens quasi dicat. Non sunt in Christo certa quaedam dona, sicut in aliis sanctis. sunt enim divisiones gratiarum, sed ipsa natura divina infudit se in carnem cum tota sua potentia, & ita se infundit, ut corporaliter ibi habitet, sicut et Iohannes ait, De plenitudine eius omnes accepimus.

Vulgarius sic enarrat hunc locum, opponit enim vocem corporaliter, nomini umbrae seu figurae, secutus exemplum paulo inferius positum, ubi Paulus antithesin fecit, umbrae & corporis, inquiens, quae sunt umbra futurorum, corpus autem est Christi. Ad eum locum alludit Vulgarius, & sentit sic esse hoc loco accipiendam vocem corporaliter, non de natura Christi, sed de actione, seu affectu, quasi dicat Paulus, Cerimoniae legis, quia umbra sunt, non iustificabant sed significabant tantum iusticiam fidei, quae erat in Evangelio revelanda. Sed Christus exhibet eam iusticiam vere, abolet peccatum, mortem, & sanctificat credentes. Haec inter pretatio mihi non displicet, nec a mea dissentit. Nos autem non tantum effectus Christi, sed naturam etiam describi diximus.

## 2:10
And in him you are made complete.

It is an antithesis, that is to say, neither by civil justice, nor by Jewish rites, nor by the traditions of monks, nor by any cleanliness of yours which is in you, are you made complete. But only on account of Christ do you have this benefit, that you are reckoned as righteous, as if you were satisfying the law. And it is a very significant opinion, worthy that all the pious always have it in their sight. First. It teaches clearly, concerning justification before God, that the conscience ought to determine that we are pronounced righteous freely on account of Christ, even if we do not yet satisfy the law. Nevertheless, in the meantime we are pleasing, but on account of Christ. Just as the Psalm also teaches. Blessed are they whose iniquities are forgiven, and whose sins are covered. That is to say: The saints have sins, but they are pronounced righteous for this reason, because God accepts them through mercy, and overlooks their sins. Therefore, this passage brings the most ample consolation to the pious, who acknowledge their own uncleanness, who see how far they are from the perfection of the law. For it commands these to look elsewhere, namely to Christ, and to determine that they are pleasing to God, that they are righteous, on account of something else outside themselves, even if the remnants of sin still cling within us. This is the huge benefit of the Gospel, which it is necessary to know, lest faith be shaken from us, which could not exist if it had to be thought that men are pleasing only when they have satisfied the law. Our newness and the righteousness of a good conscience are indeed necessary. But let the person always hold that goal, that we are pleasing on account of Christ even if we do not satisfy the law. When we hold this faith, then that obedience of ours also pleases God, as if the law had been satisfied it is, because the person is pleasing. Paul teaches this in Romans 10. The sum of the law is Christ for righteousness to everyone who believes. That is, he who trusts in Christ, he is already righteous, and has that which the Law requires; the Law does not accuse, it does not condemn him. His works are now pleasing to God, as if the law had been satisfied. For Christians have the praise of a fulfilled law, even if they do not fulfill it in reality. Just as David says: I have done judgment and justice, etc. These things are very clear; we teach most simply. First, that faith is necessary, by which it must be established that we are pleasing to God freely on account of Christ. Then we say that the righteousness of a good conscience is also necessary. But this, before God, is still unclean and imperfect, according to that: In your sight shall no living man be justified Psalm 143:2. Therefore we return to faith, and we teach that it must be established that the person is pleasing freely on account of Christ. Thus the conscience is freed from the terrors of sin and death, and on account of this faith that newness is pleasing. The scholastics do not teach these things, who dream that a man satisfies the law, likewise they dream that a man, on account of his own dignity and the fulfillment of the law, is pronounced righteous before God. These dreams obscure the benefit of Christ, and lead consciences into despair.

Secondly, Paul warns in this place, lest we seek a new perfection in human traditions. For admiration of peculiar works always sticks in men. When they feel that they are far from the perfection of the law, they seek certain specific works. When they do these, they already imagine that they satisfy the law and are entirely righteous. Hence the Jews accumulated rites and sacrifices; hence monasticism arose in the Church. Nor do I criticize these exercises here, but I criticize the opinion that seeks and chooses monasticism for that reason, as if we were already pleasing to God on account of it, as if that were the perfection and consummation of righteousness before God. For Paul recalls us from that opinion, as if he were saying: Do not summon Jewish or monastic rites when you feel that you are far from the perfection of the law. Nor think that those rites are the perfection of the law. But look to Christ, and determine that on account of Him you are pronounced righteous, as if you were perfect, as if you were entirely satisfying the law. Retain and exercise this faith in all temptations. Thirdly, we are said to be and are therefore consummated on account of Christ, because Christ is effective through the Holy Spirit in believers and effects eternal life in them. But civil righteousness, monasticism, or Levitical rites are not the knowledge of God or eternal life, but they kill along with the nature of man. Thus there is a twofold distinction of the righteousness of works and the righteousness of faith or mercy, By faith we are pronounced just and perfect on account of that which is outside of us, namely on account of Christ. And yet our obedience, insofar as it is in us, is imperfect and unclean. Furthermore, civil justice is not the knowledge of God, or spiritual life, but the justice of faith is the knowledge of God and spiritual life.

Latin:

Et in illo estis consummati.

Antithesis est, Q. d. Nec civili iusticia, nec ritibus Iudaicis, nec monachorum traditionibus nec ulla vestra mundicie, quae in vobis est, consummati estis. Sed tantum propter Christum habetis hoc beneficium ut reputemini pro iustis, ac si satisfaceretis legi. Estque sententia valde insignis, digna quam omnes pii semper in conspectu habeant. Primum. nperspicue docet, de iustificatione coram Deo, quod conscientia statuere debeat nos gratis propter Christum pronunciari iustos, etiamsi nondum satisfaciamus legi. Tamen interim placemus, sed propter Christum. Sicut & Psalmus docet. Beati quorum remissae sunt iniquitates, & quorum tecta sunt peccata. Q. d. Sancti habent peccata, sed eo pronunciantur iusti, quia Deus eos per misericordiam acceptat, & dissimulat eorum peccata. Itaque hic locus amplissimam consolationem affert piis, qui agnoscunt suam immundiciem, qui vident quam pro cul a perfectione legis absint. Hos enim iubet alio respicere, videlicet in Christum, & statuere, quod placeant Deo, quod sint iusti, propter aliud extra ipsos, etiam si in nobis hereant adhuc reliquiae peccati. Hoc est ingens Evangelii beneficium, quod cognosci necesse est, ne excutiatur nobis fides, quae non posset existere, si sentiendum esset, tunc demum placere homines, cum legi satisfecissent. Necessaria est quidem nostra novitas, & iusticia bona conscientiae. Sed persona semper illum scopum teneat, placere nos propter Christum etiamsi non satisfaci amus legi. Cum hanc fidem tenemus, iam etiam placet Deo illa nostra obedientia, quasi legi satisfactum esset, quia persona placet. Hoc docet Paul. Roma. x. Summa legis Christus est id iusticiam omni credenti. Id est, Qui confidit Chnisto, is iam iustus est, & habet id quod Lex requirit, Lex non accusat, non condemnat eum. Eius opera iam placent Deo, quasi legi satisfactum sit. Habent. n. Christiani laudem impletae legis, etiam si reipsa non impleant. Sicut David ait. Feci iudicium & iusticiam &c. Haec sunt valde perspicua, simplicissime docemus. Primum fidem esse necessariam, qua statuendum est, quod gratis Deo placeamus propter Christum. Deinde dicimus & iusticiam bonae conscientiae necessariam esse. Sed haec coram Deo adhuc est immunda & imperfecta, iuxta illud. Non iustificabitur in conspectu tuo omnis vivens. Ideo redimus ad fidem, & docemus statuendum esse, quod propter Christum persona gratis placeat. Ita conscientia liberatur a terroribus peccati & mortis, & propter hanc fidem placet illa novitas. Haec non docent scholastici, qui somnianthominem legi satissacere, item somniant hominem propter propriam dignitatem & legis impletionem, iustum coram Deo pronunciari Haec somnia obscurant Christi beneficium, & conscientias adducunt in desperationem.

Secundo monet Paulus in hoc loco, ne novam perfectionem queramus traditionibus humanis. Semper enim heret in hominibus admiratio pecularium operum. Cum sentiunt se procul a perfectione legis abesse, quaerunt certa quaedam opera. Haec cum faciunt, iam fingunt se legi satisfacere & prorsus iustos esse. Inde Iudaei cumulabant ritus & sacrificiainde in Ecclesia orti sunt monachatus. Neque hic exercitia reprehendo, sed reprehendo opinionem quae ideo monochatum expetit atque eligit, quasi propterea Deo iam placeamus, quasi illa sit perfectio & consummatio iusticiae coram Deo. Paulus enim revocat nos ab illa opinione, quasi dicat. Ne accersatis ritus Iudaicos, aut monasticos eum sentitis vos abesse a perfectione legis. Nec putate perfectionem legis esse, ritus illos. Sed intuemini Christum, & statuite propter hunc vos pronunciari iustos, quasi essetis perfecti, quasi legi prorsus satisfaceretis. Hanc fidem retinete atque exercete, in omnibus tentationibus. Tertio dicimur & ideo consummati propter Christum, quia Christus est efficax per Spiritum sanctum in credentibus & efficit in eis aeternam vitam. At civilis iusticia, monastica, aut ritus Levitici non sunt noticia Dei aut vita aeterna, sed occidunt cum hominis natura. Ita duplex est discrimen iusticiae operum, & iusticiae fidei seu misericordiae, Fide pronunciamur iusti & perfecti propter illud extra nos, scilicet propter Christum. Et tamen nostra obedientia quaetenus in nobis est, est imperfecta & immunda. Praeterea civilis iusticia non est noticia Dei, aut vita spiritualis, sed iusticia fidei est noticia Dei & vita spiritualis.

## 2:10
Who is the head of all principality and power.

The head in this place signifies a prince or lord. For Paul does not call Christ the head of the church in this place only, but he attributes to him the principality over all powers in heaven and earth, and signifies that he reigns by divine power, or sits at the right hand of the father above all creatures in heaven, earth, and the underworld, as if he were saying this. Because Christ reigns over all creatures, therefore he can defend us against the devil and against death and sin, as he says in Romans eight, If God is for us, who is against us?

I warned above in this collation that the definition of Christian righteousness is contained here, for he said that we have been made perfect through Christ, that is, that we are pronounced righteous on account of Christ, as if we had satisfied the law, when nevertheless we are still far from the perfection of the law. Now therefore he completes the definition, and teaches what Christ does in believers, and so he makes two members of Christian renewal, mortification and vivification, just as Christ also makes two members, in Luke last, Repentance and remission of sins to be preached. And in the psalm, The Lord takes pleasure in those who fear him, and in those who hope in his mercy.

Mortification or repentance is to acknowledge sin, and to be truly terrified by the judgment of God, just as Jeremiah 2 says, After you showed me, I struck my thigh, I was confounded and ashamed. Nor can there be faith without such fear or terror, just as Isaiah, last chapter To whom shall I look? Except to the poor and contrite in spirit, and him who trembles at my words. And Joel, 2. Rend your hearts.

Vivification is for the conscience to be raised up through faith, or trust, and to take hold of the consolation which thus believes that God has forgiven sins on account of Christ. And when Christ consoles the heart in this manner, he brings eternal life, as he says. He who believes in me shall not die. He also brings other gifts of the spirit, charity, humility, chastity, etc.

In whom also you were circumcised with a circumcision that is made without hands, while you put off the body of the sins of the flesh, by the circumcision of Christ, buried together with him through baptism, in whom also you rose again together with him, through the faith of the operation of God, etc.

This passage delivers the order and form of renewal of ours, for it describes mortification, or repentance, and vivification and the remission of sins. Justification should be understood relatively as the acceptance or imputation of righteousness. But this happens when we believe that we are received into grace on account of Christ. But here men must be taught how they may conceive faith. For many dream that they believe, when they are very far from faith. And that hypocrisy of faith produces a most harmful security in them.

Christ says John 16. The Holy Spirit will convict the world of sin, righteousness, and judgment. Furthermore, to convict is to condemn and to terrify. Therefore, it is repentance, so to acknowledge sins that we truly feel the wrath and judgment of God against sin, and are truly terrified.

Let readers know, however, in this place by these words of mortification, circumcision, burial, baptism, nothing else is signified but repentance. Often the variety of names causes much trouble for the unskilled, which is why I thought it useful to warn of this at present. Truly, for this reason Paul used many names, so that we might more rightly understand the force of repentance. The schools made three parts of repentance, contrition, confession, and satisfaction, which division they took more from the rites of the Church than from scripture, for satisfaction was taken from ecclesiastical censures, which were first instituted, not so that they might make satisfaction for sins, or that they might merit the remission of sins, but so that Bishops might either show to the Church the examples of the grace of God, who wished the fallen to be received, or might examine the fallen, whether they had repented from the heart. When the scholastic Theologians did not notice the cause of this custom, and the name of satisfaction nevertheless remained from the antiquated custom, they judged that satisfactions were certain works, on account of which sins are remitted, or which compensate for eternal death, but these things are foreign to the Gospel.

It is sufficiently evident that confession was taken from the custom of the Church. Scripture calls it confession, when a terrified conscience before God so acknowledges sin, that it confesses itself to be justly condemned, nor does it accuse the judgment of God, but only implores mercy and grace, and attributes praise to God, because He forgives sins. Such is the confession of David, If He shall say, you do not please, I am ready, let Him do what is good before Him. Such is the confession of Daniel, To You O Lord is justice, but to us confusion, This kind of confession is not understood, except in true terrors and afflic tions most bitter, where to confess is to acknowledge the just judgment and the just wrath of God, and to yield to the will of God, and yet to implore mercy, as the Psalm teaches, I said, I will confess my injustice against myself to the Lord, and you remit the impiety of my sin. For this every saint will pray to you. To these affections must be referred what some have taught, that repentance ought to be born from the love of justice.

The name of contrition corresponds more properly to the name of repentance, if however you understand not a sorrow feigned by human diligence, but the true terrors which God strikes into us when He shows us our sins, such as was the sorrow of either David when he was rebuked by Nathan, or of Paul when he was converted. Paul to the Romans calls it also shame or confusion. For there are in contrition, a true, not feigned, fear of divine judgment, an acknowledgment of sin, and shame before God. Nor is there need to subtly distinguish these affections. Nor is there need to subtly dispute what difference there is between the contrition of Judas and the contrition of Peter. It is enough to hold this, that those who in those terrors conceive consolation from the Gospel, and are raised up, imitate the repentance of Peter. Those who do not wish to believe the Gospel that sins are remitted, these follow the repentance of Judas.

Various terms describe the power of repentance here. Mortification signifies not only restraining external members, as some interpret, but it signifies truly to die, that is, to be terrified and to be struck with fear upon knowing the magnitude of sin and the wrath of God. Here he calls mortification the putting off of the body of sins, for repentance is not merely to bridle external members, for even hypocrites restrain these. But it is necessary to be stripped of concupiscence, that is, for it to be abolished through death, so that a new creature and spiritual life may succeed. And when he names the body of sins, he does not wish only external misdeeds to be understood, but he signifies that the whole carnal nature is corrupted by original sin.

Circumcision also signified repentance. For the flesh of the foreskin cut off was a sign that this flesh is addicted to death on account of sin. And here he says they were circumcised, because they had attained that which circumcision signified, for circumcision signified mortification and the promise of grace through the coming Christ. Baptism also designates repentance. For thus were those who were baptized taught, Repent.

Thus, therefore, is faith, which justifies, conceived, when terrified hearts hear the promise of grace and the remission of sins. That faith, when it raises up and consoles fearful consciences, is righteousness before God, and as Paul says, it brings us peace before God, that is, it causes consciences to be tranquil and to rejoice that God is appeased toward them. Therefore, faith cannot be felt without repentance or contrition. Paul taught the same thing in this place when he says that we were first circumcised, mortified, and baptized, and afterwards he adds that we were raised up with Christ through faith. And the particle is to be observed above all, that he says we were raised up THROUGH FAITH. Therefore, no merits of ours or satisfactions reconcile God or bring peace to the conscience. It is faith alone that raises us up in those terrors and justifies us before God.

Let us therefore understand that there are two parts of perfect repentance: contrition or mortification, and vivification or justification, when terrified minds obtain the remission of sins and peace through faith. And because a new life is born in hearts through this repentance, it follows that external members also are restrained so that they do not design anything against the will of God, ἀλλος βιος, ἀλλη διαιτα they say, For since there is another life and nature of the heart renewed by the Holy Spirit, it is necessary that the morals also be different. And throughout their whole life the saints are perfected and renewed in this manner, so that they may recognize more and more their own uncleanness, unbelief, and other diseases, and in turn be raised up through faith, and faith may grow with all its fruits. For thus says Paul, he who is within is renewed from day to day.

It is to be diligently observed in this place, that Paul says we are circumcised through Christ. For thus he speaks, in whom also you are circumcised, so that we may understand that repentance is effected in hearts by the Spirit of Christ. Therefore, repentance is not to be understood as a simulation of sadness or some external work, but it is a work of the Holy Spirit, who convicts hearts through the word concerning sin, concerning righteousness, and concerning judgment. For reason does not see that unbelief is a sin, and because it does not know that righteousness is to believe in Christ, therefore it attempts to justify itself by its own works. The Holy Spirit also convicts of that, and he convicts us of judgment because we do not judge, we do not condemn, but we love concupiscence. Then he also consoles and raises up the terrified conscience, when the remission of sins is announced, which, since it is a preaching of grace, not of wrath, is proper to the Gospel. Just as therefore Christ Luce ultimo commands repentance to be preached and the remission of sins in his name, so let us understand that these are the two parts of Christian doctrine, the one which shows and accuses sin, for which purpose Christ also used the preaching of the law Matth. 5. For through the law is the knowledge of sin, as Paul says. The other is that which properly has the name of Gospel, namely the preaching of the remission of sins, and the promise of the Holy Spirit. Thus the preaching of the Prophet Nathan is twofold, for first he rebukes the king on account of the adultery committed. Afterwards he consoles him, announcing the remission of sins, for he says, The Lord has taken away your sin.

Nor must it be concealed that in this place Paul says that faith is the efficacy of God, for thus he says, through faith the efficacy of God, δια της πιστεως της ενεργειας του θεου. For he warns that faith is the work of God, by which we are freed from death and terrors, and by which a new life is created in believers. Therefore we cannot produce faith in ourselves without the Holy Spirit. And how great the difference is between philosophical and Christian righteousness can be understood from this. For they err greatly who think that Christian righteousness is nothing other than civil morals.

Forgiving all things.

Vivification contains two things, the remission of sin or grace, and the gift of the Holy Spirit, or governance through the Holy Spirit. Paul treated these two things in this place with various figures. For first he says simply, forgiving all trespasses. Afterwards he adds figures, blotting out the handwriting that is against us, which was contrary to us through the laws, that is, taking away the evil conscience, because when God has remitted sins, he effects peace and joy in our hearts through faith, so that we may dare to establish what he has forgiven us, and we may dare to call him Father, and we may feel that he covers and helps us in all dangers, just as Rom. v. ait, Being justified by faith, we have peace through our Lord Jesus Christ, and he adds, through whom we have access, as if he were to say, through faith in Christ we approach the Father, and appease him, and obtain all things which we ask in the name of the Son, just as it is written in Hebrews 4, Let us approach with confidence to the throne of grace.

He calls the conscience a handwriting, because it testifies against us, and condemns us, as it is in the Psalm, My sin is always before me. And because the Law effects wrath, that is, it terrifies the conscience by showing sin, therefore he adds that Handwriting to be occupied with Decrees, that is, with the law. For he calls the law by a certain name, so that he may distinguish it from the spirit, just as elsewhere he calls it the letter, so also elsewhere he says, The sting of death is sin, and the power of sin is the Law, that is, sin itself terrifies and kills us, but it would not kill us unless the Law showed it to us, unless through the law we knew the wrath of God.

He calls the law a testimony, because it testifies against us, that we are cursed, that we are guilty of death, according to that, Cursed is he who has not remained in all things which are written in the book of the law &c. Therefore it is a handwriting deleted, through the remission of sins, that is, the evil conscience has been removed, peace has been granted, and the power of the law has been abolished, just as he says elsewhere, Death is swallowed up in victory.

Nailed to the cross

When he says the handwriting was nailed to the cross, he alludes to the custom of erecting trophies and from there he took the metaphor. For it was the custom in those places where enemies had been overcome, for trophies to be erected, that is, columns or arches on which they wrote and painted the deed done. Thus Paul says that Christ nailed the handwriting to the cross, that is, he erected a trophy, as if it were that on which he wrote or painted his victory. Furthermore, that figure signifies nothing else, except that Christ has conquered, and has made that victory manifest to all nations through the resurrection, and through the holy spirit.

This opinion also pleases. That the handwriting was affixed to the cross, that is, that sin or death was crucified, or abolished, by which personification we see that Paul has used it elsewhere, Roma. 7. He condemned sin concerning sin. Likewise, I will be your death, O death. And Eph. 2. Through the cross killing our enmities.

But it seems that Paul for this reason as it were painted the triumph of Christ with these figures, so that he might set it before us like a tablet, upon which a fearful conscience might always gaze, lest we should allow faith to be shaken from us through desperation. Great is the power of Satan, who attacks our hearts in various ways, and strives to drive them both to other sins and to desperation. But let this picture remind us, so that we may know that help is promised to us by Christ, so that we may know that the power of Satan has been broken by Christ. Therefore, this picture is not to be passed over cursorily, but the pious ought to resist by it, nay, they ought always to carry it around in their mind, and always gaze upon it with their whole mind.

Latin:

Qui est caput omnis principatus & po.

Caput hoc loco significat principem seu dominum. Non enim vocat Paulus Christum hoc loco caput ecclesiae tantum, sed principatum ei tribuit supra omnes potestates in caelo & terra, & significat eum regnare divina potentia, seu sedere ad dexteram patris supra omnes creaturas in caelo, terra, & inferis, quasi sic dicat. Quia Christus regnat supra omnes creaturas, ergo potest nos defendere ad versus diabolum & adversus mortem ac peccatum, ut Romanorum octavo ait, Si Deus pro nobis, quis contra nos?

Supra monui in hac collatiove definitionem Christianae iusticiae contineri, dixit enim, nos per Christum consummatos esse, id est, nos propter Christum iustos pronunciari, quasi legi satisfecerimus cum tamen adhuc procula perfectione legis absimus. Nunc igitur absolutt definitionem, & docet, quid agat Christus in credentibus, facit itaque duo membra Christianae renovationis, mortificationem et vivificationem, sicut & Christus duo membra facit, Lucae ultimo, Praedicari paenitenttam, & remissionem peccatorum. Et in psal. Beneplacitum est domino super timentes eum, & super his qui sperant in misericordia eius.

Mortificatio seu paenitentia, est cognoscere peceatum, & vere terrer iudicio Dei sicut Iere. 2 ait, Postquam ostendisti mihi, percussi femur meum confusus sum & erubui. Nec potest esse fides sine tali timore, vel terrore sicut Isaias, cap. ultimo Ad quem respiciam? nisi ad pauperculum & contritum spiritu, & trementem sermones meos. & Iohelis, 2. Scindite corda vestra.

Vivificatio, est conscientiam erigi per fidem, seu fiduciam, & consolationem capere quae, ita credat Deum condonasse peccata propter Christum. Et cum Christus consolatur ad hunc modum cor, adfert vitam aeternam, sicut ait. Qui credit in me, non morietur, Adfert & dona alia spiritus, caritatem, humilitatem, castitatem, &c.

Per quem & circumcisi estis circum-cisione, quae fit sine manibus, dum exuistis corpus peccatorum carnis, per circumcisionem Christi, conse-pulti simul cum illo per baptismum, in quo simul etiam cum illo resurrex-istis, per fidem operationis Dei &c.

Hic locus ordinem ac forman renofationis nostrae tradit, describit enim mortificationem, seu paenitentiam, & vivificationem ac remissionem peccatorum. Iustificatio relative intelligatur pro acceptatione seu imputatione Iusticiae. Haec autem fit, cum credimus nos recipi in gratiam propter Christum. Sed hic docendi sunt homines, quomodo fidem concipiant. Nam multi somniant se credere, cum longissime a fide absunt. Atque ea fidei hypocrisis nocentissimam securitatem in illis parit.

Christus inquit Ioh. 16. Spiritus Sanctus arguet mundum de peccato, iusticia ac iudicio. Porro aguere est condemnare ac perterrefacere. Est itaque paenitentia, ita agnoscere peccata, ut vere sentiamus iram ac iudicium Dei adversus peccatum, ac vere terreamur.

Sciant autem lectores hoc loco his verbis mortificationis, circumcisionis, sepulturae, baptismi nihil aliud nisi paenitentiam significari. Saepe varietas nominum imperitis multum negocii facit, quare arbitrabar prodesse, ut hoc in praesentia monerem. Verum ideo multis nominibus Paulus usus est, ut paenitentiae vim rectius intelligeremus. Scholae fecerunt partes paenitentiae tres, contritionem, confessionem, & satisfactionem, quam partitionem magis ab Ecclesiae ritibus sumpserunt, quam ex scriptura, nam satisfactio sumpta est a censuris ecclesiasticis, quae primum institutae sunt, non ut satis facerent pro peccatis, aut ut mererentur remissionem peccatorum, sed ut Episcopi vel ostenderent Ecclesiae, exempla gratiae Dei, qui lapsos recipi vellet, vel explorarent lapsos, an ex animo resipuisset. Huius moris caussam cum non animadverterent scholastici Theologi, et more antiquato nomen tamen satisfactionis extaret, arbitrati sunt satisfactiones certa opera esse, propter quae remittantur peccata, aut quae compensent mortem aeternam, sed haec sunt aliena ab Evangelio.

Confessionem a more Ecclesiae sumptam esse satis constat. Scriptura vocat confessionem, quando conscientia territa coram Deo, ita agnoscit peccatum, ut fateatur se iuste condemnari, nec accusat iudicium Dei, sed tantum misericordiam & gratiam implorat, & Deo laudem tribuit, quod condonet peccata. Talis est confessio David, Si dixerit, non places, presto sum, faciat quod bonum est coram se. Talis est confessio Danielis, Tibi Domine iusticia, nobis autem confusio, Non intelligi hoc genus confessionis, nisi in veris pavoribus & adflic tionibus acerbissimis potest, ubi confiteri, est agnos cere iustum iudicium et iustam iram Dei, & cedere voluntati Dei, et tamen implorare misericordiam, Sicut docet Psalmus, Dixi, confitebor adversum me iniusticiam meam Domino, & tu remittis impietatem peccati mei. Pro hoc orabit ad te omnis sanctus. Ad hos adfectus referendum est, quod quidam docuerunt paenitentiam ex amore iusticiae nasci debere.

Contritionis nomen magis proprie respondet nomini paenitentiae, si tamen intelligas non humana diligentia simulatum dolorem, sed veros terrores quo Deus incutit, cum ostendit nobis peccata nostra, qualis erat dolor vel Davidis cum a Nathan obiurgaretur, vel Pauli cum covuerteretur. Paulus ad Romanos pudorem seu confusionem etiam vocat. Sunt enim in contritione, metus iudicii divine verus, non simulatus, agnitio peccati, & pudor coram Deo. Nec opus est hos adfectus subtiliter discernere. Nec opus est opus subtiliter disputare quid inter contritionem Iudae, & contritionem Petri intersit. Satis est hoc tenere, quod qui in illis pavoribus concipiunt consolationem ex Evangelio, & eriguntur Petri paenitentiam imitantur. Qui non volunt Evangelio credere remittendi peccata hi Iudae penitentiam sequuntur.

Hic paenitentiae vim varia vocabula describunt. Mortificatio, significat non tantum externa membra continere, ut quidam interpretantur, sed significat vere mori hoc est perterrefieri, & expavescere cognita magnitudine peccati, & ira Dei. Hic vocat mortificationem, exuere corpus peccatorum, neque enim est paenitentia tantum frenare externa membra, cohercent enim haec etiam Hypocritae. Sed exui concupuscentiam oportet, hoc est per mortem aboleri, ut nova creatura & spiritualis vita succedat. Et cum corpus peccatorum nominat, non tantum intelligi vult externa malefacta, sed significat totam carnalem naturam corruptam esse peccato originis.

Significavit paenitentiam & circumcisio. Nam praecisa praeputii caro signum fuit, quod haec caro sit addicta morti propter peccatum. Et hic ait circumcisos esse, quia id quod significabat circumcisio, consecuti erant, significabat enim circumcisio mortificationem, & promissionem gratiae per venturum Christum. Designat paenitentiam etiam Baptismus. Sic enim docebantur hi qui baptisabantur, Agite paenitentiam.

Sic igitur concipitur fides, quae iustificat, cum corda perterrefacta audiunt promissionem gratiae, & remissionis peccatorum. Ea fides, cum erigit, & consolatur pavidas conscicutias, iusticia est coram Deo, et ut Paulus ait, Pacem nobis coram Deo adfert, id est efficit, ut conscientiae sint tranquillae, & gaudeant sibi Deum placatum esse, Non igitur sine paenitentia, seu contritione fides sentiri potest. Idem docuit hoc loco Paulus, cum ait, primum esse circumcisos, mortificatos, baptisatos, postea addit, resuscitatos esse cum Christo, per fidem. Estque in primis observanda particula, quod inquit nos PER FIDEM resuscitatos esse. Non igitur aliqua merita nostra, aut satisfactiones reconciliant Deum, aut pacem adferunt conscientiae, Sola fides est, quae in illis terroribus erigit nos, & coram Deo iustificat.

Duas igitur perfectae paenitentiae partes esse intelligamus, Contritionem seu mortificationem, & vivificationem, seu iustificationem, cum per fidem consequuntur remissionem peccatorum, & pacem mentes territae. Et quia per hanc paenitentiam nova vita cordibus innascitur, sequitur ut et externa men bra contineantur ne quid designent contra voluntatem Dei, ἀλλος βιος, ἀλλη διαιτα inquiunt, eum enim alia vita sit & natura cordis renovati a spiritusancto, necesse est & mores alios esse. Et per omnem vitam ad hunc modum perficiuntur ac renovantur sancti, ut magis atque magis agnoscant immundiciem suam, incredulitatem, & alios morbos, & rursus erigantur per fidem, & erescat fides cum omnibus fructibus suis. Sic enim ait Paulus, is qui intus est renovatur de die in diem.

Diligenter est hoc loco observandum, quod Paulus ait, nos esse per Christum circumcisos. Sic. n. loquitur in quo & circumcisi estis, ut intelligamus spiritu Christi paenitentiam effici in cordibus, Non est igitur simulatio tristiciae intelligenda paenitentia seu opus aliquod externum, sed opus spiritus sancti est, qui corda per verbum arguit de peccato, de iusticia, de iudicio. Nam ratio non videt incredulitatem esse peccatum, & quia non novit iusticiam esse, credere in Christum, ideo conatur se iustificare suis operibus, Id quoque arguit spiritus sanctus, Et de iudicio arguit nos quod concupiscentiam non iudicemus, non condemnemus, sed amemus. Deinde consolatur etiam & erigit conscientiam territam, cum annunciatur remissio peccatorum, quae cum sit gratiae, non irae praedicatio, propria Evangelii est. Sicut itaque Christus Luce ultimo praecipit praedicari paenitentiam & remissionem peccatorum in nomine ipsius, ita intelligamus has duas esse partes doctrinae Christianae, alteram quae ostendit, & accusat peccatum, quam ad rem & legis praedicatione, Christus usus est Matth. 5. Nam per legem cognitio peccati est, ut ait Paulus. Altera est, quae proprie habet Evangelii nomen, scilicet praedicatio remissionis peccatorum, & promissio spiritus sancti. Sic Prophetae Nathan duplex est paedicatio, Primum enim obiurgat regem propter admissum adulterium. Postea tertium consolatur, adnuncians remissionem peccatorum, in quit enim, Abstulit Dominus peccatum tuum.

Neque dissimulandum est, quod hoc loco Paulus fidem esse dicit efficaciam Dei, sic enim ait, per fidem efficacia Dei, δια της πιστεως της ενεργειας του θεου. Monet enim fidem esse opus Dei, quo a morte ac pavoribus liberamur, & quo vova vita in credentibus creatur. Non igitur possumus sine spiritu sancto fidem in nobis efficere. Et quantum inter iusticiam Philosophicam, et Christianam inter sit, hinc intelligi potest. Longe. n. errant, qui Christianam iusticiam nihil esse arbitrantur aliud, quam civiles mores.

Condonans omnia.

Vivificatio duo continet, remissionem peccato rum seu gratiam, & donationem spiritus sancti, seu gubernationem per spiritum sanctum. Haec duo variis figuris hoc loco tranctavit Paulus. Primum enim simpliciter dicit, condonans omnia delicta, Postea figuras subiicit, delens chirographum, quod contra nos est, quod erat nobis contrarium per leges, id est, tollens malam conscientiam, quia quando Deus remisit peccata, efficit pacem & gaudium in cordibus nostris per fidem, ut audeamus statuere, quid ignoverit nobis, & audeamus eum appellare patrem, & sentiamus quod tegat & iuvet nos in omnibus periculis, sicut Rom. v. ait, Iustificati per fidem, pacem habemus per Dominum nostrum Iesum Christum, & addit, per quem habemus adcessum, quasi dicat, per fidem in Christum nos ad patrem adcedere, & placare eum, & impetrare omnia, quae nomine filii petimus, sicut & Hebraeos, 4. scriptum est, Adeamus cum fiducia ad thronum gratiae.

Conscientiam adpellat chirographum, quia te stificatur contra nos, & condemnat nos, sicut est in Psal. Peccatum meum in conspectu meo semper. Et quia Lex iram efficit, hoc est terret conscientiam ostenso peccato, ideo addit Chirographum illud versari per Decreta, id est, per legem. Legem. n. de certa vocat, ut distinguat a spiritu, sicut alias literam vocat, sic & alias inquit, Aculeus mortis peccatum estuirtus peccati Lex, id est ipsum peccatum terret, & occidit nos, sed non occideret nisi Lex ipsum ostenderet nobis, nisi per legem cognosceremus iram Dei.

Legem vocat testimonium, quia testisicatur contra nos, quod simus maledicti, quod simus rei mortis, iuxta illud, Maledictus qui non permanserit in omnibus quae scripta sunt in libro legis &c. Est igitur Chirographum deletum, per remissionem peccatorum, hoc est, mala conscientia sublata est, donata pax est & vis legis abolita sicut inquit alias, Absorpta est mors in victoria.

Adfixum cruci

Cum ait affixum Chirographum cruci, alludit ad mortem trophaeorum erigendorum & inde metaphoram sumpsit. Erat enim mos iis locis, ubi hostes superati erant, erigi trophaea, hoc est columnas vel arcus in quibus scribebant et pingebant rem gestam. Sic Paulus ait, Christum adfixisse Chirographum cruci, id est, tropheum erexisse, tanquam in quo scripserit aut pinxerit victoriam suam. Porro, ea figura nihil aliud significat, nisi Christum vicisse, & eam victoriam omnibus gentibus palam fecisse per resurrectionem, & per spiritum sanctum.

Placet & haec sententia. Chirographum esse affixum cruci, id est, peccatum seu mortem esse crucifixam, seu abolitam, qua prosopopoeia videmus & alias Paulum usum esse, Roma. 7. De peccato damnavit peccatum. Item, Ero mors tua o mors. Et Eph. 2. Per crucem interficiens inimicitias nostras.

Videtur autem ideo Paulus his figuris tanquam pinxisse triumphum Christi, ut velut tabulam nobis proponeret, in quam semper intueretur conscientia pavida, ne fidem excuti nobis per desperationem sineremus. Magna vis est Satanae, qui corda nostra varie oppugnat, & cum ad alia peccata, tum ad desperationem impellere conatur. Sed haec pictura commonefaciat nos, ut sciamus nobis auxilium a Christo promitti, ut sciamus a Christo fractam esse satanae potentiam. Proinde non obiter haec pictura pretereunda est, sed ad eam resistere pii, imo semper in animo circumferre eam, semper intueri tota mente debent.

## 2:15
And having despoiled principalities and pow.

As to what he says that the principalities were despoiled, to the higher part of vivification it pertains, namely, that the faithful are governed by the Holy Spirit. The impious are in the kingdom of darkness and the power of the devil, who impels them to sin, as 1. John 3. He who commits sin is of the devil, because the devil sins from the beginning. On the contrary, the faithful are in the kingdom of Christ, where Christ through the Holy Spirit guards and defends them against the snares of the devil, so that they may not fall into sins, and so that they may not perish, as we pray, Thy kingdom come, and he added, that the power of darkness was transferred, or through ignominy displayed and led in triumph, he signifies that the Gospel has been preached in the whole world, as he says, The Holy Spirit will convict the world of judgment, because the prince of this world has already been judged.

Latin:

Expoliatosque principatus & pote.

Quod ait spoliatos esse principatus, ad alto ram partem vivificationis pertinet, scilicet, quod fideles gubernentur per spiritum sanctum. Impii sunt in regno tenebràrum & potestate diaboli, qui impellit eos ad peccandum, sicut. 1. Iohan. 3. Qui facit peccatum ex diabolo est, quia ab initio diabolus peccat. Contra, fideles sunt in regno Christi, ubi Christus per spiritum sanctum custodit & defendit eos adversus insidias diaboli, ne ruant in peccata, & ne pereant, sicut oramus, Adveniat regnum tuum, & adiecit, potestatem tenebrarum esse traductam, seu per contumeliam ostentatam & ductam in triumpho, significat Evangelium praedicatum esse in toto orbe terraram, sicut dicit, Spiritus sanctus arguet mundum de iudicio, quia princeps mundi iam iudicatus est.

## 2:16
EPILOGUE. Let no one therefore judge you in food or in drink &c.

Here he institutes an Epilogue, for because he taught before what Christian righteousness is, and compared it with carnal and civil righteousness, he adds an Epilogue concerning ceremonies and the Mosaic polity, and concerning human traditions, as if he were saying thus. Since Christian righteousness is mortification and vivification, therefore the observance of is not to be demanded. of the ceremonies of the Mosaic law, or of human traditions concerning clothing, the distinction of foods, and similar bodily things, just as Christ also says, The Kingdom of God does not come with observation. Above he had not yet openly said that the observation of the law or of traditions is not to be required; he had only taught this, what the difference is between that which pertains to Christian righteousness, and that which is collected by human powers from the law and human traditions. Now, however, he says openly that the observation of the law and traditions is not to be required, or that it is not sinned when traditions or the ceremonies of the law are not performed. For thus the word, let judge, is to be accepted, because to judge is to convict and bind consciences.

And this passage is most of all to be observed and held, so that you may know for certain that it is not a sin to neglect human traditions, but we shall speak about this matter later.

Latin:

EPILOGUS. Ne quis igitur vos iudicet in cibo aut potu &c.

Hic Epilogum instituit, quia enim ante, quid sit Christiana iusticia docuit, eamque contulit cum carnali & civili iusticia, adiicit Epilogum de ceremoniis & politia Mosaica, et de traditionibus humanis, quasi sic dicat. Cum Christiana iusticia sit mortificatio & vivificatio, ergo non est exigenda observatio ceremoniarum legis Moisaicae, aut traditionum humanarum de vestitu, discrimine ciborum & similibus corporalibus rebus, quemadmo dum & Christus dicit, Regnum Dei non venit cum observatione. Supra nondum aperte dixerat, non esse exigendam observationem legis aut traditionum, tantum hoc docuerat, quid intersit inter ad Christianam iusticiam, & eam quae collecta est humanis viribus ex lege & traditionibus humanis. Nunc autem aperte dicit, non esse exigendam observationem legis & traditionum, seu non peccari, quando non fiunt tradititiones seu legis caeremoniae. Sic enim accipiendum est verbum, iudicet, quia iudicare est conscientias arguere & obligare.

Estque hic locus maxime omnium observandus & tenendus, ut certo scias, non esse peccatum traditiones humanas negligere, sed hac de re postea dicemus.

## 2:17
Which are a shadow of things to come.

They explain the shadow τυπον, as if he were saying this, The law does not justify, therefore the ceremonies are not necessary, just as it is also handed down in Act. 15.. What, therefore, were the ceremonies?

τυπον of Christ, as the passover signifies the death of Christ. The ark signifies the church. The mercy seat signifies Christ, who was about to reconcile the Father to us. I do not disapprove of this exposition, but it does not sufficiently explain the nature of the law. Therefore, I accept this sentence of Paul in this way: The law is a shadow of things to come, that is, the whole law was given, not that it might justify, but that it might signify the justification which had been promised through Christ. And when the ceremonies were performed, they did not justify, but they admonished of the promised justification. Thus were David, Samuel, and the like justified. Not because they performed the ceremonies, but because when they performed the ceremonies, they believed that justification had been promised through Christ. Just as we can say concerning the Eucharist, the eating of the body itself does not justify, but it admonishes us of the remission of sins promised to those who believe. When, therefore, we believe, we are justified. Thus let us embrace the whole law here, because Christ is the end of the law. For the whole law was given for this reason, that God might signify that He would send Christ, through whom He would justify all.

Furthermore, individual ceremonies signify some part of history, but it is dangerous to seek allegories, and it does not profit the conscience. Let someone say What then was the need for so many laws and ceremonies? I answer. God chose a certain people, in whom to show Himself to the world, and to whom He might deliver His word, and where Christ might be born, and where testimonies concerning the born Christ might exist, just as Paul teaches Rom. 3. and 9.. Therefore God established a certain πολιτειαν and corporal kingdom until the coming of Christ, and He wished it to be able to be distinguished from other nations, therefore He assigned peculiar rites. Nor indeed can there be a polity without many laws. Moreover, God willed there to be two offices of the law.

The first, to restrain the carnal man, just as Paul says, The law is laid down for the unjust, the law is a schoolmaster. And so He wished the Israelites to be accustomed to and restrained by many laws and ceremonies, and He always wishes all men to be restrained by some laws. Therefore He also gave magistrates and laws to the nations.

The second office of the law is to strike terror, to condemn, and to humble, so that we may feel that we are sinners, and be compelled to seek grace and justification through Christ, just as Paul says, By the law is the knowledge of sin, Item He has concluded all under sin, not that He might destroy, but that He might have mercy on all. This It is the highest office of the law to be humbled through the law. At the same time, however, the Law admonishes that God does not condemn us for the purpose of destroying us, or that He might have mercy on us, and justify us through Christ. Moreover, there were many laws also for this reason, that He might condemn in many ways, and that sin might abound, as Paul says, and might kill us all the more through many laws. Thus Peter also says in the Acts, that neither we, nor our fathers were able to bear it. Yet the Law does not only condemn, but it also admonishes through so many ceremonies the promise concerning Christ.

Thus in general I accept that the law is a shadow of things to come, that is, that the whole law signifies the righteousness promised through Christ.

However, as often as any Law of Moses happens to be treated, it will be profitable to teach that God gave the law for these three causes: for restraining the flesh, and for terrifying or humbling. The third pertains to the just, that they may exercise obedience.

Latin:

Quae sunt umbra futurorum.

Umbram expnunt τυπον, quasi sic dicat, Lex non iustificat, ergo ceremoniae non sunt necessariae, sicut & Act. 15. traditur. Quid igitur fuerunt ceremoniae?

τυπον Christi, ut pascha significat mortem Christi. Arca significat ecclesiam. Popitiatorium Cristum, qui placaturus erat nobis patrem. Hanc expositionem non improbo, sed non satis explicat legis naturam. Propterea sic accipio hanc Pauli sententiam Lex est umbra futurorum, id est, Lex tota data est, non ut iustificet, sed ut significet iustificationem, quae per Christum promissa erat. Et cereremoniae cum fierent, non iustificabant, sed admonebant promissae iustificationis. Sic iustificabantur David, Samuel, & similes. Non quia ceremonias faciebant, sed quia cum facerent ceremonias, credebant promissam esse iustificationem per Christum. Sicut de Eucharistia nos dicere possumus, manducatio corporis ipsa non iustificat, sed admonet nos remissionis peccatorum promissae credentihus. Cum igitur credimus, iustificamur. Ita totam legem hic complectamur, quia finis legis Christus est. Tota enim Lex ideo data est, ut significaret Deus, se Christum missurum esse, per quem iustificaret omnes.

Caeterum singulae ceremoniae aliquam partem historiae significant, sed est periculosum allegorias quaerere, & conscientiis non prodest. Dicat aliquis Quid igitur opus fuit tam multis legibus & ceremoniis? Respondeo. Deus eligit aliquem certum populum, in quo se ostendere mundo, & cui verbum suum traderet, & ubi Christus nasceretur, & ubi extarent testimonia de nato Christo Sicut Paulus Rom. 3. & 9. docet. Ergo Deus quandam πολιτειαν & corporale regnum usque ad adventum Christi constituit, & ab aliis gentibus discerni posse voluit, ideo peculiares ritus tribuit. Neque sane potest esse politia sine multis legibus. Voluit autem Deus legis duo officia esse.

Primum, cohercere carnalem hominem, sicut Paulus dicit, Lex est iniustis posita, Lex est paedagogus. Itaque multis legibus & ceremoniis assuefieri & cohercere Israelitas voluit, & semper vult omnos homines coherceri aliquibus legibus. Ideo & gentibus dedit magistratus & leges.

Secundum officium legis est, terrorem incutere, condemnare, & humiliare, ut sentiamus nos esse peccatores, & cogamur quaerere gratiam & iustifacationem per Christum, sicut Paulus dicit, Per legem cognitio peccati, Item conclusit omnia sub peccato, non ut perdat, sed ut omnium misereatur. Hoc est summum legis officium, humiliari per legem. Simul autem Lex admonet, quod non ideo condemnet nos Deus ut perdat, seu ut misercatur nostri, & per Christum iustificet. Fuerunt autem & ob hanc causam multae leges, ut multipliciter condemnarem, & peccatum abundaret, ut ait Paulus, & magis nos occideret per multas leges. Sic ait et Petrus in Actis, quod neque nos, neque patres nostri portare potuimus. Neque tamen Lex damnat tantum, sed admonet tot ceremoniis etiam promissionem de Christo.

Sic in genere accipio legem esse umbram futurorum, id est, totam legem significare promissane per Christum iusticiam.

Quoties autem aliqua Moisi Lex inciderit tractanda, profuerit docare, quod Deus dederit legem propter has tres caussas, ad cohercendam carnem, & ad terrendum seu humiliandùm. Tertia ad iustos pertinet, ut exerceant obedientiam.

## 2:17
The Body of Christ.

That is, justification happens through Christ, in whom whoever has believed, he has already obtained justification, therefore he has no need of the ceremonies of the law, so that he might be justified through them, since not even the fathers were justified through the observation of the law. As the Apostles testify in the Acts. For those ceremonies were signs of the coming Christ, and of the righteousness promised through Christ.

What then, you will ask, was the need for the observation of the law? We spoke about this matter a little while ago, but it is not at all a burden to repeat it here more often, so that it may be more clearly understood what the righteousness is which the Gospel preaches. A great part of our Theologians have been in this error, that they dreamed there were two lawgivers sent by God, Moses, and Christ, who as πολιτειας established human laws, in which one might live civilly. These understood the righteousness of the time of the law to be the works commanded by Moses. In the time of the Gospel, the works commanded in the Gospel. Nothing is more pernicious than this error, for it is apparent that many heresies have been born from this source. What deceived Mahomet? Except that he judged that men are justified by civil righteousness, or by the works of the law.

Nor did he think that Christ was sent for any other reason than to bring forth laws of such a kind, which would [make] the Gentiles [equal] to the Jews to join them, which might establish similar πολιτειας everywhere among the nations. When that had not succeeded, he himself extracted civil precepts both from the Law and from the Gospel, and delivered them to the peoples then dissenting among themselves, teaching them that by the observation of such works they are justified before God. The same error, as it appears, deceived Julian the Apostate, and many others. Furthermore, the Gospel teaches a far different righteousness, nor did Christ come to bring some new law, so that he might establish similar πολίτιας everywhere. The cause of his coming was far different. For he came to preach the remission of sins, to make satisfaction for our sins, to bestow the Holy Spirit, and eternal life. Therefore, the Gospel teaches this righteousness: to believe that sins are freely forgiven us, and that we are received by God into grace, that we are reputed righteous before God, not because of our own dignity, but because of Christ. In that manner the fathers were justified from the beginning; Abel, not because he sacrificed, did he appease God, but because he believed that he was forgiven. For grace was promised in these words: This Seed shall crush your head Genesis 3.

David was justified before God, not because he did the works of the law, but because he believed that he, on account of the promise Christ is received into grace, as he himself confesses. Blessed is the man to whom the Lord has not imputed sin, for as Paul argues, if justification is the non-imputation of sin, therefore it is not any work of ours. But that faith in David was producing obedience so that for the sake of the will of God he might perform the works of the law and use them as exercises of his faith. For there must be some exercises of faith in this life. Therefore the Law was not given because it would justify, but because God wished to separate that people by a certain πολιτεια from the nations, so that there might be some certain people in whom the promises would be revealed, in whom Christ would be born, who would testify concerning Christ. Consequently, their rites did not justify the Jews any more than Saxon πολιτειαι justify us. But those rites, besides the fact that they distinguished that people from the nations, were also figures and sacraments of the promised Christ. Thus also in this place the opinion of Paul is to be accepted, that the law was a shadow of things to come, but the body, that is, justification and sanctification itself, is attained through faith in Christ.

Thus far it has been said that we are not pronounced righteous by the Mosaic ceremonies and political rites. Now someone might ask about the third part of the law. Whether on account of moral works, men are pronounced just. In this question, this must first be established: concerning the impious who are not reborn in Christ, that their nature is vicious, and they do not provide perfect obedience to the law of God, even if men by the powers of nature can perform civil works in some way. Wherefore it can easily be understood that the impious and those not reborn in Christ are not just before God on account of moral works.

Next, concerning those who are reborn, this must be known: that the faith by which we receive the remission of sins ought to rely only on the mercy promised on account of Christ. For faith could not exist if a condition were to be added to this effect, that Christ is profitable to us only when we have satisfied the law. This condition would overturn faith. Nor would consciences have any certain consolation in seeking the remission of sins. For not even then do we provide perfect obedience to the law. And the remnants of sin, vicious affections, always cling to us. However, faith ought to be accompanied by the justice of a good conscience or of good works. But because not even the reborn as yet satisfy the law perfectly, therefore faith must always be held, that the person is reconciled on account of Christ. And yet that must necessarily accompany faith. you owe the righteousness of a good conscience, or of moral works. And that this newness pleases God, even if it is imperfect, because the person is in Christ. This is a great and necessary consolation for the pious. Thus, its own honor is attributed to Christ, because we obtain the remission of sins and the reconciliation of the person not on account of our dignity, but on account of him. And faith or trust in Christ can be retained. And it is discerned how good works are both demanded and please God, namely not because we satisfy the law, but because we have been previously reconciled. The righteousness of a good conscience is necessary, as I have said. But faith or trust in Christ must be placed far above this righteousness of ours. And this trust does not lean upon our dignity, but looks upon Christ and establishes that we please the Father on account of Christ. This trust must be raised in the conscience during temptations; by this trust the terrors of sin and death must be overcome.

The Psalm, De profundis, teaches this clearly. In which the prophet confesses that he has sins, and yet he trusts that he pleases God through mercy. If you, O Lord, should mark iniquities, O Lord, who shall stand? Because there is propitiation with you, I trust in you, etc.

From these it appears that the Origenist and scholastic imaginations are to be cast away, which feign that men are righteous on account of their own dignity, or the fulfillment of the law. And they teach nothing concerning that faith which establishes that we are righteous on account of mercy; nay, they abolish faith, when they feign that faith is profitable only if we have satisfied the law. Likewise, they feign that faith signifies only the knowledge of history and of moral precepts. It is certain, therefore, that men do not obtain the remission of sins through ceremonies, nor on account of the dignity of moral works. But here it is asked, why therefore is it permitted to omit ceremonies, while it is not permitted to omit moral duties. To these I answer, because justification brings a new and eternal life, which is obedience toward God. Moral duties, however, are a certain perpetual and interior obedience. But ceremonies were external marks of a certain time. But concerning this matter I shall speak again below.

Let no one deceive you, feigning humility, etc.

Above, the Epilogue was begun, in which he establishes that the observation of the ceremonies of the law, and of human traditions, ought not to be exacted. Here he repeats He amplifies the opinion of the epilogue, and from the circumstances, for he commands us to beware lest we be deceived by these arts, which Hypocrites use to deceive the unskilled, just as Christ also says, that false prophets are covered in sheep's clothing, by that commendation, to insinuate themselves into the minds of the people. They deceive, however, by badly twisted scriptures, and to an impious cause they add strength from their own customs; they simulate remarkable probity, and especially humility, by their clothing, gestures, and entire action; they walk about dressed in rags, girded with ropes, they do not wear shoes, except wooden ones; to these things they live celibate, they boast of visions. These things are most effective for moving and winning over the minds of the common people.

But that we may here discern the simulation of humility from true humility, we shall define what it is to think humbly of oneself. Furthermore, no shorter or more absolute definition can be conceived than this τα+ πεινοφροσυνην to be truly the fear of God toward God. For those truly terrified by the judgment of God cast away all confidence in their own powers, their own wisdom, their own righteousness. David, when rebuked by the Prophet Nathan, perceived his sin, he did not trust that he would appease the wrath of God by his own righteousness, when he saw himself being cast out from the kingdom, he acknowledged that this was done by divine counsel, therefore not he trusted in his own strength or wisdom, that he could retain the kingdom. And this humility toward God produces patience toward men. τα πεινόφροσυμην. For those who know their own uncleanness and infirmity, these do not despise others; they know their own gifts are in the power of God, which, if God does not inspire, were going to be unhappy. Whatever they do, they undertake not confused by their own strength, but by the help of God; they know God can bestow greater gifts upon others. They know God can effect greater and more useful things through those who have less strength. They know that by one Jonathan a great army was put to flight. But below I shall define more prolixly both pride and humility, for we use this name meanwhile in the manner of Scripture, for the Greek word ταπεινοφροσυνη Compare, however, to this description, such as it is, the Hypocrites; these, if they have any gifts, swell up wonderfully. First against God, they so trust in their own wisdom that they do not prefer the word of God to their own counsels; nay, they make themselves arbiters and judges of the word of God. They admit the judgment of no other; they think they alone are wise; they think all other men are insane. Now if they have any good works, here they plainly make themselves gods; they so trust in their own justice that they determine they alone are regarded by God, and that because of their sacrifices great cities and great kingdoms are preserved, They proclaim that they alone support the sky on their shoulders, which would otherwise fall. For we have seen painted panels in which certain monks were propping up a falling church.

And when they have put on the persuasion of wisdom and justice, it is a wonder how they look down from on high upon the rest of the little men, they arrogate to themselves the authority to judge concerning all things, they wish their own censure and their own judgment to be held by all as oracles. If anyone dissents even slightly, here first he reveals himself as having poorly concealed pride, here they begin to fight for their reputation, here they show that they are truly ravenous wolves, for the bitterness of hatred is extreme with which they pursue those by whom they judge themselves to be harmed. And they indulge this monstrous hatred wonderfully, they malignantly interpret whatever those men have said or done, nothing can be written, said, or done so circumspectly that they do not know how to slander it, from every side they most insidiously hunt for what can be criticized in appearance. If they discover any error, they exaggerate it so, even if it is the slightest, that no crime seems ever to have been read or heard of that is more atrocious. They even despise, when the cause seems insufficiently firm, certain lies, so that their speech may become more probable. And since Paul has most gravely commanded, So that our ἐπεικεια may be known to all, they not only apply no ἐπεικειαν, in judging, but even the utmost injustice. They often invent crimes from themselves, so that by right or by wrong they may disfigure good men. In the words or deeds of their adversaries, they conceal whatever good is in them, they only extract the worse things, they amplify these with wonderful artifice, so that by the mention of them they may completely overwhelm the good. On the other hand, they interpret their own words and deeds most conveniently, here they use a certain new candor, here they require equity from others. To these masters, if anyone wishes to learn Sycophancy, he will be able to betake himself.

And with these calumnies they often arm cruelty against the innocent. Such is the cruelty of this Pharisaical hatred. Nor is there need to seek examples from ancient Histories from afar. For this age of ours can supply a great abundance of examples, for I do not think that any men have ever been in greater hatred than are certain good men at this time, who teach rightly, and they are not in hatred to any one kind of men, but are most hated by certain Hypocrites, who suffer with difficulty that their doctrine be reproved, and who fear lest they lose the praise of wisdom.
