In these Hypocrites, since there is an incredible pride, yet they wonderfully know how to cover the arrogance of the mind with σεμνοπρωσωπειν, and a singular appearance of humility. Formerly, monks commonly used to make a pretense by the kind of clothing and gait; now another artifice, much more cunning, has been devised for feigning humility, and what kind of thing that is, I do not wish to describe here.

I only wish to warn readers of this, that they should think that what Paul said about the simulation of humility also pertains to the simulation of gravity and sadness, which is itself joined with a singular persuasion of wisdom, and not rarely are the unskilled deceived by famous men on account of the appearance of gravity. There is, however, in this kind a singular stubbornness, for the most part τίμωνι-ουσι,, and they think nothing is right except what pleases them, and they do not at all endure those who dissent in a humane way, but they hate them bitterly and persecute them.

Latin:

Corpus autem Christi.

Id est, iustificatio contingit per Christum, in quem quisquis crediderit, is iam consecutus est iustificationem, ergo non habet opus caeremoniis legis, ut per eas iustificetur, cum ne quidem patres per observationem legis instificati sint. Sicut in Actis testantur Apostoli. Nam illae ceremoniae fuerunt signa venturi Christi, & promissae iusticiae per Christum.

Quid igitur, inquies, opus erat observatione legis? Hac de re paulo ante diximus, sed nihil velut saepius hic repetere, ut clarius intelligatur quid sit iusticia, quam Evangelium praedicat. Magna pars Theologorum nostrorum, in hoc errore fuerunt, ut somniarint duos esse legum latores a Deo missos, Moysen, et Christum qui tanquam πολιτειας humanas constituerent, in quibus civiliter viveretur, Hi iusticiam esse intellexerunt legis tempore, opera a Moyse praecepta. Evangelii tempore, opera praecepta in Evangelio. Nihil hoc errore perniciosus est, nam adparet ex hoc fonte multas haereses natas esse. Quid fefellit Mahometum? nisi quod iudicavit iustificari homines civili iusticia, seu operibus legis.

Nec arbitratus est Christum ob aliud missum esse, nisi ut leges ferret eiusmodi, quae Gentes Iudeis adiungeret, quae similes πολιτειας ubique gentium constituerent, Id cum non successisset, ipse ex cerpsit & ex lege & ex Evangelio civilia praecepta, eaque tradidit dissidentibus tunc inter se populis, docens eos talium operum observatione coram Deo iustificari. Fefellit, ut adparet, error idem Iulianum Apostatam, & alios multos. Porro longe aliam iusticiam docet Evangelium, Nec venit Christus, ut novam aliquem legem ferret, ut ubique similes πολίτιας constitueret. Longe alia caussa adventus eius fuit. Venit enim ut praedicaret remissionem peccatorum, ut pro peccatis nostris satisfaceret, ut donaret spiritum sanctum, ac vitam aeternam. Hanc itaque iusticiam docet Evangelium, credere, quod gratis condonetur nobis peccata, & quod simus recepti a Deo in gratiam, quod coram Deo iusti reputemur, non propter nostram dignitatem, sed propter Christum. Ad eum modum ab initio patres iustificati sunt, Abel, non quia sacrificavit, placavit Deo, sed quia credidit sibi ignosci, Erat enim promissa gratia in his verbis. Hoc Semen conteret caput tuum Genesis. 3.

David iustificatus est coram Deo, non quiae legis opera fecit, sed quia credidit se propter promissum Christum in gratiam recipi, sicut ipse fatetur. Beatus cui non imputavit Dominus peccatum, Nam ut Paulus argumentatur, si iustificatio est non imputari peccatum, ergo non est aliquod nostrum opus. At fides illa in Davide, obedientiam pariebat ut propter voluntatem Dei praestaret opera legis et uteretur eis, velut exercitiis fidei suae. Nam oportet esse aliqua fidei exercitia in hac vita. Non itaque Lex tradita est, quia iustificaret, sed quia Deus voluit illum populum certa πολιτεια separare ea gentibus, ut esset aliquis certus populus, in quo promissiones revelarentur, in quo Christus nasceretur. qui testificaretur de Christo. Proinde non magis iustificabant Iudeos ritus sui, quam nos Saxonicae πολιτειαι iustificant. Sed illi ritus, praeter quam quod discernebant eum populum a gentibus, etiam erant figurae, & Sacramenta promissi Christi. Sic & hoc loco Pauli sententia accipienda est, legem fuisse umbram futurorum, corpus autem, id est, ipsam iustificationem & sanctificationem contingere per fidem in Christum.

Hactenus dictum est, ceremoniis & politicis ritibus Mosaicis non pronunciari nos iustos. Nunc quaerat aliquis de tertia legis parte. An propter moralia pronuncientur homines iusti. In hac quaestione primum est hoc constituendum, de impiis qui non sunt renati in Christo, naturam esse viciosam, nec praestare perfectam obediam legi Dei, etiam si homines naturae viribus civilia opera utcumque facere possunt. Quare facile intelligi potest, impios ac non renatos in Christo non esse iustos coram Deo propter moralia.

Deinde de his qui renascuntur hoc sciendum est, quod fides qua accipimus remissionem peccatorum, niti debeat tantum misericordia promissa propter Christum. Non enim posset existere fides, Si esset addenda conditio in hanc sententiam, ita demum prodesse Christum, cum nos legi satisfecerimus. Haec conditio everteret fidem. Nec certam ullam consolationem haberent conscientiae in quaerenda remissione peccatorum. Nam ne tum quidem praestamus legi perfectam obedientiam. Et semper herent in nobis reliquiae peccati, affectus viciosi. Debet autem fidem comitari iusticia bonae conscientiae seu bonorum operum. Sed quia ne quidem renaeti adhuc perfecte legi satis facimus, ideo semper tenenda est fides, quod persona propter Christum sit reconciliata. Et tamen quod necessario fidem comitari debeas iusticia bonae conscientiae, seu moralium operum. quodque hec novitas placeat Deo, etsi sit imper fecta, quia persona sit in Christo. Haec est magna & necessaria piis consolatio. Ita tribuitur Christo suus honos, quod non propter nostram dignitatem, sed propter ipsum consequamur remissionem peccatorum, & reconciliationem personae. Et fides seu fiducia Christi retineri potest. Et cernitur quomodo bona opera & flagitentur & Deo placeant, videlicet non quia legi satisfaciamus, sed quia prius reconciliati sumus. Necessaria est, ut dixi, iusticia bonae conscientiae. Sed fides seu fiducia in Christum longe collocanda est supra hanc iusticiam nostram. Et haec fiducia non nititur nostra dignitate, sed intuetur Christum & statuit nos propter Christum placere patri. Haec fiducia in tentationibus crigenda est conscientia, Hac fiducia vincendi sunt terrores peccati ac mortis.

Hoc docet perspicue Psalmus, De profundis. In quo confitetur propheta se habere peccata, Et tamen confidit se Deo placere per misericordiam. Si iniquitates observaveris domine, domine quis sustinebit. Quia apud te propiciatio est, confido inte &c.

Ex his apparet Origenicas & scholasticas imaginationes abiiciendas esse, quae fingunt homines iustos esse propter propriam dignitatem, seu impletionem legis. Et de illa fide, quae statuit nos propter misericordiam iustos esse nihil docent, imo abolent fidem, cum fingunt, ita demum prodesse fidem si legi satisfecerimus. Item fingunt fidem significare tantum noticiam historiae ac praeceptorum moralium. Constat igitur neque ceremoniis, neque propter moralium operum dignitatem consequi homines remissionem peccatorum. Sed hit quaeritur, quare igitur licet omitti ceremonias, dum non liceat omittimoralia. Ad haec respondeo, quia iustificatio affert novam & aeteram vitam, que est obedientia erga Deum. Moralia autem sunt quaedam perpetua & interior obedientia. At ceremoniae fuerunt externae notae certi temporis. Sed hac de re infra rursus dicam.

Ne quis vos decipiat símulans humi-lítatem &c.

Supra inchoatus est Epilogus, in quo constituit, non debere exigi observationem caeremoniarum legis, & traditionum humanarum. Hic repetit Epilogi sententiam, & ex circumstantiis amplifi cat, iubet enim cavere ne decipiamur his artibus, quibus utuntur Hypocritae ad fallendos imperitos, sicut & Christus ait, Pseudoprophetas vestimentis ovium tectos, per eam commendationem, in sinuare se in animos populi. Fallunt autem male detortis scripturis, & impiae caussae addunt ex suis moribus vires, simulant insignem probitatem, ac maxime humilitatem, vestitu, gestibus, & tota actionem, incedunt sordide vestiti, cincti funibus, non induunt calceos, nisi ligneos, ad haec vivunt caelibes, iactant visiones. Haec ad per movendos, & concilian dos vulgigi animos efficacissima sunt.

Sed ut hic discernamus simulationem humilitatis a vera humilitate, definiemus quid sit humiliter de se sentire. Porro nulla brevior aut absolutior definitio concipi potest, quam haec τα+ πεινοφροσυνην erga Deum vere esse timorem Dei. Nam vere territi iudicio Dei, abiiciunt omnem fiduciam suarum virium, suae sapientiae, suae iusticiae. David cum obiurgatus a Propheta Nathan, cerneret suum peccatum, non confidebat se sua iusticiae placaturum esse iram Dei, cum videret se e regno eiici, agnoscebat id fieri divino consilio, non igitur confidebat suis viribus aut sapientia, regnum se retinere posse. Atque haec humilitas erga Deum paritctiam erga homines. τα πεινόφροσυμην. Qui enim norunt suam immundiciem, & infirmitatem, hi non contemnunt alios, sciunt sua dona, in potestate Dei esse, quae si non adspiret Deus, fuit futura infelicia. Quidquid agunt, suscipiunt non confusi suis viribus, sed auxilio Dei, sciunt Deum maiora donae aliis largiri posse, Sciunt Deum posse maiores res atque utiliores efficere per eos, qui minus habent virium. Sciunt ab uno Ionatha maximum exercitum esse fugatum. Sed infra prolixius definiam & superbiam, & humilitatem, nam hoc nomine interim utimur more scripturae, pro graeca voce ταπεινοφροσυνη Confer autem ad hanc, qualemcunque de scriptionem Hypocritas, hi, si qua dona habent, mirifice intumescunt. Primum adversus Deum, sapientia sua ita confidunt, ut verbum Dei non praefernat suis consiliis, imo arbitros se aciudices verbi Dei faciant. Nullius alterius iudicium admittunt, se solos sapere existimant, reliquos homines insanire omnes putant. Iam si qua bona opera habent, hic se Deos plane faciunt, ita confidunt sua iusticia, ut se solos respici a Deo statuant, propter sua sacrificiae conservari magnas civitatse, magna regna, sentiant, se solos sustinere humeris caelum, alioqui ruiturum praedicant. Vidimus enim pictas tabulas, in quibus ruentem ecclesiam fulciebant Monachi quidam.

Et cum sapientiae, ac iusticiae persuasionem in duerunt, mirum est, ut ex alto reliquos homunculos despiciant, sibi arrogant autoritatem de omnibus iudicandi, suam censuram, suum iudicium pro oraculis haberi ab omnibus volunt, Si quis leviter dissentiat, hic primum prodit se male dissimulata superbia, hic depugnare pro existimatione incipiunt, hic ostendunt se vere esse Lupos rapaces, summa enim acerbitas odii est, quo prosequuntur eos a quibus se ledi iudicant. Et huic immani odio mirabiliter indulgent, maligne interpretantur, quidquid illi dixerint aut fecerint, nihil tam circumspecte scribi dici, fieri potest, quod non norint calumniari, undique insidiosissime aucupantur, quod in speciem reprehendi possit. Si quod erratum deprehenderint, sic exaggerant, etiam si sit levissimum, ut nullum scelus atrocius aut lectum unquam, aut auditum videatur, Adspernunt etiam, cum parum firma caussa videtur, mendacia quaedam, ut probabilior oratio fiat. Et cum Paulus gravissime praeceperit, ut ἐπεικεια nostra nota sit omnibus, non solum nullam adhibent isti in iudicando ἐπεικειαν, sed etiam sumam iniquitatem. Saepe ex se fingunt crimina, ut quo iure, qua iniuria bonos viros deforment. In adversariorum dictis aut factis, quidquid inest boni dissimulant, tantum excerpunt deteriora, haec miro artificio amplificant, ut eorum commemoratione bona penitus obruant. Econtra sua dicta ac facta commodissime interpretantur, hic novo quodam candore utuntur, hic aequitatem ab aliis requirunt. Ad hos magistros, si quis volet Sycophanticam discere, conferre se poterit.

Atque his calumniis principium seviciam saepe adversus innocentes armant. Tanta est huius Pharisaici odii crudelitas. Nec opus est procule veteribus Historiis exempla quaerere. Nam haec nostra aetas magnam copiam exemplorum suppeditare potest, nunquam enim maiore in odio fuisse ullos homines existimo, quam sunt hoc tempore boni quidam viri, qui recte docent, nec in odio sunt uni alicui generi hominum, verum maxime invisi sunt Hypocritis quibusdam, qui suam doctrinam reprehendi aegre patiuntur, quique metuunt, ne sapientiae laudem amittant. In his Hypocritis, cum sit incredibilis superbia, tamen mire norunt σεμνοπρωσωπειν, & singulari specie humilitatis fastum animi tegere Olim Monachi vulgo faciebant fucum genere vestitus ac incessus, Nunc aliud artificium multo callidius excogitatum est simulandae humilitatis, quod quale sit, non libet mihi hic describere.

Tantum hoc monere lectores volo, ut quae de simulatione humilitatis Paulus dixit, putent etiam ad gravitatis & tristiciae simulationem pertinere, quae et ipsa cum singulari sapientiae persuasione coniuncta est, nec raro decipiuntur imperiti a claris hominibus propter gravitatis speciem. Est autem in hoc genere singularis pertinacia, plerunque τίμωνι-ουσι, neque quidquam rectum putant, nisi quod ipsis placet & dissentientes neutiquam humanitus patiuntur, sed à cerbe oderunt atque persequuntur.

## 2:18
And the religion of angels.

I judge that celibacy is especially signified, which has so deceived men that some have castrated themselves with iron, so that they might merit righteousness. The hermit life has also deceived some, so that they might abandon their marriages, and there exist today constitutions, that it may be permitted for a deserted spouse, to to pass over to monasticism against a given faith. There were once heretics καθαροι who seemed to profess a religion as if of angels, because they denied that those who had fallen after baptism could obtain forgiveness of sin. This error arose mostly from the fact that they did not understand what sin was. There are sins in the saints, namely weakness of faith, concupiscence, which, since they obtain forgiveness, why should we doubt that even crimes obtain forgiveness. He sees, therefore, that those heretics were deceived by a reason which judges only crimes that are seen outwardly to be sins, and does not discern the uncleanness of the heart. And it seems to me that those καθαροι have been reborn in the Anabaptists at this time. For they taught the same errors καθαροι that the Anabaptists now scatter in Germany.

Latin:

Et religione Angelorum.

Caelibatum maxime significari arbitror, qui adeo decepit homines, ut quidam castraverint se ipsi ferro, ut iusticiam mererentur, Nonnullos & Eremitica vita fefellit, ut coniugia desererent, & extant hodie constitutiones, ut liceat deserta sponsa, ad monasticen transire contra datam fidem. Fuerunt olim Haeretici καθαροι qui tamquam angelorum religionem professi videntur, quia negabant eos, qui post baptismum lapsiessent, veniam peccatiposse consequi. Qui error maxime ex eo ortus est, quod quid esset peccatum, non intellexerunt. Sunt in sanctis peccata, scilicet imbecillitas fidei, concupiscentia, quae cum veniam consequantur, quid dubitemus etiam crimina veniam consequi. Videt igitur Haereticos illos ratione deceptos esse, quae tantum crimina quae foris conspiciuntur, iudicat esse peccata, immundiciem cordis non cernit. Ac videntur mihi καθαροι illi in Anabaptistis hoc tempore renati esse. Nam cosdem errores, quos nunc Anabaptistae spargunt in germania, olim καθαροι docuerunt.

## 2:18
Walking in those things which he has not seen, puffed up by the mind of his flesh, etc.

Some also deceive under this pretext, when they boast of singular revelations. Many of this kind are recounted in histories. But we saw Thomas Müntzer, who affirmed that he had received a command by a divine voice to wage war against the princes, this glory he claimed that he was speaking with God in a clear voice. But he boasted of these things falsely, and he was inventing these lies so that he might join the multitude to himself, with whose help he might be able to stir up a tumult in Germany, and seize tyranny. He was accustomed to observe dreams diligently, even though, divining from these, he was never not deceived. To such an extent did that which Paul says agree with this man, that false apostles walk in those things which they have not seen; he boasted of revelations, when he had none, he divined from dreams, when he could affirm nothing certain. That was clearly to walk in those things which he did not see, partly to preach false things about himself, partly to affirm things undiscovered and uncertain. Moreover, he erred in many places of Christian doctrine. He denied that men are justified by faith, he affirmed that men are just on account of the dignity of works. He clearly despised the Word of God and the Sacraments, because he taught men to perceive God by reason, and to understand honorable things, and that the Holy Spirit is obtained if reason had exercised itself for some time in good works. Thus he was hallucinating about justification, and likewise about the spiritual kingdom. He erred no less about the civil kingdom. He taught that the faculties of men ought to be common. He was waging war against magistrates, he was preaching that it would come to pass that all the impious would be destroyed before the last day, so that the pious might hold the kingdoms, and

living securely, using all things in common, as in the city of Plato, many heresies seem to have flowed together into this faction of the Anabaptists. For a good part of their dogmas agrees with the Enthusiasts. Thus were certain heretics once called, who, having despised the Word of God and the Sacraments, said that they obtained the Holy Spirit through certain prayers, and held their own dreams for the inspiration of the Holy Spirit.

Certain dogmas were taken from the Chiliasts, who interpret the promises of the Prophets concerning the kingdom of Christ corporally, and they thought that Christ would reign for a thousand years in this life with the pious, abounding in every kind of delight. Thomas had received these errors from Nicolaus Stork, whom I suspect to be still alive. They said that the kingdom of the whole world had been promised to him by God, so that he might remove all impious magistrates and purge the church. I have thought these things should be written down in this place, so that I might warn pious readers not to have faith in fanatic spirits who boast of revelations, especially those dissenting from Scripture. For the Prophet commands that we make Scripture the judge of all revelations, when he says, To the law, and to the testimony.

And Paul says, If an angel from heaven should preach to you a gospel other than that which we have preached, let him be anathema. Here therefore he says that those fanatical teachers walk in those things which they have not seen, that is, to affirm things undiscovered, and to follow uncertain revelations. For to walk signifies a certain pride such as is wont to be in heretics, sowing new dogmas. Moreover, he adds that they are puffed up by the mind of their flesh, so as to signify that they follow the judgment of reason, not of scripture or of the Holy Spirit, for, as we said above, heresies are born from probable reasons, which impious men embrace against scripture. For since the word of God hands down many things incomprehensible to reason, reason prefers to follow those things which it perceives and comprehends by itself. Thus it happens that it departs from scripture.

Paul adds that those fanatical spirits do not hold the head, Christ, who nourishes and governs the Church, that is, they do not hold the true knowledge of Christ. Monetarius was not teaching the righteousness of faith at all, he was only trifling about a certain simulation of sadness, by which they might obtain the Holy Spirit; he did not understand Christ's kingdom to be a spiritual kingdom, that is, the acknowledgment of sins, and the fear of God, and trust. toward God, and joy in God, and eternal life, chastity, and charity toward one's neighbor, but he judged the kingdom of Christ to be a just πολιτειαν in the world. These carnal opinions deceived the man, since he preferred to follow the judgment of reason rather than scripture. We have made mention of the Moneyer for this reason, that, since the remnants of his faction still breathe under the title of Anabaptists, pious readers might be warned to flee far from a faction professing so many impious dogmas. From the school of Storch also came forth Carlstadt, and since he disapproved of the baptism of children, since he had such an author, it deservedly ought to have been suspect to all of his doctrine.

Latin:

Incedens in his quae non vidit in-flatus mente carnis suae &c.

Fallunt etiam quidam hoc pretextu, cum iactant singulares revelationes. Huiusmodi multi in historiis recensentur. Sed nos vidimus Thomam Monetarium, qui adfirmabat se divina voce mandatum accepisse, ut bellum inferret principibus, Hic gloriae batur se cum Deo colloqui clara voce. Sed haec falso iactabat, & haec mendacia fingebat, ut multitudinem sibi adiungeret, cuius auxilio posset tumultum concitare in germania, & tyrannidem occupare. Somnia diligenter solebat observare, tametsi ex his divinans nunquam non falleretur. Adeo conveniebat in hunc id quod Paulus ait, incedere Pseudoapostolos in his quae non viderunt, iactabat revelationes, cum nullas haberet, divinabat ex somniis, cum nihil certi posset adfirmare. Id plane erat incedere in his quae non videbat, partim falsa de se praedicare, partim adfirmare incomperta, & incerta, Multis autem in locis Christianae doctrinae errabat. Negabat fide iustificari homines, adfirmabat homines iustos esse propter operum dignitatem Verbum Dei & Sacramenta plane contemnebat, quia docebat homines ratione Deum cernere, & intelligere honesta, impetrari spiritum sanctum, siratio se aliquandiu bonis operibus exercuisset. Sic hallucinabatur de iustificatione, adeoque de regno spirituali. De regno civili non minus errabat, Docebat facultates hominum debere communes esse. Magistratibus bellum inferebat, praedicabat futurum, ut omnes impii ante diem novissimum delerentur, ut pii regna tenerent, &

securi degerent, rebus omnibus, velut in Platonicae civitate, communiter utentes, Multae Haereses confluxisse in hanc factionem Monetarii videntur. Nam bona pars dogmatum convenit cum Enthusiastis. Sic olim adpellati sunt Haeretici quidam, qui contempto Verbo Dei, & Sacramentis, dicebant se spiritum sanctum quibusdam orationibus impetrare, & somnia sua, pro adflatu spiritus sancti habebant.

Quaedam dogmata sunt a Chiliastis sumpta, qui Prophetarum promissiones de regno Christi corporaliter interpreta ntur, & sed sentibant Christum mille annos regnaturum esse in hac vita cum piis, adfluentibus omni genere deliciarum. Acceperat hos errores Thomas a Nicolao Stork, quem adhuc superstitem esse suspicor. Huic aiebant divinitus promissum esse regnum totius orbis terrarum, ut tolleret omnes impios magistratus, &. repurgaret ecclesiam. Haec duxi hoc loco adscribenda esse, ut pios lectores monerem, ne fidem habeant fanaticis spiritibus, qui revelationes iactant, praesertim dissentientes a scriptura. Nam Propheta iubet, ut scripturam iudicem omnium revelationum faciamus, cum inquit, Ad legem, & ad testimonium.

Et paulus ait, Si angelus de caelo Evangelizaverit vobis aliud quam Evangelizaverimus, anathema sit. Hic igitur ait fanaticos illos doctores, incedere in his quae non viderunt, id est adfirmare incomperta, & incertas revelationes sequi. Nam incedere significat fastum quendam qualis esse solet in Haereticis, nova dogmata serentibus. Addit autem, inflatos esse mente carnis suae, ut significet eos sequi iudicium rationis non scripturae, aut spiritus sancti, nam, ut supra diximus, Haereses nascuntur ex probabilibus rationibus, quas contra seripturam impii homines amplectuntur. Nam cum verbum Dei pleraque tradat incomprehensibilia rationi, mavult ratio ea sequi, quae per se cernit & comprehendit. Ita fit, ut discedat a scriptura.

Paulus addit, fanaticos illos spiritus, non tenere caput Christum qui alit & gubernat Ecclesiam, hoc est, non tenent veram cognitionem Christi. Monetarius fidei iusticiam plane non docebat, tantum nugabatur de quadam simulatione tristiciae, qua impretarent spiritum sanctum, Christi regnum non intelligebat spirituale regnum esse, hoc est, agnitionem peccatorum, & timorem Dei, & fiduciam erga Deum, & gaudium in Deo, & vitam aethernam, castitatem, & caritatem erga proximum, Sed iudicabat regnum Christi esse iustam πολιτειαν in mundo. Hae carnales opiniones hominem deceperunt, cum rationis iudicium sequi mallet quam scripturam. Fecimus autem ideo Monetarii mentionem, ut, quoniam eius factionis reliquiae adhuc spirant titulo Anabaptistarum, admonerentur pii lectores, ut procul fugiant factionem tam multa impia dogmata profitentem. Ex Storkii ludo prodiit & Caro lostadius, cum puerorum baptismum improbabat, cum talem habuerit autorem, merito dibebat omnibus doctrinae eius esse suspecta.

## 2:20
Therefore if you are dead with Christ from the elements of the world, why as though living in the world, are you held by decrees.

To the epilogue the Apostle adds a reason. Christian justice requires the mortification of the flesh. Human traditions do not mortify, nor do they vivify, therefore they are not necessary for justification. Here the adversaries teach that traditions are established for this reason, so that the flesh may be mortified. For they think that these civil ordinances of fasts and other ceremonies are called the mortification of the flesh in Scripture, but they are deceived. For the Law of God has two offices. The first is to restrain, or to drive the unjust to a carnal righteousness by the fear of punishments, just as Paul I. Timoth. 1 teaches. The other office is to humble and to strike true terror, so that we may acknowledge that we have deserved eternal death, and may seek grace. Just as David was terrified when he was rebuked by the Prophet Nathan.

Human traditions, however, such as certain fasts, holidays, and clothing, have only this one office: that by a certain bodily discipline they may contain and rule the unjust, or the inexperienced, like children or the common people. Scripture does not call that mortification, because mortification is when God strikes true and vehement terror into consciences by the Word, and shows sin, and slays concupiscence, and as Paul says Gala. 5. The flesh lusts against the spirit, and the spirit against the flesh. And Roma. 8. If by the spirit you mortify the deeds of the flesh, you shall live. And that mortification or repentance does not require certain clothing, or certain days, but among the remaining fruits of the spirit and, it brings forth of its own accord chastity, sobriety, endurance of labors, and the like. Just as Paul also says. 1. Corinth. 9. I chastise my body, and bring it into subjection.

Therefore, the observance of human ordinances is not mortification, but rather a certain civil form of life, pertaining nothing to Christian righteousness, or not justifying. But they are institutions for ruling and accustoming the unskilled. Therefore, certain days have been prescribed, so that the common people may know at what time they ought to assemble . Therefore, certain songs have been instituted, so that there may not be confusion in the Church, just as Paul also 1. Corinth. [14]. says, Let all things be done decently and in order. But the observance of that order is a certain pedagogy, and civil discipline; it is not Christian righteousness, nor does it justify. Therefore, Paul concludes here, If you are dead with Christ, why are you held by traditions? as if he were saying. Traditions were made for those living carnally, so that they might be ruled by civil discipline. They were not made for justifying, but those who have the Holy Spirit have already ceased to live according to the flesh, and have no need of that bodily discipline. Truly, they use the ordinances of all places out of charity. To the Jews they become Jews, to the Greeks Greeks. It is an altogether spiritual life, and does not require a certain and his own childish discipline, but he uses the laws of all places, or serves, or is free, enters an inheritance or does not enter, eats meat or does not eat according to the laws and ordinances of the place.

Latin:

Itaque si mortui estis cum Christo ab elementis mundi, quid quasi viven-tes in mundo, decretis tenemini.

Epilogo addit Apostolus rationem. Christiana iusticia requirit mortificationem carnis. Humanae traditiones non mortificant, nec vivificant, ergo non sunt necessariae ad iustificationem. Hic docent adversarii. quod ideo condantur traditiones, ut caro mortificetur. Putant enim civiles istas ordinationes ieiuniorum, & aliarum caeremoniarum in scriptura vocari mortificationem carnis, sed falluntur. Nam Lex Dei duo habet officia. Primum, cohercere, seu ad iusticiam carnalem adigere metu poenarum iniustos sicut Paulus. I. Timoth. 1 docet. Alterum officium est humiliare, & verum terro em incutere, ut agnoscamus nos aeternam mortem meruisse, & quaer amus gratiam. Sicut Davidterrebatur, cum obiurgaretur a Propheta Nathan

Humanae vero traditiones, sicut certa ieiunia feriae, vestitus, unum hoc habent officium, ut quadam corporali disciplina contineant, & regant in iustos, seu imperitos, ut pueros, aut vulgus. Id non vocat scriptura mortificationem, quia mortificatio est cum verbo verum & vehementem terrorem Deus incutit conscientiis, & ostendit peccatum, & iugulat concupiscentiam, & sicut Paulus ait Gala. 5. Caro concupiscit adversus spiritum, & spiritus adversus carnem. Et Roma. 8. Si spiritu facta carnis mortificabitis, vivetis. Et illa mortificatio seu paenitentia non exigit certum vestitum, certos dies, sed inter reliquos fructus spiritus &, os ultro parit, castitatem, sobrietatem tolerantiam laborum, & similes. Sicut & Paulus ait. 1. Corinth. 9. Castigo crpus meum, & in servitutem redigo.

Observatio itaque humanarum ordinationum non est mortificatio, sed potius quaedam civilis forma vitae, nihil ad Christianam iusticiam pertinens, vel non iustificans. Sed instituta, ad regendos & adsuefaciendos imperitos. Ideo certi dies descripti sunt, ut vulgus sciat, quo tempore debeant conveni re, Ideo certae cantiones institutae sunt, ne sit in Ecclesia confusio, sicut & Paulus. 1. Corinth. [14]. ait, Omnia decore & ordine fiant. Sed eius ordimis observatio quaedam paedagogia est, & civilis disciplina, non est Christiana iusticia, nec iustificat. Ideo hic colligit Paulus, Si mortui estis cum Christo, cur traditionibus tenemini? quasi dicat. Traditiones factae sunt pro viventibus carnaliter, ut civili disciplina regantur. Non sunt factae ad iustificandum, sed qui habent spiritum sanctum, iam desierunt vivere secundum carnem, & illa corporali disciplina nihil opus habent. Verum utuntur ordinationibus omnium locorum per caritatem. Iudaeis fiunt Iudaei, Graecis graeci. Prorsus spiritualis quaedam vita est, & non requirit certam & propriam disciplinam puerilem, sed omnium locorum legibus utitur, aut servit, aut liber est, adit haereditatem aut non adit, edit carnes aut non edit secundum iura & ordinationes loci.

## 2:21
Do not touch, do not taste, nor handle, which all perish by the very use.

Under the person of the Pseudo-apostles are said μιμητῖκως, or as examples, so that he may show of what kind of laws or traditions he speaks, namely of ceremonies instituted, as they call it, for the worship of God, as are those. Do not touch meats on the sixth day, Let not priests take wives. Paul's epilogue is packed with arguments, and just as he makes mention of mortification above, so here he argues from vivification, as if he were saying this, Christian righteousness is eternal life just as Christ says Iohannis. 17. This is eternal life, that they may know you the only true God, and whom you sent Jesus Christ, Which if it is eternal life, therefore food and drink do not bring Christian righteousness, just as he says elsewhere, The Kingdom of God is not food and drink, and as Christ says, What enters into the mouth does not defile a man, much less does it justify a man.

But it is necessary that there be something eternal, namely the Word, by which we may be divinely regenerated and sanctified. Therefore Paul said: They are for corruption in the using or by using, that is, they perish, and do not bring eternal life.

The term ἀποχρησϊς is not to be taken as referring to evil use, lest anyone suspect that Paul wishes to say that foods do not justify only if we abuse them, but do justify if we use them usefully. But he means this: that foods do not justify when we use them usefully and rightly, and he calls ἀποχρησιν to be consumed by daily use. Moreover, what he says about foods ought to be referred to the whole bodily life and laws, to servitude and liberty, and the like.

Latin:

Ne tetigeris, ne gustaveris, neque con-trectaveris, quae omnia ipso pereunt usu.

Sub persona Pseudoapostolorum dicuntur μιμητῖκως, seu pro exemplis, ut ostendat de qualibus legibus seu traditionibus loquatur, videlicet de ceremoniis institutis, ut vocant ad cultum Dei, ut sunt illae. Ne attingite carnes die sexta, Ne ducant uxores presbyteri. Pauli Epilogus confertus est argumentis, & sicut supra mentionem facit mortificationis, ita hic argumentatur a vivificatione, quasi sic dicat, Christiana iusticia est vita aeterna sicut Christus ait Iohannis. 17. Haec est vita aetherna, ut cognoscant te solum Deum verum, & quem misisti Iesum Christum, Quod si est vita aeterna, ergo non adferunt Christianam iusticiam cibi & potus, sicut & alias ait, Regnum Dei non est esca & potus, & sicut Christus ait, Quod intrat in os non coinquinat hominem, multo minus iustificat hominem.

Sed necesse est, aeternum quidam esse, scilicet Verbum, quo regeneremur & sanctificemur divinitus. Ideo dixit Paulus. Sunt in corruptionem usu seu utendo, id est, pereunt, nec adferunt vitam aeternam.

Vocabulum ἀποχρησϊς non est accipiendum de malo usu, ne quis suspicetur Paulum velle dicere, tum demum cibos non iustificare si abutamur, iustificare vero, si utiliter utamur. Sed hoc vult, cibos non iustificare, cum utiliter et rite utimur, et vocat ἀποχρησιν consumi quotidiano usu. Quod autem de cibis dicit, ad totam vitam corporalem & leges, ad servitutem & libertatem et similia referri debet.

## 2:22
According to the commandments and doctrines of men.

Here he openly names human traditions, so that we may know that this epilogue destroys not only the law of Moses, but also human traditions, which might be established daily. Therefore, the proposition which he established above must always be held: Let no one judge you in food or drink, that is, the conscience is not to be obligated, or so bound to observe traditions human, so that it may feel itself to be justified by the observance of the law, and to sin if it does not observe it. For to judge is to condemn, or to approve the conscience.

Latin:

Iuxta mandata & doctrinas homi-num.

Hic aperte traditiones humanas nominat, ut sciamus Eptlogum hunc perimere non tantum ad legem Moysi, sed etiam ad humanas traditiones, quae quotidie conderentur. Est itaque semper tenenda propositio, quam supra instituit, Nemo vos iudicet in cibo, potu id est, conscientia non est obliganda, seu ita alliganda ad observandas traditiones humanas, ut sentiat se iustificari carum observatio ne, & peccare, si non observet. Nam iudicare est condemnare, aut adprobare conscientiam.

## 2:23
Which indeed have a show of wisdom in words, etc.

First, he attributes a show of wisdom to traditions, and it is to be understood in this way. Rites are sometimes ordained, not for justification, but for pedagogy, and bodily utility or peace, as when certain chants are prescribed, so that dissimilar chants may not excite a tumult in the Church. When certain holidays are established, in which there is leisure to hear sermons. Paul does not condemn ordinances of this kind; indeed, he himself also ordains that women should cover their heads, and that while one is speaking, others should listen.

Secondly, rites are ordained for the sake of justification, just as most fasts, likewise, to approach the Eucharist once at a certain time, have been ordained for the remission of sins, and the like. Paul condemns such ordinances, and says they have a show of wisdom. This show, however, is when bodily necessity demands certain ordinances, as I have said. From this the Pseudo-apostles gather that these things are necessary for justification, but it is not rightly gathered. For, as clothing is necessary for the body, yet it does not follow that it is necessary for justification. Thus, some ordinances of certain days or chants are necessary for the sake of peace. Yet they are not necessary for justification; thus reason is deceived when it makes a spiritual thing out of a bodily thing, but these two things deceive us.

Examples of others. For what does some married man see, that Saint Anthony lived remarkably in the desert, and being captivated by the example, he thinks the same kind of life must be followed by him, having deserted his wife, having deserted his children, as often happens. Furthermore, Saint Anthony did not live in the desert for that reason, that he might be justified through it, but for some other cause. Secondly. Reason naturally seeks justification from its own works, therefore so many diverse forms of worship have been instituted. Because one person wished to satisfy for his sins by one work, another by another, as it is in Isaiah 58. Wherefore have we fasted, and you have not looked; we have humbled our souls, and you have not known. These are the causes why we are deceived and think traditions to be necessary for justification.

Latin:

Quae verbotenus quidem habent speciem sapientiae &c.

Primum tribuit specicm sapientiae, traditionibus, id sic accipien dum est. Ritus aliquando ordinantur, non ad iustisicationem, sed ad paedagogiam, & utilitatem corporalem seu pacem, ut cum certae cantiones praescribuntur, ne dissimiles cantiones in Ecclesia tumultum excitent. Cum certae feriae coustituuntur, in quibus vacet audire contiones. Huiusmodi ordinationes non damnat Paulus, imo ipse etiam ordinat, ut mulieres tegant capita, ut loquente uno audiant alii.

Secundo ordinantur ritus propter iustificationem sicut pleraque ieiunia, item, accedere semel certo tempore ad Eucharistiam, ordinata sunt ad remissionem peccatorum, & similia. Tales ordinationes damnat Paulus, & dicit eas habere speciem sapientiae. Species autem haec est, cum corporalis necessitas exigat aliquas certas ordinationes, ut dixi sattim colligunt inde Pseudoapostoli necessarias esse ad iustification em, sed non recte colligiturNam, ut vestire est necessarium corpori, neque tamen sequitur, necessarium esse ad iustificationem. Ita ordinationes aliquae certorum dierum aut cantionum necessariae sunt pacis caussa Neque tamen sunt necessariae ad iustificationem, ita decipitur ratio cum ex corporali re spiritualem efficit, sed decipiunt nos haec duo.

Exempla aliorum. Quid enim videt aliquis maritus sanctum Antonium in eremo praeclare vix isse, captus exemplo, idem vitae genus sibi sequendum putat, deserta coniuge, desertis liberis, sicut saepe accidit. Porro sanctus Antonius non ideo degit in cremo, ut per id iustificaretur, sed propter aliquam aliam caussam. Secundo. Ratio naturaliter quaerit iustificationem ex suis operibus, ideo sunt tam diversi cultus instituti. Quia alius alio opere voluit satisfacere pro suis peccatis, sicut est apud Esaia. 58. Quare ieiunavimus & non aspexisti, humiliavimus animas nostras, & nesciisti. Istae caussae sunt, cur decipiamur & putemus traditiones necessaris esse ad iustificationem.

## 2:23
Through superstition and humility of mind, etc.

Ἐθελοδρηγκειαν calls a work without divine command, chosen or instituted by our own will. Concerning works of this kind, Christ also says elsewhere, In vain do they worship me with the commandments of men. And ταπεινοφροσυνη in this place must be understood as the simulation of humility. For true humility is partly true mortification, or the fear of God, and partly true charity. But the Holy Spirit effects these things, as was said above. We have also said above that the pseudo-apostles err when they teach that the flesh is mortified by their own ordinances. For human ordinances are bodily exercises. Mortification, however, is a work of the Holy Spirit.

Latin:

Per superstitionem ac humilitatem animi &c

Ἐθελοδρηγκειαν vocat opus sine mandato divino, nostra sponte electum, seu institutum. De eiusmodi operibus & alias dicit Christus, Frustra colunt me mandatis hominum. Est & ταπεινοφροσυνη hoc loco accipienda de simulatione humilitatis. Nam vera humilitas partim est vera mortificatio, seu timor Dei, partim vera caritas. Haec autem Spiritus sanctus effecit ut supra dictum est. Diximus & supra, errare pseudoapostolos, cum docent suis ordinationibus mortificari carnem. Nam ordinationes humanae sunt exercitia corporalia. Mortificatio autem est opus spiritus sancti.

## 2:23
Through the harming of the body, not through any honor, for the satisfying of the flesh.

It must be diligently observed in this place that Paul commands to spare the body, and to have honor for it, and to grant as much as is sufficient, just as it is also commanded in Proverbs Cap. 1. The righteous man acknowledges the souls of his beasts, but the heart of the wicked is cruel. is. For just as we ought to use other gifts of God reverently with thanksgiving, so also life and good health must be used, so that we may acknowledge them to be gifts of God, and strive to preserve them for as long as it seems good to God. Therefore Paul also says. 1. Timoth. 4. Foods which God created, to be received with thanksgiving. But what else is it to receive, than to preserve the strength of the body. But as abstinence is forbidden when it harms the body, so it is commanded for the restraining of the body, as Christ says, Take heed, lest your bodies be weighed down with surfeiting. And Paul, Drunkards shall not enter the kingdom of God. Therefore Paul added here, that one must be sparing to the body, or grant to it, only as much as is sufficient. And Ecclesiastici. 33. a measure is handed down, Fodder and a rod and a burden for an ass, bread and discipline and work for a servant. Thus the body ought to be ruled, so that necessary sustenance is also granted to it. Then, that it may also be restrained and chastised, lest it grow wanton, and indeed chastisement is not useful without labor and just works, therefore just works are also imposed, through which occasion they may be bound to doing what is right. I know that others have variously expounded the last clause, for the fulfilling of the flesh. But the opinion of Jerome pleases me, who interprets it thus, as much as is sufficient, and that Paul is commanding that as much as is sufficient be granted to the body.

And because many things have been said in this place concerning human traditions, we also wish to examine that topic more copiously. Whether it is a sin to violate human traditions. However, it must be established at the beginning that we are speaking of Ecclesiastical traditions, instituted for the sake of justification, or for obtaining the remission of sins, or in general for the worship of God, as they call it. But before I speak of those Ecclesiastical traditions, certain things must first be handed down concerning civil Magistrates, and civil laws, which ordain matters concerning bodily servitude, the contributing of tributes, the possession of goods, and similar business.

For I know there are many who think otherwise about civil magistrates than the Christian scriptures teach. And I myself have had a most bitter contention with many who seemed to themselves to be very learned, who denied that it is lawful for a Christian to hold the office of magistrate. And as far as I see, Christian doctrine offends most of the ancients on this account, that since it prohibits vengeance, it seems to disturb and dissolve the civil state, to take magistrates and judgments away from public affairs, to prohibit the punishments of the guilty, to promise impunity to criminals, to increase the boldness of the wicked, to make all things desolate with robberies and slaughters, and finally, perpetually to dissolve the fellowship of the human race by robbery.

And it appears that these crimes have been most frequently objected to our people, and it is likely that they have had much influence in alienating the wills of men from the doctrine of Christ. For who would not shudder at such paradoxes, so absurd, that it is wicked to restrain the wicked by the fear of judgments and punishments, and to repel robberies, that the resources of all are common, that it is permitted for no magistrates to exist in cities. Origen sweats in refuting these calumnies against Celsus, Nazianzen also attempts to dilute them against Julian, but whether they have satisfied the cause undertaken, let it be the judgment of others. There is a long dispute in the Epistles of Augustine on the same matter to Marcellinus, who had written to Augustine that a certain prince of the African nation was accustomed to reproach Christian doctrine on this account, that it is not suitable for public affairs, for thus he speaks. But why do I commemorate the ancients, when in these most recent years so many booklets have been published in Germany, and indeed by some who wished to be called Theologians, in which it was argued that it is permitted to no Christian to hold magistracies, that the resources of all ought to be common, and other similar things. Luther alone most constantly defends the right and authority of magistrates, and if anyone compares his writings on this matter with the disputations of the ancients, he will see that the authority of magistrates has never been illustrated and adorned as much as it has been by him. And for this great benefit, he receives a reward by no means worthy from certain sycophants, who are accustomed to calling him seditious, because they can find no abuse more plausible.

Since, therefore, it is customary for the authority of magistrates to be disputed in various ways, I have decided in this place to collect the sentences of Scripture which clearly show what we ought to think about the magistrate. Perhaps this labor of mine will benefit the consciences of some. For he who knows how honorably the Holy Spirit speaks about the authority of magistrates will surely honor them with greater piety. And those who hold magistracies, if they know that their office pleases God, will have great consolation in all dangers. For they will dare to believe that they are defended by God, because they have undertaken the administration commanded by Him. It is very profitable for both, both for those who preside and for those who obey, to hold the true opinion on this matter. Furthermore, when we understand in Christian doctrine the Magistrate to be proclaimed with the most ample praises, we shall be able to refute the heathens, who slander our doctrine as if it were destructive to republics. Moreover, nothing is more becoming to a Christian man than to defend the dignity of religion against such slanders.

Wherefore we shall willingly treat this passage, which contains as it were a certain πολιτικην of Christians. For it teaches what is the duty of magistrates, what pleases God, and what is defended and preserved by God, and that subjects ought to obey them religiously. The political books of the philosophers are read, which I indeed love, and would wish to be read, for they contain many honest precepts, but these passages of Scripture, when diligently inspected, will bring much more utility than those disputations of the philosophers, which, because they can hand down nothing concerning the will of God toward magistrates, do not show what is the firmest protection of magistrates. Nor will subjects be able to be in their duty for long if they think that republics are constituted only by human counsel, if they think it permissible to avenge the injuries of magistrates.

Finally, if anyone shall have diligently weighed these passages which we shall recite, he will understand that no has ever existed on earth to have been a doctrine which would fortify the authority of the magistrate just as the Christian doctrine fortifies it. Therefore, it must first be understood that holding the office of magistrate is not an illicit thing, or a sin, for Paul writes to the Romans: There is no power but of God. The powers that be are ordained of God. Whosoever therefore resisteth the power, resisteth the ordinance of God. Here he openly says that powers are ordained by God, and he calls power the ordinance of God. But if power were robbery, it could not be called an ordinance of God, because God is said to ordain those things which He effects, and governs, and preserves. There is no human force so great that it could retain the civil state, since men cannot be tranquil in such great malice, and since the devil is a murderer and desires to destroy the bodies and souls of all at once. Hence Paul teaches us that the magistrate is divinely ordained, that is, not only established, but also preserved by God against the fury of men and the devil, just as God ordained that the sun should rise daily, that the seasons of the year should return in turns, that the fields should bear crops, and He preserves this order of things and these turns. Furthermore, if the magistrate were robbery, it could not be called an ordinance of God. It would rather be called confusion and a disturbance of the divine ordinance. For He does not ordain And God commands those things which are done against divine precepts. And then, when Paul says that the Magistrate is a minister of God, and indeed for good, he teaches again that it is a lawful thing to hold the magistracy. For robbery is not a ministry of God, just as neither is fornication, nor are other shameful acts ministries of God. Therefore, when Paul calls the magistrate a minister of God and an avenger, he signifies that the vengeance which the magistrate exercises is exercised by divine command, and with God governing the function of the magistrate. What of the fact that robberies can be said to be sent not for good, but for evil? The robber inflicts violence upon the just and the unjust without distinction; indeed, as much as anyone is most tranquil, so is he treated most cruelly in robberies. But the magistracy is a certain ordered vengeance, not rash, but discerning the good from the bad, and raging against the wicked, so that it may defend and champion the just. Therefore also Christ in the Gospel says of kings, ἐνεργεται they are called, so that he might distinguish the kingdom from robbery. For kings guard life, and fortunes, and the common tranquility, so that we may educate offspring, so that we may instruct, so that we ourselves may be free to know honest things and especially religion. These benefits, since they are among all human things the highest, by merit kings are called ἐνεργεται, by whose ministry God grants such great things to us.

And although these opinions show clearly enough that the office of magistrates is approved and pleasing to God, nevertheless I thought that certain others should also be added. For scripture has taught us copiously about this matter. And there is a great reason why scripture extols the magistrate with such praises. For just as in other articles of faith, that appearance which strikes the senses dissents entirely from the truth. Therefore God has delivered the word to us, so that against that appearance, He might both teach and fortify our minds. When Christ dies, what else does He seem to reason than an abject man. But the word pronounces Him to be truly and by nature God. What does reason judge concerning the afflictions of the pious? Truly it can determine nothing else by itself than that they are neglected and deserted by God. But scripture pronounces the opposite, that afflictions are signs of grace. If anyone considers all the articles of faith, he will understand that the word was delivered for this reason, because reason either did not comprehend the truth, or even judged the opposite. The same thing happens in this article which teaches that the office of magistrates was ordained by God, and is preserved by God. For I ask you to consult reason, from where it thinks empires are constituted, truly when it will contemplate the beginnings and ends of all empires, when it will consider the various changes of republics, as now among this nation, now among another, a principality has been, finally when he will see that empires are often occupied by crime, when he will consider the vices of those who hold republics, when he will consider their cruelty and negligence, what else can reason determine here, other than that empires are established and held without the counsel of God, only by human strength, or rashly. When we repeat in our mind ancient histories, in which the inconstancy of empires is discerned, when we look with our eyes and mind at the present vices of princes, and the injuries of peoples, it cannot be that reason is not offended by such an appearance of things, so that it denies that the civil state in republics is the work of God, and because it judges them to be robbery and tyranny, it happens that peoples are easily led to revolt from magistrates, and to prepare force against them, as if against robberies. What other thing impelled Brutus to take the counsel of killing Caesar, except this error, for because he judged Caesar's domination to be unjust, he reasoned that it was permitted for him to remove him from the administration of the republic, and he did not see that the present magistrate holds the republic by the counsel of God, and that he must be obeyed on account of the will of God.

Against false opinions of this kind of reason The Holy Spirit teaches us that the work of God is law and all civil discipline, which the human spirit teaches us. Although the philosophers knew by experience that in such varied movements of human affairs, republics cannot be maintained or constituted by human counsel, yet they could not affirm that the civil state is ruled and preserved by the certain counsel of God. Wherefore Plato in the fourth book of the Laws, while he indeed confesses that no man can establish laws for cities, yet he judges that republics consist perhaps more by chance than by divine counsel. For thus he says, θνητων μὲν μηδένα νομοθετειν μηδεν τῦχας δειναι σχεδον ἁπαντα τα ἀνδρωπινα πραγματα. Poets write that kings are a care to God, and that they have received laws divinely. For thus says Homer, ἐν παλαμαις φορεουσι δίκασπολοι οἱτε θεμιστας προς δίος εἰς ἰαται. For many conjectures moved the wisest poet to judge thus. Perhaps he had also received an opinion of this kind from his ancestors, to whom undoubtedly the first parents, who knew the word of God, had handed down many honest precepts as if from hand to hand. But those reasons which I reviewed above led the philosophers away from this opinion, so that they were the less able to affirm that God maintains the civil state.

Since, therefore, reason is deceived in this matter, He has handed down to us God his word, so that we might recognize this benefit of his as well, which, as to how much it matters that it be recognized, we shall say a little later. Now I will recount the remaining opinions. Gen. 9. It is written, Whoever shall shed human blood, his blood shall be shed, for in the image of God man was made. This Law contains all the ordinances which were made afterwards for retaining public tranquility, and for prohibiting robberies. Since therefore in this place God commands that murderers be punished, it follows that it is lawful to hold the magistracy, and to exercise legitimate vengeance against the guilty. Nor has this Law been abrogated by the Gospel. For when Paul says, The law is laid down for the unjust, he teaches that the unjust must be restrained in the world by law and punishments. They do not correctly understand either the power of the Gospel, or the abrogation of the law, if any think that the laws concerning morals, and concerning retaining public peace, have been abolished. And we shall speak of this matter below, when we speak concerning the abrogation of the law. Solomon also approves of the Magistrate when he says. A balance and scales are the judgments of the Lord, and he himself made the weights. For by balance and weight, he signifies all the legitimate ordinances of cities. And these he calls the judgments of the Lord, that is, a right certain and ordained by God, not only among the people of Israel, but among all nations. For also

God shows to the nations just laws and civil discipline, just as Wisdom says in another place, by me kings reign, and princes decree justice. Finally Luke 3 when the soldiers asked John what he wanted them to do, John said, Be content with your wages. Now they were Roman soldiers, who both waged wars abroad and exercised judgments in the cities of the province. Since John approved of the wages, for he commanded that they should use them, he certainly approves of the way of life and the military service. To this pertains what Paul and Jeremiah command, that we should pray for the powers. But we must not pray that God should preserve robberies. Wherefore when he commands to pray that the government may be fortunate, it follows that the kingdom is not an unjust domination. Added to this are the examples of the saints who held magistracies among the Jews and among the nations, as Abraham, for it is certain that he waged wars, Joseph, Naaman the Syrian, Daniel, the Centurions in the Gospel, and in the Acts, and Sergius the proconsul.

From these passages and examples it becomes plain that it is lawful to hold magistracies, to pronounce law, to exercise judgments, and to repel robberies by war. Moreover, the knowledge of this opinion brings the greatest utility. For there is nothing more to be desired by anyone than that he should understand the kind of life in which he is engaged, and know that it pleases God. For thus says Paul, whatever is not of faith is sin, he lives in perpetual sins, who enters upon a kind of life which is not approved by God. But on the contrary, he who knows that the kind of life in which he is engaged is approved by God, who understands that he is serving God, he has the greatest consolation in all dangers, but especially those who know that the magistrate is properly the ministry of God, and sustains a vicarious office for God, these can hope for the help of God in doubtful matters, because they sustain a burden imposed and commanded by Him, as David prays Psalmo septimo. Arise, O Lord my God, for the commandment which You have commanded. And just as David, Jehoshaphat, Hezekiah, Daniel, and the like were helped, so let pious princes feel that the help of God will also be present for them. For those examples were set forth, so that they too might now believe, especially those who hold republics, that they are regarded and defended by God. For God wished to console these with many examples, because no kind of life is exposed to more dangers, for those old maxims are well known.

The huge pine is more often shaken by the winds, and tall towers fall with a heavier crash, and lightnings strike the highest mountains.

And in such a great variety of dangers there is no human thing that can protect the safety of princes. There is not enough protection in armies, in the most fortified citadels, in money; finally, not even human wisdom in such darkness of human affairs can provide everything necessary for the safety of princes and of the republic. For not only do wicked men lie in wait for public tranquility, but also the Devil strives with the greatest force for the destruction of republics and princes, for he is called a murderer in the scripture. This, therefore, is the one firm consolation against the snares of the devil and against other accidents: to know that this kind of life pleases God, and that He wishes to help and defend princes who seek aid from Him. Nothing can even be thought of that is as beneficial to princes, or that can delight a good prince as much as the true knowledge of this place. It is customary for the signs of empire, fasces, scepters, coats of arms, and infinite ornaments of this kind to be carried before magistrates. Let them think that this sentence is painted and written upon these: that they have received their empire from God, and that they have not attained their dominion by chance or rashly. If they expect aid from God, God will govern their counsels in such varied storms.

And just as this sentence teaches the Magistrate faith, and promises them help, so again he exhorts them to fear, and warns them not to undertake anything against the will of God. For because they have a commission mandated from elsewhere, let them know that they will render an account of their counsels to Him whose place they hold. When the desire for anger and vengeance, when ambition, when other affections urge them to exercise their power against the weaker contrary to justice, let it come to mind that an account of the business transacted must be rendered to God. Thus Jehoshaphat admonishes those to whom he had entrusted prefectures in the kingdom of Judah, Take heed what you do. For you do not exercise the judgment of man, but of the Lord. And whatever you judge, it will redound upon you. Let the fear of the Lord be with you, and do all things with diligence. Let the ends of all Tyrants come to mind, which if anyone thinks are fortuitous, he errs far. Let Saul, Ahab, and the like admonish that God is accustomed to avenge the impious deeds of kings. For just as God avenges the flagrant crimes of the people through Magistrates, so He avenges the offenses of Tyrants with other calamities, of which thing the histories of all times supply many examples. Murderers do not escape the punishments of the laws, wherever they may flee, because it is the ordinance of God that they pay the penalties. Thus, on account of the ordinance of God, Tyrants also pay the penalties. The ordinance of God remains, and cannot be broken. by no riches, by no forces of Tyrants can it, as it is written, The Word of the Lord abideth for ever.

But here it is accustomed to be objected, that since Christ has forbidden to avenge injuries, it is not lawful for the magistrate to exercise vengeance. This passage has been treated variously by many, although indeed the explanation is not difficult. Some have handed down that it is a counsel, not a precept, that we should not avenge ourselves, and thus Nazianzen almost escapes from this question, when he answers Julian, who was accusing Christian doctrine, that it harms the commonwealth because it forbids vengeance. Furthermore, it is likely that on account of this passage many have doubted concerning the right of magistrates, to whom the function of magistrates seemed a certain unjust force and Tyranny, because Christ had forbidden vengeance, and traces of this opinion are to be discerned in the books of some, who as often as mention of magistrates occurs, speak contumeliously of their office, collect sentences by which Tyrants are agitated, exaggerate the vices of magistrates, from which thing it happens, that they clothe readers with a false persuasion concerning the very kind of life itself, because they are not admonished that the office itself is a kind of service approved by God. Therefore it is very profitable to hold to what this precept concerning not avenging pertains. Who counsel they judge to be, not to take revenge, they do not sufficiently notice what follows. For if Christ counsels as a better work to omit vengeance, he seems to counsel the same also to magistrates. But if we admit this, the heathens rightly reproach Christian doctrine, because it is pernicious to republics, since it allows the wicked to sin with impunity.

Wherefore it must be thought otherwise, that it is commanded not to take revenge, but this commandment must be understood concerning private vengeance, not concerning the vengeance which magistrates exercise. Nor do I invent this interpretation from myself, but Scripture compels us to distinguish private vengeance from the office of magistrates. And I wonder how it has happened that the voice of Christ concerning vengeance has offended so many, when the commandment, Do not kill, offends no one. No one has judged that magistrates act against this commandment in capital punishments. Thus it ought to have been thought also concerning the voice of Christ, which prohibits vengeance, especially since it manifestly appears that he in that very place is doing nothing else but interpreting the commandment, Do not kill. For the hypocrites understand only those things to be prohibited, but Christ teaches that that commandment pertains to the affections, to prohibit anger, hatred, the desire for revenge, which affection is especially in generous souls, as they call them, most sharp and most savage, as the Poet signifies, who wrote, But revenge is a good, sweeter than life itself. Just as therefore the commandment, You shall not kill, so the sentence of Christ, ought to be understood concerning private revenge, not concerning the office of magistrates. Scripture in other places commands concerning the magistrate, as we showed above, and as if from a common commandment it excepts the Magistrate, because they do not exercise revenge in their own name, but as vicars and ministers of God. For thus Paul calls the magistrate, a minister of God and an avenger. For as the glory of wisdom and justice, so revenge belongs to God. Therefore it is written, To me belongs vengeance and I will repay Deut. 32:35. Private individuals, because they take revenge without the command of God, sin most gravely, because they snatch away glory from God. The magistrate however exercises revenge by divine command, if nevertheless he exercises it in order and legitimately. And since God willed the Magistrate to be in the highest honor in the world, he signified that many great goods happen to us through their office, peace, security in seeking food, in educating and instructing offspring, finally in all the business of life. Thus therefore let us think concerning the revenge of magistrates, that jurisdiction and public punishments are the highest and most holy works of charity. of criminals, the driving away of robberies, lawful wars, and the rest which pertain to their office. They do not understand why Christ came into the world, who think that he abolished civil administration by enacting new laws. He indeed came to preach the remission of sins, to give the holy spirit to believers, of whom since the number is small, the Magistrate nevertheless wishes to exist on account of the remaining impious multitude, which it is necessary to be restrained by the fear of punishments, and by laws, just as Paul teaches when he says, The law is laid down for the unjust. Nor did Christ establish new Magistrates, but he commands us to obey the present ones, even though the Jews, and among them the Apostles, were dreaming that Christ was about to restore a civil kingdom, with the nations subdued in war, and the Jews liberated. But Christ often censures this opinion at other times, and also condemns it in this very place, when he forbids his own to exercise vengeance. For he signifies that he does not conduct a civil kingdom, but permits that kingdom to the Romans, who held it at that time; thus therefore that whole chapter Matth. 6. ought to be accepted, lest it be understood to abolish civil ordination, or Magistrates, since it is proven by so many other testimonies of scripture.

That also is objected, The kings of the gentiles lord it over them. But you not so, But that place does not condemn The kings of the nations, he distinguishes so much from the kingdom of Christ, the kingdom of the nations. He does not say that the kings of the nations sin because they dominate, because they hold and administer empires, and pronounce law to the peoples so that each may hold his own, and by arms they prohibit robberies, and wage legitimate wars against robbers. Nor does the voice of Vulgarius please me, who in the exposition of this passage in Matthew says, τυρανικον γὰρ ἐστι το ἀρχειν, καὶ ἀλλοτριον της ἐμης διδαχης, that is, It is tyrannical to reign, and foreign to my doctrine. For to hold a magistracy, or to be a prince, is a certain just and divine ordinance, it is not tyranny, that is, unjust force. Vulgarius can be excused, because he speaks not of the office of the Magistrate, but of the vices of those who hold the Magistracy. We, however, wished to warn the reader, lest, deceived by sentences of this kind (for they exist everywhere in writers in countless numbers), he should judge the office to be condemned. Nor indeed does Christ in this place condemn the kings of the nations, but by comparison shows what is the dissimilarity of the civil kingdom and the spiritual kingdom. Therefore, when he had said that the kings of the nations dominate, he added that they are called ἐνεργετε, which Christ would not have left to them, if he had wished to vituperate the kind of life; for it signifies that by the benefit of princes our life and fortunes are defended. But what kind of kingdom of the nations it is which thus Christ calls, as we have already often said, namely that he protects public peace by judgments, laws, punishments, and war. Nor is the kingdom of the nations called so rashly, for there is need for them to be restrained, who are not sanctified by the Holy Spirit. Moreover, that kingdom is, as I said above, instituted Gene 9. Whosoever shall shed human blood, BY MAN shall his blood be shed. By man, he says, so that we may understand that vengeance is to be exercised by public ordinance through the magistrate. Nor did Christ come to abolish this kingdom, but to begin a certain spiritual kingdom of his own in which the Gospel would be preached, the Holy Spirit would be given, and the hearts of believers would be renewed. Meanwhile, since there is a great multitude of the impious, Christ does not take away the civil kingdom from the world, but rather approves it, so that the impious may be restrained. Thus, therefore, he says to his own, But you not so, as if he were saying, they hold kingdoms by force and arms, we shall hold the kingdom by teaching. I do not snatch the empire from the Romans; let them retain their own magistrates, their own laws, their own institutions. I am establishing a certain kingdom, which will not hinder their administration, but will rather confirm it. Through the Gospel I will free my own from eternal death, from sin, from the power of the devil; such doctrine does not disturb civil administration For thus the Prophets had prophesied concerning the kingdom of Christ, Zachari. 9. Behold your King comes to you, just and a savior, poor. And Sophon. 3. I will leave in the midst of you a poor and needy people, and they shall hope in the name of the Lord. But if Christ had invaded the kingdom of the world, with other kings destroyed and all nations reduced to servitude, neither would he himself be called a poor or miserable King, nor would the people be called miserable, who would securely enjoy the riches of the whole world. But this form of kingdom, which the Prophets describe, as is evident, wonderfully offended the Jews, so much so that even the Apostles were of this opinion, that Christ was about to occupy a civil kingdom in Judea. And this error, tenaciously clinging in their minds, they could scarcely be brought to have removed from them late in the day. You see them, both with this passage and often elsewhere, being rebuked for this reason; Christ was crowned with a crown of thorns, and led about in royal purple as a mockery, by which thing it was clearly signified that there would be many who would interpret that Christ was in this only, that he might establish more moderate πολιτειας, that he might hand down certain precepts of living civilly. Finally, those who would not place the kingdom of Christ in the motions of the heart and in eternal and spiritual life, but only in a certain external, equitable polity. Thus it appears that the pontiffs [held] the principate to have thought according to his own established view, just as in this time the seditious have thought, who took up arms against the Magistrates under the pretext of the Gospel. For they boasted that they wished to bring forth laws from the Gospel and to establish republics according to scripture. Those who thought thus, clearly mocked Christ in his royal purple, and crowned him with thorns. Wherefore let us prudently distinguish these two kingdoms, the kingdom of the world, and the kingdom of Christ, just as we have warned not once until now. The kingdom of Christ is occupied in the hearts of the saints, who believe according to the Gospel that they have been received into grace by God on account of Christ, who are renewed and sanctified by the holy spirit, and taste eternal life, who by good works outwardly show their faith and on account of the glory of God, do good to all, so that they may invite very many to the acknowledgment of the Gospel, they tolerate all things, nor do they suffer themselves to be armed by the desire of vengeance against those by whom they have been affected with injury, they obey the magistrates with the greatest care, they bear the Magistracies, if indeed they are entrusted to them, vigilantly and bravely, if duty compels they punish the guilty, they fight in the battle line. They do not, however, break in to bear the Magistracies of their own accord, but if they are compelled by a calling they undertake them. Furthermore, the kingdom of the world, as I have often said, is a legitimate ordinance, which by the authority of the magistrates, by laws, judgments, punishments, and war, he defends the public peace. And Christ wills that not only the impious be subject to that ordinance, for whose sake it was established, but also the holy, for the sake of example. Therefore, Christ himself also gave δίδραχμον, so that by his own example he might move others to obedience. And Paul most gravely commands that we be subject, not only because of wrath, that is, not only because we are compelled by force, but because of conscience, that is, because of the commandment of God, which, even if we were not compelled by the fear of punishments, ought to remind us that we should obey.

If there are any other passages of Scripture, besides these two which we have now explained, that are cited against the office of magistrates, they will easily be able to be explained, if one diligently weighs those things which we have said thus far. But before I make an end, a certain argument must be refuted, which seems to make greatly against the authority of magistrates. For because kingdoms are for the most part established by wickedness, the human mind is wonderfully offended, so that they think empires are nothing but unjust force and tyranny. From that opinion, I remember a certain person reasoning that it is not permitted for a Christian man to hold magistracies or empires. It is not obscure how Gaius Caesar and others invaded the empire innumerable. And Scripture attributes to Nimrod the title of hunter, who, because he was the first to compel neighboring peoples by arms to obey him, is therefore seen to be called a hunter, because he preyed upon neighboring lands and brought peoples under his own power. For because Scripture added that he was a hunter before the Lord, it signified that he did not hunt in the common manner, or lie in wait only for wild beasts, but that he exercised force against neighboring nations and added them to his own empire. Infinite examples of this kind are read, and daily we see kings, struck by ambition or anger, stir up wars and invade the possessions of others. Wherefore, since kingdoms have been established through injustices, they do not seem to be ordained by God. And I do not know whether profane men even laugh at this paradox, about which we have been disputing thus far, that kingdoms are ordained by God. For reason, when it considers the beginnings of kingdoms, their ends, and the vices of princes, thinks far otherwise.

But a pious mind ought to look not at this appearance which strikes the eyes, but at the word of God, as we have said above. And here, in order to respond to the argument, the kingdom or divine ordination must be distinguished from the persons, that is, from those who either invade or possess, just as marriage is a divine ordinance, which it is not permitted to violate, even though those who take dowered wives on account of avarice, abuse the divine ordinance. Nor, however, is it permitted for him who has taken a wife out of a desire for money to rescind the marriage contract. Nor is it right for a wife to desert her husband. In that manner one ought to judge concerning a principality or a kingdom. A principality is a divine ordinance, namely that the multitude may be restrained by the authority of magistrates, as by laws, as by right and judgments, and by other bonds of peace, so that those who preside may declare right, fortify peace with laws, and defend with judgments and arms. This ordinance is not to be numbered among evil things, which, as some say, God permits to happen, it is not said to create, but it is to be numbered among the proper works of God, Just as we know the sun was founded by God, just as we know marriage was instituted by His command, so let us know that that ordinance πολιτικὴν was founded by Him, which indeed, unless it were preserved by God, could not endure in such great wickedness of men, and in this cruelty of the devil, who, since he is a murderer, would at once overturn the whole world, and mix everything with slaughters. And although, therefore, the ordinance itself is from God, yet as with other gifts of God, so also do men abuse this gift. And because God in the highest honor, and

He placed the magistracy at the pinnacle of human affairs; therefore, ambition has stimulated and inflamed very many, for there is an immense desire for glory in human souls, so that they might covet empires, so that they might disturb republics without regard for justice, and might indulge their own desire. The abuse of the best things is always the greatest; wherefore very many also most basely abuse the principate, who either invade it, going against justice, incited by ambition, or administer it badly. Furthermore, even if some abuse the principate, it is not permitted, however, for subjects to revolt from present magistrates, just as it is not permitted for a spouse to depart from a spouse, even if the marriage was contracted out of a desire for money, or the spouse is by lot more morose.

Thus far we have spoken concerning the authority of magistrates. For it is profitable for them also to understand their office, and we are invited to obedience when we see how much God values that office. Now we shall speak concerning the duty of subjects, which I wish those people who teach in the churches would diligently explain, especially at this time.
