Interea tamen hoc praestandum est, ne traditiones quae sine peccato servari possunt, violentur apud eos, qui non recte intelliguntdoctrinam libertatis, ne cui scandalo simus. Sed de scandalo alias. Gravis est comminatio Christi. Vaehomini per quem venit scandalum, Haec debbat nos commovere, ut caveremus ne cui nostra exempla nocerent.

## 3:1 Argument
CHAPTER THREE.

Latin:

CAPUT TERTIUM.

## 3:1
If therefore &c.

Latin:

Si igitur &c.

## 3:2
IN THE PRECEDING two chapters Paul treated the proper place of the Gospel, concerning the remission of sins, or justification. Now he adds the doctrine concerning good works, which is the other part of Christian doctrine. Nor indeed is it enough for Christians to know these precepts of morals, or the law. But it is necessary first to hold the doctrine concerning the benefit of Christ. For first it is necessary to know how we may obtain the remission of sins, and be made sons of God. Concerning that matter he taught above, that for the sake of Christ reconciliation is given to us, and that it is received by faith. Then it is necessary that we obey the law of God. But since we do not perform entire obedience, on account of the horrible infirmity of our nature, it is necessary to know how that obedience pleases God. For it does not please because it satisfies the law. But it must be known that it pleases because the person is reconciled and just by faith. Thus by faith we are pronounced just, that is, we are reconciled. And yet this obedience must be added, which now pleases and becomes a certain justice, not because it satisfies the law, but because we have been made sons. This is a huge mercy, that God so approves this obedience of ours, such as it is, which is very far from the perfection of the law, just as if we were performing entire obedience. Which voice of human language can sufficiently explain this benefit? It is necessary that these things be taught concerning the doctrine of good works, so that we may understand how our obedience pleases in such great infirmity, and so that we may always hold to that goal, that the person is righteous by faith on account of Christ, and that our obedience pleases on account of Christ. Thus the conscience will have firm consolation. Paul teaches these things copiously ad Romanos capite 6. when he says, Now you are not under the law, but under grace. That is, your obedience pleases not because it satisfies the law, but because you are in grace, that is, because you have been reconciled to God by faith in Christ. Item. Roma. 8. There is now no condemnation to those who walk in Christ Jesus etc. This is, Even if great infirmity still clings to our nature, nevertheless it is pardoned to us on account of Christ. And these things ought to exhort us to do good. For despair deters from doing good, when we think, to what end does it pertain to strive when you do not know whether God approves this diligence. On the contrary, this consolation rouses us to do good, when we hear that there is such great power in the mercy of God, that He approves this imperfect obedience. Therefore let us show ourselves to be grateful to God for such a great benefit, and with an upright conscience let us obey Him and think that our pious efforts are approved by Him, and not be cast off Let us allow faith for ourselves.

Paul begins this chapter with a rhetorical circumduction. Since you have been raised, seek the things that are above, where Christ is sitting at the right hand of God. Nor is the argument drawn only from a cause or from a duty, but also from the possible, or from the easy. When he says you have been raised, he gathers from the cause or duty, as if he were saying, Another life brings other morals. You have obtained a new life and a participant of the divine nature, therefore the actions ought to be those which please God. Just as it is written in Ephesians 2. We are his workmanship, created through Christ Jesus unto good works. And Christ Matth. 12. most gravely threatens those who, having been justified, do not produce good fruits, The last state (he says) of that man will be worse than the first.

Secondly, that he admonishes us that Christ is above and sitting at the right hand of the Father, is an argument from the possible, as if he were saying thus, Go on, you will have to struggle with the flesh, with the devil, with the world, but what are such great human strengths that can stand against such powerful enemies? None. Therefore cast yourselves upon Christ, he because he sits at the right hand of God, that is, he reigns with the Father, he takes care of you, he hears you, he defends you, he helps in all dangers, from him without any doubting ask for aid and help.

Thus we shall be able both to overcome the snares of the devil, and to conquer the infirmity of the flesh. This is the exercise of faith, that as if coming into battle, to tame the flesh, to overcome the snares of the devil, one should assiduously ask for, and expect help from God. Paul alludes, however, to the passage Psalm. 67. You ascended on high, you led captivity captive, you received gifts in men. From the beginning, which he says, HE ASCENDED, Paul gathers that the son of God first descended, that is, was humbled, put on flesh, was crucified. For God, if he had not been humbled, if he had not put on human nature, would not be said to have ascended, since he was already before on high, that is, in glory. Christ therefore ascended on high, that is, he was glorified, he rose again, and reigns with the father with equal power. Perfecting all things in all. HE LED CAPTIVITY CAPTIVE, that is, he took away sin, and the power of the devil and of death. For through Christ sin is blotted out in those who believe, and strengths are given to them against the devil, and eternal life is given. Finally, HE RECEIVED GIFTS FOR MEN, this Paul reads, he gave gifts for men. This part is, however, most to be considered, this voice ought always to be kept in the mind. He gave gifts to men, or he gives gifts to men, for it signifies that for this reason Christ ascended that he might give gifts, that is, that he might help us, might give the Holy Spirit, and the fruits of the Holy Spirit. If you hear that Christ ascended for this reason, that he might bestow gifts, we ought therefore to ask for and expect help. Others read he received gifts for men, so that the meaning is that Christ received gifts from the Father, but which he might distribute among men. If anyone prefers to read, HE RECEIVED GIFTS IN MEN, the meaning will return to the same thing, for it signifies that Christ ascended in such a way that he might hear us. For to receive gifts from us is to hear our prayers, that we are a care to him. For the gods of the nations have ears and do not hear. But our God hears and helps those praying, just as it is also written, Whatever you ask the Father in my name, it will be given to you, Item, Whoever shall call upon the name of the Lord, shall be saved. There is therefore in this place of Paul to the Colossians an argument proposed from the possible or the easy. And thus he exhorts us, that we do not suffer ourselves to be deceived by the devil, nor allow ourselves to be overcome by the desires of the flesh, as if he were saying, I command a great thing to you, but an easy one, if you look to where Christ is, what he promises, namely that he will give gifts to men, that he will help us, but happy are those who dare to ask, and expect heavenly help. Certainly God desires much more to bestow, than we desire. to receive, as the Lord says, If men who are evil can give good gifts to men, how much more your Father who is in heaven?

Latin:

SUPERIORIBUS duobus capitibus tractavit Paulus proprium Evangelii locum, de remissione peccatorum, seu iustificatione. Nunc addit doctrinam de bonis operibus, quae est altera pars doctrinae Christianae. Neque vero satis est Christianos nosse haec praecepta morum, seu legem. Verum oportet prius tenere doctrinam de beneficio Christi. Primum enim necesse est scire, quomodo consequamur remissionem peccatorum, & efficiamur filii Dei, De ea re supra docuit, propter Christum nobis donari reconciliationem, eamque fide accipiDeinde necesse est nos obedire legi Dei. Sed quoniam non praestamus integram obedientiam, propter horribilem naturae nostrae infirmitatem, necesse est scire, quomodo placeat ea obedientia Deo. Non enim placet ideo, quia legi satisfaciat. Sed sciendum est ideo placere, quia persona est reconciliata ac iusta fide, Ita fide pronunciamur iusti, id est, reconciliamur. Et tamen accedere debet haec obedientia, quae iam placet & fit quaedam iusticia, non quia legi satisfacit, sed quia filii facti sumus. Haec est ingens misericordia, quod Deus hanc nostram qualemcunque obedientiam, quae longissime abest a perfectione legis, ita approbat, perinde ac si integram obedientiam praestaremus. Quae vox humana potest hoc beneficium satis explicare? Haec moneri de doctrina bonorum operum necesse est ut intelligamus, quomodo nostra obedientia in tanta infirmitate placeat, & ut semper teneamus illum scopum, quod persona fide propter Christum iusta sit, quodque nostra obedientia placeat propter Christum. Ita conscientia habebit firmam consolationem. Haec docet Paulus copiose ad Romanos capite 6. cum ait, Iam non estis sub lege, sed sub gratia. Id est, placet vestra obedientia non quia legi satisfaciat, sed quia estis in gratia, hoc est, quia Deo reconciliati estis fide in Christum. Item. Roma. 8. Nulla nunc condemnatio est his, qui in Christo Iesu ambulant etc. Hoc est, Et si haeret in natura adhuc magna infirmitas, tamen condonatur nobis propter Christum. Atque haec hortari nos ad bene operandum debent. Nam desperatio deterret a bene operando, cum cogitamus, quorsum attinet conari cum nescias an Deus probet hanc diligentiam. Econtra haec consolatio exuscitat ad bene operandum, cum audimus tantam vim esse misericordiae Dei, ut approbet hanc imperfectam obedientiam. Itaque ostendamus nos esse gratos Deo pro tanto beneficio, & erecta conscientia obtemperemus ei ac pios conatus nostros probari ei cogitemus, nec excuti nobis fidem sinamus.

Orditur autem Paulus hoc caput a circumdu ctione Rhetorica. Cum resuscitati sitis, quaerite ea quae sursum sunt, ubi Christus est ad dexteram Dei sedens. Neque tantum argumentum a causa seu a debito tractum est, sed etiam a possibili, seu a facili. Cum dicit resuscitati estis, colligit a caussa seu debito, quasi dicat, Alia vita alios mores adfert. Novam vitam consecuti estis et participem divinae naturae, debent igitur actiones esse quae placeant Deo. sicut & Ephesi. ii. scriptum est. Ipsius factura sumus, conditi per Christum Iesum ad bona opera. Et Christus Matth. xii. gravissime comminatur his qui iustificati, non faciunt fructus bonos, Erunt novissima (inquit) illius peiora prioribus.

Secundo, quod admonet nos Christum sursum esse & sedere ad dexteram patris, argumentum est a possibili, quasi sic dicat, Agite, vos debebitis luctari cum carne, cum diabolo, cum mundo, quae autem sunt tantae vires humanae, quae consistere adversus tam valentes hostes possint? nullae. Proinde reiicite vos in Christum, ille quia ad dexteram Dei sedet, hoc est, regnat cum patre, vestri curam gerit, vos exaudit, vos defendit, adiuvat in omnibus periculis, ab illo opem et auxilium nihil dubitantes postulate.

Sic poterimus & diaboli insidias superare, & vincere infirmitatem carnis. Hoc est, fidei exercitium, ut tanquam in aciem veniens, ad domandam carnem, ad superandas diaboli insidias, assidue postulet, & expectet auxilium a Deo, Alludit autem Pau lus ad locum Psalm. 67. Ascendisti in altum, cepisti captivitatem, accepisti dona in hominibus. Principio quod ait, ASCENDIT, colligit Paulus filium Dei prius descendisse, hoc est, humiliatum esse, carnem induisse, crucifixum esse. Deus enim, si non esset humiliatus, si non induisset humanam naturam. non diceretur ascendisse, cum iam ante sursum, id est, in gloria esset. Ascendit igitur Christus in altum, id est, glorificatus est, resurrexit, & regnat cum patre pari potentia. Omnia in omnibus perficiens. CEPIT CAPTIVITATEM, id est, sustulit peccatum, & potentiam diaboli & mortis. Nam per Christum peccatum deletur in his, qui credunt, & dantur eis vires adversus diabolum, & datur vita aeterna. Postremo, ACCEPIT DONA PRO HOMINIBUS, id Paulus legit, dedit dona pro hominibus. Est autem haec pars maxime consideranda, haec vox semper servari in animo debet. Dedit dona hominibus, seu dat dena hominibus, significat enim, ideo Christum ascendisse, ut dona daret, id est, ut iuvaret nos, daret spiritum sanctum, & fructus spiritus sancti. Si ideo audis ascendisse Christum, ut dona largiatur, postulare igitur & expectare auxilium debemus. Alii legunt accepit dona pro hominibus, ut sit sententia, accepisse Christum a patre dona, sed quae distribueret inter homines. Si quis mavult legere, ACCEPIT DONA IN HOMINIBUS, eodem redibit sententia, significat enim, sic ascendisse Christum, ut nos exaudiret. Nam dona accipere a nobis est exaudire preces nostras, nos ei curae esse. Etenim dii gentium aures habent & non audiunt. At noster Deus exaudit & iuvat precantes, sicut & scriptum est, Quidquid rogaveritis patrem in nomine meo, dabitur vobis, Item, Quisquis invocaverit nomen domini, salvus erit. Est igitur in hoc Pauli loco ad Coloss. argumentum a possibili seu facili propositum. Et sic hortatur nos, ut ne paciamur nos decipia diabolo, neve sinamus nos a cupiditatibus carnis expugnari, quasi dicat, Magnam rem vobis praecipio, sed facilem, si respexeritis, ubi Christus sit, quid polliceatur, nempe se dona daturum hominibus, se opitulaturum nobis esse, felices vero qui audent postulare, & expectare caeleste auxilium. Certe multo magis cupit Deus largiri, quam nos cupiamus accipere, sicut Dominus ait, Si homines qui mali sunt possunt dare bona dana hominibus, quanto magis pater vester qui in caelis est?

## 3:2
Seek the things that are above, etc.

It is a figure of speech in the sacred writings, for they signify, those things that are above, heavenly or spiritual goods, such as faith, peace of heart, patience, charity, chastity, and the like. Those things that are below signify carnal goods, such as pleasures, riches, the glory of the world. Thus also Solomon speaks Prover. 15. He carries the seeds of life upward, so that he may teach to avoid hell below. For he calls carnal goods hell, just as Paul calls them death, the affection of the flesh is death.

It must be diligently considered here, what goods human nature loves and seeks by itself. For it loves, expects, or hopes for nothing else except pleasures, riches, or the glory of the world, as Paul says Rom. 8. The affection of the flesh cannot be subject to the laws of God, And 1. Ioan. 2. Everything that is in the world, is the concupiscence of the flesh, and the concupiscence of the eyes and the pride of life. Again also the flesh fears or hates nothing, except the loss of such goods. But the Holy Spirit he judges this concupiscence to be against God, therefore he produces in the saints a fear of divine judgment, and shame, as Paul speaks, so that desires may seriously displease them, and as it is written John 16, the Holy Spirit will convict the world of judgment, that is, the Holy Spirit will convict and condemn concupiscence in the hearts of men, which the world does not judge to be evil.

Then the Holy Spirit incites to the seeking of heavenly goods, that is, to faith and hope, this is, to requesting that God increase in us the knowledge of himself, that is, that the amplitude of his mercy being known may increase in us confidence toward him. Likewise, that our infirmity and the snares of the devil being known, he may cause us to feel that we cannot stand unless he governs us, and to ask ardently that he himself may rule us, as it is written, Lead us not into temptation. For if we believed it to be true what Christ says, Without me you can do nothing, and if we knew that the devil is lying in wait for us in such a way as Peter wrote, As a roaring lion he seeks whom he may devour, we would certainly not cease to ask help from God. The rest follow, the flesh hates the cross, the Holy Spirit incites to the enduring of the cross and the losing of carnal goods, and causes that we may feel God in afflictions helping and being present with us. As Paul says, We have the spirit in whom we cry, Abba Father. Thus, although carnal eyes see absolutely no help, nay, even see us falling and being deserted, yet he causes us to cling to the word, and to await those things which we do not see, as Paul says, Hope which is seen is not hope.

When therefore he says, seek, feel those things which are above, he speaks of true affections, not only of the external simulation of civil life. So also Christ said. Seek the kingdom of God.

Latin:

Quae sursum sunt quaerite &c.

Figura sermonis est in sacris literis, significant enim, ea quae sursum sunt, bona caelestia, seu spiritualia, ut sunt, fides, pax cordis, pacientia, caritas, castitas & similia. Ea quae deorsum sunt, significant bona carnalia, ut voluptates, divitias, gloriam mundi. Sic loquitur & Salomon Prover. 15. Semina vitae sursum fert, ut doceat vitare infernum deorsum. Infernum enim vocat, carnalia bona, sicut vocat illa mortem Paulus, adfectus carnis mors est.

Diligenter hic considerandum est, quae bona amet & quaerat humana natura per se. Nihil enim aliud amat, expectat, aut sperat, nisi voluptates, divitias, aut gloriam mundi, sicut Paulus ait Rom. 8. Adfectus carnis non potest leges Dei subiici, Et 1. Ioan. 2. Omne quod est in mundo, concupiscentia carnis est, & concupiscentia oculorum & superbia vitae. Rursus etiam caro nihil metuit aut edit, nisi talium bonorum iacturam. Sed Spiritus sanctus iudicat hanc concupiscentiam contra Deum esse, propterea efficit in sanctis metum divini iudicii, & pudorem sicut Paulus loquitur, ut serio displiceant cupiditates, & sicut Ioan. xvi. scriptum est, Spiritus sanctus arguet mundum de iudicio, id est, Spiritus sanctus arguet & condemnabit concupiscentiam in cordibus hominum, quam mundus non iudicat malum esse.

Deinde incitat Spiritus sanctus ad quaerenda bona caelestia, id est, ad fidem & spem, hoc est, ad postulandum ut Deus augeat in nobis cognitionem sui, hoc est, ut amplitudo misericordiae eius cognita augeat in nobis fiduciam erga ipsum. Item ut cognita nostra infirmitate, & insidiis diaboli, efficiat nos sentire, quod non possimus consistere, nisi nos gubernet, & ardenter postulare, ut ipse nes regat, sicut scriptum est, Ne nos inducas in tentationem. Etenim si crederemus verum esse, quod ait Christus, Sine me nihil potestis facere, & si sciremus nobis ita diabolum insidiari, sicut scripsit Petrus, Tanquam Leo rugiens quaerit quem devoret, profecto non desineremus a Deo opem postulare. Sequuntur caetera, caro o lit crucem, Spiritus sanctus incitat ad perferandam crucem, & amittenda bona carnalia, & efficit, ut sentiamus in adflictionibus Deum nobis opitulari & adesse. Sicut ait Paulus, Habemus spiritum in quo clamamus, Abba pater. Ita quanquam prorsus oculi carnales nihil anxilii videant, imo etiam videant nos occumbere & deseri, tamen facit, ut in verbo haereamus, & expectemus ea quae non videmus, sicut ait Paulus, Spes quae videtur, non est spes.

Cum igitur ait, quaerite, sentite ea quae sursum sunt, de veris adfectibus loquitur, non tantum de externa simulatione civilis vitae. Sic etiam Christus dixit. Quaerite regnum Dei.

## 3:3
For you are dead and your life is hidden with Christ in God.

Just as before he drew an argument from the cause, or duty, from the resurrection, so now he draws an argument of the same kind from mortification, as if he were saying, If you are truly mortified, therefore the evil and vicious fruits of the flesh, fornication, hatreds, and the like, ought not to live. Above, however, I said that mortification is true repentance, that is, to truly shudder at the judgment of God, and to fear the wrath of God. Such repentance hears the voice of Christ, Go and sin no more. For secure men, and those void of serious fear of God, neither having faith, nor exercising any good works, as Paul testifies at Ephe. 4. Because of the hardness of their heart, who being past feeling, have given themselves over unto lasciviousness &c.

He teaches that this is the sign and this the mark of the wicked, to have become past feeling, that is, to be touched by no fear, securely to despise the judgment of God. He also calls this the hardness of the heart. For those members are numb which are stiff, and as if life were lost, receive no motions, no affections. Thus these men are moved by divine threats absolutely not at all. A similar description of the wicked is with Jeremiah, when he says, They knew not how to blush.

Latin:

Mortui enim estis & vita vestra ab scondita est cum Christo in Deo.

Sicut antea a resurrectione argumentum a caussa, seu debito traxit, ita nunc eiusdem generis argumentum trahit a mortificatione, quasi dicat, Si estis vere mortificati, ergo non debebunt vivere mali & viciosi fructus carnis, scortatio, odia, & similes. Supra autem dixi mortificationem esse veram paenitentiam, hoc est, vere perhorrescere iudicio Dei, & timere iram Dei. Talis paenitentia audit vocem Christi, Vade & noli amplius peccare. Nam securi homines, & vacui serio metu Dei, neque fidem habens, neque ulla bona opera exercent, sicut testatur Paulus ad Ephe. 4. Propter stuporem cordis, qui quia dedoluerunt, se tradiderunt impudicitiae &c.

Hoc signum, & hanc notam impiorum esse docet, dedoluisse, hoc est, nullo metu tangi, securos contemnere iudicium Dei, Id etiam stuporem cordis vocat. Stupent enim ea membra quae rigent, et tanquam amissa vita nullos montus, nullos adfectus recipiunt. Sic isti prorsus nihil moventur divinis comminationibus. Similis est descriptio impiorum apud Ieremiam, cum ait, Erubescere nescierunt.

## 3:3
Your life is hid &c.

He treated the same sentiment at length at Roma. 8. For he mixes consolation with exhortation. You are dead, he says, and you are daily being put to death. And yet your glorification does not yet appear before the world; not only do the world and the Devil persecute the saints, but they themselves are in truth weak, and because of infirmity are solicited to despair, and do not dare to trust that they are so greatly loved by God, as the Gospel promises. For if the human heart could feel that we are truly a care to God, and that He looks upon us calling upon Him, and that we are loved, it would easily despise all afflictions in the world. Thus, therefore, Christ consoles the weak, Luke 12. Fear not, little flock, for it has pleased your Father to give you the kingdom. And here Paul says that life is hidden in God, that is, to lie covered and be defended by God, which nevertheless will show itself when Christ will show himself again to the world. If you refer this passage to common afflictions, it will be understood more easily. In all afflictions, because God does not immediately show help according to what human weakness desires, and we seem plainly deserted, therefore he commands us to trust and to feel that our life lies hidden covered with God, that is, that God guards it, even if he seems to have deserted, and is about to bring help opportunely. Just as those in the Psalm complain, Many say who will show us good? as if he were saying, Great things are promised concerning God, that he is about to save and defend and help those who call upon him. But we see the opposite happening, they are deserted and killed, just as Abel was killed, Abraham, David, and those like them were variously vexed. Finally, this is the common complaint of weak minds, that God delays help too long, that he does not immediately help us. Therefore, also in that Psalm he commands them to hope, he says that it is a sacrifice most pleasing to God, to wait however long it is delayed help. Thus here Paul says that life, that is, all kinds of hidden consolations, are stored up, and are to be promised and shown to you, when it seems good to God, nor are we deserted on that account, even if God allows us to be afflicted for some time.

Latin:

Vita vestra abscondi &c.

Prolixe tractavit eandem sententiam ad Roma. 8. Nam adhortationi consolationem admiscet. Mortui inquit estis, et quotidie mortificamini. Neque tamen adhuc adparet coram mundo glorificatio vestri, non solum mundus & Diabolus persequuntur sanctos, sed etiam ipsi sunt revera imbecilles, & propter infirmitatem solicitantur ad desperationem, & non audent confidere se tantopere diligi a Deo, sicut Evangelium promittit. Si enim cor humanum sentire posset, vere nos Deo curae esse, & respici invocantes eum, & diligi, facile omnes adflictiones in mundo contemneret. Sic igitur infirmos consolatur Christus, Luce. xii. Nolite timere pusillus grex, quia complacuit patri vestro dare vobis regnum. Et hic ait Paulus vitam esse absconditam in Deo, hoc est, latere tectam & defendi a Deo, quae tamen ostendet se, quando Christus ostendet se iterum mundo. Si hunc locum referas ad communes adflictiones, facilius intelligetur. In omnibus adflictionibus, quia Deus non statim pro eo, ac infirmitas humana desiderat, auxilium ostendit, planeque deserti videmur, ideo iubet nos confidere & sentire, quod vita nostra tecta lateat apud Deum, id est, quod Deus eam custodiat, etiamsi deservisse videtur, & sit auxilium oportune laturus. Sicut illi in Psalmo queruntur, Multi dicunt quis ostendet nobis bonum? quasi dicat, Magna de Deo promittuntur, quod sit servaturus & defensurus, & adiuturus eos qui invocant eum. Sed diversum fieri videmus, deseruntur & occiduntur, sicut Abel occisus est, Abraham, David, & similes varie ucxati sunt. Denique haec commmunis querela est infirmorum animorum, quod Deus nimis diu differat auxilium, quod non statim nos iuvet. Proinde & in eo Psalmo iubet eos sperare, dicit id esse sacrificium gratissimum Deo, expectare quamvis diu dilatum auxilium. Ita hic Paulus dicit vitam, hoc est, omnis generis consolationes abditas esse, & promi & ostendi vobis, quando Deo videtur, nec propterea nos desertos esse, etiamsi aliquandiu adfligi nos Deus sinit.

## 3:5
Mortify your members.

Here he begins to deliver precepts concerning morals in particular, but the world only restrains external members, the Gospel preaches concerning the motions of the heart toward God. Therefore, the first thing to be noted is what he says, mortify your members. Mortification, however, is not just some human diligence of restraining, or external torture, but when the true fear of God deters us from sinning, so that the heart truly shudders with the fear of divine judgment when it is solicited to sin. A heart struck in this way acknowledges its own weakness and implores help from God. Such mortification happens with the help of the Holy Spirit, as Roma. 8. teaches, If by the spirit you mortify the deeds of the flesh, you shall live. Externally, however, such mortification also invites one to sobriety and to labors, as Paul says, I chastise my body, and bring it into subjection.

There is also a certain diligence of restraining the flesh, which is done only by human powers, which God also demands, as appears in this sentence, The law is laid down for the unjust. Item Proverb. 27. A whip for the horse, a bridle for the ass, and a rod for the back of the foolish. Here by fasts, and other labors and punishments, or by the fear of punishments, the petulance of the flesh must be depressed. But this human or bodily discipline is not called mortification. It is, however, as I have said, necessary for the carnal. Wonderfully he calls the vices themselves members, so that he may signify that nature is vicious, just as he says at Romans 7. I see another law in my members warring against the law of my mind. And Gen. 6. Every desire of the thoughts of the human heart is evil at all times, That true impulse, and propensity to sin, is called original sin.

Latin:

Mortificate membra vestra.

Hic incipit in specie tradere praecepta de moribus, Mundus autem tantum cohercet externa membra, Evangelium concionatur de motibus cordis erga Deum. Proinde primum est animadverten dum, quod ait, mortificate membra vestra. Est autem mortificatio, non tantum humana quaedam cohercendi diligentia, aut externi cruciatus, sed quando verus metus Dei absterret nos a peccando, ita ut vere perhorrescat cor metu divini iudicii, cum ad peccandum solicitatur. Sic perculsum cor infirmitatem suam agnoscit, & opem a Deo implorat. Talis mortificatio fit adiuvante spiritusancto, sicut Roma. 8. docet, Si spiritu facta carnis mortificabitis, vivetis. Foris autem talis mortificatio etiam ad sobrietatem, ad labores invitat, sicut Paulus ait, Castigo corpus meum, & in servitutem redigo.

Est & quaedam diligentia cohercendi carnem, quae fit tantum humanis viribus, quam etiam exigit Deus, ut adparet in hac sententia, Lex est iniustis posita. Item Proverb. 27. Flagellum equo, chamus asino, & virga in dorso imprudentium. Hic iciuniis, & aliis laboribus ac poenis, seu metu poenarum, petulantia carnis deprimenda est. Sed haec disciplina humana seu corporalis non dicitur mortificatio. Est tamen, ut dixi, carnalibus necessaria. Mire ipsa vitia membra vocat, ut significet naturam viciosam esse, sicut ad Roma. 7. ait, Video aliam legem in membris meis repugnantem legi mentis meae. Et Gen. 6. Omne desiderium cogitationum cordis humani pravum est omni tempore, Ille vero impetus, & propensio ad peccandum, dicitur peccatum originale.

## 3:9
The old man etc.

A new man is called, not he who restrains himself only by reason, for reason pertains to the old man. For man brings reason with himself, when he is born. But a new man is called he who is renewed through the Holy Spirit, and in whom the Holy Spirit effects the fear of God, faith, chastity, charity and similar good fruits John 1. They are born of God. And 3.

Unless one has been born again of water and the spirit. And here he thus defined the new man to be one who is renewed unto knowledge, according to the image of him who created him, that is, the new man is one who grows in the knowledge of God, that is, one who truly perceives that God looks upon us, and is angry at sinners, and therefore fears God, and perceives that God truly forgives for the sake of Christ those who do penance. And he perceives that God is near at hand, to help and to rule the saints. He who perceives God in this way, he truly knows him. Nor is it knowing God to think many things subtly about his nature, but to know his will toward us, his anger against sin, and his mercy toward those who fear him, that is the true knowledge of God. But what is it that he says, according to the image of God? Some have placed the image of God in reason, and in the old man, that there is a certain likeness of the Trinity in our mind, because memory begets understanding. Let willing accompany memory and understanding, as the Father begets the son. For the son is called the Word for this reason, because just as in thinking of the thing about which we think, we conceive an image, so the substantial image is the son of the father, thus it is written in Epistola ad Haebre., The character of his substance. Furthermore, to the holy spirit is attributed the movement and variety of gifts. Therefore the holy spirit is compared to the will. Thus they have written that the image of the Trinity is in the human mind. I do not rebuke this thought. For it is possible to discern the traces of divinity impressed upon many creatures. Paul, however, speaks of another certain image, which belongs only to the new man. The forms of God are wisdom, justice, truth, holiness; when God imprints these upon us, we are the image of God, and participants of the divine nature. Thus he speaks to Ephe. iiii. Put on the new man who is created according to God, that is, to the likeness of God, in justice, and holiness, and truth, that is, he is just and pleasing to God, he is holy, that is, pure, or with a purified heart, with the flesh mortified, he is truthful, that is, he thinks rightly and truly about God. For the flesh by no means thinks truly, for it always dreams, neither that the cruelty of God is so great that He punishes sinners so severely, nor again that His mercy is so great that He forgives, that He helps, etc.

He speaks thus also, ii. Corinth. iii. We, beholding the glory of the Lord with open face, are transformed into the same image from glory to glory, as by the Lord the Spirit, that is, when the heart truly conceives consolation, it is raised up through faith, so that it may gaze upon the magnitude of the divine mercy and goodness. For this is to see the glory of God. There the heart conceives the knowledge of God, which is spiritual wisdom and justice, that is the image of God. But what Paul says, with unveiled face, alludes to the history of Moses. For the flesh, seeing a veiled face, sees only the justice of the world and the carnal; when it hears the word of God, it sees only bodily punishments and bodily benefits of God, just as the people of Moses saw the present destruction of Pharaoh. Likewise the present benefit, the yielding waters and the making of a way. But the new man, with face uncovered, sees the wrath of God against sin, sees the magnitude of mercy, and believes that he is forgiven, and conceives peace and joy before God. Thus also in this place is Paul to be understood, when he defines the new man, growing in the knowledge of God, which recognition of God makes him the image of God. For that knowledge is as a certain light, in which, as in a mirror, God shines forth and is discerned. To this also pertains what is written in Genesis, Let us make man to our image and likeness, that is, wise and just. For such he made Adam, and delivered his word to him. But Adam did not retain the likeness of God. It is therefore restored to us through Christ, who is the true and natural image of God and on account of whom God has called other men his image. For And on account of Christ others obtain righteousness, truth, and holiness. Therefore Paul exhorts in this place that we grow in the knowledge of God, that is, that repentance and fear may grow in us. Likewise, that faith may grow. Likewise, that in tribulation we may call upon God, and learn that He is near and helps those who call upon Him. Just as he also teaches to the Romans, Tribulation produces patience, patience probation, probation hope, but hope does not confound. That hope, because it knows God, and expects, and even in the most grievous afflictions gazes upon Him, is the very Image of God, and bringing this hope we approach God with unveiled face, and we are not confounded, just as Moses used to enter to the Lord with unveiled face.

Where there is neither Greek nor Jew etc.

This passage contains the sentiment concerning Christian liberty. For as the Roman or Attic polity is not that righteousness about which the Gospel preaches, so neither is the Jewish or monastic polity the righteousness of the Gospel. Therefore, so that it may be understood more plainly what Christian liberty is, let us establish four degrees. The first is that sins are remitted to us freely on account of Christ, not on account of the fulfillment of the law.

This is the principal degree and pertains nothing to the exterior and political life, but only to the struggle of the conscience wrestling with the judgment of God. In that agony it is necessary to hold this degree of liberty. For no firm consolation can be proposed to terrified consciences, except this doctrine concerning this degree of liberty, and concerning this Christ preaches when he says: If the son shall have made you free, you shall be truly free. To this degree also pertains this benefit, that Christ gives the Holy Spirit, who may govern and help us against the devil. Thus the first degree teaches us to be freed from the law in such a way, that is, from the condemnation of the law, that we may be freed from sin, death, and the power of the devil.

The second degree. And if the decalogue remains, as far as obedience pertains, for it is necessary as we have already often said, that the new life be a certain obedience toward God, nevertheless here it must again be known that we have this liberty, that that obedience is pleasing, not because we satisfy the law, for we are far removed from the perfection of the law, but because the person is pleasing, reconciled by trust in the mercy promised on account of Christ. Thus therefore we are freed from the law not as far as obedience pertains, but because another thing is proposed for reconciliation, or justification, and that this obedience is pleasing even if it is far from the perfection of the law. Paul taught this above when he says: In Christ you are made complete. And to the Romans: You are no longer under the law, but under grace. And to the Galatians: Christ redeemed you from the curse of the law, having become a curse for you. That is, he himself was made a victim for us, so that the Law might not curse, might not accuse, might not condemn those who are already reconciled by faith in Christ, even if the infirmity of nature still clings to them, and they do not perfectly satisfy the law. Thus, those who are reborn through Christ are freed from the decalogue, as far as condemnation pertains. And yet obedience is required, because certainly a new life must be obedience toward God. Moreover, it is a great consolation that the conscience, in this weakness, nevertheless knows that we are pleasing to God, as has often been said already.

The third degree of liberty, however, is that Christians are not bound to the ceremonies and judicial precepts, as they call them, of the Mosaic law, but they can use the legitimate polities of all nations. A Christian can hold things, buy, sell, can exercise judgments, according to the laws of that nation among whom he lives, whether they be Roman, or Saxon, or Gallic laws. And, as I said above, Christians must always obey present laws and ordinances. If Just as Christ obeyed the Romans, he gave διδραχμον.. In the law, civil liberty was promised to the sons of Israel, just as other bodily goods, wealth, peace, and the like. But just as God often punished the people for their sins with famine, wars, and other calamities, so he punished them with servitude, and subjected them to the nations. Therefore, just as they sometimes lost their fortunes, so they lost their liberty. Nor did the Jews act rightly if ever, relying on the promise of liberty, they rebelled against the nations to whom they were in servitude. For God had not commanded them to be free, just as he had not commanded them to be in sound health, for the promised liberty was like good health. We, however, ought to obey the commandments, not seize the promises against the commandment. Wherefore, since it is a commandment to obey the authorities, the Jews erred as often as they took up arms against the kings to whom they were in servitude for the sake of recovering liberty. And so Christ both obeyed the Romans, and John and Christ taught the Jews to obey, even though the common people dreamed that Christ had come to restore liberty and the kingdom to the people. We Christians, however, have absolutely no promise of civil liberty, but some use servitude, others use liberty, for there are other republics constituted otherwise. Therefore, whatever the form of the republic may be, we ought to tolerate it, and obey the present laws and ordinances. especially since a change of the republic and of the empire brings with it nothing but evil, as daily examples teach. And Xenophon says excellently, πασαι μεταβολαὶ πολιτειων θανατηφοροι. I have spoken above concerning laws, that it can be understood from the nature of Christian liberty why it is permitted for us to use the laws of the Heathens, wherefore in this place the reason for this matter and the testimonies must be gathered.

If anyone should ask why, when the law has been abrogated, the Decalogue is nevertheless taught, while the remaining parts are omitted. The ancients count three parts of the law, and we shall follow this as well. For I would wish that the memory of that partition be retained in the schools, because it brings no small amount of light in teaching. There are, therefore, threefold precepts; some are called ceremonial, others judicial, others moral. It is certain, however, that the ceremonial and judicial are not required of Christians. For they were delivered to the Jews in such a way that they would last for a time, as it is written in the Epistle to the Hebrews, Chap. 9. where he says. They were imposed until the time of correction. For because God wished there to exist some people to whom He might deliver the promise concerning Christ, and in which people Christ might be born, which people might bear witness to the promises, therefore God separated that people from the remaining nations by certain ceremonies, institutions, and a kingdom peculiar, nor are those works prescribed so that they might merit righteousness, or so that they might justify, but so that they might distinguish the Israelites from the remaining nations. However, after Christ appeared, there is no need now for that people to be distinguished from the nations. Thus Jacob prophesied in Genesis. The scepter shall not be taken away from Judah, until Shiloh comes. For thus he calls Christ, as if he were saying, when Christ has come, the corporal kingdom, civil institutions, and ceremonies will cease. Wherefore it is not necessary for Christians to observe the ceremonies or the judicial institutions of Moses. The same is taught in chapter xv. in Acts which forbids imposing the law of Moses upon the nations. Therefore, just as circumcision and the custom of slaughtering victims is not to be required of us, so too it ought not to be commanded that we follow the judicial laws of Moses, for those laws were given to that one people only, and indeed so that they might use them for a time, and although they are good, nevertheless they were more accommodated to that republic than they seem to be to ours. Wherefore some do not judge rightly, who command Magistrates that they should follow the law of Moses concerning punishing theft, concerning tithes, and similar matters. Among us it is better to use our own and received laws. For they were not established without the great counsel of the ancients, and there is no cause why they should be changed; furthermore, they cannot be changed without great public disturbance. cannot be easily changed. Wherefore the wisest writers who have been engaged in public affairs often warn that we should retain ancient laws with the greatest zeal. Demosthenes, in support of this opinion, recounts a certain Locrian law, by which it was established that if anyone were to propose a new law in the state, he should recite it to the people with his neck inserted into a noose, so that if the law displeased them, the author would be strangled immediately by the tightening of the noose. This law ensured that for two hundred years no new law was proposed, since no one wished to risk the judgment of the people, which is most uncertain. Infinite precepts pertaining to this matter are found in the most serious writers, but I prefer in this place to use the sentences of Scripture. Solomon commands the same in Ecclesiastes, where he has prefaced his remarks about a foolish prince; for he calls him a fool, not one whose foolishness the common people can judge, but him who seems very wise to himself and marvelously favors his own counsels, abrogates old laws, substitutes new ones, and changes the new ones repeatedly. Such is almost the πολῦπραγμοσυνη, in young men when they are applied to public business. Solomon censures such a prince, and concerning the changing of laws he says: He who breaks through a hedge, a serpent shall bite him, that is, when the state of the republic is dissipated, the authority of all laws is weakened, from which fact great disturbances arise, and very much of evil returns to the prince himself, who changed the laws, And he who moves stones, shall be afflicted by them. For stones signify the weights & measures of things, by which Solomon sometimes designated public ordinances. He teaches, therefore, that many inconveniences follow the changing of public laws. And to this opinion is accommodated the precept, Let not the boundaries set by ancestors be moved. But we have spoken above also about this matter. Truly, at this time, perhaps it is useful to dispute diligently about retaining old laws, since there are so many who undermine their authority with foolish and seditious speeches. Now it remains that we speak about the Decalogue. Perhaps, however, someone might dispute subtly, that the Decalogue does not pertain to us, insofar as it is a part of the political ordinances which were delivered to the people of Moses. I am not delighted by that useless subtlety, but I will enter by three ways. And it is, as the Greeks say, θαρραλεατα τη ἥ συνηθεστατη ὁδος When the Gospel teaches the justice of the heart, it does not require these parts of the law, which contain external observations, namely ceremonies, & judicials. For they are the institutions of one people only, for the use of corporal things, not useful for eternal life. But the Decalogue does not contain the institutions of one people, but while it commands concerning the purity of the heart, Thou shalt not covet Then it teaches works which nature has likewise taught all nations. Wherefore, as long as common sense remains in us, it cannot be understood that the Decalogue has been abrogated. For the Law of nature remains, as the sense of the ears and of the eyes, and as the Gospel does not take away the sense of the eyes or other members of man, so it does not take away the natural judgment concerning morals. Imagine that the laws have been deleted from all books. Honor your parents. Do not kill, etc. Yet they cannot be deleted from the mind of man. The Decalogue is retained, however, as the sum of the law of nature for three reasons. First, on account of the unjust, so that they may be restrained by discipline and by the threat of punishments, just as Paul says. The law is laid down for the unjust. Second, on account of the preaching of repentance, because in the preaching of repentance it is necessary that sins be reproved. For that purpose the Law is useful. Just as Paul says. Through the law is the knowledge of sin, that is, by the law consciences are accused and terrified, so that they may seek the benefit of the Gospel. Just as Paul also says elsewhere. The law is a schoolmaster, and indeed he adds, unto Christ. For both that discipline and the diligence of restraining the unjust is necessary for this reason, that men may be able to be taught and accustomed to knowing the Gospel. Then also this By name the Law is a pedagogue, because it terrifies consciences, so that they may seek Christ. The third cause for retaining the decalogue is that obedience may be required. For although, as I have said, our weakness is pardoned to us, because we do not satisfy the law and meanwhile we are pronounced righteous on account of Christ, nevertheless obedience is required, just as Christ says. If you wish to enter into life, keep the commandments. Likewise, unless your righteousness shall abound more than the righteousness of the Pharisees, etc. Christ also commands love, which is as it were a certain sum of the decalogue. For the decalogue is not retained because it was delivered to Moses and the Israelites, but because it is the divine Law written in the very minds of men teaching that God must be obeyed. The Gospel, however, requires obedience toward God.

The fourth degree of Christian liberty pertains to human traditions. For just as Mosaic ceremonies do not profit for justification, so human traditions profit much less for justification. Wherefore, just as it is permitted to omit Mosaic ceremonies, so it is permitted to omit human traditions if they are demanded for the sake of meriting grace or righteousness. Thus he is deceived who chooses for himself privately some work to seek grace, as if someone were to prescribe for himself certain fasts or betake himself into the desert, that he may merit the remission of sins through those works. For Paul condemns ἐθελοθρησκειας,; thus they are deceived who either establish, or demand, or observe traditions, so that they may merit the remission of sins through them. And yet it happens by nature that a fearful conscience desires to seek grace through certain works. There, when it is necessary to retain the righteousness of faith and to depend upon Christ alone, we experience how difficult a thing that is to do. Therefore, those who observe traditions ought to feel that they are not necessary for righteousness; the Canons teach that such ordinances can be made obsolete by custom. Just as long ago the Canons of penance and most fasts fell into disuse. Since this is so, it appears that the fathers did not wish to cast a snare upon consciences, but that traditions should be observed like the public custom of dress or similar civil matters, not because men sin if they do otherwise. And Scripture teaches that the use of such things is free. Wherefore it is not permitted to demand them as necessary, nor is it proper to perform them with that opinion. Therefore Paul says to the Galatians: Do not be subjected again to the yoke of slavery. Likewise: If you have died with Christ, why are you vexed as though living by traditions? Furthermore, in violating traditions, account must be taken of scandal, lest we offend those who do not the doctrine of liberty. they know, lest the unskilled think that a license to sin has been given to them by our example. For many, when they see some tradition violated, put on a contempt for all laws, just as Christ says, Old wineskins are broken, with new wine poured in. The apostles violated the traditions among the Pharisees, so that they might show a testimony of doctrine against the Pharisees, who demanded them as if they were useful for meriting grace, or necessary. But the same apostles also kept the law although they lived among the Pharisees, so that they might spare the weak multitude. And I would most desire at this time that public ceremonies be retained in the temples, insofar as they can be retained without sin, for no thing offends the unskilled multitude more than that novelty in the temples. Paul commands that all things be done in order and with decorum, where the Church gathers, but that change allows nothing to be done in order. For some, out of hatred for novelty, desert the temples, others cause a tumult with an inept zeal for novelty. Wherefore it seems better to me that the old rites which can be retained without sin be preserved, so that there may be greater tranquility when sacred things are done. And what does it pertain to change those things which have no vice? Especially when Paul also warns, that those called in circumcision should not seek foreskins, nor those called in uncircumcision, be circumcised.

Latin:

Veterem hominem &c.

Novus homo dicitur, non qui ratione tantum se cohercet, nam ratio ad veterem hominem pertinet. Adfert enim homo secum rationem, cum nascitur. Sed novus homo dicitur, qui per Spiritum sanctum renovatur, et in quo Spiritus sanctus timorem Dei, fidem, castitatem, charitatem & similes bonos fructus, efficit Ioan. 1. Ex Deo natisunt. Et. 3.

Nisi quis renatus fuerit ex aqua & spiritu. Et hic ita definivit novum hominem esse, qui renovetur ad cognitionem, iuxta imaginem eius qui condidit eum, id est, novus homo est, qui crescit cognitione Dei, hoc est, qui vere sentit Deum respicere nos, et irasci peccantibus, & propterea timet Deum, sentit et vere Deum ignoscere propter Christum, his qui agunt paenitentiam. Et sentit prope adesse Deum, iuvare & regere sanctos. Qui sic de Deo sentit, is vere novit eum. Nec est nosse Deum, multa subtiliter cogitare de natura eius, Sed voluntatem erga nos nosse, iram adversus peccatum, & misericordiam erga timentes eum, ea est vera Dei cognitio. Sed quid est, quod ait iuxta imaginem Dei. Quidam imaginem Dei in ratione posuerunt, & in veteri homine, quod sit quaedam Trinitatis effigies in nostra mente, quia memoria gignat intellectionem. Memoriam atque intellectionem comitetur velle, ut Pater filium gignit. Vocatur enim ideo filius Verbum, quia sicut in cogitando rei de qua cogitamus, simulachrum concipimus, ita substantialis imago, patris filius est, sic in Epistola ad Haebre. scriptum est, Character substantiae eius. Porro spiritui sancto tribuitur agitatio & varietas donorum. Ideo voluntati Spiritus sanctus comparatur. Sic imaginem Trinitatis in humana mente esse scripserunt. Hanc ego cogitationem non reprehendo. Est enim cernere vestigia divinitatis impressa multis creaturis. Paulus autem de alia quadam imagine loquitur, quae novi hominis tantum est. Formae Dei sunt sapientia, iusticia, veritas, sanctitas, has cum imprimit Deus nobis, sumus imago Dei, & participes divinae naturae. Sic loquitur ad Ephe. iiii. Induite novum hominem qui secundum Deum creatus est, id est, ad similitudinem Dei, in iusticia, & sanctitate, & veritate, id est, est iustus et Deo placens, est sanctus, id est, purus, seu purificato corde, mortificata carne, est verax, id est, de Deo recte & vere sentit. Caro enim nequaquam vere sentit, semper enim somniat, neque crudelitatem Dei tantam esse, ut tam severe puniat peccantes, neque rursus misericordiam tantam esse, ut ignoscat, ut iuvet &c.

Sic loquitur etiam, ii. Corinth. iii. Nos revelata facile speculantes gloriam Domini, in eandem imaginem transformamur a claritate in claritatem, tanquam a domino spiritu, id est, quando vere cor concipit consolationem, per fidem erigitur, ut intueatur magnitudinem misericordiae & bonitatis divinae, Hoc est enim videre gloriam Dei. Ibi cor concipit Dei cognitionem, quae est sapientia & iusticia spiritualis, ea est imago Dei, Quod autem Paulus ait, revelata facie, alludit ad Moisi historiam. Caro enim velatam faciem videns, videt tantum iusticiam mundi, & carnalem, cum audit verbum Dei, videt tantum poenas corporales & beneficia Dei corporalia, sicut populus Mosi videbat praesens exitium Pharaonis. Item praesens beneficium, cedentes aquas & viam facientes. Sed novus homo, retecta facie, videt iram Dei adversus peccatum, videt magnitudinem misericordiae, & credit sibi ignosci, & concipit pacem & gaudium coram Deo. Sic est et hoc loco accipiendus Paulus, cum novum hominem definit, crescentem in cognitione Dei, quae agnitio Dei efficiat eum imaginem Dei. Est enim illa cognitio tanquam lumen quoddam, in quo, velut in speculo resplendet et cernitur Deus. Huc etiam pertinet, quod in Genesi scriptum est, Faciamus hominem ad imaginem & similitudinem nostram, id est, sapientem, & iustum. Talem enim fecit Adam, & ei verbum suum tradidit. Sed similitudinem Dei non retinuit Adam. Est itaque reddita nobis per Christum, qui vera & naturalis imago Dei est & propter quem Deus alios homines vocavit imaginem suam. Nam & propter Christum alii iusticiam, veritatem, & sanctitatem consequuntur. Hortatur itaque Paulus hoc loco, ut crescamus in cognitione Dei, hoc est, ut paenitentia in nobis & timor crescant. Item, ut fides crescat. Item, ut in tribulatione invocemus Deum, & discamus eum prope adesse & iuvare invocantes se. Sicut & ad Roma. docet, Tribulatio pacientiam, pacientia probationem, probatio spem efficit, spes autem non confundit. Ea spes, quia Deum novit, & expectat, & vel in gravissimis adflictionibus intuetur, est ipsa Dei Imago, & hanc spem adferentes adimus retecta facie Deum, & non confundimur, sicut Moises retecta facie ad dominum ingrediebatur.

Ubi non est Graecus neque Iudaeus &c.

Hic locus continet sententiam de libertate Christiana, Nam ut Romana vel Attica politia non est illa iusticia, de qua concionatur Evangelium, ita nec politia Iudaica, vel monastica est iusticia Evangelii. Ut igitur planius intelligi possit, quid sit libertas Christiana, constituamus gradus quatuor. Primus est, quod gratis nobis remittuntur peccata propter Christum, non propter impletionem legis.

Hic praecipuus gradus est & nihil ad vitam exteriorem ac politicam, sed tantum ad certamen conscientiae pertinet luctantis cum iudicio Dei. In eo agone necesse est tenere hunc gradum libertatis. Nulla enim potest perterrefactis conscientiis proponi firma consolatio, praeter hanc doctrinam de hoc gradu libertatis, Et de hoc Christus concionatur cum ait. Si vos filius liberaverit, vere liberi eritis. Ad hunc gradum pertinet & hoc beneficium, quod Christus dat spiritum sanctum, qui nos gubernet ac iuvet adversus diabolum. Ita primus gradus docet nos liberari sic a lege, hoc est, a condemnatione legis, ut liberemur a peccato, morte & potestate diaboli.

Secundus gradus. Et si manet decalogus, quod ad obedientiam attinet, necesse et enim ut saepe iam diximus, novam vitam esse quandam obedientiam erga Deum, tamen hic rursus sciendum est nos hanc habere libertatem, quod ea obedientia placeat, non quia legi satisfaciamus, procul enim absumus a perfectione legis, sed quia persona placet reconciliata fiducia misericordiae promissae propter Christum. Sic igitur liberati sumus a lege non quod ad obedientiam attinet, Sed quod alia res proposita est ad reconciliationem, seu iustificationem, quodque haec obedientia placet etiamsi procul absit a perfectione legis. Id supra docuit Paulus cum ait. In Christo consummati estis. Et ad Romanos. Iam non estis sub lege, sed sub gratia. Et ad Galat. Christus vos redemit a maledictione legis, factus pro vobis maledictum. Hoc est, ipse est factus victima pro nobis, ut Lex non maledicat, non accuset, non damnet eos, qui iam sunt reconciliati fiducia Christi, etiamsi adhuc haeret in eis infirmitas naturae, nec legi perfecte satisfaciant. Ita a decalogo liberati sunt, quod ad condemnationem attinet, hi qui renati sunt per Christum. Et tamen obedientia requiritur, quia certe nova vita debet esse obedientia erga Deum. Est autem magna consolatio, quod conscientia in hac imbecillitate tamen scit nos placere Deo, ut saepe iam dictum est.

Tertius autem gradus libertatis est, quod Christiani non sunt alligati ad ceremonias, & praecepta iudicialia, ut vocant, legis Mosaicae, Sed omnium gentium legitimis politiis uti possunt, Christianus potest res tenere emere, vendere, potest exercere iudicia, iuxta eius gentis leges, apud quam vivit, sive sint Romanae, sive Saxonicae, sive Gallicae leges. Et, ut supra dixi, Christiani praesentibus legibus atque ordinationibus semper debentparere. Si cut et Christus parvit Romanis, dedit διδραχμον. In lege promissa erat libertas civilis filiis Israel, sicut alia bona corporalia, opulentia, pax, & similia Sed sicut fame, bellis, & aliis calamitatibus, saepe populum propter peccata punivit Deus, ita punivit servitute, & subiecit eos gentibus, Sicut igitur fortunas suas, ita libertatem aliquando amiserunt. Nec recte fecerunt Iudaei, siquando freti promissione libertatis rebellarunt gentibus, quibus serviebant. Non enim praeceperat eis Deus, ut liberi essent, sicut neque praeceperat, ut incolumi valetudine essent, promissa enim libertas erat sicut bona valitudo. Nos autem praeceptis obtemperare debemus non occupare promissa contra praeceptum. Quare cum praeceptum sit obedire potestatibus, erraverunt Iudaei, quoties recuperandae libertatis caussa, arma ceperunt adversus reges quibus serviebant. Itaque & Christus parebat Romanis, & parere Iudaeos docuerunt Iohannes, & Christus, tametsi vulgus somniaret venisse Christum ut restitueret populo libertatem, ac regnum. Nos vero Christiani prorsus nullam habemus promissionem civilis libertatis, sed alii servitute, alii libertate utuntur, Sunt enim aliae respublicae aliter constitutae. Proinde qualiscunque est reipublicae forma, debemus eam tollerare, & praesentibus legibus, atque ordinationibus obedire praesertim cum nihil mali non adferat mutatio reipublicae, atque imperii, ut exempla quotidiana docent. Et Xenophon praeclare inquit, πασαι μεταβολαὶ πολιτειων θανατηφοροι. Dixi supra de legibus, ex natura libertatis Christianae intelligi posse, cur nobis liceat uti Ethnicorum legibus, Quare hoc in loco ratio huius rei, ac testimonia colligenda sunt.

Si quis quaerat, cur abrogata lege, cum reliquae partes omittuntur, Decalogus tamen doceatur. Veteres numerant tres partes legis, id & nos sequemur. Optarim enim partitionis illius memoriam retineri in scholis, quia non nihil adfert in docendo lucis. Sunt itaque triplicia praecepta, Alia vocantur ceremonialia, alia iudicialia, alia moralia. Constat autem ceremonialia & iudicialia non exigi a Christianis. Sic enim erant tradita Iudaeis, ut ad tempus durarent, sicut est in Epistola ad Hebraeos scriptum Cap. 9. ubi dicit. Usque ad tempus correctionis imposita esse. Quia enim voluit Deus existere aliquem populum cui traderet promissionem de Christo, & in quo populo nasceretur Christus, qui populus de promissionibus testimonium praeberet, ideo separavit Deus illum populum a reliquis gentibus certis ceremoniis, institutis, ac regno pe culiari, Nec sunt opera illa praescripta, ut iusticiam mererentur, vel ut iustificarent, Sed ut discernerent Israelitas a reliquis gentibus. Postquam autem adparuit Christus, nihil est opus iam eum populum a gentibus discerni. Sic vaticinatus est Iacob in Genesi. Non auferetur sceptrum a Iuda, donec venerit Silo. Sic enim Christum vocat, quasi dicat, cum Christus venerit, regnum corporale, instituta civilia & ceremoniae cessabunt. Quare non est necessarium Christianis, servare ceremonias aut instituta iudiciorum Moisi. Idem docet caput xv. in Actis quod vetat gentibus imponere legem Moisi, Sicut itaque circumcisio, & consuetudo victimarum mactandarum non est a nobis exigenda, Ita neque praecipi debet, ut sequamur leges iudiciales Moisi, Nam illae leges uni tantum populo illi datae sunt, & quidem ut eis ad tempus uteretur, Et quanquam bonae sint, tamen magis accommodatae fuerunt illi reipublicae, quam videantur esse nostrae. Quare non recte iudicant nonnulli, qui precipiunt Magistratibus ut de furto puniendo, de decimis, & similibus rebus legem Moisi sequantur. Apud nos praestat uti nostris ac receptis legibus. Neque enim sine magno consilio veterum conditae sunt, & nulla est caussa cur mutentur, Ad haec mutari sine magno motu pub lico non possunt. Quare saepe monent scriptores sapientissimi qui in rebuspublicis versati sunt, ut veteres leges summo studio retineamus. Demosthenes in hanc sententiam recenset legem quandam Locrensem, qua constitutum fuit, ut si quis novam in civitate legem laturus esset, collo in laqueum inserto, recitaret eam populo, ut statim si displicuisset Lex, autor laquea constricto strangularetur. Haec Lex fecit, ut annis ducentis nulla nova Lex rogata sit, cum nemo periclitari iudicium populi, quod est incertissimum, vellet, Infinita praecepta ad hanc rem pertinentia in gravissimis scriptoribus inveniuntur, sed ego malo hoc in loco scripturae sententiis uti, Solomon praecipit idem in Ecclesiaste, ubi praefatus est de stulto principe, vocat enim stultum, non cuius stulticiam iudicare vulgus potest, Sed eum qui sibi valde sapere videtur, & suis consiliis mirifice favet, veteres leges abrogat, substi tuit novas, & novas subinde mutat. Talis est fere in iuvenibus πολῦπραγμοσυνη, cum adhibentur ad negocia publica. Talem principem vituperat Salomon, & de legum mutatione inquit. Qui dissipat sepem, mordebit cum coluber, id est, dissipato statu reipublicae legum omnium autoritas labefit Qua ex re magni motus existunt, & plurimum mali redit ad ipsum principem, qui leges mutavit, Et qui transfert lapides, adfligetur in eis. Nam lapides significant pondera & mensuras rerum, quibus Solomon aliquoties publicas ordinationes designavit, Docet itaque multa incommoda sequi mutationem legum publicarum. Et ad hanc sententiam accommodatur praeceptum, Ne transferantur limites positi a maioribus, Sed hac de re supra etiam diximus, Verum hoc tempore fortasse prodest diligenter de retinendis veteribus legibus disputare, cum tam multi sunt, qui earum autoritatem stultis ac sediciosis sermonibus labefaciunt. Nunc restat ut de Decalogo dicamus. Fortasse autem aliquis subtiliter disputet, nec Decalogum ad nos pertinere, quatenus pars sit politicarum ordinationum quae sunt traditae populo Moysi. Ego non delector illa inutili subtilitate, sed tria via ingrediar. Et est, ut graeci dicunt, θαρραλεατα τη ἥ συνηθεστατη ὁδος Cum Evangelium docet iusticiam cordis, non requirit has legis partes, quae continent externas observationes, videlicet ceremonias, & iudicialia. Sunt enim unius populi tantum instituta, ad usum rerum corporalium, non ad vitam aeternam utilia. At Decalogus non unius populi instituta continet, sed dum de puritate cordis praecipit, Non concupisces tum opera docet quae natura pariter omnes gentes docuit. Quare donec in nobis communis sensus manet, non potest intelligi Decalogus esse abrogatus. Manet enim Lex naturae, ut sensus aurium atque oculorum, Et ut Evangelium non tollit oculorum sensum aut alia hominis membra, ita naturale iudicium de moribus non tollit. Finge deletas esse leges ex omnibus libris. Honora parentes. Non occidas &c. tamen ex mente hominis deleri non possunt. Retinetur autem Decalogus, tanquam summa legis naturae propter tres causas. Primum propter iniustos, ut coherceantur disciplina, & comminatione poenarum, sicut Paulus inquit. Lex est iniustis posita. Secundo propter praedicationem paenitentiae, quia in praedicatione paenitentiae necesse est argui peccata. Ad eam rem prodest Lex. sicut Paulus dicit. Per legem cognitio peccati, hoc est, lege accusantur & perterrefiunt conscientiae, ut Evangelii beneficium quaerant. Sicut & alibi ait Paulus. Lex est paedagogus, & quidem addit in Christum. Nam & disciplina illa & diligentia cohercendi iniustos, ideo necessaria est, ut doceri homines possint, & assuefieri ad cognoscendum Evangelium. Deinde & hoc nomine Lex paedagogus est, quia perterrefacit conscientias, ut Christum quaerant. Tertia caussa est retinendi decalogi, ut requiratur obedientia. Etsi enim ut dixi condonatur nobis imbecillitas nostra, quod legi non satisfacimus & interim iusti pronunciamur propter Christum, tamen obedientia requiritur, sicut Christus ait. Si vis in vitam ingredi, serva mandata. Item nisi abundaverit iusticia vestra supra iusticiam Phariseorum &c. Praecipit & Christus dilectionem. quae est quasi summa quaedam decalogi. Nam non ideo retinetur decalogus, quia Moisi traditus est ac Israelitis, sed quia Lex est divina scripta in ipsis hominum mentibus docens obediendum esse Deo. Requirit autem Evangelium obedientiam erga Deum.

Quartus gradus Christianae libertatis, pertinet ad traditiones humanas, Sicut enim ceremoniae Mosaicae non prosunt ad iustificationem, ita multo minus prosunt ad iustificationem traditiones humanae. Quare ut ceremonias Mosaicas licet omittere, ita licet omittere traditiones humanas si exigantur ad promerendam gratiam, aut iusticiam. Ut fallitur qui sibi privatim opus aliquod deligit, ad quaerendam gratiam, ut si quis sibi certa ieiunia praescribat, Si se in Eremum conferat, ut per ea opera remissionem peccatorum mereatur. Damnat enim Paulus ἐθελοθρησκειας, ita falluntur qui aut condunt, aut exigunt, aut servant traditiones, ut per eas remissionem peccatorum mereantur. Et tamen natura fit, ut pavida conscientia per certa opera gratiam quaerere cupiat. Ibi cum oporteat retinere fidei iusticiam & pendere de solo Christo, experimur quam id difficile factu sit. Proinde qui observant traditiones, sentire debent, eas non esse ad iusticiam necessarias, Canones docent antiquari posse consuetudine huiusmodi ordinationes. Sicut iam olim exoleverunt Canones paenitentiae, & pleraque ieiunia. Quod cum ita sit, adperet patres non voluisse laqueum iniicere conscientiis, sed servari traditiones, sicut publicum morem vestitus aut similium rerum civilium, non quia peccent homines, si secus faciant. Et scriptura docet usum talium rerum liberum esse, Quare nec exigere licet tanquam necessarium, nec ea opinione praestare oportet. Ideo Paulus ait ad Galatas. Nolite iterum iugo servitutis subiici. Item, Si mortui estis cum Christo, quid tanquam viventes traditionibus vexamini? Caeterum in violandis traditionibus habenda est ratio scandali, ne offendamus eos qui doctrinam libertatis non norunt, ne ve putent imperiti licentiam peccandi nostro exemplo sibi datum esse. Nam multi cum vident violari aliquam traditionem, induunt contempcum omnium legum, sicut ait Christus, Vires veteres rumpi, musto infuso. Apostoli violarunt traditiones apud Phariseos, ut testimonium ostenderent doctrinae adversus Phariseos, qui exigebant tanquam ad gratiam promerendam utiles, aut necessarias. At iidem apostoli servarunt etiam legem quanquam inter Phariseos viverent, ut parcerent infirmae multitudini. Ac maxime optarim hoc tempore publicas ceremonias in templis retineri, quatenus sine peccato retineri possunt, Nulla enim res magis offendit imperitam multitudinem quam illa in templis novitas. Paulus iubet omnia ordine & decore geri, ubi convenit Ecclesia, Verum illa mutatio nihil geri ordine patitur. Alii enim odio novitatis deserunt templa, alii tumultuautur inepto studio novitatis. Quare mihi praestare videtur, ut ritus veteres qui sine peccato retineri possunt, conserventur ut sit tranquillitas maior cum sacra fiunt. Et quid attinet illa mutare quae nihil habent vicii? presertim cum Paulus etiam moneat, ne sibi vocati in circumcisione praeputia accersant, ne ve vocati in preputio, circumcidantur.

## 3:12
The bowels of mercies, etc.

By Hebrew custom, bowels signify what we call from the heart. He commands, therefore, that we be both merciful from the heart, and truly touched by the sense of another's calamity, just as Christ testifies by His tears that He pities Jerusalem. Therefore Isaiah commands, Do not despise your own flesh, chap. 58. For when he calls a neighbor our flesh, he signifies that we ought to be affected by his evils just as by our own, and to put on his affections entirely. Here you see that which I warned above, that the Apostles hand down precepts concerning morals differently than philosophers hand them down. Philosophers require only external duties, but the Apostles require not only civil duties, but also purity of heart, so that we may acknowledge sin clinging deeply in the flesh, and may demand that hearts be purified through the Holy Spirit.

Gentleness.

It is a certain species of χρηστοτης of that virtue which they call ἐπιεικειαν,. It is, however, a certain agreeableness in all conversation, which ensures that we are not harsh, intractable, or morose, but that we may be able to easily forgive the errors of others, and to tolerate the irascible, to interpret their seemingly harsh words with a mild and convenient interpretation, sometimes to yield to the passions of others, not to defend our right violently everywhere, but to yield from our right for the sake of public tranquility. Since this virtue is necessary in every kind of life, it ought to be most especially in those who preside. For just as domestic harmony cannot be maintained unless one dissimulates and bears the faults of another, so neither can public tranquility be safe unless those who preside know how to yield to one another. How great a disturbance arose in the Roman republic while neither Caesar would yield to Pompey, nor Pompey to Caesar. Furthermore, it was fitting that those who teach in the Churches be endowed with this virtue above all, so that one might interpret the words and writings of another conveniently, and even dissimulate if sprinkled with some insult. Now, in such a calumnious interpretation of all words, writings, and deeds, the teachers of Christian charity have flared up against one another with the most bitter hatreds, and other disputes are born one after another. But this mildness does not fall upon proud men, who wish to reign alone, to be wise alone; therefore Paul added.

Latin:

Viscera miserationum &c.

Hebraica consuetudine viscera significant, quod nos dicimus ex animo. Iubet igitur ex animo & misericordes esse, & vere tangi sensu alienae ea lamitatis, Sicut Christus testatur lachrymis suis, se misereri Ierosolymorum. Ideo Esaias precipit, Carnem tuam ne despexeris, cap. 58. Nam cum vocat proximum, carnem nostram, significatnos debere ipsius malis ita adfici, ut nostris, & induere prorsus illius affectus, Hic vides id quod supra monui Apostolos aliter tradere praecepta de moribus quam philosophi tradunt. Philosophi requirunt tantum externa officia, sed Apostoli non modo civilia officia, sed etiam puritatem cordis requirunt, ut agnoscamus peccatum penitus haerens in carne, & postulemus, ut per spiritum sanctum corda purificentur.

Lenitatem.

Species est quaedam χρηστοτης eius virtui is, quam vocant ἐπιεικειαν, Est autem commoditas quaedam, in omni conversatione, quae facit, ne simus asperi, intractabiles aut morosi, sed ut facile aliorum erratis ignoscere possimus, ac tollerare homines iracundos, illorum dicta in speciem aspera, lenie commoda interpretatione, concedere aliquando aliorum adfectibus, non ubique nostrum ius violenter tueri, de iure cedere tranquillitatis publicae caussa. Haec virtus cum sit necessaria in omni vitae genere, tum maxime in his esse debet, qui praesunt. Ut enim domestica concordia retineri non potest, nisi alter alterius vitia dissimulet ac ferat, ita nec publica tranquillitas incolumis esse potest, nisi hi qui praesunt norint inter se cedere. Quantus motus extitit in republica Romana dum neque Pompeio Caesar, nec Caesari Pompeius cedere vult. Porro in primis hac virtute praeditos esse oportuit eos, qui docent in Ecclesiis, ut alter alterius dicta & scripta commode interpretaretur, dissimularet etiam aspersus aliquo maledicto. Nunc in tam calumniosa interpretatione omnium dictorum, scriptorum, & factorum, & exarserunt inter se acerbissimis odiis doctores Christianae caritatis, & aliae lites ex aliis subinde nascuntur. Sed haec lenitas non cadit in homines superbos, qui soli regnare, soli sapere volunt, ideo Paulus addidit.

## 3:12
Humility.

What true humility is will be able to be understood more easily from a comparison. They are proud, who, when they have certain gifts, trust that by these they will obtain what they wish, and they do not wait for the will of God, and they despise other men before themselves, just as some hope that by money, others by the cunning of their intellect and learning, others by strength and power, they will accomplish whatever they wish. And so they admire themselves, as if they were certain gods, and they do not regard the will of God, nor do they judge that God opposes impious counsels. Therefore, against the commandments of God, they undertake whatever they please with confidence in their own strengths. Such was the pride of Pharaoh, who, considering the magnitude of his own forces, thought that he had victory in his hand when he led an army to oppress the children of Israel. On the contrary, however, the humble acknowledge that they themselves also have certain gifts. For David did not not know that he held a kingdom, Paul was not ignorant of how many gifts he was endowed with, but because they feel that these are benefits of God, they therefore know these gifts to be vain and useless unless God makes them fortunate. Nor do they look at their own strengths, but they acknowledge that unless they are helped by God, they can do nothing. They also know that we are deserving of the wrath of God, but because they have known the mercy of God, they raise themselves up and trust in divine aid in managing those things which they undertake. Therefore, they do not rush rashly where the desire for glory or vengeance it seizes, but they undertake only those things which they are compelled to do by divine command, but, as they are timid in undertaking tasks, so they are most brave in carrying them out, because they trust in divine aid, and know that they are governed and protected by God. Whatever strength they have, they contribute not to private display, or to oppressing others, but to public utility, and to helping others. Nor do they despise others in comparison to themselves, but they know that God can effect more good through the small gifts of others, if He wills. They love the gifts of God even in others, and they admire them without envy, they listen to the counsels of others, they yield to those advising well, and they acknowledge their own errors when warned by those teaching more correct things. They know that God resists the proud, and gives grace to the humble, wherefore they do not trust in their own judgment or their own counsels, but they consider the will of God, because they rely only on the favor and aid of God. We have some examples of this virtue in the scriptures, but none more expressive than Christ Himself, who, although He is the Son of God, nevertheless cast Himself below all men. And this image He Himself commanded us to contemplate and imitate. Learn from me, He says, for I am meek, and humble of heart. Let everyone, however, consider within himself how unlike he is to this image, how great is the pride in human souls, how vehement the desire for glory, how many tragedies because of am sometimes excite men to ambition. But since we cannot understand the magnitude of this disease, For the human heart is perverse and inscrutable, it must be requested that the Holy Spirit show us to ourselves, and effect in us true humility.

Meekness.

This is opposed to the desire for vengeance, as appears in the Gospel. Blessed are the meek, for they shall possess the earth. For men burn with anger, hatreds, and the desire for vengeance, and being thus affected they fight impiously for their power, for their wealth, and they do this not only as men approve, but most especially those who preside over the Churches. Christ on the contrary says that those possess the earth whose mind is free from the desire for vengeance. Vengeance is mine, says the Lord, and I will repay. But if it is understood that to take vengeance is the proper work of God, it can be understood how grave a sin it is to occupy the office of God. Here, however, the reader must again be warned that private vengeance is prohibited, for public vengeance, which is exercised through Magistrates, happens by divine command, wherefore it is itself also the work of God. For Paul calls the Magistrate a minister and avenger of God, therefore let us always understand that public vengeance, which the Magistrate exercises, is not prohibited, but private vengeance and anger are prohibited. Moreover, private vengeance is also to be called such when Magistrates, inflamed by a desire for vengeance, indulge their own passions and exercise vengeance contrary to the law.

Long-suffering.

Long-suffering signifies patience in the Hebrew custom. However, Philosophical patience must here be distinguished from Christian. For Philosophers also handed down many things concerning this virtue. But how we ought to feel about afflictions, and what the will of God is toward those who are afflicted, reason or Philosophy cannot hand down. Wherefore the Word of God must be consulted concerning this matter; Philosophical patience sometimes has as its cause hope, which in so many turns of human affairs expects some event that might drive away calamity. Reason attributes nothing here to the counsel or will of God. Therefore the poet says.

Many things the varied labor of changing time has brought back for the better.

Sometimes philosophical patience, with hope cast aside, has only the dignity of virtue as its cause.
