For since external things are to be placed after virtue, philosophy judges that patience must be retained in such a way that we do nothing against justice or other virtues. It is not, therefore, fitting that we, being angry, should avenge our pain and, contrary to right, harm either ourselves or others. These are truly the causes of philosophical patience.

But Christian patience has far different causes. For first, it must be felt in afflictions that we are afflicted by the certain counsel of God, as it is written. When we are judged, we are chastened by the Lord. For Paul teaches that calamity does not happen by chance, but that we are chastened by God. And the Prophet Jeremiah Threnorum 3. rebukes the impious who deny that afflictions happen by the counsel of God, for thus he says. Who is he that hath said, these things to be done, the Lord not commanding? Shall not both evil and good proceed out of the mouth of the most High? He rebukes the same Sophonias cap, 1. I will visit upon the men that are settled on their lees, that say in their hearts. The Lord will not do good, and will not do evil. And since this is the first doubt of reason, whether such occurrences are governed by God, or whether they happen rashly, hearts must be diligently fortified, so that they may be able to establish.

by the will of God we are afflicted. Moreover, the sentences which are cited concerning creation serve this purpose, as Matt. 10 Are not two sparrows sold for a farthing, and one of them shall not fall upon the ground without your Father.

Secondly, it is not enough to feel that we are afflicted by the counsel and will of God, but it behooves us to consider also the cause of the divine will. For reason judges that we are afflicted in order that we may be destroyed, and the examples of those who, having been afflicted, were cast off by God move us, such as Pharaoh, Saul, and the like. But it behooves us to depart from reason and such examples, and to adhere to the Word. Thus, however, the Word of God teaches that we are afflicted in order that we may be saved. Therefore, afflictions are not signs of wrath, but of grace, for God calls to repentance, and by affliction He as it were speaks and testifies that He wishes to forgive. Not otherwise than Baptism, or the eating of the body of the Lord, testifies to us that we are forgiven, so affliction can almost be called a certain Sacrament, because it signifies that God wishes to forgive us. Why, however, does it signify that? Because just as other Sacraments comprise the Word and the Element, so to affliction is added the Word by which grace is promised. For thus says Paul: When we are judged by the Lord we are chastened, that we may not be condemned with this world, affliction calls to repentance, lest we perish. And, The son whom he loves he corrects. Likewise, It is good for me that you have humbled me, that I might learn your justifications, And Isaiah 26 Lord, in tribulation they have visited you, your discipline was for them so that they might cry out to you in anguish. Likewise 28 Vexation gives understanding. Likewise, That he may do his work, he does a strange work, that is, that he may save, he chastises and scourges, And in the Psalm, A sacrifice to God is a contrite spirit, But a sacrifice is that which pleases God, wherefore since affliction pleases God, it does not signify that those who are afflicted are cast away by God. To this point also pertain these sentences, I do not desire the death of the sinner, but that he be converted and live. God has concluded all under sin. THAT HE MAY HAVE MERCY ON ALL, that is, Therefore he judges us, not that he may destroy, but that he may save, and indeed all, provided they believe that they are forgiven. When you add that grace is promised to all, know that you are also in that number, and do not dispute concerning predestination, whether you are in the number of the elect or not, Certainly you are in the number of those to whom grace is promised, It is necessary therefore that there be faith in affliction. First, that affliction has not happened by chance, but that it is governed by God, then, that God does not wish us to be afflicted so that he may destroy, but that he calls us to repentance, that he may exercise us, that he may save us.

Thirdly, one must approach faith, or if one prefers to call it hope, by which we hope that God will govern the outcome of the affliction. Therefore, God commands that we call upon Him in tribulation, and indeed He calls that invocation a sacrifice, that is, a true and most pleasing worship. For thus He says Psalmo 49. [50.] Call upon me in the day of tribulation. And He adds the promise, and I will deliver you, and you shall honor me. And He prefaced in that Psalm that the victims of cattle and similar works do not please Him, but that He is delighted by this sacrifice, if we call upon Him in tribulation, and believe that He will bring us aid, and once helped, we give thanks, and proclaim the benefit; thus also in the fourth Psalm, He calls hope, by which we expect help in affliction, a sacrifice: sacrifice the sacrifice of justice, and hope in the Lord, as if He were saying that this hope is a sacrifice by which we indeed attribute justice to God, because since He has promised, He will deliver and free us, because He is just and truthful. You see, therefore, how Christian patience always has hope joined to it, but that hope does not look upon creatures, but only upon the word and promise of God, and it retains, as Paul says, hope against hope, that is, when all creatures show no help, yet it hopes that God will bring aid. And even if He has not given those things which we desire, or in the way that we were desiring, yet He will govern the outcome by His wisdom and goodness, so that we do not perish nor are our prayers in vain. The same Paul teaches in that gradation, Tribulation produces patience, where he arrives at the point of teaching that hope ought to be retained in tribulation. And he adds the promise, hope does not confound, Therefore we ought to believe that we are heard by God, that God will bring aid, that we are defended by God. It behooves Christians to know these things concerning afflictions and patience. Many only hand down the precept concerning patience, they do not show the causes which produce patience, and by what it sustains itself. They do not teach how we ought to feel concerning the will of God, they say nothing about faith and hope, these only philosophize, they do not teach the Gospel. I think the most serious consolations are contained in these passages, which I have now shown, if they are diligently weighed, wherefore I have thought they should be recounted here.

Latin:

Humilitatem.

Quae sit vera humilitas intelligi ex collatione facilius poterit. Superbi sunt, qui cum dotes quasdam habeant, confidunt his se ea quae velint consecuturos esse, nec morantur voluntatem Dei, et homines alios prae se contemnunt, ut alii pecunia, alii calliditate ingenii, ac doctrina, alii viribus & potentia sperant se quidquid velint, effecturos esse, Itaque mirantur se ipsi, tanquam deos quosdam, Nec respiciunt voluntatem Dei, nec iudicant Deum impiis consiliis obsistere. Proinde contra mandata Dei, quidquid libet suscipiunt fiducia virium suarum. Talis fuit Pharaonis superbia, qui magnitudinem virium suarum considerans, putavit se victoriam in manu habere, cum exercitum duceret ad opprimendos filios Israel. Contra vero humiles agnoscunt & ipsi quasdam se dotes habere. Neque enim nesciebat David regnum se habere, non ignorabat Paulus quantis donis cumulatus esset, sed quia sentiunt esse beneficia Dei, ideo sciunt haec dona esse irrita ac inutilia, nisi fortunet ea Deus. Nec intuentur suas vires, Sed agnoscunt se nisi iuventur a Deo, nihil posse. Norunt etiam meritos nos esse iram Dei, verum quia misericordiam Dei cognoverunt, erigunt se & confidunt auxilio divino, in iis rebus gerendis, quas suscipiunt. Non igitur ruunt temere, quo gloriae aut vindictae cupiditas rapit, Sed ea tantum quae coguntur agere divino mandato suscipiunt, sed, ut sunt in suscipiendis negociis timidi, ita sunt in gerendis fortissimi, propterea quia confidunt auxilio divino, ac sciunt se a Deo gubernari ac tegi. Quidquid habent virium conferunt non ad privatam ostentationem, aut ad opprimendos alios, sed publicam utilitatem, & adiuvandos alios. Nec contemnunt prae se alios, sed sciunt Deum plus boni efficere posse per aliorum exiguas dotes, si velit. Amant in aliis etiam Dei dona, eaque sine invidia mirantur, audiunt aliorum consilia, cedunt bene monentibus, & rectiora docentibus agnoscunt errata sua moniti. Norunt Deum resistere superbis, ac humilibus dare gratiam, quare non confidunt suo iudicio aut suis consiliis, sed voluntatem Dei considerant, quia tantum Dei favore, & auxilio nituntur. Exempla huius virtutis habemus aliquot in scripturis, sed nullum expressius, quam Christum ipsum, qui cum sit filius Dei, tamen abiecit se infra omnes homines. Et hanc imaginem ipse iussit nos contemplari & imitari. Discite a me, inquit, quia mitis sum, & humilis corde. Reputet autem quisque apud se, quam dissimilis huic imagini sit, quanta sit in humanis animis superbia, quam vehemens gloriae cupiditas, quantas tragoedias propter am bitionem nonnunquam excitent homines, Cum autem huius morbi magnitudinem non possimus intelligere, Pravum enim & inscrutabile est cor humanum, postulandum est, ut Spiritus sanctus ostendat nos nobis, & efficiat in nobis veram humilitatem.

Mansuetudinem.

Haec opponitur cupiditati vindictae, sicut in Evangelio adparet. Beati mites, quoniam possidebunt terram. Nam homines ardent ira, odiis, cupiditate vindictae, & sic affecti impie dimicant de potentia sua, de suis opibus, idque faciunt non modo probant homines, sed vel maxime hi, qui praesunt Ecclesiis. Christus contra inquit, eos possidere terram, quorum animus cupiditate vindictae vacat. Mihi vindictam, inquit Dominus, & ego retribuam. Quod si Dei proprium opus est vindicare intelligi potest, quam grave peccatum sit occupare Dei officium. Hic autem rursus monendus est lector privatam vindictam prohiberi, publica enim vindicta, quae per Magistratus exercetur, sit divino mandato, quare & ipsa Dei opus est. Paulus enim vocat Magistratum, ministrum & vindiceus Dei, semper igitur intelligamus publicam vindi ctam, quam Magistratus excrcent, non prohiberi, Sed prohiberi privatam vindictam, ac iracundiam. Est autem etiam privata vindicta vocanda cum Magistratus cupiditate vindictae accensi, indulgent adfectibus suis & contra ius exercent vindictam.

Longanimitatem.

Longanimitas pacientiam Ebraica consuetudine significat. Est autem hic discernenda Philosophica pacientia a Christiana. Nam & Philosophi de hac virtute multa tradiderunt. Sed quomodo sentire de afflictionibus debeamus, & quae sit voluntas Dei erga eos qui adfliguntur, non potest ratio, aut Philosophia tradere. Quare Verbum Dei de hac re consulendum est, Philosophica pacientia caussam habet aliquando spem quae in tot vicibus rerum humanarum expectat casum aliquem, qui calamitatem depellat. Nihil hic Dei consilio aut voluntati tribuit ratio. Ideo ait poeta.

Multa dies variusque labor mutabilis aevi Retulit in melius.

Nonnunquam pacientia Philosophica spe abiecta, caussam habet tantum virtutis dignitatem, Cum enim res externae sint post virtutem collocandae, iudicat Philosophia pacientiam retinendam esse ita ut nihil contra iusticiam, aut alias virtutes faciamus. Non igitur convenit ut irati dolorem nostrum ulciscamur, & contra ius aut nos ipsos, aut alios laedamus. Haec vere caussae sunt pacientiae Philosophicae.

At Christiana pacientia longe alias caussas habet. Primum enim sentiendum est in adflictionibus quod certo Dei consilio adfligamur, sicut scriptum est. Dum iudicamur, a domino corripimur. Docet enim Paulus non casu accidere calamitatem, sed nos a Deo castigari. Et Propheta Ieremias Threnorum 3. obiurgat impios, qui negant Dei consilio adflictiones accidere, Sic enim inquit. Quis est iste qui dixit, haec fieri domino non iubente. Ex ore altissimi nec bona, nec mala egredientur? Obiurgat eosdem Sophonias cap, 1. Visitabo super viros defixos in fecibus suis, qui dicunt in cordibus suis. Non faciet bene Dominus, & non faciet male. Et cum haec prima rationis dubitatio sit, an istiusmodi casus regantur a Deo, vel utrum temere eveniant, diligenter munienda sunt corda, ut statuere possint.

voluntate Dei nos adfligi. Faciunt autem ad hoc propositum sententiae, quae de creatione citantur, ut Matth. x. Nonne duo passeres asse veneunt, & unus ex illis non cadet super terram sine patre vestro.

Secundo, non satis est sentire, quod Dei consilio & voluntate affligamur, sed oportet nos causam etiam voluntatis divinae considerare. Nam ratio iudicat, ideo nos adfligi, ut perdamur, Et exempla nos commovent eorum qui adflicti, a Deo abiecti sunt, ut Pharaonis, Saul, & similium. Sed oportet nos a ratione & talibus exemplis discedere, & verbo aehaerere. Sic autem docet Verbum Dei, quod adfligamur, ut salvemur. Proinde adflictiones non sunt irae signa, sed gratiae, vocat enim Deus ad paenitentiam, & adflictione veluti loquitur ac testatur se velle ignoscere. non aliter atque Baptismus, aut manducatio corporis domini, testatur nobis ignosci, ita adflictio propemodum Sacramentum quoddam dici potest, quia significat Deum nobis velle ignoscere. Cur autem id significat? quia sicut alia Sacramenta complectuntur Verbum & Elementum ita adflictioni additum est Verbum quo gratia promittitur. Sic enim ait Paulus. Dum iudicamur a domino corripimur, ne cum hoc mundo damnemur, Vocat ad paenitentiam adflictio, ne percamus. Et, Filium quem diligit corripit. Item, Bonum mihi quia humiliasti me, ut discerem iustificationes tuas, Et Esaiae. 26. Domine in tribulatione visitaverunt te, Disciplina tua eis ut clament ad te in angustia. Item. 28. Vexatio dat intellectum. Item, Ut faciat opus suum, facit opus alienum, id est, ut salvet, castigat & flagellat, Et in Psalmo, Sacrificium Deo spiritus contribulatus, Sacrificium autem est, quod placet Deo, quare adflictio cum placeat Deo, non significat eos, qui adfliguntur, a Deo abiectos esse. Huc pertinent & hae sententiae, Nolo mortem peccatoris, sed ut convertatur & vivat. Conclusit Deus omnes sub peccatum. UT OMNIUM misercatur, id est, Ideo iudicat nos, non ut perdat, sed ut servet, & quidem omnes, modo credant sibi ignosci. Cum omnibus promitti gratiam addis, scias in eo numero te quoque esse, nec de praedestinatione disputes, utrum sis in electorum numero nec ne, Certe in horum numero es, quibus est promissa gratia, Oportet itaque in adflictione fidem esse. Primum, quod non acciderit casu adflictio, sed quod a Deo gubernetur, deinde, quod Deus non adfligi nos ideo velit, ut perdat, sed quod vocet nos ad paenitentiam, quod exerceat nos, ut salvet nos.

Tertio accedere oportet fidem, seu quis malit spem vocare, qua speremus Deum gubernaturum esse exitum adflictionis. Ideo praecipit Deus, ut se invocemus in tribulatione, & quidem vocat eam invocationem sacrificium, hoc est verum & gratissimum cultum. Sic enim inquit Psalmo 49. [50.] Invoca me in die tribulationis. Et addit promissionem & eripiam te, & honorificabis me. Et praefatus est in eo Psalmo. Non placere sibi victimas pecudum, & similia opera, Sed hoc sacrificio, se delectari, si invocemus eum in tribulatione, & credamus eum nobis opem laturum esse, & adiuti agamus gratias, & praedicemus beneficium, sic & Psalmo quarto, Spem, qua expectamus auxilium in adflictione sacrificium vocat, sacrificate sacrificium iusticiae, & sperate in Domino, quasi dicat eam spem sacrificium esse, quo quidem Deo iusticiam tribuamus, quod cum promiserit, sit nos erepturus & liberaturus, quoniam iustus & verax est. Vides igitur quomodo pacientia Christiana semper spem habeat coniunctam, sed ea spes non intuetur creaturas, sed tantum verbum ac promissionem Dei, & retinet, ut inquit Paulus, spem contra spem, id est cum creaturae omnes nihil ostendunt auxilii, tamen sperat Deum opitulaturum esse. Et ut maxime non dederit ea quae nos volumus, aut eo modo ut volebamus, tamen gubernabit exitum sua sapientia ac bonitate, ne pereamus neve sint irritae preces nostrae. Idem Paulus docet in illa gradatione, Tribulatio pacientiam efficit, ubi eo pervenit, ut doceat debere spem retineri in tribulatione. Et addit promissionem, spes non confundit, Credere igitur debemus, nos ex audiri a Deo, Deum opem laturum esse, nos defendi a Deo. Haec Christianos de adflictionibus ac pacientia scire oportet. Multi tantum tradunt praeceptum de pacientia, non ostendunt caussas quae efficiunt pacientiam, & quo se sustentat. Non docent quomodo de voluntate Dei sentire debeamus, nihil de fide, ac spe dicunt, hi tantum Philosophantur, non docent Evangelium. Ego puto gravissimas consolationes in his locis contineri, quos iam ostendi, si diligenter expendantur, quare duxi eos hic recensendos esse.

## 3:14
Above all these things charity, which is the bond of perfection &c.

Above he named the duties of charity, or effects. Now he makes mention of charity, so that he may teach these virtues, namely to tolerate the manners of others, to forgive errata, not to avenge an injury, that duties ought not to be feigned, but to be born from true charity. However, hypocrisy cannot easily be distinguished from truth, but it can be judged that charity exists in those who perpetually perform those duties. For simulation is never long-lasting. The ancient theologians disputed that the charity by which we love God and men is the same. This subtlety has no small amount of utility, if one looks at it diligently, for it teaches the distinction between carnal love and Christian charity; carnal love is excited in us when we have beheld goods in others which we judge to be either for pleasure or for use to us. True charity looks upon the will of God, and when it sees with what great goods God heaps upon us, though we have deserved nothing of the sort, it in turn embraces men freely, and desires to help all, so that it may render some thanks to God for the greatest benefits. However, the most evident signs of true charity seem to me to be if one does not envy those who have better gifts, if they are moved by true pity for another's infirmity. Therefore, here also Paul called charity the bond of perfection, because it joins and connects the Church, and retains concord, by the weakening of which the Church is dissipated. But what evil does discord not bring? Some embrace depraved dogmas, others because they do not see what to follow among so many teachers they cast off religion entirely, and become profane and blasphemous. Corporal evils also approach here, slaughters, and wars, while the impious defend their dogmas by force. For then it clearly happens, which is in the old verse, Wisdom is driven from the midst, the matter is carried on by force. Therefore, perfection in this place is to be understood, not of the perfection of individuals, or, if I may so say, of the perfection of the person, for as charity does not justify the person, so neither does it render them perfect, if we wish to speak properly, for perfect faith is required for the perfection of the person, but Paul wished this passage to be accepted concerning the perfection of the universal Church, in which although there are dissimilar members, yet it itself is called perfect, if the members agree, if the strong yield to the weak, if the weak listen to the strong, if the uncomely members are adorned by the more comely. Concerning this matter, scripture teaches everywhere, wherefore let those who disturb public concord, and who forbid it to be mended once disturbed, know that they will pay most grave penalties to God.

Latin:

Super haec omnia caritatem, quae est vinculum perfectionis &c.

Supra nominavit officia caritatis, seu effectus. Nunc caritatis mentionem facit, ut doceat istas virtutes, scilicet tolerare mores aliorum, ignoscere erratis, non ulcisci iniuriam, non debere simulata officia esse, sed nasci ex vera caritate. Non potest autem facile hypocrisis a veritate discerni, sed existimari potest caritatem esse in his, qui perpetuo prestant illa officia. Nam simulatio nunquam diuturna est. Veteres Theologi disputaverunt caritatem eandem esse qua & Deum, & homines amamus. Haec subtilitas non nihil utilitatis habet, si quis eam diligenter intuetur, Docet enim discrimen carnalis amoris, & caritatis Christianae, carnalis amor excitatur in nobis, cum bona conspeximus in aliis, quae nobis iudicamus aut voluptati, aut usui esse. Vera caritas voluntatem Dei intuetur, & cumvidet quantis bonis nos nihil tale meritos cumulet Deus, vi eissim complectitur homines gratis, et iuvare omnes cupit, ut Deo gratiam aliquam reddat pro maximis beneficiis. Videntur autem mihi verae caritatis evidentissima signa esse, si quis non invideat his qui dona potiora habent, si verae alienae infirmitatis misericordia moventur. Proinde & hic Paulus vocavit caritatem, vinculum perfectionis, quia coniungat, & connectat Ecclesiam, & concordiam retineat, qua labefactata dissipatur Ecclesia. Quid autem mali non adfert discordia? Alii prava dogmata amplec tuntur, alii quia non vident quid sequi in tanta doctorum dissensione debeant, prorsus abiiciunt religionem, & fiunt prpohani ac blasphemi. Accedunt huc & corporalia mala, caedes, ac bella, dum sua dogmata vi defendunt impii, Tunc enim plane fit, quod est in veteri versu, Pellitur e medio sapientia, Vi geritur res. Est itaque intelligenda perfectio hoc in loco, non de singulorum perfectione, seu, ut ita dicam, de personae perfectione, Ut enim non iustificat personam caritas, ita neque perfectos reddit, si proprie loqui velimus, requiritur enim fides perfecta ad personae perfectionem, Sed Paulus voluit accipi hunc locum de perfectione universae Ecclesiae, in qua quanquam dissimilia membra sint, tamen ipsa perfecta dicitur, si membra consentiant, si cedant infirmis firmi, si audiant infirmi firmos, siinhonesta membra ornentur ab honestioribus. Hac de re ubique praecipit scriptura, quare qui publicam concordiam perturbant, quique vetant perturbatam sarciri sciant se Deo gravissimas poenas daturos esse.

## 3:15
Let the peace of God reign in your hearts &c,

We see many, although bearing most impatiently light evils, and complaining concerning their lot, concerning poverty, concerning adverse health, and similar inconveniences. Complaints of this kind proceed from a heart that either feels that our affairs are not a care to God, or is angry with God, and accuses Him of injustice, because He allows it to go well for the wicked and ill for the good, because He deals cruelly with us, and seems almost to take delight in our evils. Examples of such complaints exist in the sacred histories Exodus 17. The Israelites expostulate with Moses because he led them out of Egypt, and they no longer believe that they were led out by divine power, nor do they believe that they are a care to God, a thing which their voice indicated: Is the Lord among us? And Isaiah 8: Do not say a conspiracy, for He forbids this kind of complaint in adverse circumstances, but commands to trust in God, and to request help and aid from Him. In the same sentiment He says also in chapter 30: In silence and hope shall your strength be. Thus Paul in this place commands that peace toward God be retained, that is, to always feel that by the good will of God we have been cast into such a kind of life, that God does not afflict us in order to destroy us, that He will be about to bring aid at some time according to His supreme goodness, even if the help is delayed for a little while. And Paul commands that that peace as it were govern all our struggles, for he has used the Greek word Βραβεύειν which signifies to govern a struggle.

He adds then, And be thankful. For he commands us to give thanks to God, because in so many calamities he nevertheless does not desert us, but defends, covers, and governs us. For unless we were guarded by God, the devil would snatch away each one daily into destruction and into the foulest sins according to his own lust. Now that our life is prolonged for repentance, that other goods are present, that we are restrained from rushing into worse crimes, that we are called to repentance, to omit many other things, are these not inestimable goods, which we ought to confess are the benefits of God? Therefore it is commanded, You shall never appear empty before God, which is what else than that we always use some benefits of God, which we ought to acknowledge, and give thanks to God. For what can we render to God besides the commemoration of his benefits? Nor is a certain voice of Gerson unworthy of memory, that there are more privative thanks than positive, that is, unless we were guarded by God, we would fall into far greater evils, he said that this benefit of God extends more widely than even those very goods which he exhibits besides.

Latin:

Pax Dei regnet in cordibus vestris &c,

Videmus multos, quamvis levia mala impatien tissime ferentes, deque sua sorte, de inopia, de adversa valetudine, & similibus incommodis quaerentes. Eiusmodi querelae proficiscuntur ex corde, quod vel sentit Deo non esse curae nostras res, vel succenset Deo, eumque accusat iniusticiae, quod malis sinat bene esse, bonis male, quod crudeliter nobiscum agat, & videatur propemodum delectari nostris malis. Exempla talium querelarum extantin sacris historiis Exodi. 17. Israelitae expostulant cum Mose, quod eduxerit eos ex Aegypto, nec iam credunt se divinitus eductos esse, nec credunt se Deo curae esse, id quod indicavit vox illorum, Est ne Dominus inter nos. Et Esa. 8. Ne dicatis coniuratio, prohibet enim hoc genus querelas in adversis rebus, sed iubet Deo confidere, & ab eo opem & auxilium postulare. In eandem sententiam inquit & ea. xxx. In silentio & spe erit fortitudo vestra. Sic Paulus hoc loco praecipit retineri pacem erga Deum, hoc est, semper sentire, quod bona voluntate Dei in tale vitae genus coniecti simus, quod Deus non adfligat nos ut perdat, quod sit opem laturus aliquando pro sua summa bonitate, etiam si differatur aliquentisper auxilium. Et iubet Paulus, ut ea pax tanquam gubernet omnia certamina nostra, Est enim verbo graeco usus Βραβεύειν quod significat gubernare certamen.

Addit deinde, Et sitis grati. Iubet enim gratias agere Deo, quod nos in tot calamitatibus tamen non deserat, sed defendat, tegat, & gubernet. Etenim nisi a Deo custodiremur, diabolus abriperet pro sua libidine singulos quotidie in perniciem, & in teterrima peccata. Nunc quod vita nostra prorogatur ad paenitcutiam, quod adsunt alia bona, quod cohibemur, ne ruamus in deteriora flagitia, quod ad paenitentiam vocamur, ut alia multa omittam, nonne haec inaestimabilia bona sunt, quae fateri debemus Dei beneficia esse? Itaque praeceptum est, Nunquam adparebis vacuus coram Deo, quod quid aliud est, quam nos semper aliquibus Dei beneficiis uti, quae agnoscere debemus, & Deo gratias agere. Quid enim possumus Deo reddere praeter commemorationem beneficiorum ipsius. Nec est indigna memoria quaedam Gersonis vox, plures esse gratias privativas, quam positivas, id est, nisi a Deo custodiremur, incideremus in longe maiora mala, id Dei beneficium latius patere dixit, quam vel ea ipsa bona quae praeterea exhibet.

## 3:16
Let the word of God dwell among you abundantly etc.

This commandment is not to be despised, by which frequent treatment of Christian doctrine is commanded, just as Peter also ordered us to be ready to give an account of our faith. And against the snares of Satan there are no other weapons except prayer and the pious and pure treatment of the Word of God. As Paul teaches in the last chapter to the Ephesians, having your feet shod and prepared or instructed with the Gospel of peace, thus, he says, repel the fiery darts of the wicked spirit. But he adds, IN ALL WISDOM, as if he were saying, It is not enough to have and to treat the Word of God, unless you also treat it prudently, as he commanded in another place ὀρθοτομειν τον λογον το ἀληθειας. For unless you have learned the will of the scriptures from the scripture, you will fall into the greatest errors. How far from the meaning of scripture did the authors of the Peasant revolt depart, who, when it spoke of spiritual liberty, twisted it to civil liberty. From this all heresies have been born, because men, either little-sighted or impious, brought their own dreams to the scripture, and did not gather the meaning of the Holy Spirit from the scripture. Therefore Christ also commanded that we should search the scriptures, and Isaiah says, To the law and to the testimony, otherwise there will not be, he says, morning light for them, as if he were saying, Unless they seek the will of God in the scripture, it cannot be but that they fall into the greatest errors and into the greatest outrages. Moreover, Paul commands us to sing in grace, that is, without tumult. without confusion, as he otherwise commands in the church that all things be done decently, or to use his word ἐσχημωνος.

To sing in the heart, and to sing spiritually, is truly both to be terrified and to conceive consolation from the very Word of God, which is heard while it is being treated. For faith is from hearing. Now Paul teaches here what he wishes to be done in the church, concerning which matter he commanded copiously to the Corinthians.

Latin:

Verbum Dei habitet inter vos abun-de &c.

Non est contemnendum hoc praeceptum, quo frequens tractatio doctrinae Christianae praecipitur sicut & Petrus iussit paratos esse ad reddendam, rationem de fide nostra. Et contra Satanae insidias non alia sunt arma, nisi precatio, & pia puraque tractatio Verbi Dei. Sicut docet Paulus ad Ephesios ultimo, Calceati pades, & praeparati seu instructi Evangelio pacis, sic inquit tela ignea pravi spiritus depellite, Sed addit, IN OMNI SAPIENTIA, quasi dicat, Non satis est habere & tractare Verbum Dei, nisi prudenter etiam tractes, ut alio loco praecepit ὀρθοτομειν τον λογον το ἀληθειας. Nisi n. scripturae voluntatem ex scriptura didiceris, in maximos errores incidies. Quam procul a scripturae sententia discesserunt seditionis Rusticaeautores, qui de spirituali libertate loquentem, detorsernut ad civilem libertatem. Inde hereses omnes natae sunt, quod homines vel parum oculati, vel impii sua somnia attulerunt ad scripturam non sententiam spiritus sancti ex scriptura collegerunt. Itaque Christus etiam iussit, ut scrutaremur scripturas, & Esaias ait, Ad legem & testimonium, alioqui non erit, inquit, eis matutina lux, quasi dicat. Nisie scriptura voluntatem Dei requirant, non posse fieri, quin in maximos errores, & in maxima flagitia incidant Iubet autem Paulus canere in gratia id est, sine tumultu, sine confusione, ut alias praecipit in ecclesta omnia decore fieri, seu ut eius verbo utamur ἐσχημωνος.

Canere in corde, & canere spiritualiter, est vere rum perterrefieri, tum concipere consolationem ex ipso Verbo Dei, quod inter tractandum auditur Nam fides ex auditu est. Docet autem hic Paulus, quid fieri velit in ecclesia, qua de re copiose praecepit ad Corin.

## 3:17
In the name of the Lord Jesus etc.

He commands to do all things in the name of the Lord, which they interpret as, that we should do nothing against the commandments of God. For it befits us, as if we were ambassadors, to speak, teach, and act upon what has been entrusted by God. And all things which we do must be referred to the glory of God, that is, we ought to feel and profess that, if we have accomplished anything, it was accomplished by the help of God, not by our own counsels, prudence, or strengths. For that is to render glory to God, to acknowledge what truly helps and governs us, and by our testimony to invite others, so that they themselves may also fear God and believe in God. But besides these, the particle, In the name of the Lord, signifies invocation, so that whatever we carry out we may believe in God to be pleasing, and let us ask that he himself may govern, as it is written. Reveal your way to the Lord and he will act. Likewise, Some in chariots and some in horses, but we will call upon the name of the Lord our God, that is, others trust in their own strengths, we trust in the help of God, and therefore we call upon him. Let us therefore understand Paul when he commands us to do all things in the name of the Lord, to command that we believe that God wishes to govern all the chances of life, all counsels, all businesses, all dangers. Wherefore whatever we undertake, help must be sought from him. Those who teach, those who administer republics, those who wage wars, those who learn, those who plow the earth, those who exercise arts, let them ask for help from God in all their businesses, being admonished by this precept of Paul, Do all things in the name of the Lord.

Latin:

In nomine Domini Iesu &c.

Iubet omnia in nomine Domini facere, quod interpretantur, ne quid faciamus contra mandata Dei Nos enim velut legatos, decet commissa a Deo loqui docere & agere, Et quae agimus, omnia ad gloriam Dei referenda sunt, id est, sentire ac profiteri debemus, si quid effecimus, Dei auxilio effectum esse, non nostris consiliis, prudentia, viribus. Id enim est reddere Deo gloriam, agnoscere quod vere nos iuvet, ac gubernet, & nostro testimonio invitare alios, ut & ipsi Deum timeant, & Deo credant. Sed praeter haec, particula. In nomine Domini, significat invocationem, ut quidquid gerimus credamus Deo placere, & rogemus ut ipse gubernet, sicut scriptum est. Revela domino viam tuam et ipse faciet. Item, Hi in curribus & hi in equis, nos autem in nomine domini Dei nostri invocabimus, id est, alii confidunt viribus suis, nos confidimus auxilio Dei, & propterea invocamus eum Sentiamus igitur Paulum cum iubet nos omnia facere in nomine Domini, praecipere, ut credamus quod velit Deus regere omnes vitae casus, omnia consilia, omnia negocia, omnia pericula, Quare quidquid suscipimus, auxilium ab eo petendum est. Qui docent, qui admistrant respublicas qui gerunt bella, qui discunt, qui arant terram, qui artes exercent, postulent a Deo auxilium in omnibus negociis suis admoniti hoc praecepto Pauli, Omnia facite in nomine domini.

## 4:1 Argument
CHAPTER FOUR.

Latin:

CAPUT QUARTUM.

## 4:2
To persist in prayer, etc.

PRAYER, is to ask something from God, for those who recite prayers in such a way that they ask nothing from God, do not pray. Moreover, Paul taught elsewhere to pray without anger and without hesitation, which we explained above, that we ought not to be angry at God when we are afflicted, for they are angry with God, who judge that He neglects us, or wishes to destroy us. And we must pray without hesitation, that is, we must truly determine and believe that He wishes to hear and help us. But here we experience our weakness, for who is there who dares to affirm that he is heard by God? Thus the human heart flees from God, nor does it dare to expect anything except punishments. And yet, against this weakness, the word of God raises us up in many ways, by precepts and promises, so that we may believe that we are heard.

Therefore, these things are to be considered in prayer. First, the precept of God, which compels one to pray, for thus it is written: Ask and you shall receive. Item, It is necessary to pray always. Therefore, you shall not judge in such a way that you will pray for nothing because you are unworthy to obtain anything, but you shall pray because God commands it; therefore you shall believe that you are heard because He Himself commanded to pray; as it is a sin to do the same, so it is a sin to ask nothing from God. Moreover, this commandment concerning praying pertains to the second commandment of the Decalogue. Would that men might understand how great a consolation is placed in this passage. For certainly God signifies that He loves us wonderfully and desires to help us, since He even commands that we pray.

Secondly, the promises are to be considered, whatever you ask the Father in my name, he will give to you. Everyone who calls upon the name of the Lord will be saved. And since the promise requires faith, we ought to believe that we are heard, even if we do not immediately obtain that which we desire. Therefore, let not our infirmity deter us from daring to ask of God all things of which there is need. And although we have deserved wrath and punishment, yet because he forgives us on account of Christ, let us believe that our prayers are also accepted on account of Christ. For this reason Christ added, in my name, so that they might show us the pledge of the divine promise, for Christ both appeases the Father for us. For he is our high priest, and witness, and as it were a hostage given to us, so that we may perceive that the Father wishes to help us.

Thirdly, faith in prayer is necessary, as has already been said, by which we believe that our prayers are accepted by God, for Christ says: Whatever you ask, believing. Wherefore those who doubt whether they please God, whether they are heard, pray unhappily. But when we most strongly perceive the infirmity of our faith, we ought to follow the example of the Apostles and ask that the Lord increase our faith. Furthermore, faith is exercised when we do not immediately obtain what we ask, wherefore this also must be performed in prayer, lest, having cast away hope, we cease to pray. For even if God has not granted that which we specifically requested, he will nevertheless govern the outcome of our dangers. Therefore Paul commands us to persist here, and Christ says Luke 18, it is necessary to pray always and not to grow weary, and he illustrates that sentiment with an excellent parable.

Latin:

Orationi instare &c.

ORATIO, est aliquid a Deo petere, Nam qui ita recitant preces, ut nihil petant a Deo, non orant. Docuit autem Paulus alias, orare sine ira & sine haesitatione, quod supra enarravimus non debere nos irasci Deo, cum adfligimur, Nam succensent Deo, qui iudicant eum negligere nos, aut velle nos perdere. Et sine haesitatione orandum est, id est vere statuere, & credere debemus quod velit nos exaudire, ac iuvare. Sed hic experimur imbecillitatem nostram, Quis est enim qui ausit adfirmare se exaudiri a Deo? Ita cor humanum fugit Deum, nec audet expectare aliud, nisi paenas. Atqui contra hanc imbecillitatem erigit nos verbum Dei multifariam, praeceptis, & promissionibus, ut credamus nos exaudiri.

Sunt igitur haec in oratione consideranda. Primo, Praeceptum Dei, quod cogit orare, sic enim scriptum est. Petite & accipietis. Item, Oportet semper orare. Non igitur ita iudicabis, ideo nihil te oraturum esse, quia sis indignus qui aliquid impetres, sed ideo orabis, quia Deus praecipit, ideo credes te exaudiri, quia ipse praecepit orare, ut peccatum est eaedem facere, ita peccatum est nihil a Deo petere, Pertinet autem hoc mandatum de orando, ad secundum Decalogi praeceptum, Utinam intelligant homines quanta consolatio in hoc loco posita sit, Certe enim significat Deus se mirabiliter nos amare, & iuvare cupere, cum etiam praecipit, ut oremus.

Secundo, considerandae sunt promissiones, Quidquid petieritis patrem in nomine meo, dabit vobis. Omnis qui invocaverit nomen domini, salvus erit. Et quoniam promissio requirit fidem, credere debemus nos exaudiri, tametsi non statim id quod volumus consequimur. Proinde non absterreat nos infirmitas nostra, quo minus petere a Deo omnia quibus opus est, audeamus, Et quanquam iram ac paenam meriti simus, tamen quia nobis propter Christum ignoscit, credamus etiam propter Christum preces nostras accipi, Ideo enim addidit Christus, in nomine meo, ut ostenderent nobis pignus divinae promissionis, Christus enim & placat nobis patrem. Est enim pontifex noster, & testis, ac velut obses nobis datus est, ut sentiamus patrem velle nobis opitulari.

Tertio, fides in oratione necessaria est, sicut iam dictum est, qua credamus nostras preces accipia Deo, Christus enim ait. Quidquid petieritis credentes, Quare illi qui dubitant, utrum placeant Deo, utrum exaudiantur, infoeliciter orant. Cum autem maxime sentiamus fidei nostrae infirmitatem, sequi Apostolorum exemplum debemus ac rogare, ut Dominus augeat nobis fidem. Porro fides exercetur cum non statim quae petimus consequimur, quare id quoque in oratione praestandum est, ne abiecta spe, desinamus orare. Nam etiam si non dederit Deus id quod nos nominatim petivimus, tamen gubernabit exitum periculorum nostrorum. Ideo Paulus hic instare iubet, Et Christus Lucae. 18. inquit, oportet semper orare & non defatigari, & eam sententiam egregia similitudine illustrat.

## 4:2
In thanksgiving, etc.

Prayer is properly when we ask for something, but we misuse the name, and we call that prayer in which we give thanks to God for a benefit received. Paul has joined prayer and thanksgiving in this place, for we ought both to ask for good things from God, and when we have received them, to proclaim his benefit. For thanksgiving is to acknowledge that those good things which we use are benefits truly having proceeded from God, and to proclaim that among men, so that others, confirmed by our testimony, may learn to fear God, and to believe in him, and to glorify him. Just as David says: I believed, therefore I have spoken, that is, because I believe that I have been helped by God, therefore I proclaim his benefit, and I testify that he forgives us, that he helps us, Psalm 49 [50] he calls this a sacrifice of praise, and teaches that this worship is especially required of us, so that God may become known among all men by our testimony, just as the Prophet promises, if he is redeemed, that he will proclaim the benefit of God, Psalm 34

I will confess to you in the great Church, and in a grave people I will praise you.

Latin:

In gratiarum actione &c.

Oratio est proprie, cum aliquid flagitamus, sed abutimur nomine, ac vocamus orationem, in qua pro accepto beneficio gratias Deo agimus, Coniunxit hoc loco Paulus orationem & gratiarum actionem, Nam & bona petere a Deo debemus, & cum accepimus, praedicare eius beneficium. Est enim gratiarum actio, agnoscere, quod bona illa quibus utimur, beneficia sint vere a Deo profecta, idque praedicare apud homines, ut alii nostro testimonio confirmati discant timere Deum, eique credere, & glorificare eum. Sicut inquit David. Credidi, propter quod locutus sum, id est, quia credo me a Deo adiutum esse. ideo praedico eius beneficium, ac testificor, quod ignoscat nobis, quod iuvet nos, Psalmus. 49. [50.]vocat hoc sacrificium laudis, & docet hunc cultum praecipue a nobis requiri, ut innotescat nostro testimonio Deus apud omnes homines, sicut pollicetur Propheta, si redimatur, se praedicaturum esse beneficium Dei, Psal. 34.

Confitebor tibi in Ecclesia magna, & in populo gravi laudabo te.

## 4:5
Walk in wisdom.

This precept extends most broadly in this common life. But we shall first adapt it to one certain kind. It commands us to take care that our morals are not a scandal to anyone. And that pertains especially to the abuse of liberty, which deters the learned and those tenacious of ancient customs from the Gospel, while they think that by this new kind of doctrine, discipline is undermined, and men are made profane rather than pious. On the other hand, the common people, because by nature they hate the bonds of laws and religion, take for themselves an unbearable license and that under the pretext of Evangelical liberty. For because they see that some traditions have been mitigated, they transfer the example to all laws. They think they are now held by no laws, and clearly like wild beasts released from a prison, they are carried wherever impulse leads each one. They do not keep the faith of covenants, they despise the authority of magistrates, they plunder the resources of the Churches, they guzzle perpetually, finally they loosen the bridle on all desires. Such morals certainly do not lightly harm the Gospel. For the blame is conferred, as is wont to happen, upon the kind of doctrine. But we ought to adorn the Gospel with our morals and invite men by the best examples to know Christ, just as it is commanded. Let your light shine before men, that they may see your good works, and glorify your Father who is in heaven. Therefore, for this reason, it was necessary for us to act more circumspectly in all things, lest the example of any of us should do harm, and lest the Gospel should be ill-spoken of on our account. For we know how horrible a threat has been set forth concerning scandal. Woe to the man through whom scandal comes. And to avoid scandals, a certain great prudence and diligence is required, so that we not only avoid those things which are openly flagitious, but also in matters otherwise lawful, we moderate and curb our affections, and do not permit everything to ourselves, but consider that we must serve this theater, into which we have been brought for the sake of Christ's glory. All things are lawful for me, says Paul, but not all things are expedient. Let us therefore see what is expedient for inviting the souls of men to know the Gospel, by what things the glory of the Gospel may be made more illustrious. Let us not follow the desire for vengeance, let us forgive injuries for the sake of public tranquility, let us not contend with hatred, insults, and scurrility against the enemies of the Gospel, let no sign of ambition stand out, let us flee drunkenness, for many things break forth from the drunk which lead to a judgment about the soul. In common conver let there be in conversation a gracefulness and humanity tempered with gravity. Finally, if actors are ordered to see what is becoming on the stage, it was most fitting for us to take pains both to understand and to observe decorum in this theater, in which we are observed by the eyes of all men, and we risk not only ourselves, but also the poem itself which we represent, that is, the authority of heavenly doctrine. See what kind of philosophy this precept concerning the avoiding of scandals requires. Therefore Paul said most gravely, walk wisely toward those who are outside. By a brief saying he signified the greatest things.

But since Christ ordered us to despise certain scandals, the kinds must be distinguished. One kind they call taken, we shall call it pharisaical. It is, however, when wicked men are offended by either true and useful doctrine or a necessary deed, as when the Pharisees are offended because Christ performs a duty of charity, healing a little woman on the Sabbath. Such are scandals when the impious bear with difficulty the word of God being taught, and impious opinions being reproved. Here this rule must be held. It is necessary to obey God rather than men. When the Apostles began to teach the Gospel, a change of the divine law and of the entire Jewish commonwealth followed. However, changes of religions and of commonwealths cannot happen without the greatest scandals. And there is no doubt that Christ and the Apostles were most vehemently moved by those very scandals. Yet the doctrine and profession of the Gospel was not to be cast away. When Moses was about to lead the Israelites out of Egypt, what seditions and what tumults arose there. Finally, the kingdom, most flourishing and most beautiful in those times, was almost utterly overturned and destroyed. Such a change has infinite scandals. Yet it behooved Moses to perform his duty. These examples must be considered first so that we may understand what dangers, what offenses a change of doctrine carries with it, lest we rashly sow new and uncertain dogmas. Then, so that when, compelled by our calling, we teach what is right, pious, and useful, we may not be broken in spirit amidst such tumults and scandals. The prophets prophesy such movements, and indeed they command all things to yield to the word of God. Therefore Isaiah says, The mountains shall be humbled. Likewise, all the glory of the flesh is as the glory of hay. And the Psalm, Kings, understand. For we see that there have always been wonderful struggles of the word of God with Empires. Egypt tried to oppress Moses. The Assyrians, Chaldeans, and Syrians tried to oppress the Jews. The Jews and Romans tried to oppress Christ and the Apostles. And these struggles have always brought about huge changes in public affairs. If this disturbs minds, let us think that all things must yield to the word of God. This one Consolation is for the pious amidst these public dangers and greatest offenses. It is necessary to obey God rather than men. But it must be seen to that we teach and embrace the true and necessary things of the Church, and that we do not, incited by ambition, or by a desire for popular favor, or spurred on by other affections, rashly move the Church and scatter dogmas that are uncertain and useless for piety. These scandals must be avoided with the greatest care and vigilance. For every planting which the Father has not planted shall be rooted up. He who rashly moves the commonwealth or the Church both brings destruction upon himself, and because public disturbances have much evil and do not easily quiet down, he brings ruin to infinite numbers of people. For that is most true which Pindar said, ραδιον μὲν γὰρ πολιν σεισαι καὶ φαυροτεροις, ἀλλ' ἐπι χωρας αὐθις ἐασται δυσπαλες δη γινεται ἐξεπιναη, εἰ μη θεος ἁγεμονεαστικυβερνατηρ γενιται. that is, It is easy to move a city even for the worst of men. But to place it back in its seat again is difficult, unless God is present to the rulers who govern.

There is therefore also another kind of scandal, which they call given. That is, to harm the consciences of others by impious or useless doctrine, or by an unnecessary work done at an unseasonable time. Concerning these scandals, and especially concerning impious doctrine, Christ says, Woe to the man through whom the scandal comes. It is better that he be drowned in the depth of the sea, with a millstone hung from his neck. Of this kind, unlearned and superstitious men, sometimes even evil ones, have scattered innumerable scandals in the Church, namely idolatrous worship, the abuse of masses which they have turned to profit, impious worship of saints, impure celibacy, superstitious observations of monastic ceremonies and the like. The doctrine concerning works by which the benefit of Christ is obscured. And other things of this kind. And at this time the authors of horrible scandals are the Anabaptists, who also sow many impious opinions, then incite demented men to seditions and the dissipation of all civil affairs. They deny that it is lawful for Christians to hold magistracy, to exercise judgments, to hold property. And thus they teach a certain new monasticism, they dream that Christian righteousness is the contempt of civil affairs. This opinion first obscures the benefit of Christ and the righteousness of faith, then it is also a torch of seditions. These scandals must be fled, and prudence is needed to judge opinions. For snares cannot be detected immediately in all things, for most creep in under the appearance of piety. And from time to time the Devil excites impostors and hypocrites, who disturb the Church either with entirely impious, or with useless and frivolous opinions. They are And those scandals are to be avoided, which consist not in doctrine, but in examples, in the abuse of liberty in indifferent matters. For they greatly burden their own consciences, who render doctrine hateful to the weak, who by their petulance deter moderate men from the Gospel, who by their own examples confirm the license of the common people, or rashly stir up tumults. And how these things are to be moderated, Paul teaches, who says that he became a Jew to the Jews. For our whole life ought especially to look to this goal, that it may adorn Christ and the Gospel, so that as many as possible, moved by our examples, may embrace the Gospel. This end Christians ought to propose to themselves, wherefore they ought not to measure affairs by their own affections, but to serve the glory of Christ, and the necessity of others. That belongs to the school of Epicurus, not to Christians, to do nothing except for our own sake, to refer everything to our own glory, to our own pleasures. There are many and grave exhortations to the Romans, which teach that the use of liberty must be moderated, so that our petulance may not harm the Gospel, lest it be blasphemed, as he says, your good. Then what perversity and barbarity is this, in indifferent matters, to be unwilling to adapt oneself to the custom of others. Nothing is more humane than to love public laws and customs, and it is clearly tyranny to fight against the laws. unless we are compelled by the calling and command of God. Wherefore prudence must be applied in the use of liberty, especially in public custom among those who retain ancient customs, and it must be seen what becomes us, and what is expedient for the Gospel. Furthermore, the Apostles also declare their liberty by example, but against the stubborn who were persecuting the Gospel and were disapproving of the correct doctrine concerning liberty. Likewise, among those who were to be strengthened by their example. Such examples of the Apostles warn us when it is permitted for us to usurp liberty. Concerning private life I say nothing now, about which it is enough to know the rule of Paul. The kingdom of God is not food, nor drink. Thus far we have interpreted the admonition of Paul concerning scandals. Moreover, there are also many other cases of life in which it is greatly profitable to remember this precept, that we may walk wisely toward men, and especially for teachers there is need of great wisdom, lest they entangle themselves in civil affairs, lest they, trusting in the protections of public matters, undertake some turbulent business, or lest, led by the zeal or authority of certain men, they embrace uncertain things for certain. There is altogether a need for prudent governors of Churches of that very Epicharmian saying, μεμνησο ἀπιστειν, in which Cicero truly said that they are the nerves and joints of wisdom.

Redeeming the time.

First, he commands that each person should perform his own duty, then that we should be intent in our very calling upon all opportunities for managing affairs well, that they should use the times prudently and diligently, not otherwise than it behooves a good Emperor to observe all the moments of things to be done. In the Ephesians he adds the reason: because the days are evil. As if he were to say, all times must be skillfully bent toward accomplishing something good, because in such great vices of men, infinite impediments occur which weaken the diligence of the good. The governors of the Churches become more languid when they see the ingratitude, negligence, and sloth of men. Paul on the contrary exhorts them to fight against these inconveniences, and not to allow themselves to be wearied or deterred from doing good, they meet the occasions publicly and privately. How much evil did David see in the kingdom, yet he did not lose heart, but endured all difficulties, and took pains that, as much as he could in so many evils, he might nevertheless accomplish some good. Nor are functions to be cast aside for that reason, because events do not everywhere respond to our counsels or will. It is something to advance as far as one can if it is not given to go further. The Prophet says it will be that Israel shall be snatched away just as if someone were to snatch two legs of a torn sheep from the mouth of a lion. And this is for the most part the meaning of the discourse in Ecclesiastes, that we should accommodate ourselves to our calling and not be broken in spirit, even if sometimes we have made little progress. In the morning sow your seed, and in the evening do not withhold your hand, for you do not know which will prosper. So also we, even if the days are evil, that is, even if in these vices of men many things delay us and dissuade us from carrying out and administering affairs, nevertheless let us not despair nor be wearied, but let us strive as much as we can. And indeed it befits Christians to be solicitous and vigilant, and not to be wanting in opportunities of doing well. In the same sentiment there are many maxims among Solomon. The sluggard says, There is a lion in the way; that is, he despairs, he casts away all zeal for working, because he fears the difficulties that are wont to occur in carrying out affairs. But Christians ought to excel, as Paul says, in good works. However, they will never carry out anything, they will do nothing good, if they do not despise those difficulties. One must serve one's calling, and fidelity and vigilance must be shown. But when we experience by the thing itself that our strengths are not equal to the magnitude of the business, that must be held which Paul says, our sufficiency is from God.

Latin:

In sapientia ambulate.

Latissime patet hoc praeceptum in hac communi vita. Sed nos primum ad unum quoddam genus accommodabimus. Iubet cavere ne cui mores nostri scandalo sint. Idque maxime pertinet ad abusum libertatis, qui in doctos ac tenaces veterum morum deterret ab Evangelio, dum existimant hoc novo doctrinae genere, disciplinam labefactari, & effici homines prophanos potius, quam pios. E regione vero vulgus, quia natura odit legum ac religionis vincula, Sumit sibi non ferendam licentiam idque pretextu Evangelicae libertatis. Nam quia vident aliquas traditiones mitigatas esse, transferunt exemplum ad omnes leges. Nullis se legibus iam teneri existimant, & plane ceu ferae emissae ex carcere, quo quenque ducit impetus feruntur. Non servant fidem pactorum, contemnunt autoritatem magistratuum, diripiunt facultates Ecclesiarum, helluantur perpetuo, denique omnibus cupiditatibus frenum laxant. Tales mores profecto non leviter nocent Evangelio. Confertur enim culpa, ut solet fieri, in genus doctrinae. Debebamus autem Evangelium moribus nostris ornare, & optimis exemplis invitare homines ad Christum cognoscendum, sicut praeceptum est. Luceat lux vestra coram hominibut videant homines opera vestra bona, & glorificent patrem vestrum qui in caelis est. Itaque ob hanc caussam circumspectius agere nos omnia oportuit, ne cui nostrum exemplum noceret, ac ne male audiret Evangelium propter nos. Scimus enim quam horribilis comminatio de scandalo proposita sit. Vae homini per quem venit scandalum. Et ad scandala cavenda requiritur magna quaedam & prudentia & diligentia, ut non solum ea vitemus quae palam flagiciosa sunt, sed etiam in rebus alioqui licitis, moderemur & frenemus affectus, nec nobis omnia permittamus, sed cogitemus serviendum esse isti theatro, in quod propter Christi gloriam producti sumus. Omnia mihi licent, inquit Paulus, sed non omnia expediunt. Illud igitur videamus quid expediat ad invitandas hominum animos, ad cognoscendum Evangelium, quibus rebus Evangelii gloria fiat illustrior. Non obsequamur cupiditati vindictae, Iniurias publicae tranquillitati condonemus, non certemus odio, conviciis & scurrilitate cum hostibus Evangelii, nulla emineat ambitionis significatio, Fugiamus temulentiam, multa enim ebriis erumpunt ex quibus fit iudicium de animo. In communi conver satione sit gravitate temperata comitas atque humanitas Denique si histriones in scena videre iubentur qui deceat, maxime nos oportuit dare operam ut decorum & intelligeremus, & observaremus in hoc theatro, in quo oculis observamur omnium hominum, & non solum nos periclitamur, sed etiam poema ipsum quod representamus, hoc est, caelestis doctrinae autoritas. Vide qualem Philosophiam requirat hoc praeceptum de cavendis scandalis. Ideo Paulus gravissime dixit, sapienter ambulate erga eos qui foris sunt. Brevi dicto maximas res significavit.

Sed quoniam Christus nos iussit quaedam scandala contemnere, discernenda sunt genera. Quoddam scandalum vocant acceptum, nos pharisaicum appellabimus. Est autem cum vel vera atque utili doctrina vel necessario facto, offenduntur homines iniqui, ut cum offenduntur pharisei, quod Christus facit officium caritatis, curans mulierculam in Sabbato. Talia scandala sunt, cum impii aegre ferunt doceri verbum Dei, ac reprehendi impias opiniones Hic tenenda est regula. Oportet Deo magis obedire quam hominibus Cum Apostoli inciperent Evangelium docere, sequebatur mutatio legis divinae, & totius reipub. Iu daicae. Non possunt autem fieri mutationes religionum ac rerum publicarum sine max imis scandalis. Nec dubium est quin illis ipsis scandalis vehementissime commoti sint & Christus & Apostoli. Non fuit tamen abiicienda Evangelii doctrina ac professio Cum Moises educturus esset Israelitas ex aegypto, quae ibi seditiones qui tumultus exorti sunt. Tandem regnum illis temporibus florentissimum & pulcherrimum pene funditus eversum ac deletum est. Talis mutatio habet infinita scandala. Moisen tamen oportuit suo fungi officio. Haec exempla consideranda sunt primum ut intelligamus, quae pericula, quas offensiones secum trahat doctrinae mutatio, ne temere seramus novae & incerta dogmata. Deinde ut cum vocatione nostra coacti, recta, pia atque utilia docemus, non frangamur animis inter huiusmodi tumultus & scandala. Prophetae vaticinantur tales motus, & quidem iubent omnia cedere verbo Dei. Ideo inquit Esaias Montes humiliabuntur. Item omnis gloria carnis ceu gloria foeni. Et Psalmus Reges intelligite. Videmus enim verbo Dei cum Imperiis mirifica certamina semper fuisse. Aegyptus conabatur opprimere Moisen. Assyrii, Chaldei, Syri, Iudeos. Iudei ac Romani Christum & Apostolos. Atque haec certamina semper ingentes mutationes rerum publicarum attulerunt. Hoc si perturbat animos, cogitemus omnia debere verbo Dei cedere. Haec una consolatio est piorum inter haec pericula publica et summas offensiones. Oportet Deo magis obedire, quam hominibus. Sed videndum est, ut vera & necessaria Ecclesiae doceamus, & amplectamur nec incitati ambitione, aut popularis aurae studio, aut aliis affectibus extimulati temere moveamus Ecclesiam, ac spargamus dogmata incerta, & inutilia ad pietatem. Haec scandala cavenda sunt summa cura & vigilantia. Nam omnis plantatio quam non plantavit pater, eradicabitur. Qui temere movet rem publicam seu Ecclesiam, & sibi ipsi exitium accersit, & quia publici motus plurimum habent mali, nec facile consilescunt, infinitis hominibus perniciem affert. Verissimum enim illud est, quod dixit Pindarus, ραδιον μὲν γὰρ πολιν σεισαι καὶ φαυροτεροις, ἀλλ' ἐπι χωρας αὐθις ἐασται δυσπαλες δη γινεται ἐξεπιναη, εἰ μη θεος ἁγεμονεαστικυβερνατηρ γενιται. hoc est, Facile est civitatem movere etiam deterrimis. Sed iterum in sua sede eum collocare difficile est, nisi Deus adsit principibus gubernator.

Est itaque & alterum scandali genus, quod datum vocant. Id est doctrina impia, aut inutili, aut opere non necessario intempestive facto nocere aliorum conscientiis. De his scandalis, ac praecipue de doctrina impia ait Christus, Vae homini per quem venit scandalum. Praestar et eum demergi in profundum mare, mola de collo suspensa. Huius generis scandala innumerabilia sparserunt in Ecclesia homines indocti & supersticiosi, interdum etiam mali, videlicet cultus Idolatricos, missarum abusum quas ad quaestum contulerunt, Cultus impios divorum, Impurum celibatum, supersticiosas observationes ceremoniarum monasticarum et similium. Doctrinam de operibus qua obscuratum est beneficium Christi. Et hoc genus alia. Et hoc tempore horribilium scandalorum autores sunt Anabaptistae, qui & multas impias opiniones serunt, deinde dementatos homines incitant ad seditiones & dissipationem omnium civilium rerum. Negat Christianis licere Magistratus gerere, indicia exercere, tenere proprium. Atque ita novum quendam monachatum docent, somniant iusticiam Christianam esse, contemptum rerum civilium. Haec opinio primum obscurat beneficium Christi & iusticiam fidei, deinde etiam fax est seditionum. Haec scandala fugienda sunt, & prudentia opus est, ad diiudicandas opiniones. Non enim in omnibus statim deprehendi insidiae possunt, pleraeque enim obrepunt specie pictatis. Et subinde excitat Diabolus imposteres ac hypocritas, qui Ecclesiam perturbent aut prorsus impiis, aut inutilibus ac frivolis opinionibus. Sunt & illa scandala cavenda, quae non in doctrina, sed in exemplis versantur, in abusu libertatis in rebus adiaphoris. Valde enim onerant conscientias suas, qui reddunt invisam doctrinam infirmis, qui petulantia sua deterrent homines moderatos ab Evangelio, qui suis exemplis licentiam vulgi confirmant, aut temere tumultus excitant. Atque haec quomodo moderanda sint, docet Paulus, qui ait se Iudaeis Iudaeum factum esse. Tota enim vita nostra praecipue ad hunc scopum spectare debet, ut ornet Christum & Evangelium, ut quam plurimi nostris exemplis commoti amplectamur Evangelium. Hunc finem debent sibi proponere Christiani, quare non debent suis affectibus metiri negocia, sed servire gloriae Christi, & alienae necessitati. Illud ad Epicuri soholam, non ad Christianos pertinet, nihil nisi nostra causa facere, omnia referre ad nostram gloriam, ad nostras voluptates. Multae sunt & graves adhortationes ad Romanos, quae decent moderandum esse libertatis usum, ita ne nostra petulantia noceat Evangelio, ne blasphemetur ut inquit, bonum vestrum Deinde quae est haec perversitas ac barbaries, in rebus adiaphoris, nolle se attemperare aliorum consuetudini. Nihil humanius est, quam leges ac mores publicos amare, ac plane Tyrannis est cum legibus pugnare, nisi cum. vocatione & mandato Dei cogimur. Quare prudentia in usu libertatis adhibenda est praesertim in publica consuetudine apud eos qui veteres mores retinent, & videndum quid nos deceat, & quid expediat Evangelio. Caeterum & Apostoli declarant exemplo libertatem, sed adversus praefractos qui Evangelium persequebantur, & rectam de libertate doctrinam improbabant. Item apud eos qui exemplo confirmandi erant. Talia Apostolorum exempla monent, quando nobis libertatem usurpare liceat. De privata vita nunc nihil dico, de qua satis est nosse regulam Pauli. Regnum Dei non est esca, nec potus. Hactenus de scandalis interpretati sumus Pauli admonitionem. Ceterum multi alii vitae casus etiam sunt. in quibus magnopere prodest meminisse hoc praeceptum, ut sapienter ambulemus erga homines, ac praesertim doctoribus magna sapientia opus est, ne implicent se negociis civilibus, ne praesidiis rerum publicarum confisi suscipiant aliquod turbulentum negocium, neve studio vel autoritate certorum hominum adducti amplectentur incerta pro certis. Omnino opus est prudentibus gubernatoribus Eclesiarum illo ipso Epicharmio dicto, μεμνησο ἀπιστειν, in quo vere dixit Cicero nervos atque artus esse sapientiae.

Occasionem redimentes.

Primum praecipit ut suum quisque officium faciat, deinde ut in ipsa vocatione intenti simus in omnes occasiones rei bene gerendae, prudenter & gnaviter utantur temporibus, non aliter atque Imperatorem bonum oportet omnia rerum gerendarum momenta observare. In Ephesiis addit rationem. quia dies mali sunt. quasi dicat, omnia tempora arte flectenda sunt ad aliquid boni efficiendum, quia in tantis hominum viciis, infinita incidunt impedimenta quae diligentiam bonorum debilitant. Gubernatores Ecclesiarum fiunt languidiores cum vident hominum ingratitudinem, negligentiam, ignaviam. Paulus contra hortatur eos, ut cum his incommodis pugnent, nec defatigari, aut deterreri se a benefaciendo sinant, occurunt occasionibus publice ac privatim. Quantum mali vidit in regno David, tamen non despondit animum, sed omnes difficultates pertulit, & dedit operam, ut quantum posset in tot malis, aliquid boni tamen efficeret, Nec propterea abiiciendae sunt functiones, quia non ubique respondent eventus nostris consiliis aut voluntati. Est aliquo prodire tenus si non datur ultra. Propheta ait fore, ut eripiatur Israel velut si quis duo crura laceratae pecudis, ex ore leonis eripiat. Atque haec magna ex parte sententia est concionis in Ecclesiaste, ut vocationi morem geramus, nec frangamur animis, etiamsi interdum parum profecerimus. Manere & vespere inquit seras semen tuum, quia nescis utrum proveniat. Ita & nos etsi dies mali sunt, hoc est etsi in his hominum viciis multa nos remorantur, ac dehortantur a rebus gerendis atque administrandis, tamen non desperemus nec defatigemur, sed connitamur quantum possumus. Et quidem Christianos decet esse sollicitos, ac vigilantes, et non deesse occasionibus bene merendi. In eandem sententiam multae sunt gnomae apud Salomonem. Piger dicit Leo est in viae hoc est desperat, abiicit omne studium operandi, quia metuit difficultates quae solent incidere in rebus gerendis. Verum Christiani debent excellere, ut inquit Paulus, in bonis operibus. Nihil autem unquam gerent, nihil boni facient, si illas difficultates non contemnent, Vocationi serviendum est, & praestanda fides ac vigilantia. Cum autem re ipsa experiamur nostras vires magnitudini negociorum non esse pares, id tenendum est, quod Paulus ait, sufficientia nostra ex Deo est.

## 4:6
Let your speech be seasoned with salt that you may be able to answer &c.

This sentiment ought to be understood concerning public sermons and private conversations. For first, public sermons ought to have some salt, and salt in the Holy Scriptures signifies the word of God, just as it was signified in the law that no sacrifice should be offered without salt, that is, that the word of God should be employed in ceremonies. Let sermons, therefore, not be empty fables, such as were once recited, fictitious and prodigious histories of the saints, such as were the disputations about purgatory, about indulgences, and about many other things which contribute nothing to edification. And even now many consume entire sermons only in insults against monks. Sermons of this kind are truly insipid and tasteless; they do not teach, they do not stir any pious affection in the mind of the listener. Yet these ought to be the ends of sermons: that they both teach the listener about some point necessary for piety, and send him away with some affection, namely, that they may rouse fear, and raise up faith, and kindle love toward neighbors. And since Paul therefore wishes sermons to be seasoned with salt, so that we may be able to answer others concerning religion and explain the doctrine of the Gospel, he requires learned sermons, in which men are taught in an orderly, apt, and complete manner. Nor is this a light craft, as the common people think, for it requires both exceptional prudence, and perfect doctrine, and the knowledge of those letters and arts which hand down the method of teaching. But these things are not of this place; I only wished to warn what kind of sermons Paul demands to be held in the Church. Now they do not even do this. Those who preside over the Churches should teach the people, so that they may heal doubting consciences. The matter is conducted with threatening edicts. And many demand that they be believed without any testimonies, without any probable reason, as if that αὐτος ἐφα. were enough for consciences. It is to be desired, therefore, that God may give the Church pious and good teachers. Furthermore, even private conversations ought to be seasoned with salt, that is, with the word of God. The deliberations, speeches, and colloquies of Christians ought to be seasoned with sentiments of piety, and referred to the word and will of God. And indeed, just as harshness in speech and scurrility ought to be absent from Christians, so the greatest effort must be made that there be a certain charm and urbanity mixed with gravity, and sweetness, which cannot exist except from a great abundance and variety of doctrine. Finally, we see here that it is commanded to all Christians that they diligently learn the scriptures so that we may be able to confirm both our own conscience and others. This commandment, often repeated elsewhere, ought to be a care to all the pious.

PRINTED AT WITTENBERG. BY JOSEPH KLUG.

Latin:

Sermo vester sit sale conditus ut po-ssitis respondere &c.

Haec sententia de publicis concionibus & de privatis colloquiis intelligi debet. Primum enim publicae conciones debent aliquid habere salis, significat autem sal in sacris literis verbum Dei sicut in lege significabatur ne qua hostia offerretur sine sale, hoc est ut in ceremoniis adhiberetur verbum Dei. Sint igitur conciones non inanes fabulae, quales olim recitabantur commenticiae ac prodigiosae divorum historiae, quales erant disputationes de purgatorio, de indulgentiis, deque aliis multis rebus quae nihil ad aedificationem faciunt. Et nunc coniumunt multi totas conciones tantumin conviciis monachorum. Huiusmodi conciones vere sunt insipidae atque insulsae, non docent, non citent aliquem pium affectum in animo auditoris. Debent autem concionum hi fines esse, ut & doceant auditorem de aliquo loco ad pietatem necessario, et dimittant eum in aliquo affectu, videlicet ut timorem exuscitent, & erigant fidem, & incendant dilectionem erga proximos. Et quoniam Paul. ideo vult condiri conciones sale, ut respondere aliis de religione, & explicare doctrinam Evangelii possimus, requirit eruditas conciones, in quibus ordine, apte & integre doceantur homines. Neque id est leve artificium ut vulgo existimant, requirit enim & eximiam prudentiam, & perfectam doctrinam, & earum literarum atque artium cognitionem, quae rationem docendi tradunt. Sed haec non sunt huius loci, tantum monere volui, quales conciones Paulus in Ecclesia haberi postulet. Nunc ne id quidem agunt hi qui praesunt Ecclesiis ut doceant populum, ut sanent ambigentes conscientias. Res geritur minacibus edictis. Et multi postulant sibi credisine ullis testimoniis, sine ulla probabili ratione, quasisatis sit conscientiis illud αὐτος ἐφα. Optandum est igitur ut Deus Ecclesiae det pios & bonos doctores. Caeterum & privati sermones debent sale hoc est, verbo dei condiri. Christianorum deliberationes, sermones atque colloquia debent condiri sententiis pietatis, & adverbum ac voluntatem Dei referri. Et quidem ut abesse a Christianis debent asperitas in sermone, & scurrilitas, ita maxime danda est opera, ut sit quidam gravitate mixtus lepos atque urbanitas, ac suavitas, quae non potest existere, nisi ex magna quadam doctrinae copia ac varictate, Postremo videmus hic praecipi omnibus Christianis, ut diligenter discant scripturas ut & nostram conscientiam & alios confirmare possimus. Id praeceptum sepe alias repetitum, curae omnibus piis esse debet.

IMPRESSUM VITEBERGAE. PER IOSEPHUM KLUG.
